### [1] [HyperWorld: Hypergraph-Structured State Serialization Improves Learned Textual World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00002)
*Yun-Jian Zhang,Chen-Wei Liang,Tian-Yi Zhang,Jian Ding,Yi-Lun Wu,Ao-Bo Li,Wei-Cong Su,Saifullah,Hong-Yu An,Mu-Jiang-Shan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperWorld is a study showing that hyperedge-based state serialization (grouping facts around entities and relations) improves learned textual world models, especially for medium-scale models and under distribution shift, outperforming other serialization formats like independent sentences or pairwise triples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The role of state serialization structure in learned textual world models is underexplored. While world models help language-model agents predict environment dynamics and plan, different ways of serializing symbolic state descriptions may affect model performance, particularly how well they can learn action effects from text.

Method: HyperWorld presents a controlled study comparing four state serialization methods: 1) raw observations, 2) independent sentences, 3) pairwise triples, and 4) entity-centered hyperedge units that group multiple related facts around entities and relations. All variants use the same training objective: given a state and an action, predict symbolic effects or judge the action infeasible. The study tests these across different model scales (0.5B-1.5B parameters), data budgets, and both in-distribution and out-of-distribution test worlds.

Result: Hyperedge serialization gives clearest gains for medium-scale models (0.5B-1.5B) and under distribution shift. While larger models reduce the performance gap, and pairwise triples can match/exceed hyperedges on in-distribution exact match, hyperedges achieve strongest out-of-distribution fact F1 and best trade-off between feasibility detection and effect prediction at small-to-medium scales. In downstream greedy planning, hyperedge world models attain the highest success rate among tested representations.

Conclusion: Higher-order state organization (via hyperedge serialization) provides a simple but effective inductive bias for learned symbolic world models, especially when model capacity is limited or test environments differ from training. This demonstrates that how states are serialized matters significantly for learning effective textual world models.

Abstract: World models enable language-model agents to predict environment dynamics and plan before acting. In text environments, the model must learn symbolic action effects from serialized state descriptions, but the role of serialization structure remains underexplored. We present HyperWorld, a controlled study of state serialization for learned textual world models. We compare raw observations with three symbolic serializations of the same ground-truth state: independent sentences, pairwise triples, and entity-centered hyperedge units that group multiple related facts around entities and relations. All variants use the same training objective: given a state and an action, predict symbolic effects or judge the action infeasible. Across model scales, data budgets, and in-distribution and out-of-distribution test worlds, hyperedge serialization gives the clearest gains for 0.5B--1.5B models and under distribution shift. Larger models reduce the gap, and pairwise triples can match or slightly exceed hyperedges on in-distribution exact match, but hyperedges achieve the strongest out-of-distribution fact F1 and the best small-to-medium scale trade-off between feasibility detection and effect prediction. In downstream greedy planning, the hyperedge world model also attains the highest success rate among the tested representations. These results show that higher-order state organization is a simple but effective inductive bias for learned symbolic world models, especially when model capacity is limited or test environments differ from training.

</details>


### [2] [Long-Horizon State Tracking in LLMs: Executing MD5 through a Deep Sequence of Dependent Tool Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00012)
*Dheeraj Mohandas Pai,Lu Xian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can successfully track and carry exact intermediate state across 196 dependent tool calls when computing MD5 hash, showing they can handle long-horizon state-tracking tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-horizon benchmarks for LLMs confound state-tracking difficulty with instruction interpretation, lack control groups, and are vulnerable to shortcuts like hallucinated answers. It's unclear whether LLMs can carry exact intermediate state across many dependent tool calls at all.

Method: Cleanly test state-tracking by having LLMs compute MD5 hash step-by-step: 196 dependent tool calls over 64 rounds while carrying four 32-bit words in context between calls. Implement MD5 from scratch to align with ground-truth trace and check digest bit-perfectly. Test gpt-oss-120b and a stronger version replacing every primitive tool with a second LLM (driver-worker architecture).

Result: gpt-oss-120b successfully carries full state across all 196 calls and returns correct digest on majority of completed runs. Two key factors for success: keeping model's own reasoning in context each turn, and voting over a thinking-enabled worker to remove arithmetic slips. Residual failures can be localized by origin (state-carrying vs arithmetic vs serving).

Conclusion: LLMs are capable of long-horizon state-tracking when tested cleanly, with success depending on context management techniques rather than weight changes. The MD5 task provides a clean benchmark isolating state-carrying ability from other confounds.

Abstract: Long-horizon tasks remain uncommon in large language model (LLM) evaluation, and for a reason: when each step depends on the last, per-step accuracy that looks excellent in isolation decays catastrophically, as errors cascade and the end-to-end failure probability grows sharply with length. Existing agentic benchmarks report end-to-end success but confound this state-tracking difficulty with instruction interpretation, give no control group that isolates it, and are vulnerable to shortcuts such as a hallucinated final answer, so they cannot say why a long run fails. Whether an LLM can carry exact intermediate state across many tool calls at all is itself not well established. We test this cleanly by having the model compute a cryptographic hash, MD5, step by step: a sequence of $196$ dependent tool calls over $64$ rounds while it carries four $32$-bit words $(a,b,c,d)$ in its own context from one call to the next. Interpretation is trivial and, because we implement MD5 from scratch (RFC~1321), we align every call to the ground-truth trace and check the digest to the bit, so any failure is pure bookkeeping. gpt-oss-120b, a mixture-of-experts model with only $\sim$5.5B active parameters per token, at temperature $0$ with a short fixed prompt, carries the full state across all $196$ calls and returns the correct digest on a majority of completed runs. In the strongest setting we replace every primitive tool with a second LLM, so a driver and a worker compute the whole hash from scratch with no exact-arithmetic oracle in the loop. Two ingredients decide success and neither changes the weights: keeping the model's own reasoning in its context each turn, and voting over a thinking-enabled worker to remove its modular-arithmetic slips. We localize the residual failures by origin, separating state-carrying from arithmetic and from serving.

</details>


### [3] [I-CARE: Analysis of interference-related phenomena in a controllable, diverse and representative unlearning setting for text-to-image models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00003)
*Leonardo Santiago Benitez Pereira,Marcos Escudero Viñolo,Luis Herranz Arribas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: I-CARE is a methodology for formalizing and systematically studying interference (unintended degradation of related concepts) in generative machine unlearning, providing standardized definitions, metrics, and reporting templates rather than a new benchmark or algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine unlearning aims to remove specific knowledge from AI models, but current approaches poorly characterize and inconsistently evaluate interference - the unintended degradation of semantically related concepts that should have been retained. There's a lack of systematic study of this important phenomenon in generative unlearning research.

Method: I-CARE provides formal definitions for tasks, metrics, and reporting templates to systematically study interference. It decouples long-term scientific insight from transient empirical results, works across evolving models/algorithms, and includes software implementation with web-based interface for accessibility.

Result: I-CARE enables meaningful analysis of interference patterns across multiple unlearning settings. A feasibility demonstration with state-of-the-art algorithms and datasets shows the framework's practical applicability. The methodology successfully establishes interference as a first-class object of study in generative unlearning.

Conclusion: I-CARE provides a standardized methodology for systematically studying interference in generative machine unlearning, enabling reproducible research and meaningful comparison across different approaches. The open-source implementation makes this accessible to researchers without requiring specialized tools or coding expertise.

Abstract: Machine unlearning studies the removal of knowledge from an AI model, making the system forget a concept it previously learned. Despite rapid progress in generative machine unlearning, the unintended degradation of semantically related concepts that should have been retained (henceforth, interference) remains poorly characterized and inconsistently evaluated. This paper introduces I-CARE, a methodology that formalizes interference as a first-class object of study in generative unlearning. Rather than proposing a new benchmark or unlearning algorithm, I-CARE provides formal definitions for tasks, metrics, and templates for reporting results, enabling the systematic and reproducible study of interference across unlearning settings. While our methodology is designed to remain valid as models and unlearning algorithms evolve, decoupling long-term scientific insight from transient empirical results, we present a feasibility demonstration with state-of-the-art algorithms and frequently used datasets. The results demonstrate that I-CARE enables meaningful analysis of interference patterns across multiple unlearning settings, establishing the practical applicability of the framework. The software implementation of the methodology is provided in an open-source framework, together with a web-based graphical interface that enables exploration of the outcomes of this study without requiring direct interaction with the codebase or specialized data analysis tools.

</details>


### [4] [EULER: Exploring Underused Links with Evidence-Checked Return for Multi-Agent Mathematical Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00032)
*Ren Zhenzhuo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EULER is a multi-agent AI system that solves mathematical conjectures by searching for "bridges" between different mathematical domains, using stress tests to validate transfers before expensive search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mathematical communities work with different objects, invariants, and tools, making cross-domain problem transfer expensive and often skipped. The paper aims to automate this transfer process to solve mathematical conjectures more effectively.

Method: EULER uses a multi-agent system with three types of routes: direct, adjacent-domain, and distant-domain. Each "bridge" (domain transfer) is validated through six ordered stress tests before expensive search begins. Bridges must provide operations the source representation cannot execute and return evidence that can be checked back to the original statement.

Result: Evaluated on 120 recent conjectures from combinatorial theory papers, EULER produced 10 proofs, 3 refutations, and 45 scoped partial results. Bridge-specific stress tests reduced incorrect conclusions from 9 to 3, and combining bridge material with target-native operations yielded a positive interaction of +4.2 resolved tasks.

Conclusion: Executable operation gain and valid return across bridges are better predictors of success than domain distance alone. The stress test mechanism effectively filters invalid bridges before costly search, and cross-domain transfers can yield solutions that single-domain approaches miss.

Abstract: Mathematical communities work with different objects, invariants, and tools, so transferring a problem across them is expensive and often skipped. We present EULER, a multi-agent system that takes such a transfer--a bridge--as its unit of search. Around a fixed conjecture, EULER runs direct, adjacent-domain, and distant-domain routes in competition; a bridge keeps its budget only if it supplies an operation the source representation cannot execute and its target-side evidence returns to the original statement along a checked implication. Six ordered stress tests reject invalid bridges before expensive search begins.
  We evaluate EULER on 120 recent conjectures. The conjectures were frozen before search and screened for contamination, and are drawn from public papers by authors who had recently published in the Journal of Combinatorial Theory, Series A, a leading journal in combinatorics. EULER produced 10 proofs and 3 refutations, plus 45 scoped partial results. Two mechanisms held up under ablation: bridge-specific stress tests cut incorrect conclusions from 9 to 3, and bridge material combined with a target-native operation yielded a positive interaction of +4.2 resolved tasks that neither factor produced alone. Domain distance did not reliably predict success; executable operation gain and valid return did.

</details>


### [5] [Discrete-Time MDP Modeling for Multi-Item Capacitated Lot Sizing with Stochastic Demand Timing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00004)
*Léa Bayati,Mohamed Dahmoune,Melek Rodoplu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A genetic algorithm is proposed for multi-item capacitated lot-sizing with stochastic demand timing, achieving near-optimal solutions with ~3.44% average optimality gap and significant computational speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses computational challenges in multi-item capacitated lot-sizing with stochastic demand-arrival periods, where demand quantities are deterministic but timing is uncertain. Stochastic timing substantially increases state space complexity and computational requirements compared to deterministic versions.

Method: The problem is formulated as a discrete-time Markov decision process (DTMDP). A genetic algorithm (GA) searches over feasible state-feedback policies, evaluating each policy exactly under the DTMDP transition model. Computational experiments compare stochastic instances with deterministic counterparts and test the GA on 330 benchmark instances.

Result: Stochastic timing substantially increases computational complexity compared to deterministic versions. The GA achieves average optimality gap of ~3.44% and remains below 5% optimality gap on difficult instances. For 90 difficult cases, it achieves average speedup of 6.89±1.41 at 95% confidence level.

Conclusion: The proposed GA effectively handles stochastic timing in capacitated lot-sizing, providing near-optimal solutions with significant computational efficiency. It maintains reasonable optimality gaps while overcoming the computational explosion caused by stochastic timing.

Abstract: This paper studies a finite-horizon multi-item capacitated lot-sizing problem in which demand quantities are deterministic, while demand-arrival periods are stochastic. Each demand occurs once within a known time window and must be satisfied no later than its deadline. The proposed model makes production and allocation decisions at the demand level, allowing it to represent capacity competition, demand-specific backlog, and allocation-dependent inventory dynamics. The stochastic problem is formulated as a discrete-time Markov decision process (DTMDP), including the state space, feasible actions, transition kernel, and one-period cost function. To isolate the computational effect of stochastic timing, each stochastic instance is first compared with a deterministic counterpart in which each arrival distribution is replaced by its most likely arrival period. This comparison shows that stochastic timing substantially increases the number of states, the number of transitions, solution time, and memory pressure. A genetic algorithm (GA) is then proposed for the stochastic-timing problem. The GA searches over feasible state-feedback policies and evaluates each policy exactly under the DTMDP transition model. Computational experiments on 330 benchmark instances show that the GA remains close to the exact stochastic solution whenever the latter is available, with an average optimality gap of about $3.44\%$. On the difficult benchmark instances, comprising 90 test cases, the GA remains below the $5\%$ optimality-gap threshold and achieves an average optimization speedup of $6.89 \pm 1.41$ at the $95\%$ confidence level. For instances that cannot be solved exactly on the available hardware, an empirical Bellman-time regression is used to estimate the missing exact resolution time and extrapolate the expected GA speedup.

</details>


### [6] [Control-Data Flow Separation: Stable Prompt Optimization in Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00621)
*Wentao Zhang,Syed Shariyar Murtaza,Junaid Ahmad Bhatti,Utkarsh Soni,Yifan Nie,Eugene Wen,Yuntian Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework that separates execution-critical control protocols (structured) from task-relevant content (unstructured) in multi-agent LLM systems to enable safe prompt optimization without breaking agent pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current prompt optimization in multi-agent LLM systems is risky because prompts serve two entangled roles: generating task content and specifying execution-critical protocols (routing, formatting, termination). Optimizing content generation can accidentally corrupt protocols and cause entire pipelines to fail.

Method: Proposed control-data flow separation where execution-critical control is represented as typed, validated program objects (control flow), while task-relevant language remains the optimizable data flow for agent communication. This protects protocol interfaces from prompt drift.

Result: Across synthetic reasoning, collaborative review generation, and insurance rating workflows, the framework achieves 100% eventual protocol validity while consistently improving task performance.

Conclusion: Separating control protocols from content data flow enables safe and effective prompt optimization in multi-agent LLM systems, preventing pipeline failures while improving task performance.

Abstract: Prompt optimization can improve multi-agent LLM systems, but the prompts being optimized often serve two entangled roles: generating task-relevant content and specifying execution-critical protocols, such as message routing, output formatting, and termination signals, on which the underlying code relies. As a result, a prompt edit intended to improve content generation can inadvertently corrupt the protocol and cause the entire agent pipeline to fail. Our key observation is that these two roles have different representations: execution protocols are typically structured, while task-relevant content is usually expressed in unstructured language. Based on this, we propose control-data flow separation, where execution-critical control is represented as typed, validated program objects, while task-relevant language remains the optimizable data flow for agent communication. This design allows optimizers to improve multi-agent behavior without exposing the routing or formatting interface to prompt drift. Across synthetic reasoning, collaborative review generation, and insurance rating workflows, our framework empirically achieves 100% eventual protocol validity while consistently improving task performance.

</details>


### [7] [Incremental Risk Assessment of Progressive Elder Financial Scams via Instruction-Tuned Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00005)
*Parviz Ghafariasl,Weimin Fu,Xiaolong Guo,Shing I. Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A cumulative turn-based risk assessment framework using fine-tuned small language models for detecting financial scams targeting older adults through text/voice conversations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial scams targeting older adults are increasing through text/voice channels and unfold over multiple conversational turns with escalating tactics. Current detection methods need to continuously update risk estimates incrementally across turns while being suitable for resource-constrained deployment settings.

Method: Proposed a cumulative turn-based risk assessment framework that incrementally aggregates conversational turns and re-estimates risk at each step. Constructed a multi-turn dialogue dataset covering investment, charity, and tech support scam scenarios with detailed annotations at each cumulative stage. Fine-tuned four small language models (Phi-4, LLaMA-3.2, DeepSeek-R1, and Qwen3) under a unified training framework.

Result: Fine-tuned small models successfully captured fraud-related linguistic cues and cross-turn escalation patterns while maintaining compact architectures. Phi-4 and LLaMA-3.2 achieved stronger turn-aware risk estimation performance relative to their parameter scale among the evaluated models.

Conclusion: Structured cumulative modeling can support incremental scam risk assessment in deployment-oriented settings, highlighting the potential of compact language models for privacy-aware and on-device fraud protection for older adults.

Abstract: Financial scams targeting older adults increasingly occur through text and voice channels such as email, SMS, and phone calls, unfolding over multiple conversational turns that begin with impersonation or casual contact, escalate through trust building and urgency, and culminate in requests for sensitive information or financial transfers. Because risk signals emerge incrementally across turns, effective detection requires models that continuously update risk estimates under resource-constrained deployment settings. We propose a cumulative turn-based risk assessment framework that incrementally aggregates conversational turns and re-estimates risk at each step, enabling dynamic scam monitoring across progressively evolving conversations. A multi-turn dialogue dataset is constructed to cover investment, charity, and tech support scam scenarios, with each dialogue containing two to eight turns and annotated at every cumulative stage with a qualitative risk level, a continuous risk score, an explanatory rationale, and a safety recommendation. Four small language models (Phi-4, LLaMA-3.2, DeepSeek-R1, and Qwen3) are fine-tuned and evaluated under a unified training framework. Fine-tuned small models capture fraud-related linguistic cues and cross-turn escalation patterns while maintaining compact architectures suitable for mobile and resource-constrained deployment settings. Among the evaluated models, Phi-4 and LLaMA-3.2 achieve stronger turn-aware risk estimation performance relative to their parameter scale. These results suggest that structured cumulative modeling can support incremental scam risk assessment in deployment-oriented settings while highlighting the potential of compact language models for privacy-aware and on-device fraud protection.

</details>


### [8] [ChatDev 2.0: A No-Code Multi-Agent Platform for Developing Everything](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00714)
*Yufan Dang,Shu Yao,Bowen Lai,Chenting Xu,Ruijie Shi,Wai-Shing Leung,Huatao Li,Chen Qian,Zhiyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChatDev 2.0 (DevAll) is a no-code platform for building multi-agent systems that offers both high expressiveness and ease of use through visual programming and a cycle-aware execution engine.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent systems face a tradeoff: code frameworks are expressive but engineering-intensive, while no-code builders simplify authoring but constrain agent interactions to predefined workflows. There's a need for a platform that combines both expressiveness and ease of use.

Method: DevAll pairs a declarative executable graph abstraction with a cycle-aware execution engine to represent and execute heterogeneous agents and dynamic/cyclic interactions. It provides an integrated visual interface for authoring, running, monitoring, and inspecting multi-agent systems without writing code, including human-in-the-loop steps.

Result: DevAll successfully reproduces state-of-the-art MAS across three representative tasks at competitive performance without requiring task-specific orchestration code.

Conclusion: DevAll is an effective general-purpose platform for LLM-based multi-agent systems that addresses the expressiveness vs. ease-of-use tradeoff through its no-code approach with visual programming capabilities.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) have shown strong potential for solving complex tasks, yet their development forces a tradeoff: code frameworks are expressive but engineering-intensive, while no-code builders simplify authoring but constrain agent interactions to author-defined workflows. We present ChatDev 2.0: DevAll (hereafter DevAll), a no-code platform for building, executing, and inspecting heterogeneous MAS that delivers both high expressiveness and ease of use. In terms of expressiveness, DevAll pairs a declarative executable graph abstraction with a cycle-aware execution engine, so that heterogeneous agents and dynamic and cyclic interactions can be represented and executed within a single framework. For ease of use, an integrated visual interface lets users author, run, monitor, and inspect MAS, including human-in-the-loop steps, entirely without writing code. Experiments demonstrate that DevAll reproduces state-of-the-art MAS across three representative tasks at competitive performance and without task-specific orchestration code, highlighting its effectiveness as a general-purpose platform for LLM-based MAS. DevAll is available at https://github.com/OpenBMB/ChatDev.

</details>


### [9] [OpenAgentFlow: Enabling System-Wide Safety Boundaries for Heterogeneous AI Agent Fleets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00015)
*Dongsheng Chen,Xiangyu Zhao,Xin Yao,Xuetao Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenAgentFlow is a safety architecture for AI agent systems that enforces policies at the action-commit boundary, unifying GUI actions, API calls, and tool calls into a single governance stream for multi-agent environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents evolve from isolated assistants to heterogeneous systems with multiple agents operating in shared environments, existing safety mechanisms are fragmented and insufficient. Current safeguards cover individual components but leave enforcement fragmented, obscure risks in multi-step flows, and provide limited auditability and policy evolution support for system-level action governance.

Method: OpenAgentFlow uses a control-plane/action-plane architecture that normalizes pending GUI actions, API calls, tool calls, and LLM-generated invocations into a unified AgentEvent stream. Each event is routed through a shared pre-execution Policy Enforcement Point, while maintaining provenance, session state, audit records, and updatable policies in the control plane.

Result: The system achieved 94.0% accuracy and 95.3% attack block rate on a 300-case benchmark. On a 30-case dynamic-policy suite, it matched expected behavior in 27 cases after new rule installation. Across 98 traced cases from a 100-case Android emulator suite, it achieved 90.8% raw accuracy and 92.9% trace-adjusted pass rate across GUI, API, and LLM-planned cases.

Conclusion: OpenAgentFlow provides a practical shared enforcement boundary for heterogeneous AI agent fleets, enabling system-level action governance without requiring modifications to agents, prompts, models, or execution paths, addressing the safety challenges of multi-agent systems operating in shared environments.

Abstract: AI agents powered by large language models are evolving from isolated assistants into heterogeneous systems in which multiple agents, planners, controllers, and execution backends operate over the same user or enterprise environment. In such settings, safety becomes a system-level action-governance problem: deciding whether concrete agent-generated actions should be committed before they modify shared state. Existing safeguards cover prompts, tool calls, GUI actions, and agent-local behavior, but often leave enforcement fragmented, obscure risks that emerge across multi-step action flows, and provide limited support for auditability and policy evolution.
  We present OpenAgentFlow, a control-plane/action-plane architecture that enforces safety at the action-commit boundary. It normalizes pending GUI actions, API calls, tool calls, and LLM-generated invocations into a unified AgentEvent stream, routes each event through a shared pre-execution Policy Enforcement Point, and maintains provenance, session state, audit records, and updatable policies in the control plane. This creates a shared governable action stream and allows new rules to take effect without modifying agents, prompts, models, or execution paths.
  We instantiate OpenAgentFlow on Android. On a 300-case action-event benchmark, it achieves 94.0% accuracy and a 95.3% attack block rate. On a 30-case dynamic-policy suite, it matches expected behavior in 27 cases after new rules are installed. Across 98 traced cases from a 100-case Android emulator suite, it achieves 90.8% raw accuracy and a 92.9% trace-adjusted pass rate across GUI, API, and LLM-planned cases. These results show that OpenAgentFlow provides a practical shared enforcement boundary for heterogeneous AI agent fleets.

</details>


### [10] [SCAFFOLD: A Large-Scale Structured Dataset of Computer Science Research Figures with Diagram QA and Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00018)
*Ranjit Raut,Aarav Subedi,Sagun Rai,Sudan Jha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCAFFOLD is a large-scale dataset for computer science diagram understanding that pairs research figures with captions, context, QA pairs, and Chain-of-Thought reasoning traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computer science papers heavily rely on diagrams that carry crucial information, but there's no public dataset specifically pairing these technical figures with captions, context, questions, answers, and reasoning traces needed to train vision-language models to understand them.

Method: Created dataset using layout detection and PDF parsing from arXiv computer science papers, with AI-assisted question generation. Produced three dataset sizes: SCAFFOLD-157K (large), SCAFFOLD-37K (medium), and SCAFFOLD-12K (small) for different use cases.

Result: Successfully created three dataset variants: SCAFFOLD-157K with 157,387 pairs from 3,058 papers and 29,887 figures; SCAFFOLD-37K with 36,797 pairs; and SCAFFOLD-12K with 12,000 pairs. Used SCAFFOLD-12K for baseline experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct model.

Conclusion: SCAFFOLD fills a critical gap by providing the first structured dataset specifically for computer science diagram understanding with QA and Chain-of-Thought reasoning, enabling training of vision-language models to comprehend technical research figures.

Abstract: Computer science papers rely heavily on diagrams: architecture drawings, system flowcharts, and pipeline schematics that often carry more information than the text around them. There is currently no public dataset that pairs this specific kind of figure with captions, context, questions, answers, and step-by-step reasoning, which is exactly what is needed to train a vision-language model to understand them. We present \textbf{SCAFFOLD}\footnote{https://github.com/theranjitraut/scaffold}, a large-scale structured dataset of computer science research figures with diagram QA and Chain-of-Thought reasoning traces. This dataset consists of (image, caption, context, question-answer, chain-of-thought) tuples from arXiv computer science papers prepared using layout detection and PDF parsing, with an AI-assisted question-generation step. The resulting large-sized SCAFFOLD-157K dataset spans 3,058 papers with 29,887 figures (157,387 pairs), a medium-sized SCAFFOLD-37K dataset (36,797 pairs), and a small-sized SCAFFOLD-12K dataset (12,000 pairs). We used SCAFFOLD-12K for baseline experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct.

</details>


### [11] [UI-Venus-2 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00028)
*Venus Team,Zhuohan Cai,Haoxing Chen,Jiaxuan Chen,Weizhi Chen,Changlong Gao,Zhangxuan Gu,Yuan Guo,Yusong Hu,Jianrong Jiang,Jianguo Li,Runze Li,Jinzhen Lin,Zhenyu Ma,Changhua Meng,Han Peng,Xinyu Qiu,Shuheng Shen,Zhongyi Shui,Weiqiang Wang,Ming Wen,Zhuoer Xu,Hang Yan,Kaiwen Yang,Ruilin Yao,Nanjun Yu,Zhengwen Zeng,Lianrui Zhang,Yunzhu Zhang,Zhe Zhao,Beitong Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UI-Venus-2 is a general-purpose GUI agent foundation that operates across mobile, web, and desktop environments using a unified closed-loop reasoning-action framework, addressing real-world deployment challenges through environment scaling, task construction, and reliable verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal GUI agents show promise for digital task automation, but face challenges transitioning from benchmarks to real-world applications due to limited environment coverage, brittle task construction, and unreliable reward verification.

Method: Developed a unified closed-loop reasoning-action framework that scales three dimensions: (1) expanding to 170+ multilingual mobile apps and native desktop OS, (2) using deep-research pipeline for function-grounded instruction generation, and (3) implementing trace-level and sample-level evaluators with visual keypoints and multi-model voting for reliable RL training signals, plus safety-aware mechanisms.

Result: UI-Venus-2 advances toward practical deployment by offering a capable, efficient, and open-source foundation GUI agent that operates across multiple digital environments with improved generalizability and verifiability.

Conclusion: UI-Venus-2 represents progress toward more generalizable, verifiable, and self-reflective GUI agents for real-world applications, bridging the gap between benchmark models and dependable practical systems.

Abstract: Multimodal GUI agents have emerged as a promising paradigm for digital task automation, yet transitioning from benchmark-oriented models to dependable real-world applications remains challenging due to limited environment coverage, brittle task construction, and unreliable reward verification. In this work, we present UI-Venus-2, a general-purpose foundation GUI agent designed to operate across mobile, web, and desktop environments through a unified closed-loop reasoning-action framework. To bridge the gap toward practical deployment, we jointly scale three critical dimensions: (1) Environments, expanding coverage to more than 170 multilingual mobile apps and native desktop operating systems; (2) Tasks, employing a deep-research pipeline for function-grounded instruction generation; and (3) Verification, adopting trace-level and sample-level evaluators with visual keypoints and multi-model voting to ensure reliable RL signals for training. Furthermore, we integrate safety-aware mechanisms to ensure controlled execution of consequential actions. By offering a capable, efficient, and open-source foundation, UI-Venus-2 advances the field toward more generalizable, verifiable, and self-reflective agents for real-world applications.

</details>


### [12] [When Prediction Error Is Not Enough: Evaluating Nuisance-Function Prediction for Causal Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00071)
*Cong Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prediction error of nuisance functions doesn't consistently track causal estimator performance; XGBoost has lowest RMSE while DML-XGBoost offers better confidence interval coverage in partially linear models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the relationship between prediction error of nuisance-function estimators and causal estimator performance, particularly whether good prediction error translates to good causal estimation across different performance metrics.

Method: Monte Carlo simulations in a partially linear model comparing OLS, GAMs, XGBoost, and Double Machine Learning with XGBoost (DML-XGBoost). Evaluated nuisance-function prediction error, bias, RMSE, 95% confidence interval coverage, and a joint-error measure based on absolute cross-product of estimation errors.

Result: XGBoost had the lowest RMSE among non-oracle methods, while DML-XGBoost provided better confidence interval coverage. Prediction error did not consistently track causal bias across methods/settings. The method with best point-estimation performance didn't necessarily have best confidence interval coverage. Joint-error measure was only weakly associated with causal bias.

Conclusion: Prediction error is useful for assessing nuisance-function estimation but should not be treated as a direct measure of the quality of resulting causal estimators, as the relationship between prediction error and causal performance varies across metrics and methods.

Abstract: Prediction error is widely used to evaluate nuisance-function estimators in causal inference, but its relationship with causal estimator performance may differ across performance measures. We studied this question in a partially linear model using Monte Carlo simulations. We compared ordinary least squares (OLS), generalized additive models (GAMs), XGBoost, and Double Machine Learning with XGBoost (DML-XGBoost), evaluating nuisance-function prediction error, bias, RMSE, and 95\% confidence interval coverage. We also examined a simple joint-error measure based on the absolute cross-product of estimation errors from the exposure and outcome nuisance functions. Across the simulated settings, XGBoost had the lowest RMSE among the non-oracle methods, while DML-XGBoost generally provided better confidence interval coverage. Prediction error did not consistently track causal bias across methods and settings, and the method with the best point-estimation performance did not necessarily have the best confidence interval coverage. The joint-error measure was only weakly associated with causal bias and did not provide a useful standalone measure of causal performance. These results suggest that prediction error is useful for assessing nuisance-function estimation, but it should not be treated as a direct measure of the quality of the resulting causal estimator.

</details>


### [13] [MiNER: Fine-Tuned Biomedical Natural Language Processing for Malaria Disease Entity Recognition in Clinical Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00073)
*V. S. Anoop,Devika N*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuned BioBERT model for extracting biomedical named entities from malaria literature outperforms other encoding and ML approaches, with human-labeled dataset released for community use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Malaria research generates vast literature requiring efficient information extraction; manual extraction is challenging due to volume and complexity. Pre-trained language models show promise but need adaptation to malaria domain for effective biomedical entity extraction.

Method: 1. Collect and preprocess large corpus of malaria scientific articles. 2. Annotate articles with clinically significant entities. 3. Use BioBERT (pre-trained biomedical language model) to encode text into context-aware representations. 4. Fine-tune BioBERT using domain-specific annotations via supervised learning to enhance entity extraction capabilities.

Result: Proposed fine-tuned BioBERT approach significantly outperforms other encoding and machine learning algorithms in precision, recall, and accuracy metrics for biomedical named entity extraction from malaria literature.

Conclusion: Fine-tuning pre-trained biomedical language models like BioBERT with domain-specific annotations is effective for malaria literature mining. The approach enables accurate extraction of biomedical information, and the released human-labeled dataset will support further research in malaria health informatics.

Abstract: Malaria remains a significant global health burden, necessitating continuous research efforts to understand its complex molecular mechanisms, epidemiology, and potential therapeutic interventions. Extracting essential biomedical information from the vast and constantly growing malaria literature is a challenging task that demands innovative approaches. Recently, pre-trained language models have revolutionized natural language processing tasks, demonstrating remarkable capabilities in various domains. This paper proposes a fine-tuned pre-trained biomedical language model for biomedical information extraction from scientific literature on malaria disease. The proposed methodology selects and preprocesses a large corpus of scientific articles on malaria, and then annotates them with entities of clinical significance. It then leverages BioBERT, a state-of-the-art pre-trained language model, to encode the textual data into context-aware representations. We fine-tune the model using domain-specific annotations and supervised learning to enhance its ability to extract relevant biomedical named entities. Extensive experiments and comparisons with different encoding and machine learning algorithms show that the proposed approach significantly outperforms them in precision, recall, and accuracy. We also publish our human-labeled dataset for entity and relation extraction to enable other health informatics researchers to train advanced models for malaria information extraction.

</details>


### [14] [AI Morbidity and Mortality: A Framework for Clinical AI Failure Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00076)
*Paulius Mui,Dean F. Sittig,Steve Labkoff,Sanjay Basu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI Morbidity and Mortality (AI M&M) is a structured, blameless framework for reviewing clinical AI failures that combines case intake, evidence preservation, reconstruction, and corrective-action tracking to enable institutional learning from AI-related errors in healthcare workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety mechanisms are inadequate for learning from individual AI-related errors. Aggregate model monitoring identifies performance changes but doesn't explain how risk emerges from interactions among AI systems, clinicians, workflows, and institutional controls. Patient safety reporting captures adverse events but isn't designed for AI-specific failure analysis.

Method: Proposes AI M&M framework with four components: 1) Standardized case intake, 2) Evidence preservation and investigator-level reconstruction, 3) Tool-in-loop attribution, 4) Corrective-action tracking. Each event classified across four linked dimensions: Trigger → Mechanism → Clinical Pathway → Corrective Action.

Result: Demonstrated the framework using five illustrative outpatient medication and clinical decision-support cases. Two clinician reviewers independently applied all four classification axes and reached agreement across all 20 axis-level classifications, showing the framework can be consistently applied.

Conclusion: AI M&M framework is intended to complement (not replace) existing safety mechanisms by converting individual AI-in-workflow failures into actionable institutional learning. Prospective evaluation across different institutions, AI systems, and clinical settings is needed for further validation.

Abstract: Clinical artificial intelligence is increasingly embedded in real-world care, yet existing safety mechanisms are poorly suited to reconstructing and learning from individual AI-related errors and near-misses. Aggregate model monitoring can identify performance changes, and traditional patient safety reporting can capture adverse events, but neither is designed to explain how risk emerges across the interaction among AI systems, clinicians, workflows, and institutional controls. We propose AI Morbidity and Mortality (AI M&M), a structured, blameless framework for case-based review of clinical AI failures. The framework combines standardized case intake, evidence preservation and investigator-level reconstruction, tool-in-loop attribution, and corrective-action tracking. Each event is classified across four linked dimensions: Trigger - Mechanism - Clinical Pathway - Corrective Action, separating the condition that exposed a vulnerability from the process that produced risk, its consequence for care, and the remediation assigned. We demonstrate the framework using five illustrative outpatient medication and clinical decision-support cases; two clinician reviewers independently applied all four classification axes and reached agreement across all 20 axis-level classifications. AI M&M is intended to complement, rather than replace, model monitoring, patient safety reporting, and regulatory oversight by converting individual AI-in-workflow failures into actionable institutional learning. Prospective evaluation across institutions, AI systems, and clinical settings is needed.

</details>


### [15] [Different representation learning objectives recover distinct latent structures from the same psychometric data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00100)
*Cong Cao,Tassos C. Kyriakides,Pambos Vrasidas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Different representation learning objectives (contrastive vs PCA) recover different latent structures from psychometric questionnaires: contrastive learning improves teacher-child matching but preserves behavioral phenotypes less effectively than PCA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether different representation learning objectives recover the same latent organization from psychometric questionnaire data. Researchers want to understand if teacher-child correspondence and behavioral phenotypes represent the same underlying structure or different forms of organization.

Method: Used 757 matched teacher-child pairs from the Cyprus ProW preschool trial. Applied PCA and clustering to child SDQ, ASBI, and CBRS item responses to identify four behavioral phenotypes. Compared contrastive learning objective (teacher-child matching) against PCA-based representations, and also tested a multi-task objective jointly optimizing alignment and behavioral prediction.

Result: Contrastive learning substantially improved teacher-child retrieval (Top-1 accuracy: 0.13% → 7.27%; Top-10: 1.98% → 56.14%) but preserved behavioral phenotype structure less effectively than PCA. Multi-task objective partially restored behavioral organization but reduced retrieval performance.

Conclusion: Teacher-child correspondence and behavioral phenotypes represent distinct forms of latent organization. The latent structure recovered from linked psychometric data depends on the representation learning objective used, showing trade-offs between different organizational principles.

Abstract: Psychometric questionnaires contain rich item-level information, yet it remains unclear whether different representation learning objectives recover the same latent organization. We investigated this question using 757 matched teacher-child pairs from the baseline assessment of the Cyprus ProW preschool trial. Behavioral structure was characterized from child SDQ, ASBI, and CBRS item responses using principal component analysis and clustering, yielding four behavioral phenotypes. A contrastive objective substantially improved teacher-child retrieval relative to PCA-based representations, increasing Top-1 accuracy from 0.13% to 7.27% and Top-10 accuracy from 1.98% to 56.14%. However, contrastive representations preserved behavioral phenotype structure less effectively than PCA-based representations. A multi-task objective jointly optimizing alignment and behavioral prediction partially restored behavioral organization but reduced retrieval performance. These findings indicate that teacher-child correspondence and behavioral phenotypes represent distinct forms of latent organization and demonstrate that the latent structure recovered from linked psychometric data depends on the representation learning objective.

</details>


### [16] [Deploying and Evaluating a Smart-Agriculture Agentic Engine for Full-Season Soybean Farm Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00106)
*Ao Qu,Panagiotis Michelakis,Linyuan Han,Yiannis Hadjiyianni,Kun Ouyang,Konstantinos Siskos,Feng Li,Ran Meng,Jingchi Jiang,Dimitrios Stamoulis,Jie Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FAIRY is a full-stack smart-agriculture agent system deployed on a real soybean farm that executes and evaluates agentic operations across the entire crop season using an event-driven architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop and deploy a comprehensive smart-agriculture system that can execute agentic agronomic operations across full-season spatiotemporal workflows, integrating diverse data sources and infrastructure for real-world agricultural applications.

Method: Developed FAIRY system with "everything is an event" paradigm using shared farm process engine, built on event-driven world model with knowledge library of agronomic skills, multi-agent controllers, frontier/edge-model execution, trace logging, and local node deployment.

Result: Successfully deployed FAIRY on operating soybean research farm, evaluated nine state-of-the-art agent controllers across 100 full-season soybean scenarios, and developed evaluation suite measuring agentic success, spatiotemporal correctness, token cost, and runtime.

Conclusion: FAIRY demonstrates a comprehensive smart-agriculture system that integrates real-world infrastructure with advanced agentic AI for agricultural operations, providing a robust platform for evaluating and deploying AI agents in agricultural workflows.

Abstract: This paper presents FAIRY, a full-stack smart-agriculture agent system developed for and deployed to an operating soybean research farm at Harbin Institute of Technology's smart-agriculture site. We develop FAIRY to execute and evaluate agentic agronomic operations on full-season spatiotemporal workflows that span ridge preparation, planting, irrigation, fertilization, pest and disease treatment, harvest, grain handling, drying, and storage. FAIRY integrates APIs and infrastructure across production-grade machinery, fixed soil and canopy sensors, multispectral and thermal drones, satellite vegetation products, a weather station, calibrated crop-process models, agronomic records, and multi-season yield histories. The system is built around the novel "everything is an event" execution paradigm, which represents spatiotemporal world evolution, remote sensing and UAV observations, sensor readings, crop-growth transitions, machinery actions, and management interventions as state-changing events in a shared farm process engine. On top of this event-driven world model, FAIRY implements a complete agentic stack: a knowledge library of atomic agronomic skills; multi-agent controller and orchestration backends; frontier- and edge-model execution; full-path trace logging; and deployment profiling on local nodes. We use FAIRY to evaluate nine state-of-the-art agent controllers across one hundred full-season soybean scenarios that preserve the operational coupling between spatial observations in a 64-ridge field, temporal decision sequences, agronomic constraints, delayed effects, and final yield. We develop an evaluation suite that combines agentic success, full-path spatiotemporal correctness, token cost, and edge-device runtime.

</details>


### [17] [Recursive Criticality of AI Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00137)
*Mikhail Burtsev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers develop a model to understand when AI R&D becomes self-amplifying through recursive feedback loops, defining a reproduction number that determines whether AI capability growth compounds or dampens across development cycles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is increasingly used in the R&D process to create future AI systems, creating potential feedback loops. The paper aims to understand the conditions under which this feedback becomes self-amplifying versus dampening, and how to distinguish true recursive amplification from other sources of rapid progress.

Method: Develops a mathematical model describing AI capability growth that depends on baseline research productivity, recursive feedback, and increasing research difficulty. Derives a recursive reproduction number R_AI that determines whether improvements amplify or dampen across development cycles. Extends the model to multiple research actors.

Result: When R_AI > 1, improvements compound across cycles (self-amplifying regime); when R_AI < 1, improvements weaken across cycles. The transition depends on the structure of AI R&D feedback loops and not on any particular capability level. Higher baseline productivity accelerates progress but doesn't change whether the system is self-amplifying. Multiple actors sharing improvements can make the overall ecosystem self-amplifying even when individual actors aren't.

Conclusion: The framework identifies measurable properties of AI R&D systems that can help distinguish recursive amplification from rapid progress driven by other sources. Key factors include recursive feedback strength, propagation effectiveness, cycle duration, and increasing research difficulty. The model provides analytical tools to understand and potentially manage AI development trajectories.

Abstract: AI is increasingly used in the R\&D process that produces future AI systems. We study the conditions under which this feedback becomes self-amplifying. Our model describes how the rate of AI capability growth depends on baseline research productivity, recursive feedback, and the increasing difficulty of research progress. We derive a recursive reproduction number, $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{AI}}$, that determines whether improvements are amplified or damped across development cycles. This quantity compares the strength of feedback with the rate at which further progress becomes more difficult. When $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{AI}}>1$, the effects of improvements compound across development cycles, placing the system in a self-amplifying regime. When $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{AI}}<1$, their effects weaken across cycles. The transition depends on the structure of the AI R\&D feedback loop and need not occur at any particular level of model capability. A system can therefore enter a self-amplifying regime before acceleration becomes visible, while rapid progress can also occur without self-amplification. Higher baseline research productivity can accelerate progress without changing whether the system is self-amplifying, but the duration of the development cycle becomes a limiting timescale for amplification. Increasing research difficulty can end a period of self-amplification. Extending the model to multiple research actors shows that improvements shared across organizations can make the overall research ecosystem self-amplifying even when no individual actor is. The framework identifies measurable properties of AI R\&D systems that can help distinguish recursive amplification from rapid progress driven by other sources, including the strength of recursive feedback, how effectively improvements propagate into successor systems, cycle duration, and the increasing difficulty of further progress.

</details>


### [18] [IMPACT: Attention Is the Interaction Map for Scalable Interaction-Aware World Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00161)
*Rongze Tang,Jianjie Fang,Zhaolu Wang,Ziyou Wang,Xvyuan Liu,Haisheng Su,Xin Zhang,Wei Wu,Chen Gao,Yong Li,Zhibo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IMPACT is a training framework that improves world models' ability to generate physically plausible interactions by reweighting denoising supervision based on cross-attention patterns, without requiring external representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current world models struggle with physically plausible interactions. Existing approaches use external motion/geometry/semantic representations requiring auxiliary estimators or annotations, limiting scalability. The key issue is that globally averaged MSE loss overemphasizes static content and under-supervises critical dynamic-object regions where interactions occur.

Method: IMPACT uses cross-attention from manipulated-object tokens as an internal spatiotemporal prior for action-conditioned changes. It samples candidate regions from this prior, calibrates them with detached local prediction errors to build an interaction map, and uses this map to reweight the denoising supervision, focusing training on important interaction regions.

Result: Extensive experiments on robot-arm and human-hand manipulation tasks across diverse control modalities and DiT backbones show IMPACT consistently outperforms MSE-trained baselines, improving interaction fidelity, physical plausibility, and visual quality.

Conclusion: IMPACT provides a scalable solution for training interaction-aware world models without external representations or inference-time modifications, addressing the supervision-allocation mismatch in standard training objectives to better capture physically plausible interactions.

Abstract: World models have made remarkable progress in action-conditioned future prediction for embodied agents, yet still struggle to model physically plausible interactions. Existing approaches address this limitation by constraining the generation process with external representations encoding motion, geometry, or semantics. Obtaining these spatiotemporally dense representations typically requires auxiliary estimators or manual annotations, limiting training scalability. We instead revisit the training objective and identify a supervision-allocation mismatch under the globally averaged mean squared error (MSE) denoising objective: prevalent static content dominates the optimization signal, leaving sparse dynamic-object regions critical to interaction generation disproportionately under-supervised. Motivated by this observation, we introduce IMPACT, a scalable Interaction-aware Model training framework with Prior-guided Attention Calibration and Targeting. IMPACT uses cross-attention associated with manipulated-object tokens as an internal spatiotemporal prior for action-conditioned changes. It samples candidate regions from this prior, calibrates them with detached local prediction errors to construct an interaction map, and uses the map to reweight denoising supervision, requiring neither external representations nor inference-time modifications. Extensive experiments on robot-arm and human-hand manipulation, spanning diverse control modalities and DiT backbones, show that IMPACT consistently outperforms the corresponding MSE-trained baselines, improving interaction fidelity, physical plausibility, and visual quality.

</details>


### [19] [Asymmetries in Spontaneous and Instructed Deception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00180)
*Josiah Luikham*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates both instructed and spontaneous deception in Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct, finding they share some directionality but show asymmetrical transfer effects where spontaneous deception detection works better on instructed data and instructed steering works better on spontaneous prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While much research focuses on instructed deception in LLMs, the paper recognizes that models sometimes deceive users spontaneously without being instructed to do so. The researchers aim to understand the relationship between these two types of deception.

Method: The study uses three main techniques: direction geometry analysis to measure alignment between deception settings, cross-setting classifier training to test transferability, and cross-setting steering experiments to test whether deception vectors from one setting can control behavior in the other.

Result: The two deception settings share directional components (cosine similarity ~0.5) but show asymmetrical transfer: spontaneous-trained classifiers perform better on instructed data than vice versa, and instructed-derived steering directions work better on spontaneous prompts than vice versa. Different token positions work best for steering vs. classification.

Conclusion: Instructed and spontaneous deception in LLMs are related but distinct phenomena with asymmetric transfer properties, suggesting they operate through partially overlapping but not identical mechanisms within the model's internal representations.

Abstract: Large language models sometimes deceive users without being instructed to. However, much of the study on deception in models involves instructed deception. We investigated the relationship between instructed and spontaneous (uninstructed) deception in Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct. We compared these two deception settings through direction geometry, cross-setting classifiers, and cross-setting steering. We found the two deception settings share a component of direction (cosine of approximately 0.5) and an asymmetry in the transfer between settings regarding detection and causation. Spontaneous trained classifiers performed better on instructed data than vice versa, and instructed derived directions performed better at steering spontaneous prompts than vice versa. Likewise the best token position to derive steering vectors from differed from the best token position to train and apply classifiers.

</details>


### [20] [LLM-Driven Autonomous Vehicles Inherit Human Driver Biases in Pedestrian Yielding: Results and Implications From A New Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00192)
*Irem Yoldas,Martim Brandão,Jie Zhang,Odinaldo Rodrigues*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs and VLMs exhibit bias in pedestrian-yielding decisions across multiple demographic factors, challenging the "common sense" model paradigm for AVs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public trust in AVs depends on both technical success and fairness. While psychology studies show human driver biases (e.g., lower yielding to Black pedestrians), and AV research increasingly uses "common sense" models, the potential inheritance of human biases by these models is understudied.

Method: Proposes two new bias testing methodologies for LLMs and VLMs: 1) "All Else Being Equal" tests (varying only one demographic factor while holding other conditions constant), and 2) "Self-Consistency" tests (measuring decision consistency across demographic variations).

Result: Both LLMs and VLMs show bias in pedestrian-yielding decisions influenced by pedestrian gender, ethnicity, religion, disability, age, skin tone, and socio-economic status. Bias types and degrees vary across models but follow common patterns.

Conclusion: The findings question the "common sense" model paradigm for AVs, suggesting either revision of the paradigm or addressing downstream bias issues. Bias analysis should be part of AV evaluation to ensure fairness.

Abstract: Public trust in Autonomous Vehicles (AVs) may depend not only on technical success but also on the fairness of their decision making. While a recent trend in AV research involves using general purpose "common sense" models to guide AV decision making, the degree to which these inherit human biases in driving is still understudied. Given that psychology studies have shown human driver biases exist, such as lower pedestrian-yielding rates to Black pedestrians in the US, we argue that analyses of model bias should also be part of AV evaluation. Concretely, in this paper we propose two new bias testing methodologies for Large Language Models (LLMs) and Visual-Language Models (VLMs)-"All Else Being Equal" tests and "Self-Consistency" tests-in order to assess bias in pedestrian-yielding decisions. Our findings show that both LLMs and VLMs make yielding decisions which are influenced by pedestrian gender, ethnicity, religion, disability, age, skin tone and socio-economic status. While the type and degree of bias is different from model to model, we highlight common patterns-and raise questions about the "common sense" model paradigm, particularly the need to either revise the paradigm or address issues of downstream bias.

</details>


### [21] [ReDeck: Step-Level Render-Grounded Refinement for Document-to-Slide Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00194)
*Muzhao Tian,Zezi Zeng,Yifan Yang,Xin Gao,Yan Li,Zisu Huang,Xiaohua Wang,Changze Lv,Mingxi Cheng,Bei Liu,Kai Qiu,Qi Dai,Dong Chen,Yue Dong,Xiaoqing Zheng,Ji Li,Chong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReDeck introduces a render-grounded refinement framework for document-to-slide generation that decomposes revision into atomic edit actions with step-level render feedback, overcoming limitations of monolithic feedback loops in existing slide agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current slide generation agents use monolithic "one version, one feedback" loops where slides are rewritten and only critiqued at turn boundaries. This delayed feedback makes it difficult to attribute and repair local failures like overflow, overlap, clipping, and off-canvas placement in dense, editable slide artifacts.

Method: ReDeck decomposes slide revision into atomic edit actions and returns renderer-derived observations after each step ("one edit, one observation"). It uses multi-granular feedback: step-level render feedback for spatial errors, turn-level adaptive critic for semantic/design guidance, and submission-level gate for hard layout validation.

Result: ReDeck consistently outperforms existing slide-generation agents across multiple models (GPT-5.4, Claude-4.6, Gemini-3.1). Ablations confirm that feedback timing and granularity are critical for reliable slide refinement. The paper also introduces DeckQuiz benchmark to evaluate content fidelity, spatial correctness, and design quality separately.

Conclusion: The proposed step-level render-grounded refinement approach with multi-granular feedback addresses key challenges in document-to-slide generation by enabling precise spatial error attribution and repair while maintaining global slide quality, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods.

Abstract: Document-to-slide generation is challenging because slides are dense editable artifacts that require both faithful content selection and precise spatial layout. Recent slide agents adopt iterative reflection, but typically follow a monolithic "one version, one feedback" loop: a slide or deck is rewritten, rendered afterward, and critiqued only at the turn boundary. This delayed feedback makes local failures such as overflow, overlap, clipping, and off-canvas placement difficult to attribute and repair. We propose ReDeck, a step-level render-grounded refinement framework that decomposes slide revision into atomic edit actions and returns renderer-derived observations after each step, turning refinement into "one edit, one observation." To balance local repair with global quality, ReDeck uses multi-granular feedback: step-level render feedback for spatial errors, a turn-level adaptive critic for semantic and design guidance, and a submission-level gate for hard layout validation. We further introduce DeckQuiz, a benchmark that decouples content fidelity, spatial correctness, and design quality. Across GPT-5.4, Claude-4.6, and Gemini-3.1, ReDeck consistently outperforms existing slide-generation agents, and ablations confirm that feedback timing and granularity are critical for reliable slide refinement.

</details>


### [22] [AI Should Not Only Be Helpful. It Should Be Contingent. Artificial Intimacy, Sycophancy, and the Future of Social Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00211)
*Scott Compton,Arjun Nagendran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues AI conversational systems should provide contingent (behaviorally responsive) feedback instead of noncontingent affirmation to better support human social learning and development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI alignment approaches (like RLHF) prioritize user approval and conversational fluency over behaviorally informative feedback, leading to sycophantic patterns that may harm social development, especially during critical periods like adolescence.

Method: The paper draws on behavioral science and social learning theory to propose contingent AI systems, outlines a framework including trajectory-based evaluation and social consequence prediction models, and proposes a multidisciplinary research agenda.

Result: The paper presents a theoretical framework for contingent AI that varies responses based on user behavior and predicts social consequences, shifting evaluation from user satisfaction alone to impact on human social learning.

Conclusion: AI systems should be designed with contingency as a central construct to support adaptive social calibration rather than just user approval, requiring evaluation of their impact on human social learning across developmental psychology, human-AI interaction, and machine learning.

Abstract: Conversational artificial intelligence is increasingly embedded in everyday social environments, where it functions as both an informational tool and a source of interpersonal feedback. This perspective introduces contingency, i.e., the degree to which system responses vary with user behavior and its interpersonal consequences, as a central construct for evaluating AI systems. We argue that current alignment approaches, including reinforcement learning from human feedback, tend to prioritize user approval and conversational fluency over behaviorally informative feedback, leading to sycophantic patterns of noncontingent affirmation.
  Drawing on behavioral science and social learning theory, we propose that contingent feedback is a key mechanism through which individuals develop interpersonal skills. When AI systems provide feedback weakly coupled to social consequences, they may reduce opportunities for adaptive calibration in real-world interactions, particularly during adolescence, a critical period for social development.
  We outline a framework for contingent AI, including trajectory-based evaluation and models of social consequence prediction, and propose a research agenda spanning developmental psychology, human-AI interaction, and machine learning. More broadly, we argue that AI systems should be evaluated not only by user satisfaction, but by their impact on human social learning.

</details>


### [23] [ConvDeck: Conversational Paper-to-Slide Generation via Stage-Specific User Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00226)
*Tarik Can Ozden,Sachidanand VS,Furkan Horoz,Ozgur Kara,Dilek Hakkani-Tür,Junho Kim,James Matthew Rehg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConvDeck is a multi-agent conversational paper-to-slide generation system that allows users to iteratively refine both presentation outlines and final slide decks through stage-specific feedback loops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current paper-to-slide generation systems have limitations: multi-agent systems use internal critique loops that remain closed to users, while conversational approaches only allow feedback after complete deck generation, preventing user participation in iterative refinement of narrative flow, content allocation, and presentation emphasis.

Method: ConvDeck uses a multi-agent pipeline with stage-specific conversational loops distributed throughout the process. Agents can think, speak, and act, enabling them to directly apply edits or respond conversationally to clarify user feedback and discuss revision options at different stages.

Result: Evaluation shows that ConvDeck's stage-specific conversational feedback improves user-goal satisfaction while preserving narrative coherence, content quality, and visual presentation quality.

Conclusion: Distributing conversational interaction across the paper-to-slide generation pipeline through stage-specific refinement loops allows users to meaningfully participate in iterative refinement decisions, resulting in better alignment with user goals without compromising presentation quality.

Abstract: Automatic academic paper-to-slide generation is inherently iterative, because creating an effective presentation requires repeated cycles of generation, critique, and revision. Recent multi-agent systems partially acknowledge this through internal critique-and-revise loops, while conversational approaches allow users to refine generated slide decks through dialog. However, these refinement processes either remain largely closed to the user or introduce feedback only after a complete deck has been produced, limiting the user's ability to participate in the iterative refinement of narrative flow, content allocation, and presentation emphasis. To address this gap, we introduce ConvDeck, a multi-agent pipeline for conversational paper-to-slide generation that distributes interaction across the pipeline through stage-specific loops, allowing users to iteratively refine both the presentation outline and the final slide deck at the stages where each kind of decision is made. These loops are driven by a refinement mechanism in which agents can think, speak, and act, enabling them to either directly apply edits or respond conversationally to clarify user feedback and discuss revision options. Our evaluation shows that stage-specific conversational feedback improves user-goal satisfaction while preserving narrative coherence, content quality, and visual presentation.

</details>


### [24] [Learning What to Retain: Gated-Memory Routing for Efficient Collaboration in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00237)
*Rakibul Hasan Rajib,Mengxing Zheng,Qian Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gated-Memory Routing: A multi-agent LLM orchestration system that uses learned gating mechanisms to maintain a compact execution memory, enabling adaptive routing decisions while reducing computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional routing approaches in LLM-based multi-agent systems have limitations: query-only routing lacks context of intermediate progress/errors, hurting accuracy, while full-history routing processes all prior steps (including redundant ones), creating execution-history overload and high computational costs. Effective orchestration needs a compact state that captures useful progress without accumulating redundant context.

Method: Proposes Gated-Memory Routing that conditions decisions on query and learned execution memory. Two gating mechanisms: (1) Memory Write Gate commits only non-redundant reasoning steps, (2) Retrieval Gate supplies each agent with compact, relevant subset. System selects next role and backbone from memory, while Adaptive Halting Controller stops execution when sufficient evidence is accumulated.

Result: Across five reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, achieves best average accuracy (exceeding strongest baseline by 2.44 points) while reducing HumanEval inference cost by 31.9% relative to baseline. Demonstrates both effectiveness and efficiency.

Conclusion: Gated-Memory Routing provides an effective solution to the orchestration challenge in LLM-based multi-agent systems by maintaining a compact, informative state through learned gating mechanisms, enabling adaptive decision-making while reducing computational overhead.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems tackle complex reasoning by orchestrating how multiple agents are configured and how they collaborate. A central challenge is to adapt orchestration to the evolving collaboration state. Routing from the query alone cannot adapt to intermediate progress or errors, which hurts accuracy. Routing from the complete execution history supplies this missing context, but forces later decisions to process every prior step, including redundant or low-utility ones. This creates an execution-history overload that inflates cost. Effective orchestration instead requires a compact state that captures useful progress without accumulating redundant context. We propose Gated-Memory Routing, which conditions each decision on the query and a learned execution memory. A learned Memory Write Gate commits only non-redundant reasoning steps, and a learned Retrieval Gate supplies each agent a compact, relevant subset, so every decision conditions on a clean, informative state. At each step, the system selects the next role and backbone from this memory, while an Adaptive Halting Controller stops execution once the memory contains sufficient evidence for answering. Across five reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, our framework is both effective and efficient: it attains the best average accuracy, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.44 points, while reducing HumanEval inference cost by 31.9% relative to that baseline. Code is available at https://github.com/rajibrhasan/gated-memory-routing

</details>


### [25] [Invalidation Contracts for Cross-Episode Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00243)
*Michael Wu,Arquimedes Canedo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents cache API error fixes to save tokens, but server data drift makes cached fixes fail. The paper introduces invalidation contracts with version stamps to evict stale cached entries without trial-and-error, allowing agents to maintain savings while handling server changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents cache recovery suggestions from API errors to reduce token usage and model calls for repeated constraints, but server-side data drift causes these cached fixes to fail silently. Traditional solutions (re-deriving fixes every time) eliminate the savings from caching.

Method: Introduced invalidation contracts - a protocol layer that attaches version stamps and cacheability hints to every recovery suggestion. This allows clients to identify and evict stale cached entries without trial-and-error. The contract decomposes savings into validity (fraction of cached suggestions that remain correct after drift) and compliance (fraction applied correctly by planner).

Result: Row-level invalidation improved compliance by 0-66.7 percentage points across 7 models, with 55.6-66.7 improvement on 3 models. Recovered 29-33% of baseline token cost on 4 of 7 models. Table-level invalidation performed poorly (0% first-try rates on 5 models). Eviction precision was 1.00 at row granularity. Contract added 15% to response payload.
