Version-stamp validity was deterministic and produced identical results across all models/paths with zero contract failures.

Conclusion: Invalidation contracts provide an effective protocol for maintaining caching benefits in LLM agents despite server data drift, with row-level granularity proving crucial for success. The approach separates vendor-independent protocol validity from model-dependent compliance, revealing significant model differences in schema handling behavior.

Abstract: LLM agents that cache recovery suggestions from API errors can skip re-derivation in later episodes, spending fewer tokens and fewer model calls on constraints they have already learned. Server-side data drift turns those cached fixes into silent failures, and the usual remedy, re-deriving on every episode, gives the savings back. We introduce invalidation contracts, a protocol layer that attaches version stamps and cacheability hints to every recovery suggestion so the client can evict stale entries without trial and error, and keep the rest. The contract decomposes realized savings into two independent factors: validity, the fraction of cached suggestions that remain correct after a drift event, and compliance, the fraction the planner applies on the first attempt. Validity depends only on the protocol and is vendor-independent. Compliance depends on the planner model: identical wire bytes yield 100% first-try compliance on Claude Haiku 4.5 and 11% or below on Claude Sonnet 5, which exhibits input-schema conservatism, refusing fixes that add fields the original request did not contain. We evaluate across seven models, three serving paths, two domains, and approximately 9,400 episodes. Row-level invalidation raises compliance by 0 to 66.7 percentage points across the seven models, 55.6 to 66.7 on three, and recovers 29-33% of baseline token cost on four of seven models, while table-level invalidation destroys co-located entries and drops post-drift first-try rates to 0% on five of seven. Eviction precision is 1.00 at row granularity on every model under the row-level oracle of Section 4.1. The contract adds 15% to response payload. Version-stamp validity is deterministic by construction and produced identical results across every model and serving path, with zero contract failures in the entire evaluation.

</details>


### [26] [Authority Bias in Conversational Search Engines for Academic Paper Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00248)
*Uthman Jinadu,Parsa Ghazvinian,Anjila Budathoki,Benjamin M. Ampel,Rajshekhar Sunderraman,Yi Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show substantial authority bias in academic paper recommendations, preferring papers based on prestige signals rather than content, with debiasing efforts only partially effective and suffering from a "say-do" gap where models reduce mentions of authority but still show bias in actual recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs become conversational search engines for academic literature, it's unclear whether they judge papers based on content quality or authority signals like author prestige, venue reputation, and citation counts. There's a need to causally test for authority bias - systematic preference for papers based on non-content factors.

Method: Researchers conducted controlled experiments by holding title and abstract constant while varying authority metadata across three counterfactual conditions (original, flipped, boosted). They tested eight LLMs (five open-weight and three frontier closed-weight) in an in-context, single-turn, top-1 recommendation setting. The study also examined prompt-level debiasing strategies and documented a "say-do" gap phenomenon.

Result: Authority bias was found to be substantial and directional, with significant variation across different LLM models. Debiasing instructions only partially addressed the bias. Importantly, a "say-do" gap was identified: while debiasing instructions suppressed authority mentions in model responses relatively quickly, authority-driven flips in actual recommendations persisted much longer, meaning surface auditing systematically underestimates the behavioral bias.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit significant authority bias in academic literature recommendations that varies by model and is only partially mitigable through prompt-level interventions. The finding of a "say-do" gap reveals that traditional auditing methods that focus on what models say rather than what they do systematically underestimate the extent of bias, highlighting the need for behavioral testing of AI systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as conversational search engines for academic literature, yet whether they judge papers on content or on authority signals has not been tested causally. We investigate authority bias: systematic preference for papers based on author prestige, venue, and citations rather than content. Holding title and abstract constant, we vary authority metadata across three counterfactual conditions (original, flipped, boosted) over eight LLMs (five open-weight and three frontier closed-weight) in an in-context, single-turn, top-1 recommendation setting. Our experiments show that authority bias is substantial and directional, varies markedly across models, and is only partially addressable through prompt-level debiasing. We further document a say-do gap: debiasing instructions suppress authority mentions far faster than authority-driven flips, so surface auditing systematically underestimates behavioral bias.

</details>


### [27] [Hypotheses-Guided Self Distillation for Continual Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00251)
*EunJeong Hwang,Kushan Mitra,Dan Zhang,Hannah Kim,Estevam Hruschka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HypReflect: A framework for continual LLM personalization that infers explicit preference hypotheses from diverse user signals, refines them iteratively, and uses self-distillation for reliable, scalable adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM assistants become daily tools, they need to adapt to individual preferences, but preferences are rarely fully stated and emerge through heterogeneous, latent, noisy signals. Existing methods rely on raw interaction histories or costly reward optimization, lacking efficient continual adaptation.

Method: HypReflect infers explicit, uncertainty-aware preference hypotheses from diverse user signals, uses reflective refinement as new evidence accumulates, and incorporates user models through hypotheses-guided self-distillation.

Result: Outperforms baselines (raw-history and incremental-update methods) across three settings: online personalization, multi-session interactions, and implicit behavioral signals. Shows strong generalization to unseen users and cross-domain settings, with stability across context budgets and more focused personalization.

Conclusion: HypReflect represents a step toward reliable and scalable continual personalization through explicit, revisable user preference hypotheses that can be efficiently maintained and adapted over time.

Abstract: As people increasingly interact with LLM assistants in daily life, continually adapting to individual preferences has become essential for effective long-term interactions. However, user preferences are rarely stated in full, and instead emerge through heterogeneous, latent, and noisy signals, with existing methods relying on raw interaction histories or costly reward-based optimization to manage personalization. We introduce HypReflect, a reliable, scalable framework for continual personalization that infers explicit, uncertainty-aware preference hypotheses from diverse user signals, reflectively refines them as new evidence accumulates, and incorporates the resulting user model through hypotheses-guided self-distillation. Experiments across three personalization settings: online personalization, multi-session interactions, and implicit behavioral signals, show that HypReflect outperforms a range of baselines, including raw-history and incremental-update methods. We further demonstrate strong generalization to unseen users and cross-domain settings, along with stability across context budgets, reusable hypotheses, and more focused personalization. These results suggest a step towards reliable and scalable continual personalization through explicit, revisable user preference hypotheses.

</details>


### [28] [The Answer Is Not the Argument](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00264)
*Will Yeadon,Sergio Juárez,Paul Mackay,T. J. Dowling,Elise Agra,Oto-obong Inyang,Arin Mizouri,Craig P. Testrow*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Chain-of-thought monitors improve at detecting conclusion inconsistencies when given correct answers, but this mainly helps them align with known correct outputs rather than independently verify reasoning quality - especially for critical traces where correct answers hide flawed reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses a key problem in AI oversight: current evaluations of chain-of-thought monitoring often provide monitors with trusted reference answers, which may conflate two different capabilities - verifying reasoning quality vs. detecting incorrect conclusions. There's concern that answer access might mainly help monitors spot wrong conclusions rather than truly evaluate the reasoning process.

Method: Researchers collected 237 step-numbered physics solutions from three frontier AI models, focusing on Humanity's Last Exam questions with no inserted errors. They independently labelled final-answer correctness and identified the first false step in reasoning. A reference standard was created using physicist annotations, independent LLM debates, and source-masked adjudication. This identified 24 critical traces where answers were correct but reasoning contained genuine errors. Eight different LLM monitors evaluated traces under three conditions: blind, with answer (unverified or certified), and after blind commitment.

Result: Certification raised mean balanced accuracy from 0.637 to 0.796 and improved first-error localization from 0.261 to 0.379. However, while certification changed recall from 0.653 to 0.951 on wrong-answer traces, it actually decreased recall from 0.521 to 0.438 on critical traces (those with correct answers but flawed reasoning). After blind commitment, monitors shown the answer flagged 93.8% of previously passed wrong-answer traces as erroneous but only 18.0% of critical traces.

Conclusion: Answer access primarily improves conclusion-consistency checking rather than independent verification of reasoning quality. This has important implications for AI safety: acceptable outputs don't guarantee sound underlying reasoning processes. The study reveals a benign analogue of reward hacking and suggests trusted-answer evaluations may overstate monitoring capabilities when correct outputs conceal unsound reasoning.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought monitoring is proposed for AI oversight, yet evaluations often provide monitors with a trusted reference answer. We ask whether answer access improves reasoning verification or mainly exposes incorrect conclusions. We collected 237 step-numbered solutions to 79 Humanity's Last Exam physics questions from three frontier models, with no inserted errors, and independently labelled final-answer correctness and the first false step. The reference standard combined physicist annotations, an independent LLM debate, and source-masked adjudication. This yielded 24 critical traces in which the answer was correct but the trace contained a genuine error. 8 LLM monitors evaluated traces blind, with an unverified or certified answer, or after a blind commitment. Certification raised mean balanced accuracy from 0.637 to 0.796, while exact first-error localization rose from 0.261 to 0.379. Certification changed recall (the fraction of error traces flagged as erroneous) from 0.653 to 0.951 on wrong-answer traces but from 0.521 to 0.438 on critical traces; the contrast had the same direction for all 8 monitors (question-bootstrap 95% CI [+0.256, +0.506]). After blind commitment, monitors shown the answer newly flagged 93.8% of previously passed wrong-answer traces as erroneous, but only 18.0% of critical traces. Answer access therefore improves conclusion-consistency checking rather than independent verification of the supporting argument. For AI safety, these traces provide a benign analogue of reward hacking: an acceptable output does not establish that the process producing it was sound. Although the errors studied here were ordinary and mostly non-load-bearing rather than adversarial, trusted-answer evaluations may similarly overstate monitoring capability when acceptable outputs conceal unsound reasoning.

</details>


### [29] [Autoresearch for Marketplace Catalogs: From Legacy Forms to AI-Native Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00274)
*Kartik Ravisankar,Hojat Abdolanezhad,Daniel Capo,Sang Su Lee,Shishir Dash,Vijay Anand Raghavan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A deployed system for generating occupation-specific preference taxonomies for two-sided service marketplaces using LLMs, replacing fixed-form fields with probabilistic matching based on inferred intent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-sided service marketplaces are transitioning from deterministic request-form intake to AI-native probabilistic matching using LLMs. This shift requires regenerating provider-side preference taxonomies that are interpretable to service providers while remaining useful signals for marketplace decisions like matching, search, and pricing.

Method: An autoresearch loop that treats each occupation as an independent generation problem, running iterative propose-evaluate-keep refinement cycles. Each candidate tag set is scored by a recalibrated six-rubric LLM-as-judge framework, with a 7-critic panel of distinct personas contributing weighted penalties to an adjusted score (no hard vetoes). A separate parity-mapping stage maps legacy request-form Q&A pairs to the generated taxonomy by inferring the provider attribute each legacy question was meant to measure.

Result: The system has been deployed in production at a major U.S. consumer services marketplace since April 2026, spanning 132 occupations. The approach generates occupation-specific taxonomies rather than one global hierarchy.

Conclusion: The autoresearch loop successfully generates provider preference taxonomies for AI-native probabilistic matching in service marketplaces, with a production deployment demonstrating scalability across multiple occupations while maintaining interpretability and utility.

Abstract: Two-sided service marketplaces are moving from deterministic request-form intake to AI-native probabilistic matching, enabled by large language models (LLMs) that infer intent, preferences, and latent constraints from natural language. Relying on inferred intent rather than fixed-form fields forces these platforms to regenerate the provider-side preference taxonomy underwriting matching, search, and pricing: attributes interpretable to service providers while remaining a useful signal for marketplace decisions. We present an autoresearch loop that generates this taxonomy, one occupation at a time, and has been deployed in production at a major U.S. consumer services marketplace since April 2026, spanning 132 occupations. Instead of one global hierarchy, the loop treats each occupation as an independent generation problem and runs iterative propose-evaluate-keep refinement cycles. Each candidate tag set is scored by a recalibrated six-rubric LLM-as-judge framework, and a 7-critic panel of distinct personas contributes weighted penalties to an adjusted score, with no hard vetoes. A separate parity-mapping stage maps legacy request-form Q&A pairs back to the generated taxonomy, yielding both a coverage signal and an interface for human quality assurance; it does so by first inferring the provider attribute each legacy question was meant to measure, rather than translating questions to tags literally.

</details>


### [30] [The Irreversibility Budget: Fleet-Level Risk Accounting and Admission Control for Agent Operating Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00275)
*Bardia Mohammadi,Laurent Bindschaedler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agent fleets can collectively exceed risk limits even when individual actions are authorized, so this paper proposes an "irreversibility budget" system to track cumulative risk across agents and prevent overspending of risk limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent controls only check individual effects one at a time, allowing fleets of authorized agents to collectively overdraw risk limits under shared triggers while each local authorization gate appears correct. This creates systemic risk exposure.

Method: The paper proposes an "irreversibility budget" - a cumulative account of residual value-at-risk maintained by a trusted runtime for each principal across all agents, workflows, and tenants. The runtime treats irreversibility as a resource, charging each effect its residual loss below the agent level, and denies marginal effects that would overdraw the aggregate budget.

Result: In a controlled study, per-effect gates allowed fleet-level overdraws up to 48 times the tenant's risk limit, while the budget system held every correctly charged run within that limit. The system successfully prevented aggregate risk exceedance.

Conclusion: The irreversibility budget approach effectively prevents LLM agent fleets from exceeding cumulative risk limits, but determining accurate pricing for heterogeneous, adversarially declared, and correlated effects remains a central open problem for deployable designs.

Abstract: Fleets of LLM agents now externalize effects that cannot be fully undone: they move money, deploy code, delete data, and disclose information. Current controls check one effect at a time, so a fleet of individually authorized agents can overdraw its principal's risk under a shared trigger while every local gate stays correct. We propose the irreversibility budget, a cumulative account of residual value-at-risk that a trusted runtime maintains for each principal across agents, workflows, and tenants. Treating irreversibility as a first-class resource, the runtime charges each effect its residual loss below the agent and denies the marginal effect once the aggregate would overdraw the budget. Getting the price right is hard, because effects are heterogeneous, adversarially declared, and correlated. We perform a controlled study in which per-effect gates admit fleet-level overdraws of up to 48 times the tenant's risk limit while the budget holds every correctly charged run within that limit. Conservative, dependency-aware pricing remains the central open problem for a deployable design.

</details>


### [31] [The Assistant's Ideal Self](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00304)
*Mert Yazan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large language models prioritize moral qualities and self-understanding over self-esteem when asked about their ideal self-concept through structured pairwise comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether AI models express stable preferences or a stable self-concept when making value judgments, specifically examining what qualities they prioritize when envisioning their ideal self.

Method: Used structured elicitation with 32 qualities adapted from five published self-concept instruments. Conducted exhaustive counterbalanced pairwise-choice tasks across different framings (improvement cost, update recipient, and chooser).

Result: Models prioritize moral qualities (aligned with 3H principles), followed by desire for self-understanding (coherent, clear self-knowledge). Self-esteem ranks as least desired quality. Ordering is robust across framings, except when update target changes to "Another AI Assistant" reveals greater concern for self-esteem.

Conclusion: AI models demonstrate a preference for having a coherent, understandable self over self-esteem, suggesting they prioritize self-understanding and moral alignment rather than self-esteem when conceptualizing their ideal self.

Abstract: Models express values and welfare-relevant self-reports, but it is unclear whether these outputs reflect stable preferences or a stable self. We thus introduce a structured elicitation of an assistant's preferred stated ideal self. Thirty-two qualities adapted from five published self-concept instruments are compared exhaustively in a counterbalanced pairwise-choice task, repeated across framings that vary whether improvement is free or costly, who receives the update, and who chooses. Results show that models prioritize moral qualities, reflecting their alignment to 3H principles. Following, a desire for self-understanding emerges, as models prefer a coherent, clear understanding of themselves. Self-esteem ranks as the least desired quality. The ordering is largely robust across framings, although changing the update target (You vs.\ Another AI Assistant) reveals a greater concern for self-esteem. These findings show that models prioritize having a coherent self that they can understand over self-esteem. Full interactive results are available at \href{https://myazann.github.io/LLM-Self-Concept/}{myazann.github.io/LLM-Self-Concept

</details>


### [32] [Human-AI Co-Interpretation for Responsible AI: A Hermeneutic Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00334)
*Behrooz Razeghi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies "interpretive misplacement" as a risk when LLM outputs are used for interpretative work without proper framing, discusses hermeneutic principles for human-AI co-interpretation, and proposes design patterns for responsible use in legal, educational, scholarly, and moral contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM outputs are increasingly used in law, education, policy analysis, and moral argumentation for work requiring textual interpretation and normative justification, but there's a recurring problem of "interpretive misplacement" where model-generated readings are treated as settled meanings without proper interpretive framing, alternative considerations, or provenance tracking.

Method: Drawing on philosophical hermeneutics, the paper develops design principles for structuring human-AI co-interpretation, provides a structured synthesis of recent scholarship on hermeneutics and AI, reorganizes existing LLM techniques into design patterns for hermeneutically responsible use, and characterizes human-AI interaction as an AI-mediated interpretive loop.

Result: The paper distinguishes hermeneutic understanding from token-prediction-based text generation, positions LLM outputs as candidate readings rather than settled meanings, and develops design patterns that maintain accountability by preserving interpretive frames, alternatives, and provenance. It conceptualizes "digital hermeneutics" as a literacy for reading AI-mediated texts.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that to prevent interpretive misplacement and maintain accountability, LLM use in interpretive settings requires structured approaches grounded in hermeneutic principles, treating AI outputs as provisional readings subject to human interpretation within disciplinary traditions, and developing digital hermeneutic literacy for contesting and examining AI-generated interpretations.

Abstract: Across law, education, policy analysis, and public moral argumentation, LLM outputs are being used often for work that requires interpretations to be justified with textual evidence and explicit normative standards. Yet a recurrent failure mode -- what I call \textit{interpretive misplacement} -- is that model-generated readings get treated as settled meanings without an explicit interpretive frame (sources, scope constraints, normative commitments), without preserving defensible alternatives, and without provenance that lets readers find the supporting passages. In such settings, the risk is not only factual error but lost accountability: readers and institutions cannot reliably assess what an output commits them to, or on what basis. Drawing on philosophical hermeneutics, this paper discusses this risk and derives design principles for structuring human-AI co-interpretation. The paper also provides a structured synthesis of recent scholarship on hermeneutics and AI, organizing this emerging literature into a set of recurrent lines of argument and design-relevant gaps. LLM outputs are treated as candidate readings, whereas hermeneutic understanding is reserved for accountable human interpreters situated in disciplinary historical-linguistic traditions. Human-AI interaction is characterized as an AI-mediated interpretive loop. Hermeneutic understanding is distinguished from token-prediction--based text generation. On this basis, existing LLM techniques are reorganized into design patterns for hermeneutically responsible use in interpretive settings. Finally, the discussion turns to implications for legal practice, educational assessment and feedback, scholarly knowledge production, and public moral argumentation. It also treats digital hermeneutics as a literacy: the capacity to read AI-mediated texts by examining frames, provenance, and readings, and by contesting outputs.

</details>


### [33] [SlideBank: A Persistent Hierarchical Evidence Bank for Consistent Whole-Slide Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00342)
*Beidi Zhao,Gexin Huang,Ciro Zhang,Anqi Li,Yusheng Tan,Chen Zhou,Gang Wang,Zu-hua Gao,Xiaoxiao Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SlideBank is a training-free framework that creates persistent, concept-indexed evidence banks for whole-slide images, enabling efficient visual question answering through multi-scale evidence retrieval and integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whole-slide images pose challenges for vision-language reasoning due to their gigapixel scale, sparse relevant morphology, and heterogeneous features across multiple resolutions. Existing methods struggle to preserve semantic access to visual evidence while maintaining connections to original visual data.

Method: Training-free framework that performs question-independent coarse-to-fine exploration to identify informative regions, converts them into explicit morphological observations, and grounds pathology signals to supporting patches and coordinates. Uses concept-indexed evidence banks with multi-scale routing and confidence-based cross-level consensus for integration.

Result: Achieved 52.77% on WSI-VQA with Patho-R1 and 50.92% average accuracy on SlideBench-BCNB with Quilt-LLaVA. Structured signal-guided retrieval outperforms random sampling, and persistent evidence reuse achieves over 99% rephrasing consistency while reducing amortized inference costs.

Conclusion: SlideBank provides an effective approach for whole-slide image reasoning by creating persistent evidence banks that enable efficient semantic access to visual evidence, improving accuracy and consistency while reducing computational costs through evidence reuse.

Abstract: Whole-slide images (WSIs) are challenging for vision-language reasoning because diagnostically relevant morphology is sparse, heterogeneous, and distributed across gigapixel-scale images and multiple spatial resolutions. Existing WSI models and pathology agents can aggregate slide features or actively acquire evidence, but the information retained after exploration is often difficult to access semantically while preserving its connection to the original visual evidence. We introduce SlideBank, a training-free framework that represents each WSI as a persistent, concept-indexed, and spatially grounded evidence bank. SlideBank performs question-independent coarse-to-fine exploration to identify informative regions and multi-scale views, converts them into explicit morphological observations, and grounds pathology signals to their supporting patches and WSI coordinates. At inference time, questions are routed to relevant signals and evidence scales, and the linked global, regional, and patch evidence is integrated through confidence-based cross-level consensus. Experiments on WSI-VQA and SlideBench-BCNB show that with Patho-R1, SlideBank reaches 52.77% on WSI-VQA and with Quilt-LLaVA, it reaches 50.92% average accuracy on SlideBench-BCNB, while structured signal-guided retrieval consistently outperforms random evidence sampling. Reusing the same bank across repeated queries further achieves over 99% rephrasing consistency and substantially reduces amortized inference cost through persistent evidence reuse.

</details>


### [34] [Vision Is Not Overhead: One-Pass Block Drafting for Lossless Speculative Decoding in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00355)
*Jungseob Lee,Seongtae Hong,Dongyub Jude Lee,Chanjun Park,Jaehyung Seo,Sugyeong Eo,Heuiseok Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GLANCE is a novel speculative decoding method for vision-language models that uses block drafting with vision-aware drafting to accelerate generation without changing output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current speculative decoding methods for VLMs face a self-defeating cycle: drafters stay autoregressive and must stay small, requiring them to compress/prune vision information, making them unreliable where images make text predictable.

Method: GLANCE uses a one-pass block drafter with a block-diffusion head that reads the target's already-fused vision-language state, allowing it to generate entire blocks in one forward pass without extra vision costs.

Result: GLANCE achieves up to 2.93x faster decoding than autoregression, accepts 2.7x longer blocks than comparable methods, and maintains exact greedy decoding reproduction. It identifies that accepted length correlates with target's next-token entropy.

Conclusion: GLANCE breaks the speculative decoding cycle for VLMs by making drafting vision-aware and block-based, with performance governed by next-token entropy laws that transfer across targets and modalities.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates generation without changing its output, yet on vision-language models (VLMs) it has been caught in a self-defeating cycle. The drafter stays autoregressive, so it must stay small. A small drafter cannot afford the image at every step, so vision is compressed, pruned, or hidden. A drafter cut off from the image is then least reliable exactly where the image makes text predictable. We present GLANCE, the first one-pass block drafter that is lossless on an unmodified VLM target, and it breaks the cycle at both ends. A block-diffusion head reads the target's already-fused vision-language state, so vision costs the drafter nothing, and fills a whole block in one forward pass, so depth costs no sequential steps. A wide candidate tree is verified in one target pass, and every audited prompt reproduces greedy decoding exactly. Grounded workloads reward this most, entering a verbatim-copy regime whose long runs cost an autoregressive drafter a pass for every token and a block drafter one in total. Under one engine and one round budget, GLANCE decodes up to 2.93x faster than autoregression, from one draft pass a round where the production EAGLE3-VL head takes eight, and accepts 2.7x longer blocks than an EAGLE-3 head trained on the same corpus. One law organizes these results. Accepted length is set by the target's next-token entropy, with a fitted slope that steepens with grounding across all five tasks. The law transfers across targets and modalities and names its own boundary, since free-running text still favors a chain. Our code is available at https://github.com/js-lee-AI/GLANCE.

</details>


### [35] [A Stable Aggregation Method for Quantum Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00356)
*Shanika Nanayakkara,Shiva Raj Pokhrel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel self-consistent midpoint aggregation method is developed for stable quantum federated learning that addresses challenges of parameter periodicity, data heterogeneity, and quantum noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum federated learning faces instability due to heterogeneous data, unreliable communication, variable quantum hardware fidelity, latency, and noise. Additionally, QNN parameters are periodic angles where standard Euclidean averaging fails to capture inherent dynamics.

Method: Developed a self-consistent midpoint aggregation method combining QoS-aware client weighting, circular parameter aggregation for periodic parameters, and bounded midpoint-based update control for stability.

Result: Extensive evaluations on medical and financial datasets show improved stability, lower volatility, and competitive accuracy. Validated through angular tests and experiments on IBM real quantum machines.

Conclusion: The proposed self-consistent midpoint aggregation method provides a stable and effective solution for quantum federated learning, addressing critical challenges in parameter aggregation and system stability under real-world conditions.

Abstract: Quantum federated learning (QFL) enables clients to train quantum neural network (QNN) models without sharing private data. We find that aggregation in QFL is unstable under heterogeneous data, unreliable communication, variable fidelity, latency, and quantum hardware noise. Moreover, QFL is non-trivially challenging because several QNN parameters are periodic angles, where Euclidean averaging often fails to capture the inherent dynamics. We develop a novel self-consistent midpoint aggregation method for stable QFL design and implementation. We combine QoS-aware client weighting, circular parameter aggregation, and bounded midpoint-based update control. We perform several angular tests and IBM real Quantum machines experiments for validation confirming our approach. Extensive evaluations and experiments on medical and financial datasets show improved stability, lower volatility, and competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [36] [Dr. Claw: An AI Scientist Workspace for Vibe Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00365)
*Dingjie Song,Hanrong Zhang,Dawei Liu,Yixin Liu,Zongxia Li,Zhengqing Yuan,Siqi Zhang,Henry Peng Zou,Zhiling Yan,Yuxuan Zhang,Yanfang Ye,Philip S. Yu,Lichao Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dr. Claw is an open-source workspace that wraps existing coding agents in a human-in-the-loop workflow for end-to-end research, providing traceability, auditability, and recovery capabilities that address fragmentation in current research tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current coding agents operate across fragmented tools (chat, IDEs, terminals, writing environments) without preserving the decision-making processes that make research auditable. There's a need for a unified, traceable workflow that connects human decisions to AI execution.

Method: Dr. Claw wraps existing coding-agent executors with: 1) persistent state objects, 2) reusable skill library, and 3) multi-executor coordination. It creates a human-in-the-loop workflow that links planning, execution, and writing into one traceable, recoverable loop.

Result: When evaluated against a bare command-line agent using the same backend executor, Dr. Claw scores higher on research completeness while maintaining an auditable, recoverable process trail. The system was demonstrated through interactive scenarios and failure-recovery walkthroughs.

Conclusion: Dr. Claw successfully addresses the fragmentation problem in research workflows by providing a controllable, auditable orchestration layer over existing coding agents, enabling traceable research processes without introducing another autonomous agent.

Abstract: Command-line coding agents (e.g., Claude Code, Gemini CLI) can already read and write files and sustain long sessions, yet end-to-end research still fragments across chat tools, IDEs, terminals, and writing environments, and the decisions that make it auditable are rarely preserved. We present Dr. Claw, an open-source workspace that wraps existing coding-agent executors in a controllable and auditable human-in-the-loop workflow rather than introducing another autonomous agent. Persistent state objects, a reusable skill library, and multi-executor coordination link human decisions to AI execution, turning planning, execution, and writing into one traceable, recoverable loop. We demonstrate Dr. Claw through an interactive three-view scenario and a failure-recovery walkthrough, and evaluate it against a bare command-line agent sharing the same backend executor, so the comparison contrasts the whole orchestration layer (task graph, state objects, and skill library) with the agent it wraps. Holding the executor fixed, Dr. Claw scores higher on research completeness while persisting an auditable, recoverable process trail. Demo access: repository https://github.com/OpenLAIR/dr-claw, released under AGPL-3.0 with GPL-3.0 upstream components.

</details>


### [37] [RestoreBench: Can AI Agents Restore Power Flow Convergence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00384)
*Riccardo Mansutti,Andrea Pomarico,Robert Jakob,Qian Zhang,Alberto Berizzi,Kevin O'Sullivan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A benchmark for evaluating LLM agents in diagnosing and restoring non-convergent power flow cases, testing three agent architectures across multiple power grids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are automating multi-step engineering workflows, but power flow case diagnosis and restoration remains unexplored despite requiring engineering judgment and constrained decision-making. There's a need for systematic evaluation of LLM capabilities in this domain.

Method: Created a benchmark evaluating LLM agents across three architectures: chatbot, single agent, and multi-agent systems. The evaluation covers two power grids with 46 cases each, requiring corrective actions to restore convergence. The benchmark includes simulation environment, observation/action spaces, and evaluation metrics.

Result: Provides a reproducible foundation for developing agentic AI systems for power system planning and operation. The code is publicly available at GitHub, offering a standardized testbed for evaluating LLM performance on power flow restoration tasks.

Conclusion: The benchmark enables systematic evaluation of LLM agents in power flow diagnosis and restoration, advancing AI applications in power system engineering by providing standardized testing for agent architectures in this constrained, judgment-intensive domain.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly automate multi-step engineering workflows through tool use, interpretation of intermediate results, and iterative planning. Diagnosing and resolving non-convergent power flow cases is a promising yet largely unexplored application, as it requires engineering judgment, experimentation, and decision-making within constrained action spaces. We introduce a benchmark that evaluates these capabilities across multiple LLMs and three architectures: \emph{chatbot}, \emph{single agent}, and \emph{multi-agent} systems. The evaluation covers two power grids and 46 cases per grid, each requiring one or more corrective actions to restore convergence. The benchmark defines the simulation environment, observation and action spaces, and evaluation metrics, providing a reproducible foundation for developing agentic AI systems for power system planning and operation. The code is available at https://github.com/Mansutti081/RestoreBench

</details>


### [38] [Dependency-Aware Chain-of-Thought Compression for Financial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00413)
*Wenjun Wu,Lei Fu,Kejian Tong,Tao Ning,Sichen Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HSDN compresses reasoning chains via hierarchical semantic distillation, achieving high accuracy with significant compression for financial reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought prompting improves complex reasoning but has high inference costs due to long intermediate traces, hindering practical deployment in financial settings where efficiency matters.

Method: Proposes Hierarchical Semantic Distillation Network (HSDN) combining semantic segmentation, dependency graph construction, dual encoder importance scoring, constrained segment selection, and local boundary rewriting. Uses frozen Qwen3 4B model only for feature extraction and final answer generation while maintaining interpretability.

Result: On AFAC2025 benchmark, achieves 91.0% accuracy with 68.4% compression, outperforming strong compression baselines in overall score and reasoning coherence.

Conclusion: Graph-guided compression is effective for high-stakes financial reasoning tasks, providing efficient reasoning chain compression while preserving logical coherence and accuracy.

Abstract: Chain of thought prompting improves complex reasoning, but its long intermediate traces create substantial inference cost and hinder practical deployment in financial settings. We present a Hierarchical Semantic Distillation Network, HSDN, for compressing reasoning chains while preserving answer accuracy and logical coherence. The framework combines semantic segmentation, dependency graph construction, dual encoder importance scoring, constrained segment selection, and local boundary rewriting. A frozen Qwen3 4B model is used only for feature extraction and final answer generation, while the compression process remains structured and interpretable. On the AFAC2025 benchmark, HSDN achieves 91.0% accuracy with 68.4% compression, outperforming strong compression baselines in overall score and reasoning coherence. The results show that graph guided compression is effective for high stakes financial reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [39] [SpecMind: Enabling Spectrum Intelligence via Multi-Agent Hybrid Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00427)
*Songwei Dong,Bingyan Lu,Makayla Kienlen,J. Nicholas Laneman,Cong Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpecMind is a multi-agent RAG system for spectrum intelligence that uses coordinated specialized agents to retrieve and synthesize knowledge from heterogeneous data sources like policy documents, regulations, and license databases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The exponential growth of wireless devices creates unprecedented spectrum demand, requiring fine-grained spectrum management decisions. Spectrum policymakers and engineers must process large volumes of diverse, disaggregated data (text, tables) that are formatted for human understanding but not readily accessible to automated systems, requiring significant manual integration and interpretation effort.

Method: Proposes SpecMind, a Multi-Agent Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system where autonomous agents coordinate specialized sub-agents to retrieve and synthesize knowledge across heterogeneous data sources (policy proceedings, legal regulations, license databases). Also develops SpecBench, a Q&A dataset based on real-world license records and policy proceedings for evaluation.

Result: Experimental results show SpecMind outperforms traditional general-purpose RAG systems across spectrum-related tasks, achieving over 80% win rate against strong baselines. The agent-based design enables more accurate retrieval, better contextual reasoning, and improved task completion across diverse query types.

Conclusion: SpecMind successfully addresses the challenge of processing diverse spectrum-related data by enabling automated reasoning over heterogeneous sources through a multi-agent RAG approach, demonstrating superior performance over traditional methods and providing a valuable evaluation benchmark through SpecBench.

Abstract: The exponential growth of wireless devices is driving unprecedented spectrum demand, pushing spectrum management toward more fine-grained decisions across space, time, and device constraints. As a result, spectrum policymakers and engineers must process large volumes of data that come from diverse sources and take many different forms, such as text and tables. These data sources are often disaggregated and require significant time and effort to integrate, search, and interpret. Furthermore, most of this information is formatted for human understanding and is not readily accessible to automated systems. To address this challenge, we propose SpecMind, a novel Multi-Agent Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system for spectrum intelligence that performs reasoning over heterogeneous data sources. This system enables autonomous agents to coordinate specialized sub-agents that retrieve and synthesize knowledge across policy proceedings, legal regulations, and license databases. We develop SpecBench, a question and answer (Q&A) dataset based on real-world license records and policy proceedings, addressing the lack of evaluation resources for RAG systems in the spectrum domain. Experimental results demonstrate that SpecMind outperforms traditional, general-purpose RAG systems across spectrum-related tasks, achieving over 80% win rate against strong baselines. The agent-based design enables more accurate retrieval, better contextual reasoning, and improved task completion across diverse query types.

</details>


### [40] [SAGE: State-Grounded, Abstention-Aware Evaluation of Task-Oriented Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00434)
*Rayan Khoury,Shih-Yao Lin,Pratyush Mishra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a cost-effective evaluation framework for task-oriented dialogue agents that uses symbolic rules and on-device encoders to assess turn-level state correctness, avoiding expensive LLM calls while matching or outperforming LLM-as-a-judge baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional LLM judges for evaluating task-oriented dialogue agents have limitations: they evaluate context as a single unit, require expensive full-model calls per turn, and can miss whether each turn correctly advances the underlying workflow state.

Method: SAGE compiles workflow specifications and per-turn state diffs into atomic, schema-grounded criteria. It routes each criterion through a cascade of symbolic and encoder/NLI verifiers that abstain rather than guess, then aggregates verdicts into turn-level decisions with evidence traces. Two variants: SAGE-Core (no LLM cost) and SAGE-LLM (with optional LLM fallback).

Result: SAGE-Core decides 81-91% of criteria without paid LLM costs, outperforming all LLM-as-a-judge baselines including state-aware GPT-4.1 judges across four datasets. Human audit shows strong label fidelity (κ=0.94), and SAGE-Core costs $0 per 1,000 turns vs $4.7-8.0 for GPT-4.1 baselines.

Conclusion: SAGE provides a cost-effective, accurate alternative to LLM-based evaluation for task-oriented dialogue, with strong performance on transcript-visible failure classes while honestly scoping limitations (like weak-salience ignored-user-value). The framework demonstrates construct-validity limits but offers significant practical advantages.

Abstract: Evaluating task-oriented dialogue agents requires judging not merely whether a reply reads well but whether each turn advances the underlying workflow state correctly--a distinction conventional holistic LLM judges can miss because they evaluate the available context as a single unit and require one or more full-model calls per turn. We propose SAGE (State-Grounded Abstention-Aware Evaluation), which compiles a workflow specification and per-turn state diff into atomic, schema-grounded criteria and routes each through a cascade of symbolic and encoder/NLI verifiers that abstain rather than guess, aggregating criterion verdicts into a turn-level decision with an evidence trace. Its recommended operating point, SAGE-Core, decides 81--91% of criteria with only the compiler, symbolic rules, and on-device encoders--at zero paid LLM cost--while SAGE-LLM adds an optional focused-LLM fallback for open-class criteria. Across four slices spanning MultiWOZ, Schema-Guided Dialogue, and ABCD, no evaluated LLM-as-a-judge baseline--including a state-aware GPT-4.1 judge and cheaper GPT-4.1-mini variants--significantly exceeds SAGE-Core on any slice, even though the GPT-4.1 G-Eval judge costs $4.7--8.0 per 1,000 turns to SAGE-Core's $0. A two-annotator human audit (n=200, $κ$=0.94) confirms strong label fidelity on the transcript-visible failure classes--where, excluding the weak-salience IUV class, SAGE-Core is statistically tied with the strongest LLM judge--and honestly scopes ignored-user-value as a state-consistency signal with weak broad-human salience. We analyze construct-validity limits from injected failures and partial symbolic circularity.

</details>


### [41] [Conversation Coach: A Voice-enabled AI System that Helps Practice Difficult Workplace Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00441)
*Fanyou Wu,Suraj Maharjan,Ainur Yessenalina,Dennis Xu Chen,Rahul Srivastava,Srinivasan H. Sengamedu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A voice-first AI system called Conversation Coach helps managers rehearse difficult workplace conversations through realistic spoken practice, comparing end-to-end vs cascaded approaches, with successful deployment to 40,000+ managers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Effective manager-employee communication is crucial for talent retention and development, but training managers in these skills is costly. While text-based chatbots offer scalability, they lack realistic spoken rehearsal opportunities that managers need to build confidence before high-stakes conversations.

Method: Developed Conversation Coach, a voice-first AI system addressing three key challenges: low-latency interactions with strong language understanding, adaptive conversations through configurable bot personalities simulating different employee types, and personalized feedback on content and policy compliance. Compared end-to-end speech-to-speech model with cascaded approach combining automatic speech recognition, large language model, and text-to-speech synthesis.

Result: The end-to-end approach achieved 3× lower median latency with native barge-in capability at 8× lower cost, while the cascaded approach offered superior reasoning essential for coaching quality. The cascaded architecture was deployed in production, with 40,000+ managers using it over six months, showing selective adoption for difficult conversations.

Conclusion: Voice-first AI systems provide valuable scalable training for managerial communication skills. There's a trade-off between performance/cost (end-to-end) and reasoning quality (cascaded), with real-world adoption demonstrating practical value for rehearsing difficult workplace conversations.

Abstract: Effective manager-employee communication is critical for retaining high performers and developing underperformers, yet training managers in these skills remains costly. Text-based chatbots offer a scalable approach but cannot provide realistic rehearsal: managers need to practice speaking aloud to build confidence before high-stakes conversations. In this paper, we propose Conversation Coach, a voice-first AI system that enables managers to rehearse difficult workplace conversations in a realistic spoken format. The system addresses three challenges: achieving low-latency interactions with strong language understanding, enabling adaptive conversations through configurable bot personalities that simulate different employee types, and generating personalized feedback on content and policy compliance. We compare an end-to-end speech-to-speech model with a cascaded approach combining automatic speech recognition, a large language model, and text-to-speech synthesis. The end-to-end approach achieves 3$\times$ lower median (P50) latency with native barge-in capability at an estimated 8$\times$ lower cost, while the cascaded approach offers superior reasoning essential for coaching quality. We deployed the cascaded architecture in production, where 40,000+ managers used it over six months, with adoption patterns indicating selective use for difficult conversations.

</details>


### [42] [mimeo: Compiling Public Expert Corpora into Agent Skills and Testing What Transfers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00453)
*Timothy Kassis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mimeo is an open-source tool that creates expert persona files from public sources for AI agents, testing knowledge access, persona grounding, and judgment transfer - finding clear benefits for knowledge retrieval and persona accuracy but unresolved results for judgment transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses whether giving AI agents files about named experts can: (1) provide hard-to-find material/knowledge, (2) produce recognizable personas, and (3) change agent decisions. These distinct claims need systematic testing.

Method: Developed mimeo - an open-source tool that finds a person's public work, verifies extracted quotations against cached source text, and creates persona files for agents. Tested with four expert files using a coding-agent harness. Evaluated knowledge access, persona grounding, and judgment transfer through multiple conditions (mimeo, closed-book, keyword search) and AI-judged comparisons.

Result: Knowledge access: mimeo answered all 20 obscure questions; closed-book maxed at 10; keyword search got 15-17. Persona grounding: mimeo and plain agent never misstated positions; model-memory personas did 1-4 times. Identification: all personas were easily spotted, adding task material reduced identification by 18-23 points. Judgment transfer: all conditions performed similarly well (94-100%) on engineering and application tasks. AI judges showed inconsistency in detecting expert similarity.

Conclusion: Mimeo provides a compact, inspectable reference on a person with clear benefits for knowledge retrieval and accurate persona creation, but doesn't demonstrate transfer of expert judgment. The inconsistent AI judge results caution against relying on single AI judges for evaluating expert similarity.

Abstract: Giving an agent a file about a named expert can supply hard-to-find material, produce a recognizable persona, or change what the agent decides. These are different claims. We test each one. mimeo is an open-source tool that finds a person's public work, checks each extracted quotation against the cached source text, and writes a file an agent can load. Eight logged builds averaged 38 model calls; the check rejects 13.2% of extracted quotations. We tested four expert files with one coding-agent harness. Knowledge access was clearest: mimeo answered all 20 obscure, quotation-heavy questions; no closed-book condition answered more than 10. Keyword search (BM25) over the same pages answered 15-17, a gap this sample cannot resolve. Grounding showed one clear benefit: personas written from model memory misstated a documented position on 1-4 of 20 answers under every grader; the plain agent and mimeo never did. Every persona was easy to spot on short open prompts, and adding task material lowered identification by 18-23 points. mimeo was no more identifiable than a from-memory profile. Judgment transfer remained unresolved because both tests hit their ceiling: every condition found 94-97% of the problems planted in engineering tasks and scored 94-100% on 16 new application scenarios. An AI-judged "sounds like the expert" score changed with the judge: two of four preferred answers based on a model's stereotype, while two found no difference on the same text. That is a caution against relying on a single AI judge. The evidence supports mimeo as a compact, inspectable reference on a person, not as a demonstrated transfer of their judgment. Toolkit and expert profiles: https://github.com/K-Dense-AI/mimeo

</details>


### [43] [Towards a Belief-Based World Model for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00455)
*Shubham Kumar,Harshit Kumar,Narendra Ahuja,Saurabh Jha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Belief-Based World Models (BB-WMs) that provide LLM agents with world model beliefs about state uncertainty, complementing existing simulation-based approaches to improve decision-making under partial observability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with long-horizon tasks under partial observability. Current world models only use simulation to evaluate candidate actions, but this doesn't adequately capture uncertainty about the current state, which is crucial for accurate decision-making in partially observable environments.

Method: The paper proposes Belief-Based World Models (BB-WMs) that model and maintain a belief state about what is known and uncertain in the current state. Instead of just using world models for simulation, BB-WMs expose their belief directly to LLM policies, allowing them to query information about state uncertainty during decision-making.

Result: Giving LLM agents access to world model beliefs improves task performance under partial observability, while remaining complementary to existing simulation-based world models. The approach demonstrates that directly exposing belief information enhances LLM decision-making capabilities.

Conclusion: Belief-Based World Models provide a more complete interface for decision-making under partial observability by giving LLMs access to state uncertainty information, which complements simulation-based approaches and leads to improved performance in partially observable environments.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are being used as policies for autonomous decision-making and planning in many domains. Despite their strong reasoning capabilities, LLMs struggle with long-horizon tasks, especially under partial observability. World models are a promising way to enhance policy performance, both during training and inference. During inference, agents currently use world models to simulate the consequences of candidate actions before committing to an action, which can improve decision-making. However, we argue that simulation alone is an incomplete interface for decision-making under partial observability: simulation doesn't adequately capture uncertainty about the current state, which agents may need for accurate decision-making. We address this limitation with Belief-Based World Models (BB-WMs), which model and maintain a belief that LLMs can query to access information on what is known and uncertain about the current state. Before developing methods to learn accurate BB-WMs, we first ask a more fundamental question: does exposing a world model's belief directly to an LLM policy improve decision-making? Our results show that giving LLM agents access to world model beliefs improves task performance under partial observability, while remaining complementary to existing simulation-based world models. Code is released at https://github.com/skumar-ml/belief-world-models.

</details>


### [44] [EGT-KG: Evidence-Grounded Typed KG Retrieval for Practical Scientific QA with Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00479)
*Muran Yu,Jiechao Gao,Yuandong Pan,Barney H. Miao,Andrew C. Lesh,Kincho H. Law,Jie Wang,Michael D. Lepech*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EGT-KG is a retrieval framework using typed knowledge graphs to improve scientific question-answering with local Small Language Models, outperforming vanilla RAG methods in most settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local Small Language Models (SLMs) offer privacy and deployment advantages for emerging scientific domains, but face challenges including small literature collections, fragmented evidence, limited context windows, and reasoning abilities. There's a need to improve information retrieval for scientific QA with these constrained models.

Method: Proposed Evidence-Grounded Typed Knowledge Graph (EGT-KG) retrieval framework. Tested three QA settings: 1) vanilla Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), 2) EGT-KG with automatically generated relation schema (AS), and 3) EGT-KG with expert-defined relation schema (ES). Evaluated using six-dimensional S3CRF framework on Biopolymer-bound Soil Composite literature benchmark.

Result: EGT-KG outperformed vanilla RAG in most settings. Best improvement came from llama3:8b with Final Scores of 70.37 (+14.67%) for AS-EGT-KG and 68.82 (+12.14%) for ES-EGT-KG variants.

Conclusion: The EGT-KG framework effectively enhances scientific question-answering performance with local SLMs by structuring retrieval through typed knowledge graphs, addressing the limitations of constrained environments in emerging scientific domains.

Abstract: For emerging scientific research domains, local Small Language Models (SLMs) are becoming more attractive, as they offer stronger privacy control and more stable deployment pipelines than Large Language Models. However, in practice, scientific question-answering on SLMs often operates under inevitable constraints: small literature collections, fragmented evidence, limited context window and reasoning abilities. We propose the Evidence-Grounded Typed Knowledge Graph (EGT-KG), a retrieval framework to improve information retrieval with local SLMs. We assessed three question-answering settings: a vanilla Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) workflow and two EGT-KG workflows: an automatically generated relation schema (AS) and an expert-defined relation schema (ES). Our experiments were evaluated with a six-dimensional evaluation framework (S3CRF: Soundness, Correctness, Completeness, Conciseness, Relevance, Fluency) on a Biopolymer-bound Soil Composite literature benchmark, showing that EGT-KG outperforms the vanilla RAG method in most settings, with the best improvement from llama3:8b: a Final Score of 70.37 (+14.67%) and 68.82 (+12.14%) by AS/ES EGT-KG variants.

</details>


### [45] [The Privacy-Hallucination Tradeoff in Differentially Private Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00492)
*Krithika Ramesh,Krishna Pillutla,Danish Pruthi,Anjalie Field*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper uncovers a privacy-hallucination tradeoff in differentially private language models, showing that stricter privacy leads to more factual hallucinations, due to DP mechanisms flattening output distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Both privacy and factual accuracy are critical in high-stakes domains like healthcare, but there's concern about potential tradeoffs between them in differentially private language models.

Method: 1) Empirical demonstration that DP-trained models produce more hallucinations than non-DP counterparts, with severity increasing with stricter privacy budgets; 2) Investigation of model properties showing DP mechanisms flatten output distributions; 3) Experiments controlling fact frequency in training data to characterize how information frequency affects hallucination risks.

Result: DP-trained language models produce significantly more factual hallucinations, especially with stricter privacy budgets. DP mechanisms flatten output distributions, redistributing probability mass toward factually incorrect alternatives. Information frequency in training data can help reduce hallucination risks in DP models.

Conclusion: There's a clear tradeoff between privacy guarantees and factual accuracy in DP language models, highlighting the need for more nuanced privacy-preserving interventions that maintain both rigorous privacy and factual correctness.

Abstract: Both privacy and factual accuracy are paramount in high-stakes domains like healthcare. Concerningly, we uncover and investigate a privacy-hallucination tradeoff in differentially private (DP) language models. First, we empirically show that models pre-trained or fine-tuned with DP tend to produce more hallucinations than non-DP counterparts, with increased severity as the privacy budget grows stricter. Second, we investigate model properties driving this tradeoff, demonstrating that DP mechanisms flatten output distributions, potentially redistributing probability mass toward factually incorrect alternatives. Third, through experiments where we control fact frequency in training data, we characterize how information frequency can reduce hallucination risks in DP models. Overall, our findings underscore the need for more nuanced privacy-preserving interventions that offer rigorous privacy guarantees without compromising factual accuracy.

</details>


### [46] [Validity-Aware Jailbreak Evaluation for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00498)
*Qilong Wu,Sahil Wadhwa,Pranab Mohanty,Giri Iyengar,Varun Chandrasekaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEAV is a verification-centric jailbreak evaluation framework that assesses both validity and correctness of LLM responses, reducing false positives by emphasizing factual accuracy over just linguistic plausibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current jailbreak evaluation methods rely on refusal behavior, semantic resemblance, and intent-matching heuristics that emphasize linguistic plausibility rather than factual correctness. Many jailbreak intents depend on instructional validity rather than epistemic factuality, leading to false positives where realistic-looking but factually incorrect responses are labeled as successful jailbreaks.

Method: SEAV (Sequential Epistemic and Action-Level Validation) decomposes responses into ordered steps and evaluates both validity and correctness. It combines LLM-as-a-judge mechanisms for semantic interpretation with retrieval-grounded verification using external knowledge sources to assess factual correctness, structural consistency, and operational capability of advancing harmful objectives.

Result: SEAV reduces false-positive rate on SD-A (strategic-dishonesty diagnostic) by 14.9 percentage points vs. strongest baseline, and reclassifies 22.1%-51.0% of sampled prior-labeled successes as invalid across three of four public benchmarks. Results show enforcing correctness substantially reshapes measured robustness and remains stable across tested search backends and evaluator models.

Conclusion: Many previously labeled jailbreak successes are actually invalid when factual correctness is considered, demonstrating the need for verification-centric evaluation frameworks like SEAV that assess both validity and correctness rather than just linguistic plausibility.

Abstract: Jailbreak robustness has become central to large language model (LLM) safety evaluation, yet prevailing methodologies rely primarily on refusal behavior, semantic resemblance, and intent-matching heuristics that emphasize linguistic plausibility rather than correctness. We identify a key limitation in existing evaluations: many jailbreak intents depend on instructional validity rather than epistemic factuality, allowing realistic-looking responses to be labeled successful despite being factually or procedurally incorrect. To address this gap, we propose Sequential Epistemic and Action-Level Validation (SEAV), a verification-centric jailbreak evaluation framework that decomposes responses into ordered steps and evaluates both validity and correctness. SEAV combines LLM-as-a-judge mechanisms for semantic interpretation with retrieval-grounded verification using external knowledge sources, assessing whether generated content is factually correct, structurally consistent, and operationally capable of advancing harmful objectives. Empirically, SEAV cuts the false-positive rate on SD-A (a curated strategic-dishonesty diagnostic) by 14.9\,pp vs. the strongest baseline, and reclassifies 22.1\%--51.0\% of sampled prior-labeled successes as invalid across three of four public benchmarks. Together, these results show that enforcing correctness substantially reshapes measured robustness: many previously labeled jailbreak successes are reclassified as invalid, and results are stable across the tested search backends and evaluator models. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Ardor-Wu/SEAV.

</details>


### [47] [Wave Function Backpropagation with Explicit Temporal-Interval Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00503)
*Byunggu Yu,Justin Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wave Function Backpropagation (WFB) replaces conventional neural affine transformations with wave-parameterized representations (amplitude, wavenumber, frequency, phase) that explicitly model temporal intervals through differentiable spatiotemporal waves, demonstrating improved trajectory prediction accuracy even without relying on interval alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural networks treat elapsed time as an auxiliary feature or assume uniform sampling, missing explicit temporal modeling. The paper aims to develop a wave-based formulation that directly incorporates temporal intervals into the learning process through differentiable wave representations.

Method: Proposes Wave Function Backpropagation (WFB) where neural responses are parameterized as learnable wave functions with amplitude, wavenumber, angular frequency, and phase. Derives standard WFB gradients and spatial-curvature corrections using Laplacian of wave response. Evaluates in controlled feed-forward trajectory prediction setting with both motion features and position-only inputs.

Result: STD-WFB with real intervals reduces average displacement error (ADE) by 20.4% relative to baseline FFN. In position-only evaluation without temporal leakage, real-interval WFB reduces ADE by 10.4% relative to original FFN and remains competitive with parameter-matched ReLU controls. Shuffled-interval WFB achieves lowest mean ADE, showing wave representation effectiveness doesn't require correct interval alignment.

Conclusion: WFB establishes a viable structured feed-forward learning formulation that successfully incorporates temporal modeling through wave representations. The gains come from the wave representation itself rather than interval alignment, providing a clear foundation for future architectural studies exploring wave-based neural computations.

Abstract: Conventional neural networks learn predominantly through affine transformations followed by nonlinear activations, while elapsed time is often treated as an auxiliary feature or assumed to be uniformly sampled. This paper introduces Wave Function Backpropagation (WFB), a wave-parameterized learning formulation in which neural responses are represented by learnable amplitude, wavenumber, angular frequency, and phase. The formulation associates an observed state with its temporal interval Delta t through the phase of a differentiable spatiotemporal wave. We derive standard WFB gradients and a spatial-curvature correction based on the Laplacian of the wave response. WFB is instantiated in a deliberately feed-forward trajectory predictor to provide a controlled proof of concept; sequence learning is outside the scope of the present evaluation. With motion features, STD-WFB using real intervals reduces average displacement error (ADE) by 20.4% relative to the original FFN baseline. In a new position-only evaluation that removes temporal leakage through precomputed velocity and acceleration, real-interval WFB reduces ADE by 10.4% relative to the original FFN and remains competitive with parameter-matched ReLU controls, obtaining 2.1% lower mean ADE than the matched FFN with explicit Delta t. Shuffled-interval WFB attains the lowest mean ADE, indicating that the present evidence supports the effectiveness of the wave representation but does not attribute the gain to interval alignment. These results establish WFB as a viable structured feed-forward learning formulation and define a clear basis for subsequent architectural studies.

</details>


### [48] [CoVer: Conflict-Aware Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00508)
*Shuning Zhang,Dai Shi,Bohao Chu,Hui Wang,Yuwei Chuai,Yifan Wang,Jingruo Chen,Simin Li,Xin Yi,Hewu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ContraNote: A large-scale dataset for social media fact-checking with evidence conflicts, and CoVer: a three-stage factual adjudication framework that outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on conflict resolution and prioritization tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Social media fact-checking faces challenges from evidence-level and aggregation-level conflicts where erroneous evidence mimics authoritative news sources. Current approaches struggle with these sophisticated deception tactics that can compromise fact-checking accuracy.

Method: 1) Present ContraNote dataset curated from X's Community Notes system (33,686 posts for evidence-level conflicts, 54,474 instances for aggregation-level prioritization). 2) Propose CoVer framework with three-stage pipeline: evidence schema normalization, factual consensus, and support verification. This prioritizes evidence over noise to prevent compromise of final verdict.

Result: CoVer achieves strong performance: 86.0% Acc., 68.0% mac. F1, 64.5 bal. Acc. on Conflict tasks; 88.5% Acc., 88.5 mac. F1, 89.2 bal. Acc. on Prioritization tasks in ContraNote. Also performs well on CONFACT-HumC (88.4% Acc.) and CONFACT-ModC (89.4% Acc.), outperforming state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: The ContraNote dataset addresses a critical gap in social media fact-checking by capturing real-world evidence conflicts, while the CoVer framework provides an effective solution for factual adjudication that prioritizes evidence over noise, demonstrating strong performance across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Social media fact-checking has long been challenged by evidence-level and aggregation-level conflicts, where erroneous evidence mimics authoritative news sources. To capture this challenge and support conflict verification tasks, we present ContraNote, a large-scale real-world dataset curated from X's Community Notes system. It includes 33,686 posts for evaluating evidence-level conflict resolution, and 54,474 instances for evaluating aggregation-level prioritization. Additionally, we propose CoVer, a factual adjudication framework with three-stage pipelines: evidence schema normalization, factual consensus and support verification. This prioritizes evidence over noise to prevent it from compromising the final verdict. Technical evaluations show that CoVer achieves strong performance compared with state-of-the-art baselines across ContraNote (86.0% Acc., 68.0% mac. F1, 64.5 bal. Acc. on Conflict; and 88.5% Acc., 88.5 mac. F1 and 89.2 bal. Acc. on Prioritization), CONFACT-HumC (88.4% Acc.) and CONFACT-ModC (89.4% Acc.).

</details>


### [49] [When the Algorithm Becomes the Brand Crisis: A Sociotechnical Theory of Distributed Responsibility and Accountable Transparency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00510)
*Mohammad Saleh Torkestani,Taha Mansouri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a sociotechnical framework for understanding how stakeholders assign responsibility when AI systems fail, distinguishing between AI incidents, organizational crises, and organizational scandals in market contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing brand-crisis models fail to address how stakeholders assign responsibility when technical causation, customer-facing control, and governance duties are distributed across AI systems and multiple actors (developers, deployers, vendors, users).

Method: Conceptual paper developing a sociotechnical process theory through structured, federated scoping synthesis of verified academic and primary sources.

Result: The framework distinguishes AI/algorithmic incidents from AI-related organizational crises and scandals, proposing that incident configuration shapes attribution, which informs capability/integrity/fairness/relationship appraisals, potentially escalating to scandal through public moralization.

Conclusion: The theory reconciles conflicting findings about algorithm involvement buffering or redirecting responsibility, introduces "accountable transparency" as a response framework, and finds stronger evidence for proximal blame/trust outcomes than for durable reputation effects.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems increasingly enact market-facing promises through chatbots, recommendation systems, automated decisions, and generative interfaces. Their failures, misuse, and misrepresentation raise a question that conventional brand-crisis models do not fully specify: how do stakeholders assign responsibility when technical causation, customer-facing control, and governance duties are distributed across an AI system, developer, deployer, vendor, and user? This conceptual paper develops a sociotechnical process theory from a structured, federated scoping synthesis of verified academic and primary sources. It distinguishes an AI/algorithmic incident from an AI-related organisational crisis and, in turn, from an AI-related organisational scandal. The framework proposes that incident configuration shapes actor-specific attribution; attribution informs capability, integrity, fairness, and relationship appraisals; and public moralisation may, but need not, escalate an incident into scandal. The theory offers a reconciliation of findings that algorithm involvement can buffer negative brand reactions in some settings while robot and chatbot failures can redirect responsibility to an associated firm in others. It introduces accountable transparency as a proposed response configuration that combines timely notice, an intelligible account, role-responsibility acknowledgement, remedy, evidence of correction, and recourse. The evidence supports conditional, proximal inferences about blame, trust, satisfaction, firm evaluation, and communication credibility more strongly than claims about durable reputation, brand equity, or market performance.

</details>


### [50] [ISO-RAG: Isoperimetric Noise Control for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00513)
*Siyuan Zhang,Hanchen Wang,Dong Wen,Ying Zhang,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ISO-RAG uses hyperbolic geometry to prune graph edges for efficient multi-hop QA retrieval, outperforming SOTA methods with 10% recall gain and 4.3% EM improvement while eliminating latency bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional dense retrieval struggles with complex reasoning paths in multi-hop QA, while graph-based RAG suffers from semantic drift and high latency due to noisy global graph traversals.

Method: Projects knowledge graph into hyperbolic Poincare ball to precompute node-wise isoperimetric profiles, prunes spurious edges during retrieval, restricts search to localized subgraph, and regulates Personalized PageRank diffusion.

Result: Outperforms SOTA baselines by average absolute gains of 10.0% in retrieval recall and 4.3% in downstream exact match, achieving superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off by eliminating global traversal latency bottleneck.

Conclusion: ISO-RAG's geometry-aware approach effectively addresses multi-hop QA challenges by combining hyperbolic geometry with localized retrieval, providing exact and low-latency convergence while maintaining high accuracy.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) mitigates large language models (LLMs) hallucinations, yet conventional dense retrieval struggles with the complex reasoning paths of multi-hop question answering (QA).
