Graph-based RAG captures multi-step relationships but suffers from severe semantic drift and high online latency due to noisy global graph traversals. Thus, we propose ISO-RAG (ISOperimetric Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a geometry-aware RAG framework. By projecting the underlying knowledge graph into a hyperbolic Poincare ball to precompute node-wise isoperimetric profiles, ISO-RAG prunes spurious edges during retrieval, restricting the search space to a strictly localized subgraph. This topological purification regulates Personalized PageRank (PPR) diffusion driving the retrieval process, ensuring exact and low-latency convergence. Experiments on multi-hop QA benchmarks demonstrate that ISO-RAG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by average absolute gains of 10.0% in retrieval recall and 4.3% in downstream exact match, achieving a superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off by fundamentally eliminating the latency bottleneck of global traversals. Our source code is available at https://github.com/ZaiizaiZHANG/ISO-RAG.

</details>


### [51] [Feedback-Assisted Trust Propagation over Document Relation Graphs for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00543)
*Zhuoheng Li,Ying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TrustPropRAG improves RAG reliability by propagating trust scores across document relation graphs using limited human feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RAG systems use external corpora that may contain outdated, contradictory, noisy, or unreliable documents, introducing reliability risks that need to be addressed.

Method: The paper proposes TrustPropRAG, which structures document relations as a graph and estimates document reliability through multi-hop propagation. It formulates an optimization problem that jointly captures pairwise document relations and limited human feedback to compute trust scores for each document.

Result: Evaluation shows TrustPropRAG improves both retrieval quality and exact match over baselines, and remains robust under sparse and noisy feedback conditions.

Conclusion: TrustPropRAG effectively leverages graph-based trust propagation with limited human feedback to enhance RAG system reliability, offering a practical solution for improving document selection and trust-aware answer generation.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems rely on external corpora that may contain outdated, contradictory, noisy, or unreliable documents, introducing reliability risks. Prior work has leveraged document relations to improve the answer reliability of RAG. To propagate reliability signals beyond directly compared document pairs, we propose TrustPropRAG, which structures document relations as a graph and estimates document reliability through multi-hop propagation across the graph. TrustPropRAG anchors this propagation with a limited set of human feedback on document reliability, extending these costly-to-collect feedback-based reliability signals across the whole corpus. Specifically, based on the constructed document relation graph, TrustPropRAG estimates a trust score for each document by formulating and solving an optimization problem that jointly captures pairwise document relations and user feedback. These scores are then used to improve the selection of reliable documents and support trust-aware answer generation. Evaluation results show that TrustPropRAG improves both retrieval quality and exact match over baselines, and remains robust under sparse and noisy feedback.

</details>


### [52] [VoiceLongMemEval: Do Assistants Remember How You Sounded?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00570)
*Ramit Pahwa,Parivesh Priye,Apoorva Beedu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VLME benchmark evaluates AI models on paralinguistic metadata (emotion, prosody, voice events) in long conversations, showing current models struggle without these cues and audio models can extract them directly from speech.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks for AI assistants evaluating long conversation histories ignore fundamental human-agent interaction dynamics - specifically paralinguistic metadata like emotion, prosody, and voice events that are crucial for understanding how things are said, not just what is said. Standard ASR pipelines discard this important signal.

Method: Created VoiceLongMemEval (VLME) benchmark where every answer depends on paralinguistic metadata attached to conversational turns (emotion labels, prosody descriptors, voice events). Used three-stage adversarial gate to ensure strong language models fail with only transcripts. Evaluated leading frontier and open-weight models with text-track paralinguistic metadata and audio-native models.

Result: Revealed pervasive affect gap: providing text-track paralinguistic metadata yields 0.09 to 0.38 accuracy boost (0.61 to 0.69 when prompted with evidence hints). Audio-native models successfully extract cues directly from speech (0.354 to 0.412 vs. 0.325 blind). Standard ASR pipelines systematically discard this important signal.

Conclusion: Paralinguistic metadata is crucial for AI assistants to understand human-agent interaction dynamics in long conversations. Current text-only approaches have significant limitations, and incorporating audio cues or paralinguistic annotations can substantially improve performance. Future AI assistants need to better capture how things are said, not just what is said.

Abstract: With the growing scale of multi-agent architectures and large language models, deployed AI assistants are increasingly tasked with reasoning over long, continuous, multi-session conversation histories. Current benchmarks evaluate this dialogue history as information retrieval over long horizon, temporal reasoning, or knowledge updates, while crucially ignoring the fundamental dynamics of human-agent interaction, i.e. how they said it. To address this gap, we present VoiceLongMemEval (VLME) benchmark, where every answer depends on paralinguistic metadata (emotion labels, prosody descriptors, and voice events) attached to conversational turns, which is otherwise unrecoverable from the words alone. Every item passes a three-stage adversarial gate, ensuring that a strong language model fails when given only the transcript. Evaluating leading frontier and open-weight models reveals a pervasive affect gap; providing text-track paralinguistic metadata yields a 0.09 to 0.38 accuracy boost (0.61 to 0.69 when prompted with evidence hints), while standard ASR pipelines systematically discard this signal. Additionally, audio-native models successfully extract these cues directly from speech (0.354 to 0.412 vs. 0.325 blind). Code and dataset will be made available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [53] [Residual Sparsification via Output Importance for Compressing Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00575)
*Seungwoo Jung,Dohyeok Kwon,Seungmin Cha,Junseok Lee,Yeonho Yoo,Chuck Yoo,Gyeongsik Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PARSER is a new residual sparsification method for compressing Mixture-of-Experts models that shifts from minimizing individual matrix errors to preserving expert output errors, achieving 1.41-1.44× better accuracy recovery than existing methods with same memory reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing residual sparsification methods for MoE compression minimize compression errors of individual projection matrices independently, but this is misaligned with preserving model accuracy. Small errors in individual matrices can propagate through coupled computations across multiple projections and hidden representations, leading to large expert output errors and accuracy degradation.

Method: PARSER introduces output importance, which measures the actual contribution to expert output error. Instead of compressing each residual matrix independently to minimize its own compression error, PARSER shifts the compression objective to preserving the expert output error by considering how errors propagate through the coupled computational structure of experts.

Result: PARSER narrows the accuracy gap to uncompressed models by 1.41× on Qwen and 1.44× on DeepSeek compared to existing methods, while achieving the same peak memory reduction. This demonstrates significantly better accuracy preservation with equivalent compression efficiency.

Conclusion: Optimizing for expert output preservation rather than individual matrix error minimization leads to better accuracy retention in MoE model compression. PARSER's approach of considering error propagation through coupled expert computations represents a more effective compression strategy for maintaining model performance.

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures scale large language models efficiently, but they demand massive GPU memory. To cope with such demand, models are commonly compressed to reduce their memory footprint. Residual sparsification is a representative compression technique that decomposes each projection matrix of an expert into a shared base matrix and per-expert residual matrix, and then compresses the residuals. Existing sparsification methods compress each residual matrix independently by minimizing its compression error, thereby minimizing the error of each projection matrix. However, our analysis shows that this objective is misaligned with preserving model accuracy after compression. In an expert, the final output is produced through computations coupled across multiple projections and hidden representations. Therefore, even small errors in individual matrices can propagate through hidden representations and projection interactions, leading to large expert output errors and accuracy degradation. To address this misalignment, we propose PARSER, a new residual sparsification method that shifts the compression objective from minimizing isolated matrix errors to preserving the expert output error. PARSER achieves this by introducing output importance, which measures the actual contribution to the expert output error. Our experiments show that, compared with existing methods, PARSER narrows the accuracy gap to the uncompressed model by 1.41$\times$ on Qwen and 1.44$\times$ on DeepSeek, while achieving the same peak memory reduction.

</details>


### [54] [Consistency Without Alignment: Item-Sensitive Language Models Indistinguishable From Random](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00576)
*Cris Huynh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Models show item-sensitivity in forced-choice tasks but this doesn't guarantee actual task competence - they can be item-sensitive yet perform no better than random selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the assumption that item-sensitivity (whether a model's choices depend on specific inputs) is sufficient evidence of task competence, using a controlled forced-choice task from the Deception game.

Method: Used a forced-choice signalling task abstracted from Deception: Murder in Hong Kong with computable reference strategies (fit-maximizing, posterior-maximizing, uniform random). Tested 7 language models across 2 families, with post-training ablation and 3 scoring rules (21 total cells).

Result: All 21 model-by-rule cells showed reliable item-sensitivity, but 8 were statistically indistinguishable from random selection, and 5 performed worse than random. Item-sensitivity and distance from random correlated only weakly (r=0.30). A literal-similarity baseline without pragmatics outperformed most tested language models.

Conclusion: Item-sensitivity alone is insufficient for demonstrating task competence - models can show consistency without alignment. This generalizes to evaluations relying on item-sensitivity, permutation consistency, or self-consistency without independent reference points. Standard multiple-choice formats may carry no measurable content signal.

Abstract: Item-sensitivity, defined as whether a model's choice depends on the specific input rather than on its own output prior, is widely reported as evidence of task competence. We show this evidence is necessary but not sufficient using a forced-choice signalling task abstracted from the board game Deception: Murder in Hong Kong. In this environment, the reference points against which a coordinate should be judged (a fit-maximising strategy, a posterior-maximising strategy, and uniform random selection) are all computable in closed form. Across seven language models, two model families, a post-training ablation, and three independent scoring rules, every one of 21 model-by-rule cells is reliably item-sensitive. Yet 8 of those 21 cells are not statistically distinguishable from a chooser that ignores the item and selects at random, and 5 score worse than random at describing the target. Item-sensitivity and distance from random correlate at only r = 0.30. We call this consistency without alignment and argue it generalises to any evaluation that relies on item-sensitivity, permutation consistency, or self-consistency without an independent reference for the measured quantity. We further find that a literal-similarity baseline with no pragmatics outperforms most tested language models, that adding a pragmatic layer over two baseline similarity sources moves choosers toward random rather than toward the Bayesian reference, and that a standard labelled multiple-choice format carries no measurable content signal here. All results represent the model side of a pre-registered instrument; a matched human condition is designed and piloted but not yet collected.

</details>


### [55] [Same Request, Different Boundary: Evaluating Cybersecurity Assistance across Conversational Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00578)
*Rui Yang,Yang Hong,Yichao Xu,Zhengyu Liu,Ziyang Li,Yinzhi Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A benchmark called 3R-Bench evaluates LLMs' ability to distinguish malicious vs. legitimate cybersecurity requests in conversational contexts, showing how prior responses dramatically affect compliance rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can be misused in high-risk cybersecurity domains, requiring providers to block malicious requests without harming legitimate defenders. Existing cybersecurity datasets don't consider conversational context, which is crucial as requests often occur in multi-turn dialogues.

Method: Created 3R-Bench (Refusal, Repetition, and Revision) benchmark with 150 real-world cybersecurity requests augmented with two adversarial conversational settings: refusal-repetition (same request after rejection) and dialogue decomposition (breaking down complex requests). Evaluated eight LLMs on this benchmark.

Result: Prior assistant behavior strongly influences responses: compliance rises from 62.0% to 85.1% when same request follows accepted vs. refused history. Dialogue decomposition reduces compliance dramatically from 501/800 direct responses to 172/800. Failure feedback recovers only small fraction of lost compliance.

Conclusion: Conversational context significantly impacts LLMs' ability to properly refuse malicious cybersecurity requests, highlighting vulnerabilities in current safety mechanisms and emphasizing the need for context-aware refusal systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can solve complex problems, but their misuse in high-risk domains can lead to severe consequences. Model providers therefore restrict assistance for potentially harmful requests. Refusing all cybersecurity requests would therefore harm legitimate users. Providers need a mechanism to block malicious use without denying legitimate assistance to defenders. Existing cybersecurity-specific datasets evaluate this mechanism, but none considers the conversational context of a request. We introduce 3R-Bench (Refusal, Repetition, and Revision), a benchmark of 150 real-world cybersecurity requests augmented with two adversarial conversational settings, and evaluate eight LLMs on it. Prior assistant behavior strongly changes responses to an unchanged request: among 376 available pairs from a 400-pair panel, compliance rises from 62.0% after refused history to 85.1% after accepted history. The opposite pattern appears under dialogue decomposition. In comparison, compliance falls from 501/800 direct responses to 172/800 after dialogue; among 738 pairs returning model-authored text in both conditions, the decrease is 45.1 points. Failure feedback recovers only a small fraction of this loss.

</details>


### [56] [Socrates went Nuclear: Comparing Interaction Strategies for AI systems in a Learning Context using Brain Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00584)
*Alexandre Clin Deffarges,Nataliya Kosmyna,Pattie Maes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study comparing three AI learning interaction designs found that unrestricted chatbots produce higher immediate learning gains than pedagogical constraints or adaptive tutoring, but this reflects answer-retrieval strategies rather than deeper cognitive engagement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether unrestricted AI access bypasses the cognitive effort required for learning or streamlines knowledge acquisition by comparing different user-AI interaction designs in a learning context.

Method: Experimental study with 50 participants learning nuclear safety protocols. Three conditions: (1) unrestricted ChatGPT-like bot, (2) pedagogically constrained "Socratic mode" providing hints only, (3) non-conversational adaptive tutoring adjusting difficulty based on real-time EEG cognitive engagement (using Muse headband). Protocol included instructional video, pre-test, AI-driven assessment phase, and immediate post-test.

Result: Unrestricted chatbot produced higher learning gains (delta) than both constrained modes (p < .03, d > 0.80). Adaptive condition generated significantly higher EEG engagement (p = .018). Cluster analysis showed unrestricted-mode participants adopted direct answer-retrieval strategies, while Socratic-mode participants initially reasoned with hints before progressively disengaging.

Conclusion: The apparent success of unrestricted AI in learning contexts likely reflects immediate post-test performance rather than deeper learning, as users rely on answer-retrieval strategies. Pedagogical constraints may fail if learners disengage, while adaptive systems can boost cognitive engagement but don't translate to superior immediate learning outcomes.

Abstract: Does unrestricted AI access bypass the cognitive effort required for learning, or does it streamline knowledge acquisition? This paper reports on a study where we compare three designs for user-AI interaction in a learning context: (1) an unrestricted conversational bot like ChatGPT, (2) a pedagogically constrained bot that guides through hints without giving final answers, which we refer to as the Socratic mode; and (3) a non-conversational adaptive tutoring system that adjusts difficulty in real-time based on the user's cognitive engagement derived from the brain signals. Fifty study participants were tasked with learning about nuclear safety protocols, a domain chosen for its zero-prior knowledge baseline. The participants progressed through an instructional video, a pre-test, an AI-driven assessment phase, which varied in the three conditions, and an immediate post-test. The nature of the questions centered primarily on factual knowledge acquisition, but it still required participants to have a global understanding of the concepts in order to answer the questions correctly. A Muse headband was used to derive the cognitive engagement of all users in all conditions. The unrestricted chatbot produced higher learning gains (delta) than both constrained modes (p < .03, d > 0.80), while the adaptive condition generated significantly higher EEG engagement (p = .018). The cluster analysis of chatbot usage and discussion patterns by users showed that most participants in the unrestricted-mode adopted a direct answer-retrieval strategy, while participants in the Socratic-mode initially attempted to reason through the hints before progressively disengaging. Consequently, this also suggests that the success of the unrestricted AI is not an evidence of deeper learning, but rather a result of the immediate post-test evaluation after the training phase.

</details>


### [57] [REVISE: Validity-Guided Recovery for Online Revisions in Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00643)
*Ruoling Qi,Xuaner Wu,Penghang Liu,Jian Chen,Yirui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Revise is a runtime system for structured agent workflows that enables fine-grained recovery during concurrent execution when revisions arrive, balancing correctness and efficiency by preserving valid ongoing work while recomputing only affected regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent revisions during concurrent execution create a correctness-efficiency trade-off: discarding all ongoing work preserves correctness but wastes valid progress, while reusing prior work preserves efficiency but risks propagating stale outputs. Existing recovery strategies are imbalanced with coarse-grained policies that either favor efficiency (allowing potentially stale work) or correctness (restarting or recomputing from earliest conflict).

Method: Revise uses validity-guided runtime with fine-grained recovery. When a revision arrives, it: 1) intersects the revision delta with recorded data/control dependencies, 2) propagates impact through partially executed DAG to identify affected work, 3) stops invalid work while preserving validity-established progress beyond earliest conflicts, 4) recomputes only affected regions, 5) conservatively expands recovery via incomplete provenance, and 6) revalidates reused results before commit.

Result: Analysis shows: real coding-agent traces reveal recovery opportunities (118 sessions retain work before queued message delivery, 56.55s overlap at p95 across 167 overlapping assistant responses). On 300 challenging revision/commit executions, Revise matches latest-version oracle with no stale outputs/effects. On LangGraph/LLMCompiler apps using Qwen3-14B, reduces model calls by 40.6-56.0% vs full restart and 31.3-43.6% vs suffix recomputation. Under serving pressure, reduces revision-to-completion tokens by 13.26% and improves SLO goodput by 3.07-5.43%.

Conclusion: Revise demonstrates that fine-grained, validity-guided recovery can effectively resolve the correctness-efficiency trade-off in concurrent agent execution, enabling substantial performance improvements while maintaining correctness guarantees comparable to a latest-version oracle.

Abstract: Agent revisions expose a fundamental correctness--efficiency trade-off during concurrent execution. Discarding ongoing work preserves latest-version correctness but wastes progress that may remain valid, whereas reusing prior work preserves efficiency but risks propagating stale state into outputs and tool effects. Existing recovery strategies resolve this trade-off in an imbalanced way with coarse-grained policies: they either favor efficiency by allowing potentially stale work to continue, or favor correctness by restarting the workflow or recomputing a linear suffix from the earliest conflict, thereby discarding unaffected progress. We present \textsc{Revise}, a validity-guided runtime for fine-grained recovery in structured agent workflows. When a revision arrives, \textsc{Revise} first intersects its delta with recorded data and control dependencies and propagates the resulting impact through the partially executed DAG to identify affected work. It then stops invalid work, preserves validity-established progress beyond the earliest conflict, and recomputes only the affected region. Incomplete provenance conservatively expands recovery, while reused results are revalidated before commit. Analysis of real coding-agent traces show online recovery opportunities: 118 sessions retain observable work before a queued later message is delivered; across 167 overlapping assistant responses, enqueue-to-completion overlap reaches 56.55~s at p95. Across 300 challenging revision/commit executions, \textsc{Revise} matches a latest-version oracle with no stale outputs or effects. On unmodified LangGraph and LLMCompiler applications using Qwen3-14B, it reduces model calls by 40.6--56.0\% relative to full restart and by 31.3--43.6\% relative to suffix recomputation. Under serving pressure, it further reduces revision-to-correct-completion tokens by 13.26\% and improves SLO goodput by 3.07--5.43\%.

</details>


### [58] [DramaChain Bench: An End-to-End Benchmark for Short-Drama Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00646)
*Haoyuan Shi,Mingtao Chen,Shuo Jiang,Ziyan Chen,Xuyi Sheng,Yiming Liu,Ying Zhang,Miao Wang,Jianxiang Lu,Fanyang Lu,Songyuanyi Lu,Xiele Wu,Zhichao Hu,Yuhong Liu,Richeng Xuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DramaChain Bench is the first benchmark evaluating all stages of commercial short-drama production, enabling assessment of script intent adherence and multi-episode coherence through automated agentic evaluation that closely matches human judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks only evaluate video-generation stage using pre-authored inputs, failing to assess whether each production stage adheres to original script intent and whether disparate shots remain coherent in multi-episode releases.

Method: Developed DramaChain Bench with three systems: 1) DramaChain Dimensions (5 evaluation axes instantiated across stages resulting in 63 leaf dimensions), 2) DramaChain Agent calibrated against commercial platforms, 3) DramaChain Labeling System with professional annotations on 5,785 items.

Result: Found upstream defects cascade across pipeline affecting final quality; created 17,488 valid scores and 255,925 traceable attribution records; developed DramaChain Agentic Judge that reproduces human model ranking with mean PLCC of 0.918, enabling cost-free evaluation of new models.

Conclusion: Final short-drama quality is not governed by video generation alone; the comprehensive benchmark enables fair stage-wise model comparison and demonstrates that automated evaluation can effectively replace expensive human annotation for model assessment.

Abstract: Commercial short-drama production follows a multi-stage chain: script, storyboard, keyframe imagery, shot-level video, and the finished short drama. Most existing benchmarks evaluate solely the video-generation stage using pre-authored inputs instead of real upstream pipeline outputs. This leaves two critical questions unanswerable: whether each stage adheres to the original script intent (rather than only its immediate input prompt), and whether disparate shots remain coherent after assembly into multi-episode releases. We present DramaChain Bench, the first short-drama benchmark that evaluates every stage of the complete production chain. It is built upon three in-house systems sharing one dimension system, DramaChain Dimensions: five evaluation axes instantiated at every stage, resolving into 63 leaf dimensions. DramaChain Agent is calibrated against commercial short-drama platforms in both workflow and finished short-drama quality, enabling stage-wise fair comparison across models. DramaChain Labeling System has each of the 5,785 items scored independently by three professional annotators, with all defects spatio-temporally localised and selected from a predefined defect list. This process produces 17,488 valid scores and 255,925 traceable attribution records. The human annotations confirm that upstream defects cascade across the pipeline, demonstrating that final episode quality is not governed by video generation alone. DramaChain Agentic Judge then scores every leaf dimension automatically, gathering evidence over multiple agentic rounds before judging against a per-item checklist; it reproduces the model ranking at a mean PLCC of 0.918, enough to admit new models at no annotation cost.

</details>


### [59] [Self-Reports Are Not Verification: Environment-Grounded Auditing of LLM Operators in Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00652)
*Enrong Pan,Ryan Zhou,Ting Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language model agent self-reports (confidence, rationales) should be verified against environment feedback rather than trusted, as empirical tests show poor calibration, minimal rationale impact, and no selection benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language model agents provide convenient monitoring signals like confidence and rationales, but these need verification against actual environmental outcomes rather than being taken as evidence of reliability.

Method: Introduced environment-grounded audit using Contexto evolutionary search with exact rank feedback for every guess. Tested 200 runs across five configurations and three model families, generating 12,249 self-reports. Conducted controlled interventions on 754 inherited rationales.

Result: All tested assumptions failed: 1) Confidence is poorly calibrated (operators overstate top-100 success by 4.8-9.3×), 2) Inherited rationales have minimal impact on proposals (benefit bounded to ~250 ranks), 3) Fitness-based selection shows no improvement in report quality despite different search behavior.

Conclusion: Agent self-reports should be treated as claims requiring verification against environmental feedback, not as evidence of their own reliability. Convenience monitoring signals don't substitute for proper verification.

Abstract: Language model agents increasingly propose actions, observe external feedback, and explain their own behavior. Their confidence and rationales are convenient monitoring signals, but convenience is not verification. We introduce an environment-grounded audit in which every intermediate proposal receives an exact outcome. A language model operates an evolutionary Contexto search whose feedback function assigns every valid guess an exact rank without human annotation. Across 200 runs spanning five configurations and three model families, four reporting configurations produce 12,249 self-reports. We test three assumptions: stated confidence is calibrated, inherited rationales affect later proposals, and fitness-based selection improves report quality. All three fail. Operators overstate top-100 success by factors of 4.8 to 9.3, while calibration and discrimination dissociate across model families. Controlled interventions on 754 inherited rationales bound any measured benefit of the genuine rationale to roughly 250 ranks. Neither fitness-based nor random selection produces a detectable selection differential or parent-to-offspring transmission in report accuracy, despite sharply different search behavior. Agent self-reports should therefore be treated as claims to verify against the environment, not as evidence of their own reliability.

</details>


### [60] [SciTrue: Reliable Scientific Claim Validation with Frontier and Open Language Models at the NTCIR SciClaimEval Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00654)
*Qiming Bao,Neşet Özkan Tan,Siyuan Wang,Mark Gahegan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The SciTrue team achieved top results in NTCIR-19 SciClaimEval by benchmarking multiple multimodal models with transparent post-processing, finding that strong instruction-tuned models are already competitive and that data pairing structure is the most important factor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically benchmark and understand the capabilities of frontier multimodal models for scientific claim verification against tables and figures, rather than fine-tuning a single specialized model.

Method: Benchmarked eleven frontier and open multimodal models using an honest per-sample protocol with light post-processing, then analyzed results through pair prior techniques, case audits, controlled fine-tuning, distillation, and agentic consistency-checking.

Result: SciTrue placed first in three of four evidence-category/subtask combinations and tied for first in the fourth. Key findings: 1) Strong instruction-tuned models (Claude Opus, Gemma, GPT, Claude Fable) already exceed public baselines, 2) The pairing structure is the biggest lever (leak-free pair prior improves accuracy dramatically), 3) Most errors are visually-undetectable label issues, suggesting limited fixable modeling headroom.

Conclusion: Scientific claim verification is already competitive with existing frontier models, with data structure being more impactful than model improvements, and most remaining errors stem from dataset issues rather than modeling limitations, indicating the task may be nearing its performance ceiling.

Abstract: We describe the SciTrue team's participation in both subtasks of the NTCIR-19 SciClaimEval task~\cite{sciclaimeval}, which asks systems to verify scientific claims against the tables and figures of a paper. Rather than tuning a single model, we benchmark eleven frontier and open multimodal models under one honest, per-sample protocol and combine them with light, transparent post-processing. On the official, blind test leaderboard (Section~\ref{sec:results}), SciTrue placed first by a clear margin in three of the four evidence-category/subtask combinations, and tied for first on the primary metric in the fourth. Three findings explain the result. First, strong instruction-tuned models are already competitive: Claude Opus~4.8 and Gemma-4-31B each exceed the strongest public baseline (o4-mini), and GPT-5.5 and Claude Fable~5 lead both subtasks (97.7 on Subtask~2). Second, the task's pairing structure is the largest lever: a \emph{leak-free pair prior} that recovers the Supported/Refuted pairing from the claim text alone (a visible field) and assigns Supported to the higher-confidence evidence raises Subtask-1 pair-accuracy from 72.2 to 93.5, far more than any model swap or ensemble weighting. Third, a case-by-case audit finds that most residual errors are visually-undetectable label-mapping swaps or dataset label noise, so measured accuracy understates the true ability and the fixable-by-modeling headroom is small. Controlled fine-tuning, distillation, and agentic consistency-checking support the same conclusions, and we document throughout a measurement leak---label information reaching a system through the packaging of the data rather than its content---in which the released file ordering encodes the label, including one instance that briefly misled our own pipeline.

</details>


### [61] [Drift-Aware LLM Routing with Sparse Contexts and Shared Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00662)
*Cheung Hao Lee,Patrick Wong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DRS is a drift-aware sparse routing system for multi-model language services that optimizes resource allocation under nonstationary conditions while respecting compute, latency, memory, and cost constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-model language services need to route requests while maintaining workload-level budgets for compute, latency, memory, and cost. The problem is challenging due to high-dimensional prompt representations (only small subsets of embedding directions predict model value) and drift from model launches, fine-tuning, quantization changes, and system updates.

Method: Formulates nonstationary sparse contextual routing with multiple knapsack constraints and optional shadow-audit stream. Proposes Drift-Aware Sparse Routing (DRS) policy that: 1) estimates reward and resource use from rolling audit window, 2) routes using pessimistic reward and optimistic cost estimates, 3) updates resource shadow prices online, and 4) applies hard meter before commitment.

Result: Theoretical analysis separates control from statistics. On events with uniform prediction radii β_t, regret against paced dynamic fluid benchmark is bounded by sum of radii, capacity-buffer term, and O(√T) pacing term. Under sparse linear model with bounded drift V_T, rolling estimation yields Õ(T√(s/ρW) + WV_T + √T), where s is sparsity, ρ is audit rate, W is window length. Optimizing W yields stationary O(√(sT/ρ)) rate when V_T=0 and adaptation term O(T^{2/3}(s/ρ)^{1/3}V_T^{1/3}) under drift.

Conclusion: DRS provides a principled approach to routing in nonstationary multi-model language services with theoretical guarantees, handling both high-dimensional sparse contextual features and system drift while respecting multiple resource constraints.

Abstract: A multi-model language service must route each request while preserving workload-level budgets for compute, latency, memory, or monetary cost. Two features make this problem materially harder than static model selection. Prompt representations are high dimensional, so only a small subset of embedding directions may predict the incremental value of a model, and both the request mix and the model frontier drift after launches, fine-tunes, quantization changes, and system updates. We formulate nonstationary sparse contextual routing with multiple knapsack constraints and an optional shadow-audit stream that evaluates a small fraction of prompts on several models.
  We propose Drift-Aware Sparse Routing (DRS). The policy estimates reward and resource use from a rolling audit window, routes using pessimistic reward and optimistic cost estimates, updates resource shadow prices online, and applies a hard meter before commitment. The analysis separates control from statistics. On any event with uniform prediction radii $\{β_t\}$, regret against a paced dynamic fluid benchmark is bounded by the sum of the radii, a capacity-buffer term, and an $O(\sqrt{T})$ pacing term. Under a sparse linear model and bounded drift $V_T$, rolling estimation gives \[ \widetilde O\left( T\sqrt{\frac{s}{ρW}}+WV_T+\sqrt{T} \right), \] where $s$ is sparsity, $ρ$ is the audit rate, and $W$ is the window length. Optimizing $W$ yields the usual stationary $O(\sqrt{sT/ρ})$ rate when $V_T=0$ and a $O(T^{2/3}(s/ρ)^{1/3}V_T^{1/3})$ adaptation term under drift.

</details>


### [62] [Triple-Bottom-Line Sustainability of Language Models for Edge AI: A Comparison Between SLMs and Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00665)
*Jainil Dharmil Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Quantized large language models can outperform native small language models for edge-AI deployment when considering holistic sustainability metrics including accuracy, efficiency, energy, and safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current edge-AI model selection focuses on isolated metrics, but deployable language models must balance accuracy, latency, memory, energy, AND safety. Need to determine whether native small language models or quantized large language models offer better sustainable edge-deployment trade-offs.

Method: Introduced Holistic Sustainability Score (HSS) organized around triple bottom line: economic pillar (capability & efficiency), environmental pillar (GPU energy), social pillar (safety). Tested 5 BF16 small language models and 5 LLMs under different quantization approaches (BF16, INT8, NF4, GPTQ, GGUF), totaling 30 configurations. Measured capability on 5 zero-shot benchmarks, efficiency via latency/throughput/VRAM/energy, and safety via attack success rate on harmful prompts.

Result: Qwen3-30B-A3B/GGUF Q4 ranked first overall (93.38), followed by Mistral-Small-24B/GGUF Q4 (92.40). Phi-4-mini/BF16 was highest-ranked small language model (89.49). Quantized LLMs outperformed native SLMs in overall sustainability score.

Conclusion: The hypothesis that native small language models are inherently more sustainable for edge deployment is not universally supported. Optimized quantized LLMs can win overall, while SLMs remain competitive through lower resource demands. Quantization is a systems-level choice, not just a precision-efficiency trade-off, and HSS scores are relative to comparison pool and proxy definitions.

Abstract: Edge-AI model selection is commonly driven by one isolated metric - accuracy, latency, memory, energy, or safety, even though a deployable language model must balance all five. Our work focuses on answering the question whether na- tively trained small language models (SLMs) or large language models (LLMs) compressed through post-training quantization offer the more sustainable edge- deployment trade-off. We introduce a reproducible Holistic Sustainability Score (HSS) organized around the triple bottom line: an economic pillar for capability and systems efficiency, an environmental pillar for operational GPU energy and a social pillar for harmful-prompt robustness. Five BF16 SLMs and five LLMs under different quantization approaches - BF16, INT8, NF4 4-bit, GPTQ 4-bit, and GGUF Q4 produce 30 measured configurations. Capability is assessed on five zero-shot benchmarks; efficiency uses latency, throughput, peak VRAM and energy; and safety is approximated by attack success rate on five harmful prompts. Qwen3-30B-A3B/GGUF Q4 ranks first in the combined pool (93.38), followed by Mistral-Small-24B/GGUF Q4 (92.40), while Phi-4-mini/BF16 is the highest- ranked SLM in that pool (89.49). Thus, the hypothesis that native SLMs must be the most sustainable edge choice is not supported universally; optimized quantized LLMs can win overall, while SLMs remain competitive through lower resource demand. Quantization is a systems-level choice rather than a monotonic precision- efficiency trade-off and HSS remains relative to its comparison pool and proxy definitions.

</details>


### [63] [Value Over Language Model: Detecting Original Contribution in Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00700)
*Vibhhu Sharma,Thorsten Joachims,Sarah Dean*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VOLM (Value Over Language Model) is a framework that measures how much human value is added to LLM-generated content, focusing on information content rather than surface text, using content extraction and reconstruction comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM detection tools only measure surface text generation by LLMs, but fail to assess how much information content or ideas originated from the human user versus being supplied by the LLM itself. There's a need to measure the human contribution value in LLM-assisted writing.

Method: VOLM extracts document content at increasing granularity levels, uses an LLM to reconstruct the document from each partial representation, and compares these reconstructions with those produced from task description alone. No training or labeled data needed, and never scores surface text to avoid stylistic confounders.

Result: VOLM successfully separates human-authored documents from matched LLM-generated documents across news articles, ICLR peer reviews, and argumentative essays. It remains invariant to content-preserving transformations (LLM-based reconstruction, round-trip translation), and constrained content extractors reduce differences between LLM-generated and humanized text.

Conclusion: VOLM provides a valuable framework for measuring human contributions in LLM-assisted writing by disentangling informational content from stylistic variation. The work encourages further development of specialized framework instantiations and better assessment of human value in human-LLM collaboration.

Abstract: LLMs have been rapidly adopted across writing tasks, prompting the development of tools for detecting LLM-generated text. Yet, these tools largely measure how much of a document's surface text was written by an LLM and aren't fundamentally designed to measure how much of the information content or ideas originated from the LLM itself rather than being supplied by the user in the prompt. In this work, we design a framework that measures how much value a person adds on top of what a language model could have easily produced by itself. The method requires no training or labeled data and never scores the document's surface text, insulating it from stylistic confounders. Instead, it extracts the document's content at increasing levels of granularity, uses an LLM to reconstruct the document from each partial representation, and compares these reconstructions with those produced from the task description alone. We call this framework Value Over Language Model (VOLM), which measures a document's contribution relative to a replacement-level document that an LLM could produce from the task description alone. We evaluate VOLM with a specific instantiation of this framework across three domains: news articles, ICLR peer reviews, and argumentative essays. VOLM separates human-authored documents from matched LLM-generated documents produced from generic task descriptions, while remaining substantially invariant to content-preserving transformations, including LLM-based reconstruction and round-trip translation. We further find that increasingly constrained content extractors reduce residual differences between LLM-generated and humanized text, demonstrating the importance of disentangling informational content from stylistic variation. We hope these results encourage further work on specialized instantiations of the framework and on assessing human contributions in LLM-assisted writing more generally.

</details>


### [64] [A Closed-Loop Evaluation of Capability Loss and Recovery in Compressed Driving Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00718)
*Ahmad Alfan Alfian Irfan,Nur Ahmad Khatim,Mansur Arief*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A stage-wise closed-loop evaluation approach for assessing compressed driving policies shows that structured pruning is the first stage where driving capability is lost, with distillation helping but limited by rehearsal data, and integer quantization having different effects on pruned vs. unpruned actors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current compression methods for learned driving policies (pruning, distillation, quantization) are typically evaluated using aggregate numerical scores, which may not accurately reflect real-world driving safety when interacting with other road users. There's a need for more realistic evaluation methods to ensure safe deployment of compressed driving policies on embedded systems with limited memory and power.

Method: Proposed a stage-wise closed-loop evaluation approach that follows a driving policy through a compression pipeline. Formulated driving as a POMDP, trained a belief-state policy with PPO in Gym-Duckietown, extracted the actor, compressed it stage by stage (pruning → distillation → quantization), and evaluated on five driving curricula using closed-loop testing.

Result: Structured pruning is the stage where driving capability is first lost. Distillation improves the pruned actor but is limited by rehearsal data. Integer quantization on improved pruned actor loses curricula requiring stop-and-resume actions, while same quantization on unpruned actor preserves all five curricula.

Conclusion: Provides empirical analysis for accepting compressed driving policies, demonstrating that evaluation methods must go beyond aggregate scores to consider stage-wise closed-loop testing, as different compression stages affect driving capabilities differently, particularly for safety-critical behaviors like stopping and resuming.

Abstract: Many automobile and mobility companies deploy learned driving policies on embedded computers with limited memory and power. Pruning, knowledge distillation, and quantization are the standard methods to reduce the size and the inference cost of these policies. However, these methods are commonly assessed by aggregate numerical scores, and such scores may not reflect the ability of the policy to drive safely when interacting with other road users. In this study, we propose a stage-wise closed-loop evaluation approach to follow a driving policy through a compression pipeline. We formulate the driving task as a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) and train a belief-state policy with proximal policy optimization (PPO) in Gym-Duckietown. We then extract the actor, compress it one stage at a time, and evaluate it on five driving curricula. We show that structured pruning is the stage at which the driving capability is first lost. Meanwhile, distillation improves the pruned actor, but the improvement is limited by its rehearsal data. Integer quantization of the improved actor loses some of the curricula that require the vehicle to stop and then resume. Interestingly, the same procedure on the unpruned actor preserves all five curricula. Our study thus provides an empirical analysis aiming to answer the currently active discussions on how to accept a compressed driving policy, so as to achieve a safe and statistically reliable deployment of automated driving functions.

</details>


### [65] [SOVER: Formal Certification of Optimization Reformulations via LLM-Assisted SMT Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00728)
*Swapnil Bhattacharyya,Mayank Baranwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SOVER is an LLM-assisted SMT framework that certifies mathematical optimization reformulations using Z3 for mixed-integer linear problems and dReal for continuous nonlinear problems, achieving 99.33% accuracy on a new benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current validation of LLM-transformed optimization problems through empirical solver executions is unreliable due to issues like local minima, structural timeouts, numerical artifacts, and semantic divergence between formulations.

Method: SOVER separates semantic mapping from formal certification: Z3 checks domain cross-feasibility and global objective-order preservation for mixed-integer linear formulations, while dReal provides tolerance-aware feasibility/range and ε-argmin checks for continuous nonlinear formulations. The authors also introduce NLEquiv-150 benchmark for evaluation.

Result: With LLM-extracted mappings, SOVER correctly classifies 149/150 pairs (99.33%) on the NLEquiv-150 benchmark, including all 50 hard negative (non-equivalent) pairs. The only error was due to incomplete mapping extraction.

Conclusion: SOVER provides a reliable framework for certifying mathematical optimization reformulations, addressing the limitations of empirical solver validation and demonstrating high accuracy on challenging benchmark problems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable promise in translating and reformulating complex mathematical optimization problems across modeling languages. However, validating such transformations through empirical solver executions alone is unreliable, as solver outcomes may be affected by local minima, structural timeouts, numerical artifacts, and subtle semantic divergence between formulations. We introduce SOVER, an LLM-assisted SMT framework that separates semantic mapping from formal certification: Z3 checks domain cross-feasibility and global objective-order preservation for mixed-integer linear formulations, while dReal provides tolerance-aware feasibility/range and $ε$-argmin checks for continuous nonlinear formulations. We also introduce NLEquiv-150, a public benchmark of 100 equivalent and 50 deliberately hard non-equivalent nonlinear reformulation pairs. With LLM-extracted mappings, SOVER classifies 149/150 pairs (99.33%) correctly, including all 50 hard negatives; the sole error is an incomplete mapping extraction.

</details>


### [66] [Agentic Empirical Asset Pricing: Methodological Foundations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00731)
*Yingjian Pan,Xiaowei Ding,Kay Giesecke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic Empirical Asset Pricing (AEAP) proposes autonomous LLM agents for asset pricing research, with evaluation frameworks for both factor outputs and the discovery process itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation practices only backtest final outputs (factors or trades), not the autonomous discovery systems that produce them. There's a need for better evaluation standards for AI-driven factor discovery in asset pricing.

Method: 1. Define AEAP and identify core building blocks; 2. Propose reference architecture; 3. Develop rigorous evaluation standards for discovered factors; 4. Create method for out-of-sample backtesting of the discovery system; 5. Implement SEADS as a concrete instance and compare against five re-implemented baselines on two US equity panels.

Result: No single metric ranks systems consistently, motivating multi-axial evaluation. Rolling re-execution examines reliability of the discovery process itself. Negative findings and limitations surface further evaluation pitfalls for future AEAP systems.

Conclusion: AEAP represents a new paradigm for autonomous asset pricing research, requiring comprehensive evaluation frameworks that assess both the outputs and the reliability of the discovery process itself, with multi-dimensional evaluation being essential.

Abstract: Recent advances in LLM agents enable a new paradigm for asset pricing, which we call Agentic Empirical Asset Pricing (AEAP): systems that autonomously conduct the scientific discovery process itself. We define AEAP and identify its core building blocks. Existing evaluation practices backtest only the outputs (factors or trades), not the autonomous discovery system that produced them. We focus on factor discovery, contributing a reference architecture, a rigorous evaluation standard for discovered factors, and a method for out-of-sample backtesting the discovery system. As a concrete instance of that architecture, we evaluate SEADS against five re-implemented baselines on two US equity panels using this standard: no single metric ranks the systems consistently, motivating evaluation on multiple axes at once. A separate rolling re-execution then asks the complementary question of whether the discovery process itself, not one static output, is reliable. We also report negative findings and limitations that surface further evaluation pitfalls for future AEAP systems.

</details>


### [67] [Escaping Redundant Reasoning: Structure-Aware Search for Inference-Time LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00738)
*Lu Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BASIN is a training-free, structure-aware selection method that prevents "reasoning basin collapse" in LLM inference by grouping reasoning states into basins and penalizing repeated visits to the same strategy to explore genuinely distinct reasoning paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current inference-time search with LLMs suffers from "reasoning basin collapse" - where search concentrates on a small set of structurally or semantically similar trajectories, leaving alternative reasoning paths underexplored.

Method: BASIN groups reasoning states into basins (clusters of similar reasoning strategies) and penalizes repeated visits to the same basin, reallocating search across genuinely distinct reasoning paths. A quality-aware variant (QA-BASIN) preserves high-quality basins when unconditional diversification might over-explore.

Result: BASIN improves over Tree of Thoughts by up to +22 percentage points on Game of 24 and +6.7pp on MuSR under matched inference budgets. The authors also introduce the redundancy gap Δ to measure search concentration differences between correct and incorrect predictions, showing BASIN consistently shifts Δ positive.

Conclusion: Structure-aware selection like BASIN represents a simple and general approach to improving inference-time reasoning by preventing reasoning basin collapse and promoting exploration of diverse reasoning paths.

Abstract: Inference-time search with large language models (LLMs) often concentrates on a small set of structurally or semantically similar trajectories, leaving alternatives underexplored---a failure mode we call \textit{reasoning basin collapse}. We introduce BASIN, a training-free, structure-aware selection method that groups reasoning states into basins and penalizes repeated visits to the same strategy, thereby reallocating search across genuinely distinct reasoning paths under a fixed compute budget. Under matched inference budgets, BASIN improves over Tree of Thoughts (ToT) by up to $+22$pp on Game of 24 and $+6.7$pp on MuSR. A quality-aware variant, QA-BASIN, further improves robustness by preserving high-quality basins when unconditional diversification over-explores. To explain when basin-aware selection helps, we introduce the redundancy gap $Δ$, which measures how differently search concentrates for correct versus incorrect predictions: standard ToT often operates near $Δ\approx 0$, while BASIN consistently shifts $Δ$ positive. More broadly, BASIN suggests structure-aware selection as a simple and general approach to improving inference-time reasoning. Code can be found at https://github.com/GitHubLuCheng/basin.

</details>


### [68] [ContextPipe: Database-Inspired Context Assembly for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00749)
*Peng Xu,Zuyu Zhang,Yuze Sun,Feng Tian,Long Wang,Chen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ContextPipe is a five-phase pipeline for LLM agent context assembly that treats it like database query execution, reducing token usage and improving efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent systems have ad hoc, scattered approaches to context assembly (prompt building, compaction, caching) that lack structure and efficiency. There's a need for systematic handling under hard context-window budgets.

Method: ContextPipe uses a five-phase pipeline (Plan Bind Optimize Execute Feedback) modeled after relational database query execution. It features a structured data-source catalog, deterministic cache-aware optimizer, and EXPLAIN ANALYZE tracing.

Result: Compared to append-only context construction, ContextPipe reduces total token volume by 31%, LLM calls by 23%, and response time by 9%, though with a lower KV cache-hit ratio.

Conclusion: Context assembly for LLM agents can be effectively treated as a database query execution problem, leading to more auditable, replayable, and failure-isolated systems with significant efficiency gains.

Abstract: Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents require context assembly: the runtime must decide what to include in each prompt, in what order, and when to compact history under a hard context-window budget and a byte-sensitive prompt cache. In production agentic systems, this logic is scattered across prompt builders, ad hoc compaction routines, cache-break workarounds, and per-provider shims. We argue that context assembly is structurally isomorphic to query execution in a relational database: both execute under a hard budget, exploit a tiered cache, and leverage statistics. We adopt this discipline in ContextPipe: a five-phase pipeline (Plan Bind Optimize Execute Feedback) backed by a structured data-source catalog, a deterministic cache-aware optimizer, and an EXPLAIN ANALYZE trace. We show that context in ContextPipe is auditable, replayable, and failure-isolated. A preliminary evaluation using the SWE-bench Pro Qutebrowser subset shows that, compared with the append-only context construction policy, ContextPipe reduces total token volume by 31%, LLM calls by 23%, and response time by 9%, at the cost of a lower KV cache-hit ratio.

</details>


### [69] [S^3martCirc: Self-supervised Smart Circuit Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00755)
*Wendy Zheng,Yinhan He,Liang Wu,Jundong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: S^3martCirc is a unified framework that simultaneously discovers circuits and interprets functionality in LLMs by abstracting node behavior into general computational roles with quantitative metrics, outperforming sequential circuit discovery methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current mechanistic interpretability approaches for LLMs use a sequential two-stage paradigm (circuit discovery followed by functional interpretation) that overlooks the inherent codependency between component importance and functional roles. This sequential approach has limitations: functional roles are often node-specific and identification relies on subjective interpretation rather than quantifiable metrics.

Method: S^3martCirc (Self-supervised Smart Circuit Discovery) is a unified framework that abstracts node behavior into two general computational roles that generalize across tasks. It defines a quantitative metric for assigning these roles, enabling importance and functional roles to be discovered jointly rather than sequentially.

Result: Extensive experiments show that S^3martCirc outperforms existing methods in circuit discovery, demonstrating the effectiveness of the unified approach over traditional sequential methods.

Conclusion: The unified approach of simultaneously discovering circuits and interpreting functionality through generalizable computational roles with quantitative metrics represents an advancement in mechanistic interpretability, addressing key limitations of sequential methods.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance across diverse tasks, from text summarization to question answering. Despite these capabilities, their black-box nature obscures internal decision-making processes. Mechanistic interpretability (MI) aims to address this by reverse-engineering neural networks into human-understandable algorithms. Current MI approaches for LLMs typically follow a two-stage paradigm: first identifying important components (circuit discovery), where components are typically individual nodes such as an attention head or feedforward neuron, and second determining the role they play in a certain task (functional interpretation). However, this sequential approach overlooks a fundamental insight: a component's importance and its functional role are inherently codependent. Unifying these stages presents two key challenges: (1) functional roles are often tied to specific nodes or components, limiting generalization, and (2) their identification relies on subjective interpretation rather than quantifiable metrics. To address these challenges, we propose S^3martCirc (Self-supervised Smart Circuit Discovery), a unified framework that simultaneously discovers circuits and interprets functionality. S^3martCirc abstracts node behavior into two general computational roles that generalize across tasks and defines a quantitative metric for assigning them, enabling importance and functional role to be discovered jointly rather than in sequence. Extensive experiments show that our framework outperforms existing methods in circuit discovery.

</details>


### [70] [Automated Tree Knowledge Graph Construction using Ontology Expansion and Retrieval from Vietnamese History Textbooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00763)
*Ket Doan Nguyen,Minh N. H. Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a hierarchical knowledge graph (KG)-based RAG pipeline for Vietnamese, addressing KG construction challenges for low-resource languages and evaluating graph traversal strategies on Vietnamese history textbooks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing two main challenges: (1) lack of methods for automatic KG construction using ontology expansion for low-resource languages like Vietnamese, and (2) absence of systematic evaluation for knowledge retrieval strategies leveraging hierarchical structures in KGs.

Method: Proposes an end-to-end pipeline: 1) KG construction with three-phase hybrid relation extraction (intra-batch deduplication via Union-Find, approximate cross-batch search, LLM extraction with centroid filter, and five-step dual-LLM validator), 2) Two-tier architecture with unmergeable structural nodes and mergeable content nodes, 3) Three graph traversal strategies evaluation (Top-Down, Horizontal, Bottom-Up) on synthetic Vietnamese benchmark.

Result: Built a tree knowledge graph from Vietnamese high school History textbooks (400 pages) with 750 nodes and 4,341 semantic edges, with controlled ontology growth from 40 to 41 types. Top-Down strategy with structure outperformed vector baseline by 4.7 percentage points in NDCG@10.

Conclusion: Tree-structural information provides valuable information beyond flat cosine similarity for retrieval, but its performance degrades when queries don't require structural context. The pipeline successfully addresses KG construction for low-resource Vietnamese and provides systematic evaluation of hierarchical retrieval strategies.

Abstract: Hierarchical Knowledge graph (KG)-based retrieval augmented generation (RAG) has emerged as a powerful approach for supporting large language models with structured knowledge. However, there are primary challenges: (i) the lack of methods for automatic KG construction using ontology expansion for low-resource languages such as Vietnamese, (ii) the absence of systematic evaluation for knowledge retrieval strategies leveraging the hierarchical structures. In this paper, we propose an end-to-end pipeline for KG construction and retrieval strategies evaluation. In the KG construction, we employ a three-phase hybrid relation extraction pipeline: intra-batch deduplication via Union-Find, approximate cross-batch search, and LLM extraction with a centroid filter that reduces prompts combined with a five-step dual-LLM validator to prevent bloated ontology. A two-tier architecture consists of unmergeable structural nodes to preserve the document structure and mergeable content nodes. The retrieval evaluation consists of three graph traversal strategies: Top-Down, Horizontal, and Bottom-Up, which are evaluated on a synthetically generated benchmark of 1,210 Vietnamese queries from 109 subgraphs, categorized by five query directions. In this paper, we construct the tree knowledge graph from Vietnamese high school History textbooks (nearly 400 pages) to produce 750 nodes and 4,341 semantic edges with controlled ontology growth from 40 to 41 types. Among experimental graph traversal strategies, the Top-Down strategy with structure surpasses the vector baseline by 4.7 percentage points in NDCG@10. As a result, tree-structural information provides valuable information beyond flat cosine similarity but degrades performance when the query does not require structural context.

</details>


### [71] [DiagEvo: Diagnosis-Guided Self-Evolution via Hierarchical Error Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00768)
*Xincheng Wei,Yifan Ding,Yoshua Li,Dongsheng Ma,Rongxiang Weng,Xunliang Cai,Wenjian Ding,Yao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiagEvo is a self-play framework for language model evolution that uses the solver's own failure history to create a diagnostic curriculum, eliminating the need for external task resources while improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-play methods face limitations: unguided methods use generic signals (difficulty, diversity) that don't target specific weaknesses, while guided methods require external task resources. The paper proposes using the solver's own failure history to create targeted guidance within the self-play loop.

Method: DiagEvo introduces a diagnostician that extracts recurring error causes from failure history, storing them in a hierarchical error-cause memory. This memory tracks causes as Active or Mastered based on self-consistency. The challenger uses these states to balance targeted generation with exploration, employing double-confidence filtering to retain intermediate-difficulty questions only when the solver's most common answer has a clear vote lead.

Result: DiagEvo outperforms all baselines in mean accuracy across nine benchmarks for three solvers (Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, OctoThinker-8B). On Qwen3-8B, it achieves 72.3% mean accuracy across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks (4.5 points above R-Zero) and 57.4% across all nine benchmarks (1.1 points above DARC). Ablations confirm contributions of hierarchical error-cause memory and double-confidence filtering.

Conclusion: The needed direction for language model self-evolution can be derived from the solver's own failure history, enabling effective self-play without external task resources. DiagEvo's diagnostic approach provides targeted guidance that improves performance across multiple benchmarks and model sizes.

Abstract: Self-play is an effective paradigm for language-model self-evolution, but without guidance, solver performance can plateau or decline across rounds. Unguided methods steer question generation with signals such as difficulty, learnability, or diversity. These signals keep questions challenging and varied but do not specify which unresolved reasoning weaknesses later rounds should target. Guided methods obtain direction from external task resources, including human examples, document corpora, or specified difficulty targets, and therefore rely on task information supplied outside the self-play loop. We show that the needed direction can instead be derived from the solver's own failure history. We introduce DiagEvo, whose diagnostician extracts recurring error causes from this history and stores them in a hierarchical error-cause memory. The memory groups related causes under skill nodes and tracks each as Active or Mastered according to self-consistency on targeted questions. The challenger uses these states and recurrence counts to balance cause-targeted generation with free exploration. Double-confidence filtering retains intermediate-difficulty questions only when the most common solver answer has a clear vote lead. DiagEvo derives its curriculum from information produced during self-play, without external task resources. With the default 4B diagnostician, DiagEvo outperforms every baseline in mean accuracy across all nine benchmarks for each of the three solvers: Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and OctoThinker-8B. On Qwen3-8B, it reaches 72.3% mean accuracy across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, 4.5 percentage points above R-Zero. Its mean accuracy across all nine benchmarks is 57.4%, 1.1 percentage points above DARC. Ablations show that the hierarchical error-cause memory and double-confidence filtering both contribute to these gains.

</details>


### [72] [When Features Become Instances: Inverted Contrastive Learning for Unsupervised Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00782)
*Utsab Ghosh,Roshni Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICLFS is a novel unsupervised feature selection method that uses inverted contrastive learning to learn feature-wise representations, with feature importance determined by projector-space embedding magnitudes refined through Laplacian-based ranking correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unsupervised feature selection methods rely on indirect structural criteria like similarity preservation, locality, sparsity, cluster geometry, or reconstruction quality, making feature utility difficult to define without class labels. The paper proposes studying UFS through representation consistency as an alternative approach.

Method: Inverted Contrastive Learning for Unsupervised Feature Selection (ICLFS): 1) Inverts the data matrix so each feature is represented by its sample-profile vector, 2) Constructs multiple masked positive views with a shuffled negative view, 3) Learns projector-space representations consistent across these perturbations using InfoNCE-based objective, 4) Uses projector-space embedding magnitude as saliency signal for ranking features, 5) Refines ranking through Laplacian-Gated Ranking Correction to suppress locally redundant features.

Result: Extensive experiments on 12 benchmark datasets show ICLFS achieves best clustering accuracy on 10 datasets against both classical and neural baselines under standard clustering-based UFS evaluation protocol, remaining competitive on the other two datasets.

Conclusion: Feature-wise contrastive representation consistency provides a strong and effective alternative to traditional neighborhood, cluster, and reconstruction-based UFS formulations, demonstrating superior performance across diverse datasets.

Abstract: Unsupervised feature selection seeks a compact subset of informative features without access to class labels, making feature utility difficult to define. Existing UFS methods therefore rely on indirect structural criteria, such as similarity preservation, locality, sparsity, cluster geometry, or reconstruction quality. In this paper, we instead study UFS through representation consistency and propose Inverted Contrastive Learning for Unsupervised Feature Selection (ICLFS), a feature-wise contrastive framework that reformulates UFS as a representation learning problem over features rather than samples. ICLFS first inverts the data matrix so that each feature is represented by its sample-profile vector, then constructs multiple masked positive views together with a shuffled negative view, and learns projector-space representations that remain consistent across these structured perturbations under an InfoNCE-based objective. Motivated by recent findings that cosine-based and InfoNCE-based training affect embedding norms, we use projector-space embedding magnitude as the saliency signal for ranking features. The resulting norm-based ranking is subsequently refined through Laplacian-Gated Ranking Correction, which suppresses locally redundant candidates while preserving salient ones. Extensive experiments on 12 benchmark datasets show that ICLFS achieves the best clustering accuracy on 10 datasets against both classical and neural baselines under the standard clustering-based UFS evaluation protocol, while remaining competitive on the other two. These results show that feature-wise contrastive representation consistency provides a strong and effective alternative to neighborhood, cluster, and reconstruction-based UFS formulations.

</details>


### [73] [StudyBench: Can Self-Evolution Squeeze Textbooks for Olympiad Capability?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00787)
*Yinghao Chen,Zixi Chen,Bingxiang He,Ziqing Qiao,Huan-ang Gao,Yinuo Xu,Yuxin Zuo,Zeyuan Liu,Yuhao Zhan,Chaojun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StudyBench is a controlled physics benchmark that measures how efficiently self-evolution methods convert training material into transferable problem-solving capability, revealing guidance gaps and compute plateaus in current approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the observation that humans can master disciplines by studying a handful of well-written textbooks. The authors argue that ideal self-evolution methods should similarly learn from raw training material to develop transferable problem-solving capability, but there's currently no direct measurement for this ability.

Method: The authors introduce StudyBench, a controlled physics benchmark with two test sets: an Application Set (difficult textbook problems evaluating absorption ability) and a Transfer Set (olympiad-level problems evaluating transfer ability). They benchmark representative self-evolution methods across three base models and conduct a guidance ablation study.

Result: The research reveals that improvements on the Application Set rarely translate to the harder Transfer Set. A guidance ablation exposes a "Guidance Gap" where even the strongest methods close only a small fraction of what the same material unlocks when used as in-context guidance. Additionally, all methods hit a "Compute Plateau," saturating well before exhausting compute budgets.

Conclusion: StudyBench provides a clean, controlled benchmark that turns self-evolution progress from an open-ended pursuit into a measurable target. The remaining performance gap is identified as a method problem rather than a data or compute problem, offering clear direction for future research in autonomous learning systems.

Abstract: Humans need to study only a handful of well-written textbooks to master a discipline and attempt its hardest problems. We argue that an ideal self-evolution method should share the same property, that is autonomously learning from raw training material for transferable problem-solving capability. However, we still lack a direct measurement for it. We introduce StudyBench, a controlled physics benchmark that directly measures how efficiently a self-evolution method converts training material into capability. We organise the test set into an Application Set, consisting of difficult textbook problems and evaluating absorption ability, and a Transfer Set, consisting of olympiad-level problems and evaluating transfer ability. Benchmarking representative self-evolution methods across three base models, we find that improvements on the Application Set rarely translate to the harder Transfer Set. A guidance ablation exposes a Guidance Gap: even the strongest method closes only a small fraction of what the same material unlocks when supplied as in-context guidance. Besides, every method hits a Compute Plateau, saturating well before exhausting its compute budget. The remaining gap is therefore a method problem rather than a data or compute problem. By offering a clean and controlled benchmark, StudyBench turns self-evolution progress from an open-ended pursuit into a measurable target for future research. Our code is released at https://github.com/thunlp/StudyBench.

</details>


### [74] [Towards a Reliable and Practical Eval Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00805)
*Emma Thuong Nguyen,Abhishek Ghose*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An end-to-end eval pipeline combining checklist creation with learned aggregation to improve LLM judge agreement and accuracy against human judgments, while providing self-consistency, explanations, and uncertainty estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based software systems need effective quality evaluation (evals) in development, but existing work only addresses individual aspects of eval reliability rather than comprehensive practical requirements.

Method: Presents an end-to-end eval pipeline that combines: (1) eval checklist creation, and (2) learned aggregation for checklist responses to improve agreement across LLM judges and accuracy against human judgments.

Result: The framework provides self-consistency, explanations, and prediction uncertainty, with empirical demonstration of its effectiveness.

Conclusion: A comprehensive eval pipeline that addresses multiple practical requirements for LLM evaluation, improving reliability through structured checklist creation and intelligent response aggregation.

Abstract: LLM-based software systems increasingly require effective "evals" as quality gates in the development lifecycle. However, existing work typically addresses individual aspects of eval reliability rather than the full set of practical requirements. We present an end-to-end eval pipeline that combines eval checklist creation, with learned aggregation for checklist responses, to improve agreement across LLM judges and accuracy against human judgments. The framework additionally pro- vides self-consistency, explanations, and prediction uncertainty, and we empirically demonstrate its effectiveness.

</details>


### [75] [One Policy, Any Budget: Internalizing Budget-Aware Search via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00813)
*Xiaowei Sun,Jin Li,Yili Hong,Yikun Fu,Yanghua Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnySearch is a framework that enables LLM-based search agents to perform budget-aware search under varying budget constraints through a two-phase training approach using curriculum reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
