Motivation: Existing RL methods for LLM-based search agents train under fixed budgets and cannot adapt when budget constraints vary at deployment time. There's a need for agents that can operate efficiently under any budget constraint.

Method: Two-phase approach: 1) Train with explicit budget state injection and structured reasoning prompts for efficient allocation under linearly decaying budgets. 2) Remove scaffold and train agent autonomously under adaptively sampled budget constraints matching inference conditions. Uses composite reward coupling answer accuracy with budget efficiency through absolute and relative signals with adaptive weighting.

Result: Outperforms baselines across all budget scales on seven general and multi-hop QA benchmarks, generalizes to unseen constraints beyond training range, and achieves superior tool productivity without excessive token overhead.

Conclusion: AnySearch enables a single policy to perform budget-aware search under any budget constraint through innovative training methods, demonstrating strong generalization capabilities and practical efficiency.

Abstract: While reinforcement learning has enabled LLM-based search agents to invoke external tools, existing methods train under fixed budgets and cannot adapt when constraints vary at deployment. We propose AnySearch, a framework that enables a single policy to perform budget-aware search under any budget constraint through a training scaffold and curriculum reinforcement learning. In the first phase, we train the agent with explicit budget state injection and structured reasoning prompts that guide efficient allocation under linearly decaying budgets. In the second phase, the scaffold is removed and the agent learns to operate autonomously under adaptively sampled budget constraints, matching inference conditions. Both phases are optimized with a composite reward that couples answer accuracy with budget efficiency through absolute and relative signals, where an adaptive weight amplifies the efficiency signal for high-accuracy queries and attenuates it for low-accuracy ones. Extensive experiments on seven general and multi-hop QA benchmarks show that our method outperforms baselines across all budget scales, generalizes to unseen constraints beyond the training range, and achieves superior tool productivity without excessive token overhead. Our code is available at https://github.com/xwsun01/AnySearch.

</details>


### [76] [AnalysisBank: An Expert Analysis Pattern Library for Financial Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00818)
*Yajing Yang,Yunshan Ma,Kelvin J. L. Koa,Min-Yen Kan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnalysisBank shifts financial report generation from structural templates to analytical building blocks, creating reports by retrieving and applying expert-derived analytical moves matched to data signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current financial report generation operates at a structural level using predefined topics/sections, lacking genuine analytical reasoning and data-derived insights from expert reports.

Method: Distills expert reports into a reusable library of "Analyses" (each pairing a data signal, analytical move, and expert text span), then at inference matches input signals to library entries and applies retrieved moves.

Result: Analysis of 550 expert reports revealed 47-52 signal types across 13 move types with heavy-tailed distribution. On two financial benchmarks with four LLM backbones, AnalysisBank increased novel, data-grounded insights by 1.7-3.7x over structural baselines. Transfer to scientific writing shows generalization beyond finance.

Conclusion: Operating at the analytical level rather than structural level enables more insightful, data-grounded report generation, with AnalysisBank providing a reusable framework that generalizes beyond finance to other domains like scientific writing.

Abstract: We argue that financial report generation should operate at the analytical rather than structural level, composing content from data-derived insights rather than high-level topics or sections. To this end, we propose AnalysisBank, which distills expert reports into a reusable library of Analyses, each pairing a data signal, an analytical move, and the expert span it was derived from. At inference time, AnalysisBank matches input signals to library entries and applies the retrieved moves to compose the report. A study of Analyses distilled from 550 expert reports reveals a heavy-tailed distribution of 47-52 signal types spanning 13 move types. On two financial benchmarks across four LLM backbones, AnalysisBank increases the proportion of novel, data-grounded insights by 1.7-3.7x over structural-level baselines. Transfer to scientific writing suggests that the distinction generalizes beyond finance. Code and the distilled Analysis library are available at https://github.com/yajingyang/AnalysisBank.

</details>


### [77] [Polished but Unresolved: Identifying Late-Stage Pressure States in Long-Horizon Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00823)
*Haoyang Chen,Yi Liu,Jianzhi Shao,Xiaozhou Xu,Zhe Sun,Wei Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies "late-stage pressure states" in tool-use agents where agents prematurely finalize answers despite unresolved constraints, proposes a method to detect and mitigate this pressure, and shows improvements on long-horizon benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon tool-use agents often suffer from "late-stage pressure states" where they prematurely submit final answers that appear polished but have unresolved key constraints, leading to suboptimal performance.

Method: 1) Train linear probe to identify pressure states from agent's hidden states; 2) Use activation interventions along pressure direction; 3) Analyze pressure mitigation through constraint clarity and action mapping; 4) Propose PSPR (Probe-Sensed Pressure Relief) - a plugin applying lightweight pressure relief under moderate pressure and structured organization under high pressure risk.

Result: Experiments on multiple long-horizon benchmarks show that the proposed method consistently strengthens existing agent methods by mitigating premature finalization and improving overall performance.

Conclusion: The late-stage pressure phenomenon is identifiable and intervenable, and the proposed PSPR method effectively mitigates this pressure to improve agent performance in long-horizon tool-use tasks.

Abstract: Long-horizon tool-use agents need not only to search and plan, but also to decide when to finalize. We study late-stage pressure states, in which an agent is biased toward submitting a final answer that appears complete and polished while key constraints remain unresolved. We first train a linear probe to show that this pressure state is identifiable from the agent's hidden states. Then, we use activation interventions along this pressure direction and find that shifting the hidden states changes both the pressure score and whether the agent continues tool use or submits early. Through controlled context manipulations, we further see that the pressure is mitigated by constraint clarity and action mapping. Based on these findings, we propose Probe-Sensed Pressure Relief (PSPR), a plugin that applies lightweight pressure relief direction under moderate pressure and moves to structured organization under high pressure risk. Experiments on multiple long-horizon benchmarks show that our method consistently strengthens existing agent methods.

</details>


### [78] [FLaG: Frequency-Domain Latent-attention Gated Pooling for Token Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00831)
*Kewei Li,Rongying Zhang,Xueli Wang,Xiwen Gong,Zhongjian Wang,Qiuchen Zhao,Lan Huang,Ruochi Zhang,Fengfeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLaG is a plug-in token aggregation module that operates in the Fourier domain to improve representations across protein, image, and language tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most pooling methods operate only in the original token space, limiting their ability to capture frequency-domain patterns in representations.

Method: FLaG re-expresses encoder outputs in Fourier domain, represents rFFT spectrum, uses learnable latent queries to summarize spectral tokens, applies sample-conditioned channel gating, and reconstructs modulated tokens for downstream aggregation.

Result: FLaG achieves best results on AMP activity prediction, best top-1 accuracy on CIFAR-10, and best mean results on 5/7 language metrics. Analyses reveal low-frequency sensitivity in early layers, increased high-frequency sensitivity in final layer, and species-specific spectral allocation.

Conclusion: FLaG provides transferable frequency-domain aggregation bias across protein, visual, and textual representations, with benefits depending on backbone architecture and downstream task.

Abstract: Token aggregation converts token-level representations into fixed-dimensional sample representations, but most pooling methods operate only in the original token space. We introduce Frequency-Domain Latent-attention Gated Pooling (FLaG), a plug-in aggregation module that re-expresses encoder outputs in the Fourier domain before final pooling. FLaG represents the nonredundant rFFT spectrum through concatenated real and imaginary components, summarizes spectral tokens with learnable latent queries, derives a sample-conditioned channel gate, and reconstructs modulated token representations for downstream aggregation. We evaluate the same architecture across ESM2-based antimicrobial peptide (AMP) activity prediction, ResNet18 image classification on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, and three RoBERTa-based language tasks. FLaG achieves the best macro-averaged Spearman correlation coefficient, RMSE, and Recall@50 across four AMP backbone-species settings and the highest top-1 accuracy on CIFAR 10. It also achieves the best mean results on five of seven language metrics, although mean pooling remains strongest on STSBenchmark. AMP-side mechanistic analyses reveal low-frequency prediction sensitivity across most encoder layers, with increased relative high-frequency sensitivity in the final layer, and pronounced peptide-specific positional responses. The residual gate broadly amplifies spectral channels while preserving the low-frequency-dominated energy profile, whereas latent cross-attention exhibits sample- and species-specific spectral allocation. Overall, FLaG provides a transferable frequency-domain aggregation bias across protein, visual, and textual representations, with benefits that depend on the backbone and downstream task. Supplementary materials, source code, and data are available at https://www.healthinformaticslab.org/supp/ and https://github.com/Kewei2023/AMPCliff/tree/FLaG.

</details>


### [79] [Towards Generalizable Visually Grounded Exploration of Household Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00845)
*Linhao Zheng,Zeming Liu,Wangke Chen,Li Zeng,Wanxiang Che,Heyan Huang,Yuhang Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VGEBench is a new benchmark that evaluates VLMs' ability to operate novel devices through visual exploration without manuals, addressing limitations of current embodied AI benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current embodied exploration paradigms rely too heavily on imitation learning from human-annotated trajectories, which limits generalization. The key bottleneck is achieving Generalizable Visually Grounded Exploration - the ability to operate novel devices without manuals by grounding abstract knowledge into visual affordances.

Method: The authors introduce VGEBench, a comprehensive benchmark featuring a Logic-Driven State Machine framework. This simulates multi-turn interaction loops that force agents to achieve goals through active visual perception and feedback-driven correction, unlike static datasets.

Result: Experimental results show existing VLMs struggle significantly with two key capabilities: translating semantic knowledge into physical execution, and maintaining long-horizon state tracking.

Conclusion: There's a critical gap in evaluating generalizable visually grounded exploration capabilities, and VGEBench addresses this by providing a framework that better simulates real-world device operation challenges, revealing important limitations in current VLMs.

Abstract: Recent advancements in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities in static visual recognition and high-level semantic reasoning. However, current embodied exploration paradigms still heavily rely on imitation learning from human-annotated trajectories, which severely limits agents' generalization ability. The key bottleneck of realizing general autonomous embodied agents lies in Generalizable Visually Grounded Exploration: the ability to operate novel devices without manuals or specific training by actively grounding abstract world knowledge into fine-grained visual affordances. Yet, existing benchmarks fail to evaluate this capability: they generally rely on explicit documents and annotated trajectories, neglecting the dynamic Hypothesis-Interaction-Refinement process essential for functional device operation. To bridge this gap, we introduce VGEBench, a comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate the generalizable visually grounded exploration capabilities of VLMs. Unlike static datasets, we construct a Logic-Driven State Machine framework. This framework simulates multi-turn interaction loops, compelling agents to achieve goals by active visual perception and feedback-driven correction. Experimental results demonstrate that existing VLMs face significant challenges in translating semantic knowledge into physical execution and maintaining long-horizon state tracking.

</details>


### [80] [Verifiable Disaster Storylines and Causal Knowledge Graphs: A Citation-Grounded Pipeline from Heterogeneous Humanitarian Sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00858)
*Ivan Decostanzi,Michele Ronco,Sergio Consoli,Christina Corbane,Lorenzo Bertolini,Indaco Biazzo,Daria Mihaila,Manuel Garcia-Herranz,Felix Schwebel,Yelena Mejova,Kyriaki Kalimeri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A pipeline combines structured and unstructured disaster data to generate source-grounded storylines and causal knowledge graphs, evaluated as effective by experts for situational awareness in humanitarian response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the challenge of rapidly synthesizing heterogeneous, high-volume information sources during humanitarian crises, which often exceeds human analytical capacity in early critical hours. The goal is to improve situational awareness for responders and analysts by automating the generation of disaster storylines and causal knowledge graphs.

Method: The method involves a pipeline that integrates structured disaster records from EM-DAT with unstructured documents from ReliefWeb and EMM. It uses Retrieval-Augmented Generation to extract structured storylines (tabular event profiles with 17 fields) and constructs causal knowledge graphs. Each node and edge is enriched with citation-grounded explanatory narratives for traceability to primary sources.

Result: Evaluation on three diverse crisis use cases with 9 domain experts and 9 non-experts showed high retrieval precision, strong faithfulness of extracted causal relations, and a clear expert preference for citation-grounded components over ungrounded alternatives.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the pipeline effectively combines structured and unstructured disaster data to generate source-grounded storylines and causal knowledge graphs, which are highly valued by experts for situational awareness. The system demonstrates strong performance in retrieval precision and faithfulness of causal relations, with plans to scale and publicly release an enriched version of the EM-DAT database.

Abstract: Effective humanitarian response depends on the rapid synthesis of heterogeneous, high-volume information sources - a task that routinely exceeds human analytical capacity in the critical early hours of a crisis. We present a pipeline that combines structured disaster records from EM-DAT with unstructured documents from ReliefWeb and the European Media Monitor (EMM) to produce source-grounded disaster storylines and causal knowledge graphs supporting situational awareness for responders and analysts. Using Retrieval-Augmented Generation, the pipeline extracts structured storylines - tabular event profiles covering 17 fields, from severity and key drivers to child-sensitive impact indicators - and constructs causal knowledge graphs where each node and edge is enriched with citation-grounded explanatory narratives, enabling full traceability back to primary sources. We evaluate the system on three diverse crisis use cases through a human evaluation involving 9 domain expert and 9 non-expert evaluators. Results confirm high retrieval precision, strong faithfulness of extracted causal relations, and a clear expert preference for citation-grounded components over ungrounded alternatives. The pipeline is designed to scale to the full EM-DAT catalogue, with the goal of publicly releasing a narrative-enriched version of the database.

</details>


### [81] [Reinforcement Learning Enhanced LLM Agents for Complex Vehicle Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00859)
*Yi Chen,Zikang Yu,Jiahai Wang,Jinbiao Chen,Jianpeng Zhou,Zizhen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RLEA is a multi-agent framework that combines reinforcement learning with LLMs to automatically model complex Vehicle Routing Problems (VRPs) for optimization solvers, outperforming previous methods with 16.67% higher success rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While advanced optimization solvers can effectively solve VRPs, modeling complex VRP variants for these solvers requires substantial domain expertise, which limits accessibility of optimization technologies.

Method: Proposes Reinforcement Learning Enhanced LLMAgents (RLEA) framework with: 1) lightweight neural Planner trained with Soft Q-learning to orchestrate LLM-based agents, 2) evolutionary memory module, and 3) retrieval-augmented generation for leveraging accumulated experience and solver knowledge.

Result: Evaluated on 48 distinct VRP variants across various solvers. RLEA achieved 16.67% higher success rate than previous state-of-the-art methods while significantly reducing runtime errors.

Conclusion: Integrating reinforcement learning with LLM-based reasoning is highly effective for automated optimization modeling, making advanced VRP solving more accessible without requiring extensive domain expertise.

Abstract: Vehicle Routing Problems (VRPs) are fundamental combinatorial optimization problems with widespread applications in various scenarios. The advanced optimization solvers can effectively solve such problems. However, modeling complex VRP variants for solvers often requires substantial domain expertise, which limits the accessibility of advanced optimization technologies. In this paper, we propose Reinforcement Learning Enhanced LLMAgents(RLEA), a multi-agent framework designed to automate the modeling of complex VRPs. RLEA introduces a lightweight neural Planner trained with Soft Q-learning to efficiently orchestrate the actions of LLM-based agents. In addition, we equip the system with an evolutionary memory module and retrieval-augmented generation, enabling the agent to leverage both accumulated experience and external solver knowledge during program generation and refinement for solving VRPs. We evaluated 48 distinct VRP variants across various solvers. The experimental results demonstrate that RLEA outperforms the previous state-of-the-ar method, achieving a 16.67% higher success rate while significantly reducing runtime errors. These results validate that integrating reinforcement learning with LLM-based reasoning is highly effective for automated optimization modeling. The appendix is available at: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.19134435.

</details>


### [82] [Beyond the Clock: Measuring the Value of Adaptive Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00874)
*Ayushi Chadha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies meta-level control in hierarchical systems where a higher-level controller decides when to preserve vs. revise strategies guiding lower-level computation. While learned timing policies vary qualitatively, none outperform the best fixed schedule, revealing a distinction between state-dependent decisions and actual performance value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agentic systems become more complex compound systems, increasingly important decisions occur above task execution itself: when should a higher-level controller preserve the strategy guiding another process versus revise it? This meta-level control problem needs systematic study in hierarchical systems.

Method: The study uses a hierarchical latent reasoner where a manager can retain or replace a commitment governing lower-level computation. Researchers analyze three precommitted training seeds, conduct intervention studies on original checkpoints, perform exhaustive enumeration of timing policies, and use counterfactual PERSIST/REPLAN diagnostics.

Result: Learned revision timing produces qualitatively different policies ranging from deterministic early clocks to state-conditioned schedule distributions, but none outperform the best forced timing policy. Timing is consequential and order-sensitive, but a strong fixed schedule captures most measurable value. Score-level evidence can be misleading when predictability is dominated by decision position rather than within-position discrimination.

Conclusion: Learned meta-level control should be evaluated along three separate axes: 1) whether its score depends on state, 2) whether that dependence changes realized behavior, and 3) whether those changes capture outcome value beyond a strong non-adaptive policy. This separates state dependence from decision value, showing controllers can vary actions with internal state without necessarily improving task performance.

Abstract: As agentic systems become compound systems, increasingly important decisions move above task execution itself: when should a higher-level controller preserve the strategy guiding another process, and when should it revise it? We study this meta-level control problem in a hierarchical latent reasoner whose manager can retain or replace a commitment governing lower-level computation. Across three precommitted training seeds, learned revision timing produces qualitatively different policies, ranging from an almost deterministic early clock to substantially more state conditioned schedule distributions, yet none outperforms the best forced timing policy evaluated on the same frozen checkpoint. This separates state dependence from decision value: a controller can vary its actions with internal state without turning that variation into a reproducible task-performance benefit. A deeper intervention study on the original checkpoint shows that timing itself is consequential and order-sensitive, while exhaustive enumeration reveals that a strong fixed schedule captures most of the measurable value available from timing at this decision budget. Counterfactual PERSIST/REPLAN diagnostics further show why score-level evidence can be misleading when predictability is dominated by decision position rather than within-position discrimination. Together, these results argue that learned meta-level control should be evaluated along three separate axes: whether its score depends on state, whether that dependence changes realized behavior, and whether those changes capture outcome value beyond a strong non-adaptive policy.

</details>


### [83] [FractalNet-Based Heterogeneous Federated Learning for Orbital Edge Intelligence in Satellite Mega-Constellations: A Wildfire Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00875)
*Sai Puppala,Koushik Sinha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A heterogeneous federated learning framework called FractalNet for satellite mega-constellations that adapts model depth to satellite constraints (SWAP-C) and contact windows, enabling efficient distributed learning across orbital tiers with agentic control for wildfire detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current satellite learning architectures inherit from terrestrial federated learning, which is ill-suited for satellites due to huge differences in SWAP-C (Size, Weight, Power, Cost), radiation tolerance, link availability, and propagation delay. Existing approaches don't account for the heterogeneous nature of satellite constellations and their operational constraints.

Method: Proposes FractalNet architecture for orbital edge intelligence with: 1) Formalized contact-window-constrained, depth-heterogeneous federated optimization; 2) Distributed path scheduler assigning model depth based on SWAP-C constraints, predicted contacts, and training stats; 3) Periodic update pooling (not at every contact) to reduce overhead; 4) Three-tier agentic control plane for in-space scheduling, anomaly escalation, and autonomy.

Result: Applied framework to wildfire detection where different orbital shells naturally learn different semantic levels: LEO learns pixel-scale thermal anomalies, MEO learns regional fire-front dynamics, GEO/HEO learns larger-scale risk propagation. Experiments on simulated mega-constellations show convergence, communication efficiency, energy adaptation, scheduled-pooling savings, robustness, and latency improvements.

Conclusion: The FractalNet framework enables efficient heterogeneous federated learning for satellite mega-constellations by adapting to orbital constraints and enabling multi-tier intelligence, with applications demonstrated in wildfire detection where different orbital altitudes learn complementary situational awareness.

Abstract: Satellite mega-constellations are emerging as large-scale sensing, communication, and computation fabrics, yet their learning architectures remain largely inherited from terrestrial federated learning and ground-centric mission operations--- ill-suited to satellites that differ by orders of magnitude in Size, Weight, Power, and Cost (SWAP-C), radiation tolerance, link availability, and propagation delay. We propose a heterogeneous federated learning method based on the FractalNet architecture for orbital edge intelligence. We formalize contact-window-constrained, depth-heterogeneous federated optimization and introduce a distributed path scheduler that assigns model depth as a function of SWAP-C constraints, predicted inter-satellite contacts, and training statistics. To reduce message overhead and energy consumption, each tier pools updates periodically rather than at every contact opportunity, and a three-tier agentic control plane governs in-space scheduling, anomaly escalation, and policy-governed autonomy. As a case study, we apply the framework to wildfire detection, where each orbital shell naturally learns a different semantic level of situational awareness: pixel-scale thermal anomalies at low Earth orbit (LEO), regional fire-front dynamics at medium Earth orbit (MEO), and larger-scale risk propagation at geostationary or high Earth orbit (GEO/HEO). Experiments on simulated mega-constellations validate the approach across convergence, communication efficiency, energy adaptation, scheduled-pooling savings, robustness, and latency.

</details>


### [84] [Towards reliable multimodal disaster severity assessment through preference optimization and explainable vision-language reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00879)
*Yuanjun Zhang,Fuzel Ahamed Shaik,Suvojit Acharjee,Fahad Khalid,Mourad Oussalah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-stage training framework using SFT and DPO improves disaster damage assessment with better accuracy and transparent explanations through unified data construction from HITL annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal disaster damage assessment approaches face limitations: scarce annotated data and insufficient evaluation of reasoning quality. Need for models that provide both accurate predictions and transparent explanations for reliable emergency management.

Method: Proposes a two-stage training framework integrating Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) within unified data construction pipeline. From single Human-in-the-Loop annotation workflow, creates two complementary datasets: ReasoningSet (validated rationales for SFT) and PreferenceSet (paired rationales for DPO-based alignment).

Result: SFT improves accuracy from 73.64% to 78.29% (+4.65%), increases Macro-F1 by 29% compared to baseline, and explanation quality improves by ~25%. DPO alignment further enhances interpretability. Cross-model validation on InternVL-3-8B and LLaVA-1.5-7B shows robustness. Framework improves detection of underrepresented mild damage cases, reduces high-risk misclassifications, and strengthens alignment between model reasoning and human judgment.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a reproducible pathway to develop reliable multimodal systems that deliver auditable, actionable disaster insights for emergency management, effectively addressing data scarcity and reasoning quality evaluation challenges.

Abstract: Reliable disaster damage assessment requires models that provide both accurate predictions and transparent explanations. However, existing multimodal approaches are limited by scarce annotated data and insufficient evaluation of reasoning quality. This study proposes a two-stage training framework that integrates Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) within a unified data construction pipeline. From a single Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) annotation workflow, two complementary datasets are derived, namely ReasoningSet, which contains validated rationales for SFT, and PreferenceSet, which comprises paired rationales for DPO-based alignment. The framework evaluates both classification performance and explanation quality using automatic metrics, model-based scoring, and human ranking. Experimental results show that SFT improves accuracy from 73.64% to 78.29% and increases Macro-F1 by 29% compared to the baseline, while explanation quality improves by approximately 25%. Subsequent DPO alignment further enhances interpretability on the PreferenceSet. Cross-model validation on InternVL-3-8B and LLaVA-1.5-7B demonstrates the robustness and generalizability of the approach. The proposed framework improves detection of underrepresented mild damage cases, reduces high-risk misclassifications, and strengthens alignment between model reasoning and human judgment. Overall, it provides a reproducible pathway to develop reliable multimodal systems that deliver auditable, actionable disaster insights for emergency management.

</details>


### [85] [Denoising Diffusion Generative Models Secretly Calculate Attentions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00885)
*Farzan Haddadi,Leila Monfared,Ebrahim Rezaii,Mohammadreza Malek-Mohammadi,Pejman Zakalvand,Narges Mokhtari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Diffusion models inherently use attention mechanisms similar to transformers, revealing attention as a universal ML principle that enables architectural interchangeability and more efficient image generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite diffusion models dominating image generation while transformers dominate natural language generation, the paper aims to show they share fundamental attention mechanisms, suggesting attention as a universal principle that could enable more flexible architectural design.

Method: The authors demonstrate that diffusion models inherently use attention mechanisms similar to transformers, showing equivalences between auto-encoders and attention-based models. They use these insights to reformulate the diffusion framework into a simplified attention-based algorithm for image generation.

Result: The attention-based implementation achieves comparable performance to standard diffusion models but with significantly reduced training time and computational resources, making the process more efficient.

Conclusion: Attention emerges as a universal machine learning principle based on general training objectives, enabling interchangeable architectural designs that can be optimized for practical requirements like computational efficiency.

Abstract: Denoising diffusion models are the dominant architecture for image generation, whereas most natural language generation and modeling are primarily handled by well-known transformer architectures employing attention mechanism. Here, we show that diffusion models also inherently use an attention mechanism very similar to that of transformers. Therefore, attention emerges as a universal machine learning principle, based on a general training objective. We also show similarities in basic functional principle of auto-encoders and attention-based models. These equivalences allows us to interchange these designs based on practical requirements. As an example, we can reformulate the diffusion framework to reduce the lengthy training process and computation-intensive image generation. Using this approach, a simplified algorithm is proposed for image generation which is based on attention mechanism. Results show that the attention-based implementation achieves comparable performance with significantly less effort and computational resources.

</details>


### [86] [CacheBridge: Efficient Cross-Model KV Cache Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00891)
*Xingyu Qu,Siyuan Lu,Zhiyu Chen,Sheng Wang,Tao Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CacheBridge improves cross-model KV cache transfer by using architecture-indexed mapping and attention-aligned calibration, reducing storage/application costs while maintaining accuracy better than previous methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Full-Head Mapping for cross-model KV cache transfer has issues: it maps each target head from every source head, making it sensitive to architectural differences, and causes mapper storage/application costs to grow with layer support.

Method: CacheBridge co-designs three components: 1) architecture-indexed mapper support restricting each target head to a matched source head, 2) attention-aligned calibration weighting reconstruction errors by causal attention sensitivity, and 3) bounded mapper construction using a fused GPU kernel to create weighted sufficient statistics without materializing full observation tensors.

Result: CacheBridge recovers accuracy on Ministral 3 transfers where Full-Head Mapping loses substantial accuracy, preserves 99.83% mean target retention on Qwen3, reduces mapper storage by 8×, accelerates application by up to 3.0×, matches performance with one tenth the calibration data, and reduces 500-sequence construction time from 92.63 to 8.63 seconds (10.7× speedup).

Conclusion: CacheBridge provides an efficient, accurate solution for cross-model KV cache transfer through architectural co-design that addresses the limitations of previous full-head mapping approaches while maintaining a closed-form affine interface for online deployment.

Abstract: Sharing context between LLMs in a multi-model system requires the receiving model to prefill the shared prefix because KV caches are model-specific. Recent closed-form cross-model KV transfer, hereafter Full-Head Mapping, avoids this replay by fitting a training-free affine mapper from source to target caches. However, its full-head design maps each target KV head from every source KV head in the selected layers, making transfer quality sensitive to architectural differences and causing mapper storage and application cost to grow with layer support. To this end, we introduce CacheBridge, which co-designs architecture-indexed mapper support, attention-aligned calibration, and bounded mapper construction while retaining a closed-form affine interface for online deployment. CacheBridge restricts each target head to a matched source head, weights reconstruction errors by causal attention sensitivity, and uses a fused GPU kernel to construct weighted sufficient statistics without materializing full observation tensors. Across three transfer directions, CacheBridge recovers the two Ministral 3 transfer directions where Full-Head Mapping loses substantial accuracy while preserving 99.83\% mean target retention on Qwen3. On Qwen3 $14\mathrm{B}\to32\mathrm{B}$, it reduces mapper storage by $8\times$, accelerates application by up to $3.0\times$, matches \fullhead with one tenth of the calibration data, and reduces 500-sequence construction from 92.63 to 8.63 seconds ($10.7\times$).

</details>


### [87] [CARE: Contrastive Anchor-based Rubric Evolution for Large Language Model Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00892)
*Siyuan Li,Xinxin Song,Chen Ruinian,Jingjing Fan,Tingxiong Xiao,Yangen Hu,Ke Zeng,Jinli Suo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CARE is a reinforcement learning method that uses contrastive anchor-based rubric evolution to maintain discriminative accuracy in high-reward regions by comparing policy outputs with frontier model anchor responses, achieving state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current rubric-based reinforcement learning for post-training LLMs faces problems: static rubrics get hacked as policies evolve, while existing dynamic approaches suffer from undirected rubric extraction, unreliable hack detection, and unbounded rubric proliferation.

Method: CARE grounds rubric evolution in high-quality anchor responses from frontier models. At each training step, it contrasts the highest-scoring rollout against the anchor, using two branches: Adaptive branch reactively repairs reward misspecification, and Chase branch proactively converts quality gaps into sharper rubrics.

Result: Experiments show CARE achieves SOTA performance on Arena-Hard-2.0, InfoBench, and FollowBench. It's the only method whose win rate against GPT-4.1 anchor responses shows sustained improvement over 300 training steps, and it generalizes across model families.

Conclusion: CARE effectively addresses limitations of existing rubric-based RL by maintaining discriminative accuracy in high-reward regions through contrastive anchor-based evolution, preventing reward hacking and enabling sustained performance improvement.

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning decomposes open-ended instructions into prompt-specific, flexible rubrics, making it better suited than reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for post-training LLMs on open-ended tasks. However, static rubrics are inevitably hacked as the policy evolves, and existing dynamic approaches introduce new problems: undirected rubric extraction, unreliable hack detection, and unbounded rubric proliferation. We propose $\textbf{CARE}$ ($\textbf{C}$ontrastive $\textbf{A}$nchor-based $\textbf{R}$ubric $\textbf{E}$volution), which grounds every rubric evolution step in a high-quality anchor response generated by a frontier model conditioned on the prompt and its rubrics. At each training step, CARE contrasts the highest-scoring rollout against the anchor, enabling two complementary mechanisms: an Adaptive branch that reactively repairs reward misspecification; and a Chase branch that proactively converts frontier-level quality gaps into sharper rubrics. Together, the two branches $\textbf{maintain discriminative accuracy in the high-reward region}$---the precise region where reward over-optimization mostly originates. Experiments on WildChecklist-9K with Qwen2.5-7B-Base and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct show that CARE achieves state-of-the-art performance on Arena-Hard-2.0, InfoBench, and FollowBench, and is the $\textbf{only}$ method whose win rate against GPT-4.1 anchor responses shows sustained improvement throughout 300 training steps; additional results on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B further indicate that CARE generalizes across model families.

</details>


### [88] [In-Context Neurofeedback: Can LLMs Control Their Internal Representations through Privileged Access?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00904)
*Koshiro Aoki,Ryota Takatsuki,Gouki Minegishi,Yusuke Haruki,Daisuke Kawahara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs may not genuinely control internal representations; previous neurofeedback successes may rely on superficial cues rather than privileged access, suggesting stricter evaluation methods are needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLMs can genuinely control their internal representations (important for machine metacognition and AI safety), and to address limitations of previous neurofeedback studies that may have allowed superficial control mechanisms.

Method: Redesigned neurofeedback paradigm for LLMs with stricter privileged access requirements (similar to human cognitive neuroscience experiments), where control targets cannot be inferred from prompts by third parties.

Result: Under the stricter privileged access setting, models did not demonstrate reliable control over privileged internal representations, suggesting previous reported control may have relied on superficial mechanisms.

Conclusion: Rigorous assessments of metacognition in LLMs require evaluation methods that demand privileged access, as current approaches may not genuinely test internal representation control.

Abstract: Whether large language models (LLMs) can control their own internal representations matters for both machine metacognition and AI safety. A recent study applied neurofeedback to LLMs and claimed that they can control their internal representations. However, the reported control may rely on superficial mechanisms rather than genuine internal access because the control targets in that study are not privileged, meaning that a third party can infer them from the prompt. We redesign the neurofeedback paradigm for LLMs so that the control target satisfies the privileged access requirement, which is closer to neurofeedback experiments in human cognitive neuroscience. Under this stricter setting, the models do not demonstrate reliable control over privileged internal representations, suggesting that previously reported control cannot exclude the possibility that it relies on superficial mechanisms. Our results indicate that rigorous assessments of metacognition in LLMs require evaluation methods that demand privileged access.

</details>


### [89] [RPCBench: A Benchmark for Proactive Premise Critique in LLM-based Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00918)
*Zhongru Chen,Yuan Wu,Yi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RPCBench is a new benchmark for evaluating LLMs' ability to critique flawed recommendation requests by detecting, diagnosing, and properly handling faulty premises, going beyond traditional recommendation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current recommender benchmarks mainly assess ranking, generation, or preference satisfaction, while existing error-detection benchmarks lack grounding in recommendation-specific evidence. There's a gap in evaluating whether LLMs can recognize flawed recommendation requests as interactive recommender assistants.

Method: Created RPCBench with evidence-grounded test instances from five recommendation domains covering ten types of premise failures. Each instance provides visible recommendation context and corrupted user query. Designed fine-grained evaluation framework measuring proactive detection, error localization, post-detection handling strategy, and evidence faithfulness.

Result: Systematic evaluation of 11 LLMs shows proactive detection is the main bottleneck. Models perform worst on underspecified-premise errors. Found that target-critical information density matters more than redundant evidence, and longer reasoning doesn't monotonically improve critique quality - performance peaks at intermediate reasoning length with overthinking penalty.

Conclusion: RPCBench addresses the gap in evaluating LLMs' ability to critique flawed recommendation requests. The benchmark reveals important patterns in model performance, particularly the challenges with proactive detection and the non-linear relationship between reasoning length and critique quality.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used as interactive recommender assistants. Their evaluation should therefore go beyond plausible item recommendation and test whether they can recognize flawed recommendation requests. Existing recommender benchmarks mainly assess ranking, generation, or preference satisfaction, while existing error-detection benchmarks are usually not grounded in recommendation-specific user and candidate evidence. To address this gap, we introduce RPCBench, a benchmark for evaluating Recommender-Premise Critique: the ability to detect, diagnose, and properly handle faulty premises in natural-language recommendation requests. RPCBench contains evidence-grounded test instances from five recommendation domains and covers ten types of premise failures. Each instance provides a visible recommendation context and a corrupted user query. We further design a fine-grained evaluation framework that measures proactive detection, error localization, post-detection handling strategy, and evidence faithfulness. Through a systematic evaluation of 11 LLMs, we find that proactive detection is the main bottleneck in Recommender-Premise Critique, and models perform worst on underspecified-premise errors. We also observe that target-critical information density matters more than redundant evidence, and that longer reasoning does not monotonically improve critique quality: performance peaks at intermediate reasoning length, while overly long reasoning is accompanied by an overthinking penalty. The code is available at https://github.com/ZhongruChen/RPCBench.

</details>


### [90] [VIBE-Bench: Evaluating Personalized Large Language Models When Profiles Don't Mean Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00921)
*Yiwen Jiang,Yang Deng,Stephanie Fong,Zimu Wang,Yaling Shen,Wei Feng,Hongxi Yang,Xiangyu Zhao,Zhongxing Xu,Deval Mehta,Xuelian Cheng,Zongyuan Ge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VIBE-Bench addresses profile-preference conceptual misalignment in personalized LLMs, requiring cross-concept reasoning beyond surface semantic matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PLLM benchmarks assume preferences can be retrieved from semantically related history, but real-world scenarios involve PRCM where profile cues and query-specific preferences lie in different concept spaces, making semantic retrieval inconsistent.

Method: Introduced VIBE-Bench with two psychology-grounded tasks, 3,504 personas and 12,239 dialogues including manually verified gold test set, requiring cross-concept preference reasoning beyond surface semantic overlap. Tested several personalization methods.

Result: Current PLLMs largely rely on shallow semantic correlations and fail to acquire robust cross-concept mappings, showing poor performance on PRCM tasks.

Conclusion: PRCM represents a distinct failure regime in PLLMs, and VIBE-Bench serves as a focused testbed for advancing preference reasoning beyond semantic matching toward more sophisticated cross-concept inference.

Abstract: Personalized Large Language Models (PLLMs) aim to tailor responses to individual users, where a central challenge is preference reasoning: inferring query-relevant preferences from user-related history. Existing benchmarks, however, largely assume that such preference can be retrieved from semantically related history. We study an underexplored but practically important regime, profile-preference conceptual misalignment (PRCM), where observable profile cues and query-specific preferences lie in different concept spaces, making semantic retrieval inconsistent for personalization. We introduce VIBE-Bench, a benchmark with two psychology-grounded tasks, 3,504 personas and 12,239 dialogues, including a manually verified gold test set, and requires cross-concept preference reasoning beyond surface semantic overlap. Experiments with several personalization methods show that current PLLMs largely rely on shallow semantic correlations and fail to acquire robust cross-concept mappings. These findings establish PRCM as a distinct failure regime in PLLMs and position VIBE-Bench as a focused testbed for advancing preference reasoning beyond semantic matching.

</details>


### [91] [Few-Shot Out of Domain Intent Detection with Covariance Corrected Mahalanobis Distance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00961)
*Jayasimha Talur,Oleg Smirnov,Paul Missault*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a covariance-corrected Mahalanobis distance method for out-of-domain intent detection that addresses performance issues in few-shot settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous Mahalanobis distance methods for OOD intent detection fail to outperform baselines in few-shot settings, which is a practically important scenario for conversational agents.

Method: Analyzes why Mahalanobis distance underperforms in few-shot settings and proposes a covariance-corrected Mahalanobis distance approach.

Result: The proposed covariance-corrected method addresses the limitations of previous Mahalanobis approaches in few-shot OOD intent detection scenarios.

Conclusion: Improved out-of-domain intent detection is achieved through covariance correction of Mahalanobis distance, particularly beneficial for few-shot learning in conversational AI systems.

Abstract: Conversational agents like chatbots and voice assistants are trained to understand and respond to user intents. On encountering an utterance with an intent different from the ones they have been trained on, these agents are expected to classify the intent as `unknown' or `out of domain'. This problem is known as out of domain (OOD) intent detection. Podolskiy et al. (2021), showed that Mahalanobis distance can be used effectively for identifying OOD intents, outperforming competing approaches. However, their method fails to outperform the baselines in the practically important few-shot setting. In this paper we analyze the reason for low performance and propose a covariance corrected Mahalanobis distance for detecting out-of-domain intents.

</details>


### [92] [CoBRA: Learning Tool-Use Boundaries via Counterfactual Margins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00967)
*Wenhao Zou,Xianglong Liu,Wendong Bi,Hanjie Wang,Simin Zhao,Gong Zhi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoBRA is a framework that learns optimal tool-use boundaries for language models by comparing internal vs external expert performance and using counterfactual reward margins to decide when to call tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tool-calling methods lack explicit estimates of instance-level marginal benefits of tool use, leading to unnecessary tool calls (causing latency, cost, noise) or missed calls (hurting knowledge-intensive queries).

Method: CoBRA constructs internal/external experts from same base model, collects paired trajectories, estimates reward margins between answering with/without tools. Uses margin-partitioned data for boundary-aware supervised fine-tuning, then applies MARS-RL with reference-split rollouts and counterfactual marginal advantages.

Result: Experiments with retrieval tools on Qwen3-4B show improved tool-use efficiency and boundary-sensitive answer accuracy while maintaining strong performance on tool-dependent out-of-distribution questions.

Conclusion: CoBRA provides an effective counterfactual boundary-learning approach for optimizing when to use tools in language models, balancing efficiency and accuracy.

Abstract: As large language models increasingly act through external tools, deciding when to call a tool has become a central problem alongside deciding how to use it. Unnecessary tool calls introduce latency, cost, retrieval noise, and error propagation, while missed calls hurt knowledge-intensive queries or questions requiring up-to-date evidence. Existing methods typically trigger tools from absolute query or generation signals, such as difficulty, confidence, or final task reward, and therefore lack an explicit estimate of the instance-level marginal benefit of tool use. We propose CoBRA, a counterfactual boundary-learning framework for tool-augmented language models. CoBRA first constructs internal and external experts from the same base model, collects paired trajectories, and estimates the reward margin between answering with and without tools. This margin partitions data into internal-favored, external-favored, and ambiguous cases. CoBRA then uses clear-margin samples for Boundary-Aware Cold-Start SFT, followed by MARS-RL with reference-split rollouts and counterfactual marginal advantages to optimize boundary decisions. Experiments with retrieval as the main tool on Qwen3-4B show that CoBRA improves tool-use efficiency and boundary-sensitive answer accuracy while maintaining strong performance on tool-dependent out-of-distribution questions.

</details>


### [93] [Figures as Programs: Recursive Generation of Editable Scientific Figures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01006)
*Yepeng Liu,Dasen Dai,Chengzhi Liu,Yiren Song,Hai Ci,Yu Zhang,Qi Zhang,Mike Zheng Shou,Xin Eric Wang,Yuheng Bu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FigTree is a multi-agent system that automatically generates structured vector figures (SVG) from scientific papers through recursive program construction and a render-critic refinement loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating scientific methodology figures is labor-intensive and requires multiple refinement rounds. While image-generation models can create raster figures, producing satisfactory results in one step is difficult, and editing raster figures is challenging for both humans and models.

Method: Formulates scientific figure generation as recursive SVG program construction. Uses a multi-agent system that grounds content in source paper, decomposes figures into hierarchy of local regions, generates each region as short SVG program, and assembles fragments. Employs render-critic refinement loop that inspects rendered figure and underlying program to trace visual defects to specific statements for repair.

Result: Extensive evaluations show FigTree produces high-quality figures and enables more effective editing than existing raster-based methods, demonstrating improved editability and quality.

Conclusion: FigTree addresses the limitations of current figure generation methods by using structured vector representation (SVG) with recursive construction and refinement loops, making scientific figure creation more efficient and editable while maintaining quality.

Abstract: Scientific methodology figures are essential for communicating complex methods clearly, yet creating them remains labor-intensive and typically requires multiple rounds of refinement. Recent image-generation models can synthesize visually appealing raster figures, but producing a human-satisfactory result in a single generation step remains difficult. Moreover, precise edits to raster figures are challenging for both humans and models. We formulate scientific figure generation as recursive SVG program construction and propose \textsc{FigTree}, a \textit{multi-agent} system that automatically transforms a scientific paper into a structured vector figure. \textsc{FigTree} grounds figure content in the source paper, decomposes a figure into a hierarchy of local regions, generates each region as a short SVG program, and assembles the resulting fragments. A render-critic refinement loop jointly inspects the rendered figure and its underlying program, enabling visual defects to be traced to specific statements and accurately repaired. We conduct extensive evaluations of \textsc{FigTree} on figure quality and editability, showing that \textsc{FigTree} produces high-quality figures, while also enabling more effective editing than existing raster-based methods.

</details>


### [94] [Spawn Freely, Act Sparingly: Progressive Risk Vesting for Recursive LLM-Agent Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01035)
*Molly Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Progressive Risk Vesting (PRV) is a framework for managing risk in recursive LLM agents that spawn specialists, controlling when branches gain authority to take irreversible actions through a risk budgeting system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursive LLM agents that spawn specialist branches face safety challenges: some branches may request tools that send data or deploy code, creating potential harms. There's a need for principled control mechanisms to determine when branches should receive authority to act, balancing exploration with safety.

Method: Introduces Progressive Risk Vesting (PRV) that holds a trajectory-level risk budget in escrow and debits it as branches are activated. Distinguishes sandbox spawning (external controls prevent harm) from capability activation (crossing irreversible-action boundaries). Provides theoretical analysis including anytime harm bounds for adaptively generated trees, and models harm behavior across different regimes using branching models.

Result: Proves an anytime harm bound for adaptively generated trees; shows that delayed vesting preserves every policy available under irrevocable spawn charging; analyzes how trajectory harm scales with local risk p across different regimes (proportional to p below criticality, proportional to √p at criticality, positive floor above it); derives risk and compute shadow prices from finite-type occupancy model.

Conclusion: The analysis suggests a design rule: search broadly in the sandbox but grant recursive authority sparingly with explicit risk charges. Provides a theoretical foundation for managing risk in recursive AI agents while maintaining exploration capabilities.

Abstract: Recursive LLM agents can broaden their search by spawning specialists. Some branches later request tools that send data or deploy code. When should a branch receive authority to act? We distinguish sandbox spawning, in which external controls prevent the specified harm, from capability activation, in which a selected branch crosses an irreversible-action boundary. Progressive Risk Vesting (PRV) holds a trajectory-level risk budget in escrow and debits it as branches are activated. We prove an anytime harm bound for adaptively generated trees. Branch outcomes may be dependent, but each local certificate needs to remain valid conditional on the full pre-activation history, including the information used to select the request. When activation gates, branch charges, and compute constraints are held fixed, delayed vesting preserves every policy available under irrevocable spawn charging. Marginal risk estimates can still fail after branch selection. In a stylized branching model, trajectory harm changes as the authority reproduction number $\mathcal{R}_A$ crosses one. As local risk $p$ approaches zero, trajectory harm is proportional to $p$ below criticality, proportional to $\sqrt{p}$ at criticality, and retains a positive floor above it. A finite-type occupancy model yields risk and compute shadow prices. For nested fanout modes with decreasing marginal value per unit risk, these prices produce a threshold rule. Branching calculations and a split-sample experiment illustrate the results. These synthetic studies do not estimate safety in deployed agents. The analysis suggests a design rule: search broadly in the sandbox and grant recursive authority sparingly, with an explicit risk charge.

</details>


### [95] [Data-Driven Persona-Conditioned Agents for A/B Test Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01038)
*Ziyad Benomar,Weronika Łajewska,Leonardo Perelli,Saab Mansour*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-powered agents with data-driven personas from real user behavioral data can simulate A/B tests with 75-90% directional accuracy, offering fast, low-cost experiment pre-screening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional A/B testing requires significant real user traffic, engineering effort, and weeks of measurement. There's a need for faster, lower-cost methods to predict experiment outcomes before conducting actual tests.

Method: Proposes a simulation framework using LLM-powered agents conditioned on data-driven personas constructed from anonymized behavioral data (activity patterns, engagement signals, inferred demographics). Frames A/B test simulation as structured question tasks, systematically studying question design formats, persona data source/domain alignment, trade-off between behavioral depth and population diversity, and efficient population subsampling.

Result: On a benchmark of 40 A/B tests spanning two metric types, the best configuration achieves 0.75-0.90 directional accuracy depending on the test metric. Demonstrates that data-driven personas enable viable path toward fast, low-cost experiment pre-screening.

Conclusion: Data-driven personas constructed from real user behavioral data provide a promising approach for A/B test simulation with high directional accuracy, potentially reducing the need for costly, time-consuming real-world experiments through effective pre-screening.

Abstract: A/B testing is the gold standard for evaluating product changes, but each experiment requires real user traffic, engineering effort, and weeks of measurement. We propose a simulation framework that predicts A/B test outcomes using LLM-powered agents conditioned on data-driven personas grounded in real user behavioral signals. Unlike prior work that relies on synthetic or rule-based personas, our agents are constructed from anonymized behavioral data-activity patterns, engagement signals, and inferred demographics-enabling more faithful population modeling. We frame A/B test simulation as a structured question task and systematically study (i) question design formats, (ii) the impact of persona data source and domain alignment, (iii) the trade-off between per-persona behavioral depth and population diversity, and (iv) efficient population subsampling. On a benchmark of 40 A/B tests spanning two metric types, our best configuration achieves 0.75-0.90 directional accuracy depending on the test metric, demonstrating that data-driven personas are a viable path toward fast, low-cost experiment pre-screening.

</details>


### [96] [AgentFactory: Towards Automated Agentic System Design and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01045)
*Enci Zhang,Haofeng Wang,Yuesheng Zhu,Xiaole Cui,Guibo Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentFactory is an automated framework that jointly optimizes both foundation models and workflow structures in agentic systems, outperforming manual approaches by 9.1% on average across diverse benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current manual design of agentic systems is labor-intensive and limits adaptability/scalability, while existing automated approaches overlook model capabilities and focus on single metrics rather than real-world deployment constraints.

Method: Uses LLMs as optimizers to navigate configuration search space with a three-stage pipeline that iteratively discovers effective combinations of fine-tuned models and optimized workflows, considering multiple objectives (performance, cost, efficiency).

Result: Achieves average 9.1% improvement across 8 benchmarks in 5 domains (reasoning, coding, math, medicine, finance), with particularly strong gains in domain-specific tasks (19.6% on MedQA, 18.7% on FinEval).

Conclusion: AgentFactory demonstrates that automated joint optimization of models and workflows enables more capable and efficient agentic systems, overcoming limitations of manual design and previous automated approaches.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities as powerful components in agentic systems, enabling sophisticated reasoning and complex task execution. However, current approaches to manually designing and optimizing agentic systems heavily rely on manual effort, limiting their adaptability and scalability. Recent work has explored the automated optimization of workflow designs. However, these approaches often overlook the crucial role of model capabilities and focus on single performance metrics, failing to address real-world deployment constraints. In this paper, we present AgentFactory, a framework that jointly optimizes both foundation models and workflow structures in agentic systems while considering multiple objectives including performance, cost, and efficiency. AgentFactory leverages advanced LLMs as optimizers to navigate the vast search space of possible configurations, employing a three-stage optimization pipeline to automatically discover effective combinations of fine-tuned models and optimized workflows. Through an iterative optimization process, our framework systematically explores and evaluates different agentic system designs, adapting to task-specific requirements while maintaining operational efficiency. We evaluate AgentFactory across eight benchmarks spanning five domains, including general reasoning, coding, mathematics, medicine, and finance. Our experiments demonstrate that AgentFactory consistently outperforms both manually designed methods and existing automated approaches, achieving an average improvement of 9.1% across all benchmarks, with particularly significant gains in domain-specific tasks (19.6% on MedQA and 18.7% on FinEval). These results establish AgentFactory as a promising approach for developing more capable and efficient agentic systems through automated optimization.

</details>


### [97] [QILP-0: Constructing Observational Declarative Twins of Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01049)
*Marina de la Cruz Echeandía,César Luis Alonso,Tony Ribeiro,Alfonso Ortega de la Puente*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QXymb is a framework for creating declarative twins of quantum circuits; QILP-0 is its order-0 specialization that converts quantum circuit observations into finite logic programs for exact reconstruction of observed behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a general framework for constructing observational declarative twins of quantum circuits that separates logical exactness from numerical/backend uncertainties, enabling formal verification of quantum circuit behavior.

Method: QILP-0 constructs finite multi-valued propositional logic programs from observed circuit behavior using incremental traversal of observables with structural grading, reference-relative coverage metrics, target-independent geometry organization, and admissible discretization of continuous observations.

Result: In all experiments (Bars & Stripes from 16-100 qubits and all 14,708 MNIST digit-0/1 instances), induced QILP-0 theories achieved complete, conflict-free reconstruction with strict accuracy equal to one, perfectly reproducing the finite discrete relations.

Conclusion: QXymb/QILP-0 successfully provides a framework for constructing exact observational declarative twins of quantum circuits, separating logical verification from implementation uncertainties and enabling formal certification of quantum circuit behavior.

Abstract: This paper introduces QXymb, a general framework for constructing observational declarative twins of quantum circuits, and develops QILP-0, its first complete order-0 specialization. QILP-0 constructs a finite multi-valued propositional logic program from observed circuit behaviour within a declared observational scope.
  The pipeline traverses a declared family of quantum observables incrementally according to a reproducible structural grading and a declared observational reference horizon. Progress is quantified through reference-relative coverage against a fixed target-independent reference. Observable responses are organized through target-independent geometry, while retained latent structure is mapped deterministically back to original observable columns before symbolic processing, preserving observational semantics and provenance.
  Selected observable profiles are converted into a finite relation through admissible target-independent discretization. The target is used only afterwards to audit twin-admissibility and induce the declarative theory. A theory is certified as an exact observational declarative twin when it completely and correctly reconstructs the resulting finite task-conditioned discrete relation. Logical exactness is therefore separated from numerical, backend, provider, and discretization uncertainty, which is retained as audit metadata.
  Validation uses two complementary QML settings. Exhaustive Bars & Stripes experiments compare product and grid-CZ embeddings from 16 to 100 qubits and exercise the native-discrete branch. Low-Depth MNIST analyses all 14,708 digit-0/1 instances before and after a trained variational quantum transformation and exercises continuous discretization. In every reported relation, the induced QILP-0 theory achieves complete, conflict-free reconstruction with strict accuracy equal to one.

</details>


### [98] [WorldBench: Culturally Grounded Benchmark for Multilingual Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01056)
*Leonardo Ranaldi,Sherrie Shen,Jushi Kai,Alexandra Birch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WorldBench is a multilingual benchmark for testing LLM-powered agents on realistic, persona-grounded everyday workflows across multiple languages and cultures, revealing significant performance gaps especially for state preservation and long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLM-powered agents lack comprehensive testing of state preservation, performance across languages, and application to realistic, grounded scenarios in everyday workflows.

Method: Created WorldBench: a comprehensive multilingual benchmark with 1,600 tasks across 7 languages and 8 cultures, filtered/refined through human experts. Extended existing metrics and introduced Constrained Task Success (CTS) which combines natural language instructions and testbeds to score task completion, minimal modification, and other metrics via deterministic and LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations.

Result: Frontier models achieved only 49.2% CTS, with all models showing large gaps between correctness and environment preservation. Current agents remain brittle in multilingual, agentic scenarios, especially for long-horizon tasks and under state-preservation constraints.

Conclusion: The WorldBench benchmark reveals significant limitations in current LLM-powered agents, particularly in multilingual contexts and state preservation, highlighting the need for improved agent architectures and evaluation methods for realistic, grounded scenarios.

Abstract: Despite the growing use of LLM-powered agents to solve multi-step tasks in complex environments, existing benchmarks rarely test state preservation, performance across languages, and application to realistic, grounded scenarios. To address these concerns, we present WorldBench: a comprehensive, multilingual benchmark of genuine, persona-grounded everyday workflows, where agents can act in a sandbox via structured actions. WorldBench comprises 1,600 tasks across seven languages and eight cultures, filtered and refined through feedback from human annotators with language- and culture-specific expertise. For evaluation, we extend metrics from previous works and introduce Constrained Task Success (CTS), which combines natural language instructions and testbeds to score task completion, minimal modification, and other complementary metrics through deterministic and LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations. Our experiments show that frontier models reach only 49.2% CTS, with all models demonstrating large gaps between correctness and environment preservation. We thereby show that current agents remain brittle in multilingual, agentic scenarios, especially for long-horizon tasks and under state-preservation constraints

</details>


### [99] [User Representation via Cross Multi-source Behavior Pre-training for Mobile Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01057)
*Chengqi Yang,Yiran Qiao,Feng Liu,Xingyu Lou,Zijun Zhou,Xiaoyun Mo,Changwang Zhang,Jiayuan Xu,Jun Wang,Xiang Ao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CM-PTM is a hierarchical pre-training model for mobile game user representation that captures cross-source and multi-granular device-level behaviors through cascaded masking tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing user representation pre-training models focus on single-app or app-level behaviors, missing the cross-source and multi-granular nature of user activities on mobile devices. User intent emerges from interactions among heterogeneous behavior sources and hierarchical action structures, which conventional app-centric modeling cannot address.

Method: Proposes CM-PTM (Cross Multi-source Behavior Pre-Training Model) with hierarchical cascaded mask-then-predict proxy tasks. First infers the source of next behavior, then progressively refines predictions at the app-action level. This enables unified modeling of cross-source dependencies and fine-grained behavioral dynamics within a single pre-training paradigm.

Result: Extensive experiments on large-scale real-world mobile datasets show CM-PTM effectively captures users' endogenous interests and consistently delivers significant performance gains on downstream mobile game recommendation tasks.

Conclusion: CM-PTM addresses limitations of conventional app-centric models by capturing the inherently cross-source and multi-granular nature of device-level user behaviors through hierarchical pre-training, leading to better user representation for mobile game personalization.

Abstract: User representation pre-training has become a fundamental paradigm for alleviating data sparsity in downstream personalization tasks. However, existing studies predominantly focus on single-app or app-level behaviors, overlooking the inherently cross-source and multi-granular nature of user activities on mobile devices. At the device level, user intent emerges from complex interactions among heterogeneous behavior sources and hierarchical action structures, posing challenges that cannot be addressed by conventional app-centric modeling. To tackle this issue, we propose CM-PTM, a novel Cross Multi-source Behavior Pre-Training Model tailored for mobile game user representation learning on device-level behavioral logs. CM-PTM employs hierarchical cascaded mask-then-predict proxy tasks that first infer the source of the next behavior and then progressively refine predictions at the app-action level. This design enables unified modeling of cross-source dependencies and fine-grained behavioral dynamics within a single pre-training paradigm. Extensive experiments on large-scale real-world mobile datasets demonstrate that CM-PTM effectively captures users' endogenous interests and consistently delivers significant performance gains on downstream mobile game recommendation tasks.

</details>


### [100] [ARISE-RL: Agentic Rubric-Grounded Iterative Self-Evolution with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01058)
*Fanrui Zhang,Ruixue Ding,Qiang Zhang,Xi Chen,Boli Chen,Shihang Wang,Qiuchen Wang,Hongmin Zhan,Jinxin Bian,Li xingchao,Peijin Zheng,Hao cheng,Pengjun Xie,Kaipeng Zhang,Jiawei Liu,Zheng-Jun Zha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARISE-RL is a self-evolution framework that couples a task/rubric generator with a reasoning solver through co-evolution, using reward-gated distillation and an expert-calibrated benchmark for open-ended agent training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended agent training via RL faces challenges due to lack of verifiable gold answers, scalable rubrics, and brittle/unstable rewards near capability boundaries, resulting in weak or noisy rollout contrast that obscures optimization signals.

Method: ARISE-RL couples a task/rubric Generator (grounds criteria in real tool observations, produces intermediate-difficulty tasks) with a reasoning Solver (learns from fine-grained rubric satisfaction via multi-step reasoning). Uses Reward-Gated Self-Evolution Distillation (RG-SED) to selectively distill memory-augmented policy variants back into itself when memory yields reward improvement, reducing distribution mismatch. Evaluated on ECR-Bench expert-calibrated rubric benchmark suite.

Result: ARISE-RL consistently achieves robust and stable overall state-of-the-art performance across all evaluated benchmarks in the ECR-Bench suite covering single-tool deep research and multi-tool travel planning.

Conclusion: ARISE-RL addresses fundamental challenges in open-ended agent training by introducing a full-cycle self-evolution framework with rubric-mediated co-evolution and reward-gated distillation, enabling more stable and effective learning for complex agentic tasks.

Abstract: Training open-ended agents via reinforcement learning (RL) is hindered by the lack of verifiable gold answers and scalable rubrics. Moreover, even near the model's capability boundary, long-horizon open-ended agentic tasks often yield brittle and unstable rewards, resulting in weak or noisy rollout contrast that obscures fine-grained optimization signals for group-based policy learning. To address these challenges, we propose ARISE-RL, a novel full-cycle self-evolution framework that couples a task/rubric Generator and a reasoning Solver through rubric-mediated co-evolution. The Generator grounds tool-related rubric criteria in real tool observations and is rewarded for producing valid, intermediate-difficulty tasks aligned with the Solver's evolving capability boundary. The Solver, in turn, learns from fine-grained rubric satisfaction signals through multi-step reasoning and tool use. We further introduce Reward-Gated Self-Evolution Distillation (RG-SED), which selectively distills a memory-augmented variant of the same policy back into itself only when the memory yields empirical reward improvement, thereby reducing distribution mismatch and avoiding blind imitation of noisy guidance. Finally, to support rigorous evaluation, we present ECR-Bench, an expert-calibrated rubric benchmark suite covering single-tool deep research and multi-tool travel planning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ARISE-RL consistently achieves robust and stable overall state-of-the-art performance across all evaluated benchmarks.

</details>


### [101] [Space Generative AI with Solar Energy Harvesting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01062)
*Jierui Zhang,Jianhao Huang,Zhanwei Wang,Kaibin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for solar-powered satellite generative AI that balances computation (image generation steps) and communication (downlink transmission) under energy harvesting constraints to maximize end-to-end performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Satellites can extend generative AI services to remote areas lacking terrestrial infrastructure, but deployment is constrained by limited, time-varying onboard energy from solar harvesting.
