Method: Develops a joint computation-communication resource-optimization framework using: 1) characterization of maximum downlink throughput for fixed generation depth with separation principle, and 2) formulation of joint C² utility-maximization problem with closed-form step-selection policy for constant-power regime.

Result: Extensive experiments under realistic orbital dynamics show the proposed policy dynamically balances generation quality and transmission reliability, yielding significant E2E performance gains over static computation- and communication-centric baselines.

Conclusion: The framework successfully addresses the fundamental computation-communication trade-offs in solar-powered space generative AI, enabling optimized performance under energy harvesting constraints.

Abstract: Satellites are emerging as promising platforms to extend generative \emph{artificial intelligence} (AI) services to remote areas lacking terrestrial infrastructure. However, deploying space generative AI is fundamentally constrained by the limited, time-varying onboard energy supplied by solar \emph{energy harvesting} (EH). This paper presents a framework for solar-powered space generative AI in which a satellite receives a user prompt, executes a diffusion-based image-generation model, and downlinks the compressed result within a strict time window. We identify the fundamental \emph{computation--communication} (C$^2$) trade-offs governed by the shared harvested-energy budgets. Specifically, increasing the number of generation steps improves intrinsic image quality but depletes energy and time available for downlink transmission, whereas prioritizing communication guarantees reliable delivery but sacrifices semantic quality. To balance these trade-offs and maximize \emph{end-to-end} (E2E) generative performance, we exploit the predictable solar-EH dynamics induced by deterministic orbital motion and develop a joint C$^2$ resource-optimization framework using a tractable two-step approach. First, we characterize the maximum downlink throughput for a fixed generation depth under continuous solar EH. This establishes a separation principle that decouples waiting-time selection from optimal transmit-power control. Next, we formulate a joint C$^2$ utility-maximization problem and derive a closed-form, low-complexity step-selection policy in the dominant constant-power regime. Extensive experiments under realistic orbital dynamics demonstrate that the proposed policy dynamically balances generation quality and transmission reliability. This yields significant E2E performance gains over static computation- and communication-centric baselines across diverse solar-EH states.

</details>


### [102] [Latent Recurrent Thoughts: Recurrent Refinement of Proposed Latents for Reasoning with Frozen LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01117)
*Zhaoliang Chen,Jie Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Latent Recurrent Thoughts (LRT) enables reasoning in continuous representation space using a frozen LLM with a small auxiliary network for iterative latent state refinement, outperforming both continuous-space reasoning methods and chain-of-thought prompting with much less compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought reasoning has limitations: it operates in discrete token space where errors propagate, and requires imitation traces for training. Reasoning in continuous representation space could sidestep these constraints, but the method for computing useful latent states remains an open problem.

Method: LRT uses a frozen LLM for sequence modeling/decoding while a small auxiliary network supplies continuous latent thoughts as input. A tiny recurrent reasoner iteratively refines these latents through bounded residual corrections, decoupling computation depth from model size. The system includes a task-dedicated proposer for base latents, a recurrent reasoner for refinement, and the frozen LLM for final answer decoding.

Result: LRT substantially outperforms prior frozen-decoder continuous-space reasoning methods on symbolic reasoning (Countdown-4, Sudoku) and natural-language reasoning (HumanEval, MBPP, StrategyQA) under identical conditions. It also outperforms non-thinking-mode chain-of-thought prompting on the same backbone with only a small fraction of the inference compute.

Conclusion: LRT demonstrates that continuous-space reasoning with frozen LLMs and iterative latent refinement is highly effective, offering advantages over both discrete chain-of-thought and previous continuous-space approaches while being more computationally efficient.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought reasoning unfolds in discrete token space: each step is committed as text, errors propagate, and eliciting good traces presupposes traces to imitate. Reasoning instead in a model's continuous representation space - where intermediate states are vectors rather than words - sidesteps these constraints, but leaves open how those latent states should be computed. We approach this along two axes. First, we keep a large language model (LLM) frozen and use it for what it is already good at - modeling and decoding sequences - while a small auxiliary network supplies continuous latent thoughts as input. Second, we produce those latents by recurrence: a tiny recurrent reasoner refines them over many steps, decoupling the depth of computation from the size of the model, so that the latents are a product of iterative processing rather than a single forward pass. We instantiate this as Latent Recurrent Thoughts (LRT): a task-dedicated proposer supplies base latents, a recurrent reasoner refines them through bounded residual corrections, and the frozen LLM decodes the answer. On symbolic reasoning with answer supervision but no reasoning traces (Countdown-4, Sudoku) and on natural-language reasoning (HumanEval, MBPP, StrategyQA), LRT substantially outperforms prior frozen-decoder continuous-space reasoning methods under an identical decoder, prompt, data, and training budget, and outperforms non-thinking-mode chain-of-thought prompting on the same backbone at a small fraction of its inference compute.

</details>


### [103] [Jailbreaking Text-to-Image Models Through Cracks: Navigating Heterogeneous Safety Filters via Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01168)
*Kaiyan Wen,Shijie Zhang,Lu Yu,Guangdong Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CRACK is a multi-agent debate framework that achieves high success rates (up to 99.63%) in jailbreaking text-to-image safety filters by adaptively navigating cross-layer conflicts through exploration, diagnosis, and arbitration cycles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-image models are vulnerable to jailbreak attacks despite having multi-layer safety stacks. Existing jailbreak methods either target individual filters or use aggregate feedback from the complete pipeline, making it difficult to identify active constraints and adapt to conflicts across different safety layers.

Method: The paper introduces the Detection Surface framework to characterize decision boundaries of heterogeneous safety filters and their joint effect on jailbreak search. Based on this, they propose CRACK - a multi-agent debate framework with three agents: Attack Agent (generates prompt mutations), Defense Agent (obtains layer-specific diagnostic feedback), and Judge Agent (optimizes mutation strategies through reward-guided refinement).

Result: CRACK achieves Attack Success Rates (ASR) up to 99.63% under composite defenses across multiple T2I models, datasets, and safety configurations. It requires fewer queries than existing methods while maintaining semantic fidelity of the original harmful intent.

Conclusion: The Detection Surface framework reveals that successful evasion is governed by sparse, non-convex regions shaped by cross-layer conflicts, and CRACK's multi-agent debate approach effectively navigates these complex constraints through adaptive search and layer-specific diagnostics.

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) models remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks that elicit Not-Safe-For-Work (NSFW) content, despite increasingly being guarded by heterogeneous, multi-layer safety stacks combining text filters, image classifiers, and cross-modal detectors. Existing jailbreak studies either optimize against individual filters or query the complete pipeline with aggregate feedback, making it difficult to identify the active constraint and adapt to conflicts across safety layers.In this paper, we introduce the \emph{Detection Surface}, a unified geometric framework that characterizes the decision boundaries induced by heterogeneous T2I safety filters and their joint effect on the jailbreak search space. This formulation reveals that successful evasion is governed by a sparse and non-convex region shaped by cross-layer conflicts, where mutations that bypass one filter may increase exposure to another. Motivated by this analysis, we propose \emph{CRACK}, a multi-agent debate framework for adaptive jailbreak search that decomposes jailbreak search into exploration, diagnosis, and arbitration. CRACK coordinates an Attack Agent, a Defense Agent, and a Judge Agent to iteratively generate prompt mutations, obtain layer-specific diagnostic feedback, and optimize mutation strategies through reward-guided refinement. Through repeated rounds of debate, CRACK adapts its search direction to the evolving cross-layer constraints while preserving the original harmful intent. Extensive experiments across multiple T2I models, datasets, and safety configurations show that CRACK achieves Attack Success Rates (ASR) of up to 99.63\% under composite defenses, while requiring fewer queries than existing methods and maintaining semantic fidelity.

</details>


### [104] [FinLifeBench: Exhaustive Life-Event History and Financial-State Reconstruction from Longitudinal Banking Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01198)
*Hangyeul Lee,Juyoung Oh,Jaeyong Ko,Sunmin Kim,Jaeik Park,Hyunkyu Kim,Jungmin Son,Pilsung Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FinLifeBench is a new benchmark for evaluating LLMs' ability to maintain complete, current, and traceable customer records over longitudinal banking interactions, with poor performance showing models struggle with exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction despite some localization capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks focus on question answering, bounded episodes, or targeted recall, but don't evaluate exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction needed for banking assistants to maintain complete, current, and traceable customer records as life changes emerge incidentally in routine banking interactions.

Method: Introduced FinLifeBench with 6,000 eight-turn Korean banking sessions from 20 synthetic trajectories, evaluating two tasks: 1) reconstructing every life-event instance with its first-establishing session, and 2) reconstructing complete 34-path financial state at consecutive checkpoints. Uses deterministic gold standards for 24 event types and 34 state paths with consensus quality assurance.

Result: Across 11 LLMs, event-anchor recall drops from 0.591 at 15 sessions to 0.445 at 300 sessions. Errors driven mainly by omitted events rather than poor anchor localization. Financial-state reconstruction frequently treats superseded or outdated information as current, with best GCA@15 reaching 0.470. Performance on the two reconstruction tasks shows only weak association.

Conclusion: LLMs can localize evidence for recovered events while still failing to maintain complete and temporally valid longitudinal records, highlighting a significant gap in current models' ability to handle exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction needed for real-world banking applications.

Abstract: Repeated banking interactions require assistants to maintain complete, current, and traceable customer records as life changes emerge incidentally in routine requests. Existing benchmarks emphasize question answering, bounded episodes, or targeted recall rather than exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction. We introduce FinLifeBench, which evaluates two tasks over the same cumulative dialogue: reconstructing every life-event instance with its first-establishing session and reconstructing a complete 34-path financial state at consecutive checkpoints. The benchmark contains 6,000 eight-turn Korean banking sessions from 20 independent synthetic trajectories, with deterministic, exhaustive gold for 24 event types and 34 state paths and consensus quality assurance. Across eleven LLMs under a full-context condition, event-anchor recall falls from 0.591 at 15 sessions to 0.445 at 300. Errors are driven primarily by omitted events rather than poor anchor localization, while financial-state reconstruction frequently treats superseded or potentially outdated information as current; the best GCA@15 reaches 0.470. Performance on the two reconstruction tasks is only weakly associated. These results show that models can localize evidence for recovered events while still failing to maintain complete and temporally valid longitudinal records.

</details>


### [105] [H2Table: Hierarchical Hypergraph-Enhanced Large Language Models for Complex Table Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01216)
*Jia Ling,Yangfan Wang,Chen Tang,Haoming Tan,Yang Yang,Yi Guan,Jingchi Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: H2Table is a novel framework that represents complex tables as hierarchical nested hypergraphs to improve LLM reasoning on table QA tasks, achieving 22.88% average improvement on complex hierarchical tables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM approaches for table reasoning linearize tables into sequences, which inherently overlooks their intrinsic two-dimensional and hierarchical structure. This limitation makes it difficult to handle complex tables with hierarchical nested headers.

Method: The H2Table framework represents complex tables as hierarchical nested hypergraphs. It uses a tailored hypergraph encoder for message passing between hyperedges (headers) and nodes (cells), and introduces learnable query vectors to extract structural embeddings from the encoder into the LLM.

Result: H2Table achieves significant improvements on complex table question answering tasks, with an average 22.88% improvement over state-of-the-art baselines on highly complex tables with nesting depth of four in the HiTab dataset.

Conclusion: The proposed hierarchical hypergraph-enhanced table reasoning approach effectively handles complex tables with hierarchical nested structures, demonstrating that explicitly modeling table structure through hypergraphs significantly improves LLM performance on table QA tasks.

Abstract: Tables are ubiquitous across diverse domains, yet reasoning over them remains a significant challenge for modern large language models (LLMs). Current approaches typically linearize tables into sequences, inherently overlooking their intrinsic two-dimensional and hierarchical structure. To address this, we propose H2Table (Hierarchical Hypergraph-Enhanced Table Reasoning), a novel framework that represents complex tables as hierarchical nested hypergraphs. To process this representation, we design a tailored hypergraph encoder to facilitate message passing between hyperedges (headers) and nodes (cells), thereby perceiving the semantic entailment relationships between them within complex tables. Furthermore, we introduce a set of learnable query vectors acting as a lightweight bridge to extract representative structural embeddings from the encoder into the LLM. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach effectively handles complex table question answering tasks with hierarchical nested headers. Notably, on the HiTab dataset, H2Table achieves an average improvement of 22.88% over state-of-the-art baselines on highly complex tables with a nesting depth of four. Our code is available at: https://github.com/lila120/h2table.

</details>


### [106] [Prompt-Robust Language Models: Which Training Strategies Work?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01217)
*Frederic Sadrieh,Michal Štefánik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current methods for reducing LLM prompt sensitivity show limited effectiveness, with the simplest data construction strategy (one template per batch) often outperforming sophisticated robustness fine-tuning techniques like CoIN and PPCL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are highly sensitive to prompt formulation, which creates reliability issues. Prior work has attempted to address this through data construction or robustness objectives, but the effectiveness of these approaches needs systematic comparison.

Method: Reproduced and compared existing strategies under controlled conditions, including: 1) Standard fine-tuning, 2) In-context learning, 3) Current robustness fine-tuning methods (CoIN for contrastive alignment and PPCL for consistency regularization), 4) Simple data construction strategy (training on one template per batch). Measured prompt sensitivity and analyzed gradient conflicts.

Result: 1) Robustness fine-tuning improves over standard methods but leaves large performance gaps (40-57% between best and worst prompts), 2) CoIN and PPCL often fail to outperform the simple one-template-per-batch strategy, 3) Auxiliary objectives penalize specific quantities but lack generalization, 4) Per-template gradients conflict on 57-64% of parameters, causing optimization challenges when mixing formulations in batches.

Conclusion: Existing approaches to LLM prompt robustness have limited effectiveness due to gradient conflicts and lack of generalization. The simplest data construction strategy often works best, highlighting the need for better understanding of how models learn prompt-agnostic representations and more sophisticated approaches to address the fundamental optimization challenges.

Abstract: Despite their strong performance, large language models remain highly sensitive to prompt formulation. Prior work addresses this through refined data construction or through dedicated robustness objectives. We reproduce and compare these strategies under controlled conditions, and measure how effective they are in addressing models' prompt sensitivity. We find the current robustness fine-tuning methods improve over standard fine-tuning and in-context learning, but the best-to-worst prompt gap remains as high as 40-57% of performance. Moreover, the recent robustness-enhancing methods we test - CoIN for contrastive alignment and PPCL for consistency regularization - often fail to outperform the simplest data construction strategy: training on one template per batch. Our diagnostics explain these results. The auxiliary objectives move the quantity they penalize, but do not generalize beyond it. Additionally, data construction strategies differ due to the conflicting signs of per-template gradients on 57-64% of parameters. Thus, batches that mix formulations force the optimizer to reconcile competing updates instead of finding a shared, prompt-agnostic one.

</details>


### [107] [Measuring the Behavioral Fidelity of Long-Horizon Human Activity Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01257)
*Yi Fei Cheng,Fan Yang,Iremsu Bas,Koichiro Niinuma,Narishige Abe,David Lindlbauer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a framework for evaluating behavioral fidelity in long-horizon activity simulations for LLM-based human simulators, finding that no single conditioning mechanism uniformly achieves high fidelity across different metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-based human simulators are increasingly used for policy, evaluation, and training purposes, they need to faithfully reproduce real behavioral patterns. While prior work has examined fidelity in survey responses and dialogue, longer-horizon real-world activity remains largely unexplored.

Method: The authors introduce a framework for evaluating behavioral fidelity across temporal granularities and levels of analysis. They collected a 43-hour multi-camera dataset of in-the-wild office activity as a case study, comparing different trace-derived conditioning mechanisms: persona descriptors, few-shot exemplars, and statistical transition and time-of-day priors.

Result: Behavioral fidelity is not uniform across metrics. Statistical priors bring activity and sequence distributions closest to real behavior, but they over-fragment routines and suppress within-person variability. Different conditioning mechanisms excel in different aspects of fidelity.

Conclusion: The findings motivate a more holistic evaluation approach that spans multiple metrics, temporal granularities, and levels of analysis, rather than relying on single measures of fidelity for LLM-based human simulators.

Abstract: As LLM-based human simulators are increasingly used for policy, evaluation, and training, they must faithfully reproduce real behavioral patterns. While prior work has examined behavioral fidelity in survey responses and dialogue, longer-horizon real-world activity remains largely unexplored. We introduce a framework for evaluating behavioral fidelity in long-horizon activity simulations across temporal granularities and levels of analysis. As a case study, we collect a 43-hour multi-camera dataset of in-the-wild office activity and compare trace-derived conditioning mechanisms: persona descriptors, few-shot exemplars, and statistical transition and time-of-day priors. We find that behavioral fidelity is not uniform across metrics: statistical priors bring activity and sequence distributions closest to real behavior, yet over-fragment routines and suppress within-person variability. These findings motivate a more holistic evaluation that spans multiple metrics, temporal granularities, and levels of analysis.

</details>


### [108] [Dual Process Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01260)
*Jiayi Yan,Francesco Fabiano,Alessandro Abate*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic dual-process architecture for robotics that combines fast learning-based "System-1" modules with precise symbolic "System-2" solvers, dynamically orchestrated by metacognitive control to improve planning efficiency and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic systems need speed, precision, and reliability, but classical methods sacrifice efficiency while learning approaches lack guarantees. There's a need to bridge these approaches to overcome limitations and enable more capable adaptive systems.

Method: Introduced a dual-process architecture inspired by "Thinking Fast and Slow" paradigm: System-1 (fast, experience-driven modules) and System-2 (slow, precise symbolic solvers). A metacognitive controller dynamically orchestrates interaction between them, selecting when to use intuition vs. reasoning.

Result: The architecture demonstrated consistent gains in planning efficiency, accuracy, and generalization across diverse nonlinear benchmark environments. It also promotes knowledge reuse across tasks.

Conclusion: Tightly coupling learning with structured reasoning offers a scalable path toward more capable and adaptive robotic systems, with neuro-symbolic integration providing practical advantages over pure learning or pure symbolic approaches.

Abstract: Robotic systems are deeply embedded in both industry and everyday life, where they are expected to act with speed, precision, and reliability. Classical control and planning methods have long delivered strong guarantees, but often at the cost of computational efficiency and adaptability. More recently, learning-based approaches have shown promise in overcoming these limitations, enabling agents to leverage experience to accelerate decision-making and address previously intractable problems. In this work, we bridge these two approaches through a neuro-symbolic perspective on nonlinear motion planning. Inspired by the Thinking Fast and Slow paradigm, we introduce a dual-process architecture that combines the strengths of robust reasoning and learning. Our framework integrates state-of-the-art symbolic solvers as a ``System-2'' component with experience-driven ``System-1'' modules. A metacognitive controller dynamically orchestrates their interaction, selecting when to rely on fast intuition versus slower, more precise reasoning. By evaluating the framework across diverse nonlinear benchmark environments, we demonstrate that this architecture yields consistent gains in planning efficiency, accuracy, and generalization, while promoting reuse across tasks. The results suggest that tightly coupling learning with structured reasoning offers a scalable path toward more capable and adaptive robotic systems.

</details>


### [109] [Making Prospective Memory SLM-Shaped: Typed Intention Stores for Small-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01272)
*Jinqing Zhao,Chengcan Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new Prospective Intention Store (PIS) framework enables small language models to achieve state-of-the-art performance on prospective memory benchmarks by treating intention execution as schema-constrained state tracking rather than open-ended reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs struggle with prospective memory (carrying out deferred intentions while multitasking), with the best existing PM-Bench scaffold reaching only 65.1% Set-F1. The authors argue this is because the task requires schema-constrained state tracking rather than open-ended reasoning, suggesting small models could excel if given properly structured action spaces.

Method: Proposed Prospective Intention Store (PIS) that puts lifecycle logic in code and keeps only scoped language work for the model. The approach is agentic and training-free, requiring no selector fine-tuning or trajectory distillation. It structures the prospective memory task as schema-constrained state tracking with typed action spaces.

Result: PIS achieves 82.9% Set-F1 with DeepSeek-Chat on PM-Bench. With Gemma-E2B, PIS reaches 66.2% Set-F1 compared to only 4.2% without a store and maximum 6.6% with retrospective memories. PIS further reaches 70.1% Set-F1 where retrospective methods max out at 54.4%. This represents a new state-of-the-art and enables small models to surpass previous large-model scaffold performance.

Conclusion: Prospective Intention Store successfully reframes prospective memory as schema-constrained state tracking, enabling small models to achieve superior performance through structured code-based lifecycle management and scoped language work, setting a new benchmark standard without requiring additional training.

Abstract: Prospective memory means carrying out a deferred intention at the right future cue while other work continues. Benchmarks now isolate it as an agent skill, yet frontier LLMs still struggle: the best published PM-Bench scaffold reaches only 65.1% Set-F1. We argue that this loop is schema-constrained state tracking rather than open-ended reasoning, and that small models can execute it when the action space is typed. We propose the Prospective Intention Store (PIS) that puts lifecycle logic in code and scoped language work on the model. The scaffold is agentic and training-free: no selector fine-tuning and no trajectory distillation. On PM-Bench, DeepSeek-Chat with PIS reaches 82.9% Set-F1. On Gemma-E2B, Set-F1 is only 4.2% without a store and at most 6.6% under seven retrospective memories, while PIS reaches 66.2%. PIS further reaches 70.1% Set-F1, where retrospective memory methods stay at most 54.4%. PIS sets a new state of the art on this benchmark and enables small models to surpass the published large-model scaffold.

</details>


### [110] [Analog-DB: An Agent-First Analog Integrated Circuit Database, From Blocks to Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01286)
*Danial Noori Zadeh,Mohamed B. Elamien*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: analog-db is an open-source database that enables sharing and verification of analog IC designs through a process-neutral representation, reusable testbenches, and composable parameterization, supporting AI-driven design reuse and verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sharing analog IC designs is difficult due to foundry NDA restrictions on process details and lack of released testbenches behind published results. This makes design verification, reuse, and AI-assisted design challenging.

Method: Developed analog-db database with: 1) Domain-specific language for process-neutral topology representation, 2) Reusable testbenches and machine-readable datasheets, 3) Parameterization scheme exposing functional sub-blocks and device sizes with matching constraints, 4) Schema-governed contract and queryable catalog for AI design agents.

Result: Across regulator corpus: 23 circuit-kit bindings on 3 open kits met specification bands; 10/23 met common class band; 17/23 imported sizings failed testbenches but closed under gm/ID sizing loop within 1-3 iterations. Case study: AI agent sized op-amp cores, found 4 hand-entry defects and missing common-mode feedback loop. Database contains 68 circuits across 16 classes with schematic-level verification.

Conclusion: analog-db provides a comprehensive solution for sharing, verifying, and reusing analog IC designs, enabling AI-assisted design workflows and addressing current barriers in analog design collaboration and verification. The open-source release supports broader adoption and further development.

Abstract: Sharing analog integrated circuit designs remains difficult: foundry non-disclosure agreements restrict the process details a design depends on, and the testbenches behind published results are rarely released. We present analog-db, an open-source, versioned database built on a shareable design representation. A domain-specific language captures each design as a process-neutral topology, reusable testbenches, and a machine-readable datasheet under one schema, so a design is shared in full and re-simulates on the process kits it is bound to. A parameterization scheme exposes functional sub-blocks and device sizes as named parameters that carry their matching constraints, making circuits composable and retargetable; a schema-governed contract and queryable catalog let AI design agents discover and reuse them directly. Across the regulator corpus, all 23 circuit-kit bindings on three open kits meet their own recorded specification bands (typical corner, matched devices, no layout) and 10 of 23 meet a common class band. Seventeen of the 23 imported sizings failed their testbenches and closed under a gm/ID sizing loop driven by the annotated sub-block roles, typically within one to three iterations. In a supervised case study, a coding agent working from the released artifacts sized the op-amp cores of a chopper instrumentation amplifier on an open 130nm kit, locating four hand-entry defects and a missing common-mode feedback loop that the sizing-only baseline did not repair. The database holds 68 circuits across sixteen classes, verifiable at schematic level under a tiered harness and tracked on a power/performance scoreboard, released at https://github.com/MacAnalog/spicexplorer-release.

</details>


### [111] [A Composable Evaluation System for Reproducible Omni-Modal Foundation Model Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01315)
*Hodong Lee,Sanghee Park,Dohoon Ryu,Jungwhan Kim,Junyeob Kim,Soyoon Kim,Geewook Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniEvaluator is a unified evaluation framework that connects existing inference engines and evaluation libraries across text, image, video, and audio modalities through a single interface, enabling reproducible cross-modal benchmarking and cost-efficient scoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from the challenge of evaluating omni-modal foundation models across different modalities (text, image, video, audio). Existing evaluation toolkits for each modality have incompatible inference engines, prompt conventions, and metric implementations, forcing practitioners to maintain separate environments and struggle with cross-modal result comparison.

Method: Rather than reimplementing benchmarks, OmniEvaluator connects existing inference engines (four backends) and curated evaluation libraries (four frameworks) at a higher level, exposing over a thousand benchmarks through a single interface. It includes artifact recording for exact reproduction, a shared results dashboard, federated GPU sharing across concurrent evaluations, and a built-in CPU-efficient verifier that maintains score stability across different engines and prompts.

Result: The system provides a unified evaluation platform with over a thousand benchmarks accessible through a single interface. It enables reproducible evaluations with full configuration capture, cross-model comparison through a shared dashboard, efficient resource utilization via federated GPU sharing, and stable scoring through a built-in verifier that matches commercial LLM judges without recurring API costs.

Conclusion: OmniEvaluator addresses the fragmentation in multimodal evaluation by providing a comprehensive, unified framework that bridges existing toolchains while ensuring reproducibility, cross-modal comparability, and cost efficiency, making it an essential tool for developing and evaluating omni-modal foundation models.

Abstract: Building an omni-modal foundation model means evaluating it across text, image, video, and audio. Excellent evaluation toolkits exist for each modality, but their inference engines, prompt conventions, and metric implementations are mutually incompatible, so practitioners end up maintaining separate environments for every toolchain and still struggle to compare results across them. OmniEvaluator grew out of this need in our own model development: rather than reimplementing benchmarks, it connects existing inference engines and curated evaluation libraries at a higher level, exposing four inference backends, four evaluation frameworks, and over a thousand benchmarks through a single interface. Every run is recorded as an artifact capturing the full configuration for exact reproduction, and results flow into a shared dashboard for cross-model comparison. A federated mode shares GPU inference servers across concurrent evaluations, and a built-in verifier, small enough to run on CPU, keeps its score stable across engines and prompts where rule-based scoring fluctuates under configuration mismatch, matching cost-efficient commercial LLM judges without their recurring API cost. The system, demo video, and dashboard are publicly available. (https://github.com/naver-ai/omni-evaluator)

</details>


### [112] [Automated Event Log Generation from Unstructured Text Using Finetuned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01320)
*Maximilian Seeth,Gabriel Marques Tavares,Daniel Schuster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Using fine-tuned LLMs to automatically convert unstructured organizational text into structured event logs for process mining, overcoming the bottleneck of manual log creation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process mining requires structured event logs, but creating them manually from organizational text resources (incident tickets, manuals, reports) is costly and labor-intensive, leaving much valuable data underutilized.

Method: Propose a scalable framework using LLMs as automated data translators. Fine-tune LLMs on a newly created text-to-log dataset to extract structured event data from unstructured textual resources.

Result: Fine-tuned LLMs successfully extract high-fidelity event logs from unstructured resources, significantly outperforming few-shot or zero-shot prompting approaches, demonstrating fine-tuning as necessary for generating reliable event data.

Conclusion: The method provides a promising pipeline for making previously unused textual data available to process mining ecosystems, expanding possibilities for investigating organizational workflows using process mining techniques.

Abstract: Process mining (PM) provides a powerful framework for discovering and optimizing operational processes from event data. However, the efficacy of PM techniques is strictly predicated on the availability of structured event logs. Thus far, event logs have often been laboriously created by domain and process mining experts. This costly effort causes large portions of organizational knowledge, including incident tickets, manuals, and textual reports, to remain underutilized. We address this bottleneck by investigating the efficacy of Large Language Models (LLMs) as automated data translators. We propose a scalable framework that leverages LLMs as data translators to bridge the gap between unstructured textual resources and structured event data. We finetune LLMs on a newly created text-to-log dataset, demonstrating that the resulting models can extract high-fidelity event logs from unstructured resources. Our results show that this finetuning approach outperforms few-shot or zero-shot prompting by a large amount, highlighting finetuning as a necessary pre-condition for generating reliable event data. We conclude that our method provides a promising pipeline for making previously unused data available to process mining ecosystems, effectively expanding the possibilities of using PM to further investigate organizational workflows.

</details>


### [113] [LEAP: Likelihood Elicitation and Aggregation for LLM-based Probabilistic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01337)
*Yufei Chen,Yiran Zhao,Xiaogang Xu,Qipeng Xie,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LEAP proposes a new probabilistic forecasting framework that replaces monolithic LLM prediction with item-by-item evidence analysis using likelihood elicitation and explicit probabilistic aggregation, improving prediction quality and calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based forecasting systems using Monolithic Prediction obscure how individual evidence items affect results and collapse uncertainty across competing outcomes, limiting transparency and probabilistic reasoning.

Method: LEAP examines each evidence item separately, elicits likelihood parameters describing its implications, then combines these using an explicit prior and deterministic probabilistic model to produce posterior distributions.

Result: LEAP improves most prediction and calibration metrics across models, maintains strength under controlled comparisons of prior access, inference budget, and aggregation, and supports continuous, single-choice, and multi-choice forecasts.

Conclusion: Reorganizing evidence use through likelihood elicitation and explicit probabilistic aggregation provides better forecasting performance and calibration than monolithic LLM prediction while preserving reproducible evidence contributions.

Abstract: LLM-based forecasting systems have improved on real-world tasks such as financial markets and sports outcomes, largely through stronger search and tool use. Many systems still ask an LLM to read all collected evidence together and produce the final forecast. We call this design Monolithic Prediction. It can obscure how individual evidence items affect the result and collapse uncertainty across competing outcomes. We propose LEAP (Likelihood Elicitation and Aggregation for Probabilistic forecasting), which reorganizes how collected evidence is used in the prediction stage. LEAP examines each evidence item separately and elicits likelihood parameters that describe its implications for the target. An explicit prior and a deterministic probabilistic model then combine these likelihoods into a posterior distribution. This procedure supports continuous, single-choice, and multi-choice forecasts while preserving reproducible evidence contributions. We build a benchmark covering forecasting, information-seeking, and browsing tasks, and evaluate LEAP on our own agent loop and several agent CLI frameworks. Given the same evidence, LEAP improves most prediction and calibration metrics across models and remains stronger under controlled comparisons of prior access, inference budget, and aggregation.

</details>


### [114] [Cheap Verifiers, Large Blind Spots: Measuring the Reliability Cost of Cost-Saving Cascades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01345)
*Dushyant Rajput*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-improving inference cascades where cheap models escalate to frontier verifiers actually degrade performance despite appearing to improve through the verifier's metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce inference costs by using cheap models for most queries while escalating hard cases to frontier models, with the goal of creating a self-improving loop where the student learns from verifier rejections.

Method: Measured real LLM cascades across different model sizes, analyzed verifier blind spots, examined corrective fine-tuning on verifier rejections, and developed theoretical analysis with conservation law ε_∞ ≲ q_0 β_0 to explain the blindness phenomenon.

Result: Found that verifier blind spots grow with student capability, naive corrective fine-tuning degrades rather than improves students, and cascade metrics reported through the verifier show flat 3% error while true error can be as high as 32%, creating dangerous blindness to system degradation.

Conclusion: The reliability of self-improving cascades cannot be assessed through metrics computed by the verifier itself due to inherent systemic blindness, requiring alternative evaluation approaches.

Abstract: Inference cascades cut cost by answering most queries with a cheap model and escalating a hard tail to a frontier model that acts as verifier. A natural extension closes the loop: fine-tune the cheap student on the verifier's rejections so the escalation rate, and cost, fall each round. We measure this loop on real LLMs and report four findings. First, the verifier's blind spot, the fraction of the student's wrong answers it accepts, is large and moves adversarially: it grows with student capability ($β$ from 0.12 to 0.55 as the student scales 0.5B to 32B) and shrinks with verifier capability, so it is worst in the cheap-student, cheap-verifier regime cascades exist to create. Second, buying it away returns the saving: a frontier verifier drives $β$ to about 0.05 but then escalates on 46% of hard-MATH queries against a 39% true error rate, paying the frontier price on nearly half of all traffic. Third, naive corrective fine-tuning on the verifier-rejected tail does not improve the small student but degrades and ultimately collapses it, across every teacher we tried (cross-family and same-family), so at this scale the self-improving loop is self-defeating. Fourth, through all of this the cascade's own dashboard, every metric computed through the verifier, reads a flat 3% error while true delivered error swings up to 32%: the system is blind to its own degradation by construction. We then give the theory that explains the blindness, a two-population conservation law, $ε_\infty \lesssim q_0 β_0$, under which every in-loop metric improves while true quality does not, and a synthetic study that validates the mechanism. The practical conclusion: the reliability of a self-improving cascade cannot be read from any metric computed through its own verifier.

</details>


### [115] [SymFold: Synergizing Evolutionary and Structural Priors for Accurate Protein Inverse Folding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01353)
*Handong Wang,Jiaxin Qi,Baisheng Lai,Jianqiang Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A symmetric dual-path architecture combines PLMs and MPLMs to iteratively guide protein sequence generation, achieving state-of-the-art performance in protein inverse folding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for protein inverse folding use a serial pipeline where a structure encoder predicts a coarse sequence, which is then refined by protein language models (PLMs). However, this refinement is limited by the quality of the initial prediction. While recent multimodal protein language models (MPLMs) can encode structure directly, they are not effective for inverse folding. Thus, there is a need for a method that effectively combines both sequence and structural knowledge.

Method: The authors introduce a symmetric dual-path architecture that iteratively guides protein sequence generation by leveraging both protein language models (PLMs) for pretrained sequence evolution knowledge and multimodal protein language models (MPLMs) for pretrained structural knowledge.

Result: Extensive experiments across standard protein inverse folding benchmarks show that the proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing prior approaches. Ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of the symmetric design.

Conclusion: The symmetric dual-path architecture, which leverages both PLMs for sequence evolution knowledge and MPLMs for structural knowledge, achieves state-of-the-art performance in protein inverse folding. Ablation studies validate the design rationale, indicating a promising direction for future research in the field.

Abstract: Protein inverse folding aims to recover amino acid sequences for a given 3D protein structure, underpinning broad applications such as enzyme engineering and drug discovery.Current methods often follow a serial pipeline, in which a structure encoder predicts a coarse sequence, which is then refined by protein language models (PLMs). However, because PLMs only perform post-hoc sequence edits, the refinement is bounded by the quality of upstream predictions.Thanks to recent multimodal protein language models (MPLMs), we could directly encode structure to generate sequences with pretrained structural knowledge, but we observe that they are not effective for inverse folding. Therefore, we introduce a symmetric dual-path architecture that both leverages PLMs for pretrained sequence evolution knowledge and MPLMs for pretrained structural knowledge to iteratively guide protein sequence generation.Through extensive experiments across standard protein inverse folding benchmarks, our method achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing prior approaches, and ablation studies validate the rationale of our symmetric design, revealing a promising direction for the community.

</details>


### [116] [EDGE: Error Dependency Graph-Guided Multi-Error Attribution in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01360)
*Jun Hou,Priya Pitre,Yi Fang,Xuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EDGE introduces an error dependency graph framework for attributing multiple related errors in LLM agents, improving multi-error attribution over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agent failures often contain multiple related errors rather than single mistakes, and existing attribution methods don't explicitly model dependencies between errors.

Method: EDGE constructs an error dependency graph from observed errors, validates a causal subset through counterfactual rollout, and uses this graph to guide a two-stage LLM-as-judge detector for error attribution.

Result: Experiments on TRAIL and MAST show EDGE improves category-level multi-error attribution across most evaluated models and settings, and works across different prompting strategies.

Conclusion: Dependency structure serves as a useful diagnostic prior for agent failures beyond isolated root-cause prediction.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agent failures often contain multiple related errors rather than a single mistake. Existing attribution methods usually identify a responsible agent, step, or root cause, but do not explicitly model dependency between errors. We introduce EDGE, an Error Dependency Graph-guided multi-Error attribution framework. EDGE constructs an error dependency graph from observed error events and validates a reliable causal subset through counterfactual rollout. The inference graph guides a two-stage LLM-as-judge detector for error attribution, and the intervention-validated subgraph provides a more reliable basis for explanation and repair analysis. Experiments on TRAIL and MAST show that EDGE improves category-level multi-error attribution across most evaluated models and settings. Experiments with adapted Who&When-style prompts show that the graph helps across prompting strategies. These results suggest that dependency structure is a useful diagnostic prior for agent failures beyond isolated root-cause prediction.

</details>


### [117] [Neuro-Symbolic Geometric Abstraction (NeuSOGA): From Observations to Symbolic Mathematical Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01408)
*Qingde Li,Qingqi Hong,Jie Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeuSOGA is a neuro-symbolic framework that transforms geometric observations into explicit symbolic mathematical representations through progressive abstraction layers, achieving interpretable and editable models from diverse visual inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the fundamental AI challenge of transforming observations into explicit symbolic representations suitable for abstraction, interpretation, and reasoning. While modern AI systems achieve strong perceptual capabilities through statistical learning, their knowledge is encoded in latent parameters that are difficult to inspect or manipulate analytically. The work is inspired by Neuro-Symbolic AI and theories of human abstraction to bridge the gap between perception and symbolic reasoning.

Method: NeuSOGA progressively transforms observations through three abstraction layers: 1) Topological abstractions using Euclidean Distance Transforms, 2) Geometric abstractions using Segment Anything foundation models with adaptive multi-scale processing, and 3) Symbolic mathematical representations using Implicit Area Splines. The architecture combines topology-guided structural discovery, foundation-model perception, adaptive geometric abstraction, and symbolic synthesis.

Result: The framework generates analytical implicit models that support arbitrary-order smoothness, additive composition, and closed-form evaluation. Unlike neural latent encodings, these representations remain interpretable, editable, and mathematically explicit. Experiments on ModelNet40 point clouds, arbitrary-view projections, and segmented optical observations show that NeuSOGA transforms diverse observations into compact symbolic representations while preserving essential geometric and topological structure across different sensing modalities and viewing directions.

Conclusion: NeuSOGA provides an interpretable and explainable pathway from observation to symbol, establishing a bridge between perceptual AI systems and symbolic reasoning capabilities. The framework successfully transforms geometric observations into explicit mathematical representations that maintain both interpretability and analytical properties, addressing a fundamental challenge in artificial intelligence.

Abstract: A fundamental challenge in artificial intelligence is the transformation of observations into explicit symbolic representations suitable for abstraction, interpretation, and reasoning. While modern AI systems achieve remarkable perceptual capabilities through large-scale statistical learning, the resulting knowledge is typically encoded within latent parameters that are difficult to inspect or manipulate analytically. Inspired by Neuro-Symbolic AI and theories of human abstraction, this paper investigates the formation of symbolic mathematical representations from geometric observations.
  We propose NeuSOGA (Neuro-Symbolic Geometric Abstraction), a framework that progressively transforms observations into topological abstractions, geometric abstractions, and ultimately symbolic mathematical representations. The architecture combines topology-guided structural discovery using Euclidean Distance Transforms, foundation-model perception using Segment Anything, adaptive multi-scale geometric abstraction, and symbolic synthesis through Implicit Area Splines.
  The resulting representation is an analytical implicit model supporting arbitrary-order smoothness, additive composition, and closed-form evaluation. Unlike neural latent encodings, the generated representation remains interpretable, editable, and mathematically explicit. Experiments on ModelNet40 point clouds, arbitrary-view projections, and segmented optical observations demonstrate that NeuSOGA transforms diverse observations into compact symbolic representations while preserving essential geometric and topological structure across sensing modalities and viewing directions.
  NeuSOGA provides an interpretable and explainable pathway from observation to symbol and establishes

</details>


### [118] [EdiTikZ: Scientific Figure Editing from Revision Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01409)
*Christian Greisinger,Zhixue Zhao,Steffen Eger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DaEdiTikZ introduces the first large-scale dataset of scientific figure edits mined from real revision histories, trains compact models that outperform or match state-of-the-art VLMs, and establishes benchmarks for scientific figure editing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific figure editing is crucial for creating publication-ready figures, but existing approaches are limited by costly proprietary systems, focus on evaluation rather than training, or rely on synthetic data. There's a need for scalable supervision from naturally occurring scientific revisions.

Method: 1) Construct DaEdiTikZ dataset by mining 391K TikZ edit pairs from arXiv, GitHub, and TeX SE, inferring 781K edit instructions using a VLM; 2) Create DaEdiTikZ-Bench benchmark (790 human-refined instances); 3) Train compact Qwen3.5-based models (4B and 9B) via joint reconstruction-editing learning followed by reinforcement learning with complementary rewards for rendered fidelity and edit application.

Result: 9B model outperforms all tested baselines in automatic evaluation. Human evaluation with 9 annotators and 4,320 ratings shows it surpasses GPT-5.6-Sol and matches Gemini-3.1-Pro. Maintains competitiveness with GPT-5.6-Sol under severe out-of-distribution shifts near its 2K training sequence length.

Conclusion: Natural scientific revision trajectories provide scalable supervision for training effective figure editing models. The approach demonstrates that compact models trained on mined real-world edits can compete with much larger proprietary systems, advancing the field of scientific figure generation and editing.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong performance in generating scientific figures from text or images. However, producing publication-ready figures requires iterative refinement, making scientific figure editing an important yet largely unexplored task. Existing approaches rely on costly proprietary agentic systems, focus primarily on evaluation, or construct training supervision from synthetically generated edits. Instead, we leverage naturally occurring scientific revision and development trajectories as a scalable source of supervision. To this end, we introduce DaEdiTikZ, the first large-scale dataset of revision-derived scientific figure edits, constructed by mining 391K plausible TikZ edit pairs from arXiv, GitHub, and TeX SE and inferring 781K directed edit instructions with a VLM conditioned on rendered figures and TikZ code. We further introduce DaEdiTikZ-Bench, a human-refined benchmark with 790 instances, and train two compact Qwen3.5-based EdiTikZ models (4B and 9B) by jointly learning reconstruction and editing, followed by reinforcement learning (RL) with complementary rewards for rendered fidelity and edit application. Automatic evaluation places our 9B model above all tested baselines, while human evaluation with 9 annotators and 4,320 ratings places it above GPT-5.6-Sol and on par with Gemini-3.1-Pro. Under severe out-of-distribution shifts, it remains competitive with GPT-5.6-Sol near its 2K training sequence-length regime. Models and datasets will be released.

</details>


### [119] [Parsing the Stream: A Live Trace Model for Long-Horizon Agents and Their Observers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01466)
*Egor Pakhomov,Erik Nijkamp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a "live trace model" for managing long-running agent executions by incrementally folding an append-only event ledger into typed run state, with compiled views for both human observers and the agent itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agents generate traces that grow beyond what both human observers can monitor effectively and what agents with bounded context can process. There's a need to manage these growing traces efficiently for both monitoring and agent operation.

Method: Developed a live trace model using an append-only event ledger that's incrementally folded into typed run state, compiled into per-consumer views. Evaluated with LLM readers for monitoring questions and on 120-link sequential-dependency tasks comparing various approaches.

Result: Compiled views reduced input tokens by 14-15x and costs by 5-7x compared to raw trace reading, with higher accuracy (0.85-0.87 vs 0.48). For agent tasks, state-maintaining mechanisms succeeded where full-context prompting failed (30/30 vs 8/30). Derived 11 candidate requirements for trace folding.

Conclusion: The live trace model provides efficient trace management for both monitoring and agent operation, with deterministic auditability being a key remaining value over cheaper alternatives. The approach works well until trace length exceeds a certain threshold, as demonstrated with an order-sensitive task family.

Abstract: A long-horizon agent's trace outgrows both of its consumers: the human observer monitoring the run, and the agent itself, whose bounded context the trace must be folded back into. We present a live trace model, an append-only event ledger folded incrementally into typed run state and compiled into per-consumer views, and evaluate it for both consumers against deterministic ground truth. For the observer side, evaluated with an LLM reader as proxy, the compiled view answers monitoring questions using approximately 14x and 15x fewer input tokens (by reader) and at 5-7x lower cost than a budget-capped single-call reading of the raw trace, with higher accuracy (0.85-0.87 versus 0.48). Because the questions were co-designed with the view schema, we treat the token and cost reduction, conditional on schema coverage, as the transferable result. For the agent, on 120-link sequential-dependency tasks, mechanisms that maintain the task's running statistic in per-step state succeed where full-context prompting fails (30/30 versus 8/30 under a clean protocol, n=30, labeled descriptive owing to benchmark-system co-development); a prompt-level scratchpad matches the fold's accuracy at lower cost, and a two-arm decomposition attributes the fold's accuracy to its deterministic aggregate and its cost advantage to its compactness. The fold's remaining value over cheaper alternatives is deterministic auditability and serving the observer from the same state. We derive eleven candidate requirements for trace folding from observed failures and delimit them with an order-sensitive task family on which the fold ceases to help. Code, benchmarks, a regenerable synthetic corpus, and all workbench traces are released.

</details>


### [120] [Harness-of-Harness: Multi-Day Autonomous Software Development with Continual Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01481)
*Haoyang Yan,Min-le Su,Hangfan Zhang,Zhanhao Li,Chen Zhang,Shao Zhang,Yang Chen,Lei Bai,Shuyue Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HoH (Harness-of-Harness) framework enables LLM-based coding agents to autonomously develop software through iterative planning-coding-testing loops, achieving significant performance improvements over standalone coding harnesses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing autonomous software development where LLM-based coding agents need to transform high-level requirements into complete, functional software systems without human intervention. Current coding agents lack mechanisms for continual improvement during development cycles.

Method: HoH framework operates on existing coding-agent harnesses by organizing executions into iterative planning-coding-testing loops. It balances repair with capability growth, scopes development into small verifiable increments, separates implementation testing from independent evaluation, constrains outputs rather than prescribing workflows, progressively exposes deliverables and tools, encourages reuse, and maintains versioned project histories.

Result: On three benchmarks (GameCraft-Bench, FrontierSWE, ProgramBench) with three harness-model pairs, HoH consistently outperforms standalone harnesses with average relative gain of 52.25% and maximum gain of 82.86% after three iterations. In a multi-day deployment with 70+ iterations, HoH autonomously developed a first-person-shooter game with coherent storyline, implemented core mechanics, playable experience, polished visuals, and integrated audio.

Conclusion: HoH successfully enables autonomous software development by providing a framework for continual improvement through iterative loops, demonstrating significant performance gains over existing approaches and showing practical viability through successful deployment in complex game development.

Abstract: This paper studies autonomous software development, in which LLM-based coding agents transform high-level requirements into complete, functional, and usable software systems without human intervention. We introduce Harness-of-Harness (HoH), a framework that enables coding agents to continually improve software during autonomous development. HoH operates on existing coding-agent harnesses, and organizes their executions into iterative planning-coding-testing loops. To sustain improvement across loops, HoH balances repair with capability growth, scopes development into small and verifiable increments, separates implementation-time testing from independent evaluation, and constrains verifiable outputs rather than prescribing agent workflows. It progressively exposes deliverables, role-specific tools, and skills, encourages reuse rather than recreation, and maintains versioned project histories. On GameCraft-Bench, FrontierSWE, and ProgramBench, three harness-model pairs (Codex with GPT-5.5, OpenCode with DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and Pi with MiniMax-M3), HoH consistently outperforms the corresponding standalone harnesses, achieving an average relative gain of 52.25 percent and a maximum gain of 82.86 percent after three iterations. In a multi-day deployment with more than 70 iterations, HoH autonomously develops a first-person-shooter game, featuring a coherent storyline, fully implemented core mechanics, human-playable experience, polished visuals and integrated audio. Github: https://github.com/Flesymeb/HarnessOfHarness Project Page: https://flesymeb.github.io/HarnessOfHarness/

</details>


### [121] [When Guardrails Look Effective: Construct Validity Failures in LLM Agent Commerce Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01519)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based economic simulations can produce misleading welfare estimates due to protocol artifacts and unverified agent behavior, requiring construct-validity checks before policy claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interactive simulations using language-model agents are increasingly used to evaluate economic policies, producing outputs like prices and welfare measures. However, these outputs may not reflect actual economic behavior, raising concerns about construct validity in agent-based market simulations.

Method: The authors audit this risk using a multi-turn buyer-seller testbed for hotel transactions. They implement controlled experiments comparing original welfare gain estimates (+87.4, +35.0, +28.8) with protocol-isolated results, fix agent offer schemas and choice procedures, run generation stability checks with bootstrap intervals, and implement seller-incentive controls including explicit profit-maximizing sellers.

Result: When holding offer schema and buyer chooser fixed, welfare gains changed dramatically (+7.2, -13.9, +23.8). Single-generation effects averaged +229 at 14B scale but reduced to +37.6 after stabilization. Generation residuals accounted for 49.9% of variation. A seller-incentive check showed non-monotonic behavior where increased profit pressure produced less profit than default prompts. Scripted controls revealed that guardrails mostly redistribute rather than create welfare.

Conclusion: The paper introduces a construct-validity contract requiring checks for incentive validity, protocol isolation, stochastic stability, and welfare accounting before substantive policy claims. The original estimate was INVALID under protocol isolation, while the controlled study remains INCONCLUSIVE due to incentive validity and stochastic stability concerns. The study doesn't prove guardrails ineffective but shows their value is unidentified until simulations pass validity checks.

Abstract: Interactive simulations increasingly evaluate policies in markets populated by language-model agents. Their outputs can look economic---prices, profits, consumer surplus, and welfare---without instantiating the behavior named in the claim. We audit this risk in a multi-turn buyer--seller testbed for configurable hotel transactions. An initial implementation reported welfare gains from two marketplace guardrails of +87.4, +35.0, and +28.8 across a Qwen2.5 1.5B--14B ladder. It also gave guarded and unguarded agents different offer schemas and choice procedures. Holding the schema and buyer chooser fixed changes the paired contrasts to +7.2, -13.9, and +23.8. The four largest 14B single-generation effects averaged +229; after three generations per profile-condition, they averaged +37.6 (95% bootstrap interval [-34.2, 109.3]), while generation residuals account for 49.9% of variation in this post-hoc probe. A seller-incentive check is non-monotone: increasing profit pressure produces less profit than the default seller prompt. Scripted positive controls show why this matters. A profit-maximizing seller already attains first-best welfare, so guardrails mostly redistribute and reduce welfare; they create welfare only when the seller is explicitly programmed to force inefficient bundles. We contribute a construct-validity contract separating incentive validity, protocol isolation, stochastic stability, and welfare accounting, and returning INVALID or INCONCLUSIVE before substantive policy claims. In our case, the original estimate is INVALID under protocol isolation, while the controlled study remains INCONCLUSIVE under incentive validity and stochastic stability. The case does not show that guardrails are ineffective; it shows their apparent value is unidentified until the simulated agents and protocol pass these checks.

</details>


### [122] [Can LLMs Discover Scientific Laws in Real and Parallel Worlds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01552)
*Yiming Huang,Ziche Liu,Zhuohang Wu,Yiqian Wang,Junxia Cui,Xinkai Zou,Linjun Mao,Nan Huang,Naicheng Yu,Kaijie Zhu,Yue Ma,Kun Zhou,Letian Peng,Jingbo Shang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new benchmark (SCILAWS-BENCH) for evaluating LLMs' ability to discover scientific laws, using real scientific data and papers with two complementary testing settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLMs for scientific discovery often use simplified synthetic settings or re-use published targets that may be familiar to models, making it unclear whether LLMs can genuinely discover scientific laws.

Method: Created a benchmark from 381 scientific papers with 118 problems, 291 candidate laws, and ~8M real data points across six disciplines. Each problem has two settings: SCILAWS-REAL (propose laws from fixed observations) and SCILAWS-PARALLEL (actively query residual-calibrated worlds to recover synthesized hidden laws).

Result: Found that predictive fit can diverge from scientific validity, memorization influences whether models reproduce or move beyond published formulas, and identified a selection bottleneck in best-of-N studies.

Conclusion: The paper provides a realistic, paper-grounded benchmark and new empirical perspectives for evaluating AI's capability for genuine scientific discovery beyond memorization.

Abstract: Scientific equation discovery has long been central to scientific progress, proceeding through iterative cycles of hypothesis generation, observational testing, and refinement under scientific constraints. As LLM capabilities advance and their role in AI for Science expands, it remains an open problem whether they can genuinely discover scientific laws and how this ability should be evaluated. Existing evaluations, however, often either simplify discovery through synthetic settings or reuse published targets that may already be familiar to LLMs. We therefore introduce SCILAWS-BENCH, a benchmark for scientific law discovery built from published research and real scientific data. It comprises 118 problems drawn from 381 scientific papers, covering 291 candidate laws and roughly 8M real data points across six scientific disciplines. Each problem is instantiated in two complementary settings: (1) SCILAWS-REAL asks models to propose laws from fixed real observations and evaluates held-out predictive fit and scientific validity derived from the source literature, and (2) SCILAWS-PARALLEL asks models to actively query residual-calibrated worlds and recover synthesized hidden laws derived from published forms. This two-setting task design preserves each problem's scientific context while separately evaluating fixed-record law discovery and active recovery of a newly synthesized hidden law. We find that predictive fit can diverge from scientific validity, memorization shapes whether models reproduce or move beyond published formulas, and our best-of-N study reveals a selection bottleneck. Our work provides a paper-grounded benchmark and new empirical perspectives for evaluating AI for scientific discovery. Project page: https://yiyihum.github.io/SciLaws-Bench

</details>


### [123] [EvoSCM: Scientific Belief Revision Through Causal Model Evolution and Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01526)
*Qing Zhao,Haowei Li,Weijian Deng,Pengxu Wei,Liang Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoSCM enables LLM-based scientific agents to evolve explicit structural causal models through experimental discovery loops, outperforming baselines in uncovering hidden physics dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents express scientific hypotheses as implicit free-form text, making beliefs difficult to test or revise. There's a need for explicit, testable causal models that can evolve with experimental evidence.

Method: EvoSCM maintains a population of competing Structural Causal Model hypotheses that evolve through a closed discovery loop: abducing latent mechanisms, designing discriminative interventions, making falsifiable predictions, testing through experimentation, and revising models based on discrepancies using correction rules.

Result: EvoSCM consistently improves scientific discovery over baselines on the DiscoverPhysics benchmark, yielding more accurate explanations and predictions while making more effective use of experimental interactions.

Conclusion: Explicit structural causal models that evolve through experimental evidence collection provide a more robust framework for scientific discovery by LLM agents, enabling systematic hypothesis testing and revision.

Abstract: Scientific agents must learn not only how to reason, but also what to believe. However, existing LLM agents typically express scientific hypotheses in free-form text, leaving their beliefs implicit and difficult to test or revise. We introduce EvoSCM, which equips scientific agents with explicit structural causal models that evolve as new experimental evidence is collected. EvoSCM maintains a population of competing SCM hypotheses, each encoding a candidate causal explanation of the environment, and evolves them through a closed discovery loop. In each round, the agent abduces latent mechanisms from accumulated evidence, designs discriminative interventions, and commits to falsifiable predictions that it tests through experimentation. Discrepancies between prediction and observation are inductively distilled into correction rules that revise the causal structures and mechanisms of each hypothesis, and the agent then deductively validates the revised population against accumulated evidence and structural consistency to guide the next round. We evaluate EvoSCM on DiscoverPhysics, a benchmark requiring agents to uncover the hidden dynamics of noncanonical physical worlds through experimentation. EvoSCM consistently improves scientific discovery over baselines, yielding more accurate explanations and predictions while making more effective use of experimental interactions.

</details>


### [124] [Selective Agent Guidance via Entropy: Learning Autonomous Policies from Imperfect VLM Teachers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01567)
*Matteo Merler,Giovanni Bonetta,Davide Zago,Rossella Cancelliere,Bernardo Magnini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a framework that learns cheap autonomous policies by selectively querying expensive Vision-Language Models only when the learner is uncertain, distilling their guidance into lightweight RL policies through selective weighting based on advantage estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs are expensive and brittle as direct policies - they require per-step queries, don't improve from interaction, and repeat systematic errors. There's a need to leverage VLMs' useful priors while overcoming their limitations for efficient autonomous decision-making.

Method: SAGE (Selective Agent Guidance via Entropy) queries VLMs only when learner uncertainty is high, executes suggested actions during training, and distills guidance into lightweight RL policies. It weights teacher-action distillation using environment-derived advantages rather than treating all VLM suggestions equally.

Result: SAGE learns policies that act without VLM guidance at test time, improves over unguided RL in sparse-reward visual reasoning/navigation tasks, sometimes exceeding VLM teacher performance. Selective guidance works best when VLMs help discover high-reward trajectories, reduces VLM usage (fraction of training steps), and requires no VLM calls at deployment.

Conclusion: VLMs don't need to be fixed policies; they can serve as temporary, imperfect guidance sources whose value is tested and internalized through interaction. Selective guidance via entropy and advantage-weighted distillation enables efficient learning of autonomous policies from expensive VLM teachers.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) provide useful priors for interactive decision-making, but using them directly as policies is expensive and brittle: they must be queried at every step, do not improve from environment interaction, and can repeat systematic errors. We study how to learn a cheap autonomous policy from an online, expensive, and imperfect but informative VLM teacher. We propose SAGE (Selective Agent Guidance via Entropy), a framework that queries a VLM only when the learner is uncertain, executes the suggested action during training, and distills guidance into a lightweight Reinforcement Learning (RL) policy. Because VLM advice is not always reliable, SAGE can weight teacher-action distillation using environment-derived advantages rather than treating all suggestions as equally useful. Across sparse-reward visual reasoning and navigation tasks, SAGE learns policies that act without VLM guidance at evaluation time and improves over unguided RL in several environments, including settings where the learned policy exceeds its VLM teacher. The results show that selective guidance is most beneficial when the VLM can help the agent discover high-reward trajectories, and less useful when unguided exploration already succeeds or teacher actions do not lead to informative experience. SAGE also reduces VLM usage by prompting the teacher only on a fraction of training steps and requiring no VLM calls at deployment. Overall, our results suggest that VLMs don't need to be used as fixed policies to be useful; they can instead act as temporary, imperfect sources of guidance whose value is tested and internalized through interaction.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [125] [Task-Specific Prompt with Global Context for Multi-Task Graph Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00047)
*Zhiyang Qiu,Yangtao Wang,Xiaocui Li,Yanzhao Xie,Siyuan Chen,Wensheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TPGC is a dual-prior prompt initialization method for graph pre-training that improves task relevance and structural awareness by injecting task and structure priors into prompt initialization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-task graph pre-training frameworks use randomly initialized prompts, which leads to poor alignment between prompt space, pretext objectives, and graph structural characteristics. This results in weak task relevance, structural awareness, and transferability of prompt representations.

Method: TPGC proposes a dual-prior prompt initialization solution with two modules: 1) Task-Prior Injection Module performs short homologous multi-task pre-training on an auxiliary graph to inherit optimization preferences from multiple pretext tasks; 2) Structure-Prior Injection Module extracts transferable global structural context from the auxiliary graph and converts it into layer-wise prompt vectors by aggregating structurally informative node embeddings.

Result: Extensive experiments on 6 mainstream benchmarks for node and graph classification show TPGC achieves consistently better performance under few-shot settings than state-of-the-art baselines, with fewer downstream tunable parameters and lower runtime.

Conclusion: TPGC successfully addresses the challenge of poor prompt initialization in graph pre-training by explicitly modeling the synergy between task prior and structural prior, leading to more effective transfer learning in low-resource scenarios.

Abstract: Graph prompt learning is an effective paradigm to adapt pre-trained graph models to downstream tasks in low-resource scenarios. However, existing multi-task graph pre-training frameworks generally use randomly initialized prompts, leading to poor alignment between the prompt space, pretext objectives and graph structural characteristics. This greatly weakens the task relevance, structural awareness and transferability of prompt representations. To address this challenge, we propose TPGC, a dual-prior prompt initialization solution that explicitly models the synergy between task prior and structural prior. Specifically, the Task-Prior Injection Module first conducts a short homologous multi-task pre-training on an auxiliary graph, enabling prompt initialization to inherit optimization preferences associated with multiple pretext tasks.
