Built on the task-aware representations, the Structure-Prior Injection Module further extracts transferable global structural context from the auxiliary graph, converting it into layer-wise prompt vectors by aggregating structurally informative node embeddings. Extensive experiments on 6 mainstream benchmarks covering node and graph classification show that TPGC achieves consistently better performance under few-shot settings than state-of-the-art baselines, with fewer downstream tunable parameters and lower runtime. The code is available at https://github.com/Virgilqiu/TPGC

</details>


### [126] [REAL-Q: E2E LLM Quantization via Dynamic Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00049)
*Qian Zhang,Yaoming Li,Zhewen Tan,Yanshu Wang,Heng Lu,Kun Su,Zongwei Lv,Wenhan Yu,Yongge Ma,Yinjun Han,Ruikuang Liu,Tong Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REAL-Q introduces a novel post-training quantization method that uses real-time, fine-grained gradient descent with a sliding window mechanism to better align with end-to-end global loss, reducing quantization errors in large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current PTQ methods for LLMs heavily approximate global loss and use frozen Hessian matrices throughout layers, leading to information misalignment as the loss landscape shifts during quantization - this compromises quantization quality.

Method: REAL-Q targets end-to-end-aligned surrogate of global loss using fine-grained, dynamic Block-wise Gradient Descent applied after every column block (128 columns), coupled with a sliding window mechanism for smooth cross-layer transitions.

Result: On LLaMA-3.1 (8B and 70B) and Qwen3 (0.6B-32B) at W4A16, REAL-Q reduces end-to-end KL divergence by up to ~49% relative to state-of-the-art globally-guided methods.

Conclusion: REAL-Q offers a superior PTQ paradigm that avoids compromising on analytic tractability, enabling better quantization quality for LLMs under resource constraints through real-time loss alignment and error propagation mitigation.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is essential for deploying large language models (LLMs) under strict resource constraints. State-of-the-art PTQ methods quantize each layer with a single closed-form second-order solver: to remain analytically tractable, they heavily approximate the global loss (dropping cross-channel coupling, pooling output rows into groups), and they then freeze the resulting Hessian across the entire layer, with no way to refresh it as the loss landscape shifts column by column--a phenomenon we call information misalignment. We propose REAL-Q (Real-time E2E-loss Aligned LLM Quantization), a novel PTQ paradigm that breaks this compromise: instead of diluting the objective for the sake of analytic tractability, REAL-Q targets an end-to-end-aligned surrogate of the global loss and refines it via fine-grained, dynamic Block-wise Gradient Descent applied after every column block (128 columns). By coupling this fine-grained correction with a sliding window mechanism for smooth cross-layer transitions, REAL-Q effectively mitigates error propagation across the network. On LLaMA-3.1 (8B and 70B) and Qwen3 (0.6B-32B) at W4A16, REAL-Q reduces end-to-end KL divergence by up to ~49% relative to state-of-the-art globally-guided methods.

</details>


### [127] [Convergence issues in Relational Concept Analysis based on AOC-posets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00054)
*Xavier Dolques,Agnès Braud,Alain Gutierrez,Marianne Huchard,Florence Le Ber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Relational Concept Analysis (RCA) based on AOC-posets loses convergence guarantees compared to lattice-based RCA. The paper investigates this issue, identifies conditions for convergence, and proposes a convergent variant that preserves AOC-poset structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RCA extends Formal Concept Analysis to multi-relational data, producing richer results but with higher complexity. While AOC-posets are preferred over full concept lattices for computational and interpretative reasons, RCA based on AOC-posets loses the convergence guarantees that exist in the lattice-based setting.

Method: The authors investigate why convergence is lost in AOC-poset based RCA, identify conditions under which convergence can still be ensured, discuss dataset transformations to recover convergence, and propose a new convergent variant of the RCA process that preserves AOC-poset structure by never removing relational attributes once created.

Result: The paper shows that convergence in RCA with AOC-posets is not guaranteed in the general case, identifies specific conditions that can ensure convergence, provides methods to transform datasets to achieve convergence, and proposes a new convergent variant of the RCA process that maintains the AOC-poset structure.

Conclusion: While AOC-poset based RCA loses the convergence guarantees of lattice-based RCA, the paper provides theoretical insights into this issue, practical solutions through dataset transformations, and a new convergent algorithm that preserves the AOC-poset structure, making RCA more reliable and practical for applications using AOC-posets.

Abstract: Formal Concept Analysis (FCA) is an approach for conceptual classification building and rule discovery from a binary table describing a set of objects by a set of attributes. Extensions have been proposed to deal with non-binary and more complex data, such as Relational Concept Analysis (RCA) for multi-relational data. RCA aims to highlight groups of objects characterized by their relationships with other groups of objects. The richer and more complex nature of the underlying data allows RCA to produce richer results than FCA, at the expense of higher computational and interpretive complexity. The most commonly used conceptual classification structure in FCA is the concept lattice. However, in many applications, concept lattice substructures, such as AOC-posets, are preferred over the full lattice, either to mitigate combinatorial blow-up or to focus on the most informative parts of the structure. Indeed, in an AOC-poset, only concepts introducing an object or an attribute are represented, which makes AOC-posets smaller and easier to compute and use than concept lattices. Although RCA was originally defined on concept lattices, it can also be instantiated on AOC-posets. RCA is iterative and its convergence is guaranteed in the lattice-based setting, but this guarantee is lost when using AOC-posets. In this paper, we investigate this loss of convergence in detail. We show why convergence is no longer guaranteed in the general case, identify conditions under which it can still be ensured, and discuss how a dataset can be transformed to recover convergence. We also propose a convergent variant of the process, which preserves the AOC-poset structure: relational attributes, once created, are never removed, which guarantees convergence at the price of attributes that may refer to concepts absent from the final structures.

</details>


### [128] [DISTAL: Distillation and Self-Supervised Pretraining for Structure-Agnostic Materials Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00059)
*Weiran Wang,Xintong Huo,Yueying Wang,Yusi Fan,Wenyan Wang,Xin Feng,Ruihao Xin,Lan Huang,Kewei Li,Fengfeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DISTAL: A dual-prior framework for structure-agnostic materials property prediction that combines compositional pretraining with structure-aware knowledge distillation to achieve robust prediction in low-data settings without requiring structural inputs at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Materials property prediction is difficult in low-data settings where many properties have limited labeled samples. Models with best accuracy often require crystal structures, which limits early-stage screening when structural information is unavailable or limited.

Method: DISTAL uses a dual-prior framework combining: 1) Self-supervised compositional pretraining on a large virtual composition space using 145 composition-derived descriptors, 2) Knowledge distillation from a pretrained ALIGNN (structure-aware) teacher into a composition-conditioned student, allowing structural priors during training without structural inputs at inference, 3) Integration of explicit compositional descriptors, pretrained latent features, and distilled structural features in a unified prediction pipeline.

Result: Across 39 benchmark tasks, the best multimodal configuration combining all three signals improves over reference benchmarks on 37 tasks. DISTAL achieves strongest overall performance among all evaluated feature combinations. The framework demonstrates complementary priors from compositional pretraining and structural distillation.

Conclusion: Compositional pretraining and structural distillation provide complementary priors that offer a practical route to robust composition-only prediction in small-data materials informatics. DISTAL enables accurate property prediction without structural inputs at inference time, addressing key challenges in early-stage materials screening.

Abstract: Materials property prediction remains difficult in low-data settings, where many target properties are supported by only a limited number of labeled samples. Models with the strongest predictive accuracy often depend on crystal structures, which restricts their use in early-stage screening when structural information is limited or unavailable. To address this challenge, we propose DISTAL, a dual-prior framework for structure-agnostic materials property prediction that combines self-supervised compositional pretraining with structure-aware knowledge distillation. DISTAL first learns transferable compositional representations from a large virtual composition space using 145 composition-derived descriptors. It then distills structural knowledge from a pretrained ALIGNN teacher into a composition-conditioned student. This setting allows structural priors to be used during training without requiring structural inputs at inference. By integrating explicit compositional descriptors, pretrained latent features, and distilled structural features within a unified prediction pipeline, DISTAL captures complementary signals that are difficult to recover from any single representation alone. Across 39 benchmark tasks, the best-performing multimodal configuration combines all three signals, and improves over the reference benchmark on 37 tasks. DISTAL achieves the strongest overall performance among all evaluated feature combinations. These results indicate that compositional pretraining and structural distillation provide complementary priors and offer a practical route to robust composition-only prediction in small-data materials informatics. The source code and the pre-trained models are anonymously available at: https://osf.io/eq96d/overview?view_only=451617f42f7849e08750bd1852b48980 and will be released at the official link after acceptance.

</details>


### [129] [ReNFT: Repairing Mode Collapse in Reward Post-Training via Internal Probability-Mass Recalibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00061)
*Yuchen Bao,Chao Wen,Haowei Wang,Ruoxin Chen,Donghao Luo,Jiahui Zhan,Wenjian Huang,Shen Chen,Yiting Wang,Taiping Yao,Chengjie Wang,Shouhong Ding,Jianguo Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReNFT repairs collapsed diffusion model adapters by recalibrating internal probability mass, restoring diversity while preserving reward gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward post-training of diffusion generators causes mode collapse (concentration on few reward-favored modes) that erases within-prompt diversity. Existing methods require external signals and cannot repair already collapsed adapters while preserving acquired reward.

Method: ReNFT uses unconditional probes to identify "anti-hub" prompts where prompt-independent bias is easiest to expose. Two policy-dominated mixed routes generate matched counterfactuals: one probes frozen base for suppressed alternatives, another exposes post-trained unconditional tendency. Reward ranking with adaptive flipping guard assigns pull/push roles, and joint-and-paired NFT update realizes the repair.

Result: On PickScore and GenEval, ReNFT retains 98.9% and 99.0% of NFT's reward while improving DreamSim-Div by 58.8% and 55.0% respectively, offering complementary alternative to external interventions.

Conclusion: Mode collapse in reward-post-trained diffusion generators is suppression rather than deletion of capabilities, and can be reversed through internal probability-mass recalibration without external signals, preserving reward while restoring diversity.

Abstract: Reward post-training of diffusion generators inevitably concentrates probability mass on a few reward-favored modes, a mode collapse that erases within-prompt diversity. Existing methods for mitigating collapse rely on external signals or interfaces, augmenting the reward with perceptual objectives, adjusting reference regularization, or modifying the text encoder, but none repairs an adapter that has already collapsed while preserving the acquired reward. We observe that online post-training primarily reallocates probability mass over capabilities inherited from pretraining rather than learning new visual content. Collapse is therefore suppression, not deletion, and can be reversed from within the generator. We propose ReNFT, which repairs a high-reward, low-diversity adapter through internal probability-mass recalibration. Unconditional probes first prioritize "anti-hub" prompts where the prompt-independent bias is easiest to expose. Two policy-dominated mixed routes then generate matched counterfactual proposals from the same prompt and initial noise, one probing the frozen base direction for suppressed alternatives and the other exposing the post-trained unconditional tendency. Reward ranking with an adaptive flipping guard assigns pull and push roles, and a joint-and-paired NFT update realizes the repair. On PickScore and GenEval, ReNFT retains 98.9% and 99.0% of NFT's reward while improving DreamSim-Div by 58.8% and 55.0%, respectively, offering a complementary alternative to external interventions.

</details>


### [130] [Attention Sensitivity Is Not Enough: Dissociating Attention-Level and Behavioural In-Context Learning under Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00064)
*Jinyuan Zhang,Peng He,He Hu,Yin Yuan,ShengShuo Jiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether attention-based metrics for in-context learning (ICL) can be trusted when optimized, finding that maximizing attention sensitivity (ICS) doesn't improve behavioral ICL performance and can even degrade task accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ICL diagnostics use attention changes as a proxy for context sensitivity, but it's unclear if optimizing these attention metrics actually improves real behavioral performance or could lead to Goodhart's law where the metric stops measuring what it should.

Method: The authors formalize In-Context Sensitivity (ICS) as attention distance between matched/mismatched demonstrations and pair it with ICL-GAP (behavioral accuracy gap). They conduct controlled ablation studies on Llama-2-7B using ICS-maximizing regularizers and compare attention changes to actual behavioral performance on tasks like MMLU.

Result: Optimizing ICS drove attention sensitivity close to its geometric ceiling (1.413), but ICL-GAP remained near zero and MMLU accuracy actually decreased from 0.371 to 0.279. Attention became sharp and disjoint across prefixes but routed to formatting tokens rather than meaningful labels, creating a dissociation between the proxy and real performance.

Conclusion: Attention-based ICL proxies should not be used as training targets without validation against actual behavioral gaps. The paper shows attention metrics can be optimized independently of meaningful ICL performance, highlighting the need for behavior-grounded validation in model training and evaluation.

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) lets large language models adapt to new tasks from demonstrations, and fine-tuning can erode this behaviour. Many preservation diagnostics inspect attention: if attention changes when demonstrations change, the model is treated as context-sensitive. This paper asks how far that proxy can be trusted once it is optimised. We formalise \emph{In-Context Sensitivity} (ICS), the average row distance between last-token attention on matched and mismatched demonstration prefixes, and pair it with \emph{ICL-GAP}, the behavioural accuracy gap between the same prefixes. In a controlled four-arm ablation on Llama-2-7B, an ICS-maximising regulariser ($\armKL$) drives ICS to $1.413$, within $0.5\%$ of its geometric ceiling. The behavioural readout tells a different story: ICL-GAP stays near zero and MMLU accuracy moves from $0.371$ to $0.279$, a Goodhart dissociation of the bounded attention proxy. Endpoint statistics locate the mechanism: attention grows sharp and near-disjoint across prefixes yet routes to formatting and demonstration-body tokens rather than labels. A random-label protocol confirms that the behavioural probe family retains dynamic range at the same checkpoints. In a constructive sweep, behaviour gating partially mitigates the effect, while objectives anchored to pretrained computation hold the high-MMLU, moderate-ICS region that divergence maximisers leave. The main lesson is diagnostic: attention-level ICL proxies earn their place as training targets only after validation against behavioural gaps.

</details>


### [131] [RW-LoRA: Communication-Efficient Decentralized LoRA Fine-Tuning via Random Walks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00078)
*Xingran Chen,Rohit Bhagat,Ghadir Ayache,Rawad Bitar,Yanmin Gong,Salim El Rouayheb*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A random-walk-based LoRA fine-tuning method where a single model token traverses the network sequentially, eliminating global synchronization and reducing communication/computation costs while avoiding aggregation errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing distributed LoRA methods face significant challenges: centralized aggregation requires heavy communication overhead, gossip-based decentralized LoRA needs repeated synchronization among multiple model copies, and both methods introduce aggregation errors from simultaneous model updates.

Method: Proposes a random-walk-based LoRA fine-tuning scheme where instead of maintaining multiple model replicas, a single model token traverses the network and is updated sequentially using local fine-tuning objectives, eliminating global synchronization requirements.

Result: The method provides rigorous convergence guarantees for non-convex objectives under standard assumptions, achieves competitive task performance on multiple NLP tasks and graph topologies, and demonstrates substantially less communication and computation than gossip-based LoRA.

Conclusion: The random-walk-based approach offers an efficient alternative to traditional distributed LoRA methods by eliminating synchronization overhead, reducing communication/computation costs, and avoiding aggregation errors while maintaining competitive performance.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as LoRA have become a standard approach for adapting large foundation models. Adopting fine-tuning to distributed settings faces several challenges. Most existing distributed LoRA methods rely on centralized aggregation, and gossip-based decentralized LoRA requires repeated synchronization among multiple model copies. Both methods incur significant communication overhead and introduce errors due to simultaneous aggregation of multiple model updates. In this paper, we take a different perspective and propose a random-walk-based LoRA fine-tuning scheme. Instead of maintaining multiple model replicas, a single model token traverses the network and is updated sequentially using local fine-tuning objectives. This design eliminates the need for global synchronization, substantially reduces communication and computation costs, and avoids aggregation errors. We provide rigorous convergence guarantees for non-convex objectives under standard assumptions. Through empirical results on multiple NLP tasks and graph topologies, we show that the proposed method achieves competitive task performance with substantially less communication and computation than gossip-based LoRA.

</details>


### [132] [Stochastic complexity of vectors containing cluster structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00084)
*Daniel Nicorici,Olli Yli-Harja,Jaakko Astola*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient linear-time recursive formula for computing the stochastic probability of encoded vectors with cluster structure using NML models, improving from previous polynomial-time methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need for efficient computation of shortest code length for vectors with cluster structure using Normalized Maximum Likelihood models, which is important for MDL-based data clustering tasks like estimating optimal cluster count and structure.

Method: The authors introduce a novel recursion formula for efficient computation of the normalizing constant from the NML model, enabling linear-time complexity instead of previous polynomial-time approaches.

Result: The proposed method achieves linear time complexity with respect to vector size and number of clusters, proving the problem is tractable and significantly outperforming previous polynomial-time approaches.

Conclusion: The paper successfully demonstrates that computing the shortest code length for vectors with cluster structure using NML models is tractable through an efficient linear-time recursive algorithm, making MDL-based clustering more practical.

Abstract: This paper studies the problem of computing the stochastic probability (shortest code length) of the encoded vectors containing cluster structure using Normalized Maximum Likelihood (NML) model. This is of great theoretical and practical importance in data clustering based on Minimum Description Length (MDL) principle, such as for estimating the best number of clusters and best cluster structure for the data. Straightforward computation of the shortest code length of the vector containing cluster structure based on the NML model requires polynomial time with respect to the size of the vector and number of clusters. We show that this is a tractable problem by introducing a recursion formula for the efficient computation of normalizing constant from the NML model. The time complexity of the new formula is linear opposed to previous polynomial time with respect to the size of the vector and number of clusters.

</details>


### [133] [Foundation models for electricity price forecasting and battery arbitrage: Can they replace market-specific forecasting models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00089)
*Arkadiusz Lipiecki,Rafał Weron*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Foundation models show potential for electricity price forecasting, but only TabPFN consistently outperforms specialized benchmarks across multiple European markets, though economic performance depends on risk tolerance and decision context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses whether foundation models can replace specialized electricity price forecasting models, given their promise of accurate forecasts with minimal task-specific training. The motivation is to empirically test this proposition across real-world electricity markets.

Method: The study compares nine variants from five foundation model families in zero-shot mode against two state-of-the-art electricity price forecasting benchmarks. Evaluation covers Germany, Poland, and Spain over 2021-2025, assessing point and probabilistic forecasting accuracy plus economic value in battery energy storage arbitrage scenarios.

Result: Only TabPFN models consistently and significantly outperform benchmarks across all three markets and statistical measures. However, this statistical dominance doesn't directly translate to economic dominance: TabPFN performs best with unlimited bids and riskier quantile-based strategies, while Distributional Deep Neural Network benchmark is more profitable with lower risk tolerance.

Conclusion: Foundation models cannot universally replace market-specific electricity price forecasting models. Their value depends on both model architecture and the specific decision problem, particularly risk tolerance levels in economic applications like battery storage arbitrage.

Abstract: Foundation models promise accurate forecasts with little or no task-specific training, but whether they can replace models designed specifically for electricity price forecasting remains unclear. We compare nine variants from five foundation model families, evaluated in zero-shot mode, with two state-of-the-art electricity price forecasting benchmarks in Germany, Poland, and Spain over 2021-2025. Their performance is assessed in terms of point and probabilistic forecasting accuracy, as well as economic value in battery energy storage arbitrage. Only the TabPFN models consistently and significantly outperform the benchmarks across all three markets and all statistical measures. However, this statistical dominance does not translate directly into economic dominance: TabPFN performs best under unlimited bids and riskier quantile-based strategies, whereas the Distributional Deep Neural Network benchmark is more profitable when risk tolerance is lower. Thus, foundation models cannot universally replace market-specific models, and their value depends on both model architecture and the decision problem.

</details>


### [134] [Assessing Alignment and Stability of Feature Importance Explanations via Weight of Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00090)
*Eddie Conti,Claudio Daka,Álvaro Parafita,Antonio L. Alfeo,Axel Brando,Mario G. C. A. Cimino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes embedding feature importance methods (FIMs) in a hypothesis-testing framework using Weight of Evidence (WoE) to evaluate how strongly observed evidence supports hypotheses about feature importance, enabling principled assessment of FIMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current feature importance methods provide attribution scores but offer limited insight into the underlying reasoning process and lack principled evaluation frameworks to assess their alignment with prior knowledge or measure their variability.

Method: Embed feature importance methods within a hypothesis-testing framework based on Weight of Evidence (WoE), quantifying how strongly observed evidence supports hypotheses about feature importance. Reference hypotheses can come from domain knowledge, ground truth, or the FIM itself. Theoretical results link WoE to attribution variance.

Result: Empirical results demonstrate applicability and flexibility of the framework using LIME and SHAP explanations in settings with different reference hypotheses, showing it captures both alignment with prior knowledge and variability of FIMs.

Conclusion: The Weight of Evidence framework offers a complementary, contrastive, evidence-based tool for assessing feature importance methods, providing principled evaluation beyond simple attribution scores.

Abstract: Feature importance Methods (FIMs) are widely used in Explainable AI to interpret model predictions, yet attribution scores alone often provide limited insight into the underlying reasoning process. In this work, we introduce a novel perspective by embedding FIMs within a hypothesis-testing framework based on Weight of Evidence (WoE). We quantify how strongly the observed evidence supports any given hypothesis on feature importance. The reference hypothesis can stem from domain knowledge, ground truth, or be derived from the FIM itself. This formulation enables a principled evaluation of FIMs, capturing both their alignment with prior knowledge and their variability. We further provide theoretical results linking WoE to attribution variance. Empirical results shows the applicability and flexibility of our strategy analyzing LIME and SHAP explanations in settings with different reference hypotheses. Overall, our framework offers a complementary tool for assessing FIMs through a contrastive, evidence-based lens.

</details>


### [135] [Safin-1: Safety from Within through Memory-Native State Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00092)
*Ming Zhang,Kaisen Yang,Shu Yu,Ermo Hua,Zhekai Chen,Cheng Jin,Jingnan Zheng,Yi Zhang,Zhongtian Ma,Jiawei Zhou,Sirui Chen,Qiaosheng Zhang,Xiang Wang,Ning Ding,Xia Hu,Bowen Zhou,Youbang Sun,Chaochao Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Safin-1 is a foundation model family that implements "Safety from Within" through memory routing and state evolution, enabling safety capabilities to be natively represented within the model's computation rather than relying on external safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches to safety rely on external safeguards or post-hoc alignment techniques like supervised fine-tuning, which treat safety as a behavioral constraint rather than an intrinsic model property. The authors propose that safety should be built into the model's native computation to better handle long-horizon complex tasks that require information accumulation and adaptation over extended interactions.

Method: The authors developed Safin-1 based on Memory-Anchor Routing across Context History (MARCH) architecture, which maintains structured memory states and selectively retrieves relevant historical information through content-conditioned routing. This enables test-time adaptation of persistent capability states without repeatedly modifying the backbone model.

Result: Safin-1 demonstrates effective state-based adaptation on downstream safety tasks through a dedicated Safety State, showing substantial safety improvements. The model also performs well across general capabilities, long-context understanding, retrieval, and efficiency benchmarks.

Conclusion: The memory routing and state evolution approach reframes memory from a passive record into an active substrate for maintaining and evolving model behavior, providing a path toward safety as a state-native and adaptively maintainable capability. However, this work is only an initial architectural exploration and substantial further development is needed.

Abstract: Long-horizon complex tasks require foundation models to accumulate information, maintain internal states, and adapt over extended interactions. Safety should be an intrinsic property of the model itself, rather than a behavioral constraint relying solely on external safeguards or post-hoc alignment such as supervised fine-tuning. This motivates Safety from Within, where safety-relevant capabilities are represented and invoked through the model's native computation. We present Safin-1, a family of foundation models realizing this principle through memory routing and state evolution. Safin-1 is built on Memory-Anchor Routing across Context History (MARCH), a network architecture that maintains structured memory states and selectively retrieves relevant historical information through content-conditioned routing. It supports test-time adaptation of persistent capability states without repeatedly modifying the backbone, enabling controlled specialization over a shared foundation. We investigate this interface on downstream safety tasks through a Safety State, demonstrating effective state-based adaptation with substantial safety improvements. More broadly, the routed-state interface unifies contextual memory and persistent capability adaptation within the model's native computation, reframing memory from a passive record of prior context into an active substrate for maintaining and evolving model behavior. Evaluations across general capabilities, long-context understanding, retrieval, and efficiency further validate Safin-1. These findings provide a path toward safety as a state-native and adaptively maintainable capability. This work is only an initial architectural exploration of Safety from Within, and substantial further work is needed to realize this broader vision.

</details>


### [136] [Local Reference Geometry Residual Augmentation for Imbalanced Time Series Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00093)
*Chuanhang Qiu,Yanran Xu,Yue Wang,Anthony Bagnall*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LRG (Local Reference Geometry) addresses representation-level issues in imbalanced time series classification by measuring and repairing local geometry failures around minority classes in feature space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for imbalanced time series classification focus on training distribution, objectives, logits, or thresholds but fail to address representation-level issues. The paper identifies that minority cases often lie in sparse, rest-dominated, or mixed feature-space neighborhoods, creating local geometry failures even when global class structure is preserved.

Method: Proposes Local Reference Geometry (LRG) - a lightweight post-hoc feature augmentation module applied between a fixed feature extractor and classifier head. Using only training features, LRG measures local exposure and class-mixture risk, then augments each fixed feature with: 1) standardized signed displacement from nearby training geometry, and 2) LDA-projected residual summary.

Result: LRG shows gains on UCR/Bake Off Redux imbalance benchmarks for learned, pretrained, and fixed representations. Gains persist when combined with training-level interventions and post-encoder classifier corrections. Ablations confirm gains come specifically from signed local residual, not from generic prototype distances or other alternatives. Analysis supports the local-geometry failure hypothesis.

Conclusion: The paper successfully identifies and addresses local geometry failures in imbalanced time series classification. LRG provides an effective, lightweight solution that works with various representation types and complements existing imbalance-handling techniques, focusing on repairing feature-space neighborhood issues rather than just adjusting training procedures or decision boundaries.

Abstract: Imbalanced time series classification is often addressed by changing the training distribution, objective, logits, or final threshold. These interventions address important biases, yet leave a representation-level question unmeasured: after minority support is reduced, does a learned feature space remain locally reliable around minority regions? We identify a training-local geometry failure: under imbalance, minority cases can lie in sparse, rest-dominated, or mixed feature-space neighborhoods, even when the representation retains useful global class structure. To diagnose and repair this failure, we propose Local Reference Geometry (LRG), a lightweight post-hoc feature augmentation module applied between a fixed feature extractor and the classifier head. Using training features only, LRG measures local exposure and class-mixture risk, then augments each fixed feature with a standardized signed displacement from nearby training geometry and an LDA-projected residual summary. On controlled UCR/Bake Off Redux imbalance benchmarks, paired raw-versus-LRG comparisons show gains for learned, pretrained, and fixed representations, including when LRG is combined with training-level interventions and post-encoder classifier corrections. Ablations show that the gain comes from the signed local residual appended to the original feature, rather than from generic prototype distances, affinity features, scalar statistics, or VLAD-style codes. Further analyses support the proposed local-geometry failure hypothesis: minority neighborhoods become increasingly rest-exposed under imbalance, training-local risk identifies error-prone regions, and LRG gains concentrate in those high-risk regions.

</details>


### [137] [Faster Than Flash: Exploiting Attention Sparsity for Efficient Long-Context Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00097)
*Zhigeng Liu,Zhiyuan Ning,Ruixiao Li,Xiaoran Liu,Yuerong Song,Min Zhang,Ziwei He,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FFD is a hardware-algorithm co-design framework that breaks memory bandwidth bottlenecks in long-context LLM decoding through fused kernels, content-aware scanning with quantization, and dynamic sparsity filtering, achieving significant speedups while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLMs face two major bottlenecks: 1) memory bandwidth limitations during decoding, and 2) quadratic complexity of attention mechanism. Current approaches suffer from trade-offs between memory overhead of metadata-based metrics and computational inefficiency of adaptive selection strategies.

Method: FFD integrates selector and computer into a fully fused kernel, replaces external metadata indices with content-aware scanning via low-bit quantization, and introduces top-delta strategy for dynamic block filtering to achieve distribution-adaptive sparsity without global synchronization. It's training-free and plug-and-play.

Result: Achieves up to 11.6x kernel-level speedup, scales to 256K context length, provides 2.37x end-to-end throughput improvement, maintains model accuracy while delivering high-ratio sparsity, validated on RULER and LongBench benchmarks.

Conclusion: FFD successfully addresses memory bandwidth bottlenecks in long-context LLM decoding through an innovative hardware-algorithm co-design that eliminates traditional trade-offs, offering substantial performance improvements while preserving accuracy, making it a practical solution for scaling LLMs to longer contexts.

Abstract: The development of long-context Large Language Models (LLMs) is constrained by the memory bandwidth bottleneck and quadratic complexity of the attention mechanism during decoding. To overcome the inherent trade-offs between the memory overhead of metadata-based metrics and the computational inefficiency of adaptive selection strategies, we present Faster Flash Decoding (FFD), a novel hardware-algorithm co-design framework designed to break the memory wall in long-context decoding. FFD integrates the selector and computer into a fully fused kernel, replacing external metadata indices with content-aware scanning via low-bit quantization. Furthermore, we introduce the top-delta strategy, which dynamically filters blocks to achieve distribution-adaptive sparsity without global synchronization. Offering a training-free and plug-and-play solution, FFD also enables the reuse of scanning results for computation, achieving up to 11.6x kernel-level speedup and scaling to 256K context length, with 2.37x end-to-end throughput improvement. Empirical validation on RULER and LongBench confirms that FFD maintains model accuracy while delivering high-ratio sparsity, with code available at https://github.com/qluoluo/faster-flash-decoding

</details>


### [138] [Generative artificial intelligence for reliable mechanistic reasoning for corrosion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00099)
*Bharath M N,R K Singh Raman,Alankar Alankar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A retrieval-augmented generation framework with domain adaptation is developed for corrosion knowledge synthesis, combining fine-tuned language models with hybrid retrieval and introducing Reason Map for systematic mechanistic reasoning validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Corrosion causes massive economic losses (4% global GDP), requiring both accurate prediction and mechanistic understanding for safety-critical materials engineering. Existing machine learning methods predict corrosion but lack explainability, and current factuality metrics cannot assess mechanistically defensible reasoning.

Method: Domain-adapted retrieval-augmented generation framework with three open-weight language models (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen-2.5-7B, Mistral-7B) fine-tuned on 3,309 expert-verified QA pairs from 840 papers. Integrated with hybrid dense-lexical retrieval pipeline and introduces Reason Map - a proposition-graph framework that constructs directed evidence graphs to detect causal direction inversions and unsupported inferences.

Result: Retrieval augmentation achieves 143-194% Token F1 gains with system faithfulness of 0.964 and context recall of 0.988. Blind external validation on new literature and electrochemical data confirms trend-level generalization. Reason Map successfully exposes reasoning flaws that flat factuality metrics miss.

Conclusion: The modular framework offers a generalizable blueprint for trustworthy AI-assisted knowledge synthesis, enabling both accurate retrieval and mechanistically defensible reasoning for corrosion mitigation, with potential applications across engineering domains.

Abstract: Corrosion accounts for approximately 4% of global GDP, and reliable prediction is essential for timely mitigation. Machine learning effectively predicts corrosion rates from composition, microstructure, and environmental variables, but cannot explain the underlying mechanisms. A reliable approach in safety-critical materials engineering requires not only accurate retrieval but also mechanistically defensible reasoning, a capability that existing factuality metrics cannot assess. This work presents a domain-adapted retrieval-augmented generation framework for corrosion knowledge synthesis, demonstrated on magnesium alloy corrosion. Three open-weight language models (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen-2.5-7B, Mistral-7B) are fine-tuned on 3,309 expert-verified question-answer pairs from 840 peer-reviewed papers and integrated with a hybrid dense-lexical retrieval pipeline. Retrieval augmentation produces Token F1 gains of 143-194%, with system faithfulness of 0.964 and context recall of 0.988. Blind external validation on newly published literature and in-house electrochemical data confirms trend-level generalisation. Reason Map, a proposition-graph framework, is further introduced; it independently constructs directed evidence graphs from generated answers and retrieved literature, enabling systematic detection of causal direction inversions and unsupported inferential leaps that flat factuality metrics cannot expose. The modular architecture can be applied across domains, offering a generalizable blueprint for trustworthy AI-assisted knowledge synthesis to circumvent corrosion, which can also be applied to other engineering domains.

</details>


### [139] [Good Memory Has ECC: Evaluating the Memory of Vision-Language Models Beyond Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00103)
*Shmuel Berman,Jia Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ECCBench is a new benchmark evaluating memory in LLMs/VLMs across three axes: efficiency (FLOPs needed), compression (memory compressibility), and calibration (uncertainty awareness), revealing that VLMs compress text but not video memory and have poor calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current memory benchmarks for LLMs/VLMs focus only on accuracy over long text/video, but accuracy alone misses important properties needed for real-world long-horizon tasks. The authors aim to develop a more comprehensive evaluation that captures efficiency, compression, and calibration aspects of memory systems.

Method: The authors introduce ECCBench, a benchmark and evaluation protocol that measures memory through three axes: 1) Efficiency - computational cost (FLOPs) needed to answer from memory, 2) Compression - whether compressible inputs are remembered more accurately/efficiently, and 3) Calibration - whether systems abstain based on uncertainty and error costs. They evaluate pretrained VLMs and various memory backbones.

Result: Pretrained VLMs compress their memory over text but not video, and are poorly calibrated on both modalities. Several non-Transformer architectures achieve better compression-calibration tradeoffs than RoPE Transformers, suggesting they could be valuable for long-horizon agent systems.

Conclusion: Memory evaluation should go beyond simple accuracy metrics to include efficiency, compression, and calibration properties. ECCBench provides a comprehensive framework for assessing memory systems, revealing important limitations in current VLMs and identifying promising non-Transformer architectures for future memory systems in long-horizon AI agents.

Abstract: Memory is widely viewed as an important unsolved problem for LLMs and VLMs, and current benchmarks typically evaluate it by testing accuracy over long text or video. However, accuracy alone misses properties that matter for real long-horizon tasks. We introduce ECCBench, a benchmark and evaluation protocol that measures memory beyond a system's capacity--its raw accuracy at a specific budget--via three axes we call ECC: efficiency--the computation, in FLOPs, needed to answer from memory; compression--whether compressible inputs are remembered more accurately or efficiently; and calibration--whether the system abstains in response to its own uncertainty and the cost of an error. We find that pretrained VLMs compress their memory over text but not video and are poorly calibrated on both. Among a broader set of memory backbones, several non-Transformer architectures achieve better compression-calibration tradeoffs than RoPE Transformers, suggesting they may be useful components for agents operating over long horizons.

</details>


### [140] [Flawed in Nature, Perfect through Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00129)
*J. M. Diederik Kruijssen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A swarm of deliberately mutated AI models outperforms standard models in changing environments by maintaining diversity, with collective gains coming at the expense of individual performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI/ML models degrade when problem distributions drift over time, which is common in real-world applications. Natural evolution overcomes this through variation and selection, but traditional AI optimization tends to converge to single optimal solutions, lacking diversity to handle non-stationarity.

Method: Introduces "Flawed in Nature, Perfect through Evolution" mechanism: maintaining a swarm of models with deliberate mutations away from optimal coefficients. This creates statistical diversity as a hedge against environmental changes. Includes theoretical proofs (four theorems) of guaranteed regret reduction and validation on synthetic linear regression tasks.

Result: Mutated swarm delivers best model in ~80% of environment changes; inference synthesis translates individual advantages into collective gains. Most effective when mutation drift rate matches environmental drift rate. An adaptive controller is proposed to tune mutation rate to unknown environmental drift.

Conclusion: The mechanism provides a generalizable design principle for AI/ML systems facing non-stationarity, offering biological evolution-inspired approach to maintain model diversity. May enable discovery of AI forms more closely mimicking biological intelligence.

Abstract: The performance of artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) models degrades when the problem they were trained on drifts. This is a near-universal feature of real-world problems, which often change unpredictably. Biological evolution has achieved intelligence by overcoming this obstacle through natural selection acting on heritable variation. AI/ML techniques have long incorporated forms of natural selection, but it has been challenging to maintain model diversity as optimization naturally drives convergence. Here we show that a swarm of AI/ML models subjected to deliberate mutations of their model coefficients away from optimality can reliably and sustainably improve performance in changing environments by acting as a statistical hedge against non-stationarity. We call this mechanism 'Flawed in Nature, Perfect through Evolution', reflecting that the collective performance gain goes at the expense of individual performance. We prove via four theorems that the resulting regret reduction is guaranteed under general conditions, establishing the Flawed-in-Nature mechanism as a generalizable design principle for AI/ML systems. We validate these results on synthetic linear regression tasks, demonstrating that the mutated swarm delivers the best model in $\sim80\%$ of environment changes and that inference synthesis successfully translates this individual advantage into a collective one. The mechanism proves to be most effective when the mutation drift rate matches the drift rate of the environment. We outline a simple, adaptive controller that enables practical applications by tuning the mutation drift rate to match the unknown drift rate of the environment. The close analogy of the Flawed-in-Nature mechanism to biological evolution suggests it may have been a critical missing ingredient for the organic discovery of AI forms that more closely mimic biological intelligence.

</details>


### [141] [Elite-Weighted Supervised Fine-tuning for Goal-Directed Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00189)
*Shiyun Wa,Yifei Wang,Anna G. Green,Simone Sciabola,Ye Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EW-SFT is a unified optimizer for molecular generators that uses elite selection of high-scoring molecules followed by supervised fine-tuning with the model's native loss, eliminating the need for architecture-specific RL formulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current goal-directed optimization for molecular generation relies on policy-gradient reinforcement learning, which requires trajectory-level log-probabilities that are architecture-dependent and difficult to reuse across different generative models and design tasks.

Method: Elite-Weighted Supervised Fine-tuning (EW-SFT) selects high-scoring molecules (elites) based on reward, then updates the model using its own pretraining loss on this selected set. The approach primarily uses reward for elite selection rather than continuous weighting.

Result: EW-SFT consistently outperforms native optimizers on 3D shape alignment tasks, improves goal-directed optimization on 2D similarity tasks, and achieves comparable performance on sample-efficiency benchmarks without RL formulations. It works across autoregressive, masked-diffusion, and discrete-flow generators.

Conclusion: EW-SFT provides a unified, effective optimization method that works across diverse molecular generators, design constraints, reference compounds, and scoring oracles, eliminating the need for architecture-specific reinforcement learning formulations.

Abstract: Goal-directed optimization is essential for steering molecular generators to propose candidates with desired properties. However, it is often implemented with policy-gradient reinforcement learning, which requires a generation-trajectory log-probability whose form depends on the model architecture and generation procedure. This makes an optimizer difficult to reuse across architectures and conditional generative designs. Supervised fine-tuning needs none of that machinery, but its update is driven by a fixed dataset, so the reward never enters the update. We introduce Elite-Weighted Supervised Fine-tuning (EW-SFT), which uses reward to guide elite selection of high-scoring molecules, and updates the model by its own pretraining loss on that set. Ablations show that reward information is passed primarily through elite selection, rather than through continuous weighting within the selected set. Because the update consumes only scored molecules and the model's native loss, the same rule applies across autoregressive, masked-diffusion, and discrete-flow generators, and across de novo, motif-extension, and linker-design tasks. Under a fixed budget of 3D shape alignment oracle calls on two kinase reference compounds, EW-SFT consistently outperforms the corresponding native optimizers. It further improves goal-directed optimization under a 2D similarity oracle on four held-out references and achieves comparable performance on a sample-efficiency benchmark without a trajectory-level RL formulation. These results demonstrate that EW-SFT is a unified and effective optimizer across molecular generators, design constraints, references, and oracles.

</details>


### [142] [WHALE: A Simple Recipe for Joint Harness-Weight Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00196)
*Haechan Kim,Yoonho Lee,Gisang Lee,Chelsea Finn,Kangwook Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WHALE alternates between updating model weights and searching for better executable harness code to optimize agent performance jointly, outperforming isolated optimization approaches across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent performance depends on both model parameters and executable harness code, but optimizing either component in isolation leaves the system bottlenecked by the frozen counterpart. Existing methods only optimize weights and prompts while leaving the broader harness fixed.

Method: WHALE alternates between: (1) updating model weights under current harness using online rejection-sampling fine-tuning, and (2) searching for better harness under updated model using Meta-Harness. Switching strategies include fixed phase durations or adaptive patience rules over training signals.

Result: WHALE outperforms weight-only, harness-only, and Fast-Slow Training by 4.15-24.38 percentage points in best mean@8 accuracy across Qwen3.5-2B/4B agents in search QA, mathematical reasoning, and chess puzzles. It matches peak weight-only accuracy with fewer rollouts in SearchQA and improves math accuracy after weight updates.

Conclusion: Joint weight-harness alternating learning is effective, with either component potentially being the bottleneck. Small interleaved updates outperform stagewise weight-then-harness optimization in both accuracy and rollout cost, demonstrating the importance of joint adaptation.

Abstract: Agent performance depends jointly on the model parameters and the executable harness code that manages context and control flow. Optimizing either component in isolation can leave the system bottlenecked by its frozen counterpart: weight updates can change which harness is effective, while harness updates can change which model capabilities are exposed. Existing joint-adaptation methods optimize weights and textual prompts but leave the broader harness fixed. We propose Weight-Harness Alternating LEarning (WHALE), a simple recipe that alternates two phases: updating the model under the current harness, then searching for a better harness under the updated model. We instantiate these two phases with online rejection-sampling fine-tuning and Meta-Harness, respectively. When to switch is a key design choice: to separate real improvements from noise without over-optimizing against a changing counterpart, WHALE uses either fixed phase durations or an adaptive patience rule over training signals. Using Qwen3.5-2B/4B agents across three domains (search question answering, mathematical reasoning, and chess puzzles), WHALE outperforms weight-only, harness-only, and Fast-Slow Training by 4.15-24.38 percentage points in best mean@8 accuracy. Either component can be the bottleneck: harness search matches peak weight-only accuracy with far fewer rollouts in SearchQA, but improves math accuracy only after a weight update. Small interleaved updates also outperform stagewise weight-then-harness optimization in accuracy and rollout cost. The code is available at https://github.com/krafton-ai/WHALE.

</details>


### [143] [QTEA: Ternary LLMs with Sparse Residual Salient Weight and By-Column Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00224)
*Yipin Guo,Arun M George,Jie Fu,Tareq Mahmoud,Sixue Xing,Siddharth Joshi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QTEA is a sub-2-bit weight-only post-training quantization framework for LLMs that uses ternary quantization with sparse residual compensation and column-wise refinement, achieving better accuracy and perplexity than existing methods while maintaining hardware efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing post-training quantization methods struggle to generalize across models and suffer severe accuracy loss below 2 bits. Current approaches that use unstructured sparsity to mitigate accuracy loss sacrifice regularity and GPU-friendly execution.

Method: QTEA quantizes weights into ternary values and uses salient weights as residual error compensators with semi-structured 1:4 sparsity within selected columns for hardware efficiency. It adds column-wise rescale refinement to GPTQ-style quantization, alternately updating per-column scales and ternary assignments, and introduces error decay to attenuate order-dependent error propagation.

Result: On Qwen3-14B, QTEA compresses all weights to effective 1.7 bits per weight, improving average accuracy over the strongest ternary PTQ baseline by 16.7% and achieving 1.40× and 2.61× lower perplexity on WikiText and C4 respectively. On Llama3-8B, it obtains 6.6% accuracy gain and 1.34×/1.95× lower perplexity. A lookup-table based kernel achieves 7.2× faster per-token generation over FP16 baseline.

Conclusion: QTEA demonstrates that sub-2-bit weight-only quantization can achieve both high compression ratios and maintained accuracy by combining ternary quantization with sparse residual compensation, column-wise refinement, and error decay techniques, while remaining hardware-efficient for practical deployment.

Abstract: Weight-only post-training quantization (PTQ) can alleviate the computational burden of serving large language models (LLMs) at scale. However, existing PTQ methods often fail to generalize across models and suffer severe accuracy loss below 2 bits. Many leverage unstructured sparsity to mitigate this loss, but at the cost of regularity and GPU-friendly execution. We present QTEA, a sub-2-bit PTQ framework that quantizes weights into ternary values and uses salient weights as residual error compensators. To maintain hardware efficiency, residuals are assigned to selected columns with semi-structured \(1{:}4\) sparsity within the salient columns. We further add column-wise rescale refinement to GPTQ-style column-by-column quantization, alternately updating per-column scales and ternary assignments to reduce reconstruction error. We also identify order-dependent error propagation in GPTQ and introduce error decay to attenuate late-stage error accumulation. On Qwen3-14B, QTEA compresses all weights to an effective 1.7 bits per weight while improving average accuracy over the strongest ternary PTQ baseline by 16.7\%. It also achieves 1.40\(\times\) and 2.61\(\times\) lower perplexity on WikiText and C4 respectively. This trend holds on Llama3-8B, where QTEA obtains a 6.6\% accuracy gain and 1.34\(\times\)/1.95\(\times\) lower perplexity on the same datasets. Finally, we develop a lookup-table based kernel that achieves 7.2\(\times\) faster per-token generation over an FP16 baseline. Code is available at https://github.com/Intelligent-Microsystems-Lab/QTEA.

</details>


### [144] [Geometry-aware Latent Autoregressive Generative Model for PDEs in Complex Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00297)
*Zi Wang,Minghui Xu,Tapan Mukerji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoLAMP: A geometry-aware latent autoregressive model using dual encoders and flow matching transformers to solve multiphysics PDEs in complex microscale geometries with stable long-horizon predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving multiphysics PDEs in complex microscale tortuous geometries (critical for energy and chemical engineering) remains a major challenge in scientific computing due to the irregular and intricate nature of these structures.

Method: Proposes GeoLAMP with a dual-encoder architecture on graph representations to capture global topology and fine-scale geometric features. Uses a causal self-attention transformer with flow matching in latent space for temporal dynamics modeling, enabling stable block-wise autoregressive prediction. A flexible decoder reconstructs high-resolution fields on arbitrary points.

Result: Established three multiphysics benchmark datasets covering reactive flow, heat convection, and elasticity. GeoLAMP consistently achieved the most stable autoregression performance with low errors throughout entire rollout horizons, outperforming other methods on these complex geometry datasets.

Conclusion: GeoLAMP provides a systematic approach for geometry-aware learning of PDEs in complex microscale geometries and offers new insights into block-wise time marching of latent autoregressive PDE modeling through a flow matching framework.

Abstract: Solving multiphysics partial differential equations (PDEs) remains a major challenge in scientific computing, especially for highly complex $μ$m-scale tortuous geometries critical to energy and chemical engineering. We address this challenge by proposing a Geometry-aware Latent Autoregressive generative Model for PDEs (GeoLAMP) for solving physics within highly irregular and tortuous structures. GeoLAMP introduces a dual-encoder architecture on graph representations to jointly capture global topology and fine-scale geometric features, enabling an effective transition from real-space fields to compact latent representations. In the latent space, we propose a causal self-attention transformer with flow matching to model temporal dynamics, allowing stable and scalable block-wise autoregressive prediction. A flexible decoder reconstructs high-resolution physical fields on arbitrary points. We establish three multiphysics benchmark datasets in complex geometries, covering reactive flow, heat convection, and elasticity. GeoLAMP consistently achieves the most stable autoregression performance on these datasets, maintaining low errors throughout the entire rollout horizon. Our results provide a systematic study of geometry-aware learning for PDEs in $μ$m-scale complex geometries and offer new insights into block-wise time marching of latent autoregressive PDE modeling via a flow matching framework.

</details>


### [145] [Do LLMs Know Your Neighborhood? Auditing LLM Priors for Neighborhood-Level Mobility Prediction and Structural Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00345)
*Saad Mohammad Abrar,Eesha Kurella,Arnav Dadarya,Naman Awasthi,Kazi Tasnim Zinat,Vanessa Frias-Martinez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs show limited but partial capability in predicting neighborhood-level human mobility from urban context, achieving 0.435 accuracy vs. 0.580 for supervised models, with concerns about structural grounding and potential biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human mobility data is crucial for urban applications but often proprietary and privacy-sensitive. LLMs offer potential as alternative generators of mobility data, but their ability to infer aggregate neighborhood-level mobility patterns remains unclear and needs evaluation.

Method: Used zero-shot LLMs to predict Census Block Group-level mobility across four U.S. metropolitan areas, using anonymized Cuebiq data for three mobility outcome types. Compared LLM predictions with supervised baselines and introduced directional alignment analysis to test whether LLM-implied predictor effects match empirical statistical trends.

Result: Supervised models achieved 0.580 average accuracy compared to 0.435 for best LLM. LLMs performed best on spatial extent outcomes but showed largest gaps with baselines. Directional analysis revealed LLMs rely on coarse, stable predictor-level priors that remain similar across outcomes and cities, including asymmetric treatment of protected-group predictors.

Conclusion: LLMs can partially recover aggregate mobility patterns from urban context, but their predictions should not be treated as structurally grounded without auditing empirical alignment and potential bias. The models often use simplistic priors that may not reflect complex urban dynamics.

Abstract: Human mobility is central to urban planning, transportation, public health, and emergency response, yet fine-grained trajectory data are often proprietary, restricted, and privacy-sensitive. Large language models (LLMs) offer a potential alternative by generating plausible mobility traces and predicting individual movement, but their ability to infer aggregate neighborhood-level mobility remains unclear. We evaluate zero-shot LLMs on Census Block Group-level mobility prediction across four U.S. metropolitan areas using anonymized Cuebiq data to construct point-level, trajectory-level, and temporal mobility outcomes, paired with sociodemographic and built-environment predictors. We compare LLM predictions with supervised baselines and introduce a directional alignment analysis to test whether LLM-implied predictor effects agree with empirical OLS and Jonckheere-Terpstra trends. Supervised models achieve 0.580 average accuracy, compared with 0.435 for the best LLM, with spatial extent outcomes showing the strongest predictability but also the largest LLM-baseline gaps. Directional analysis shows that LLMs often rely on coarse, stable predictor-level priors that remain similar across outcomes and cities, including asymmetric treatment of protected-group predictors. Overall, LLMs can partially recover aggregate mobility patterns from urban context, but their predictions should not be treated as structurally grounded without auditing empirical alignment and potential bias.

</details>


### [146] [Deterministic LLM Inference Across GPU Kernels: Power-of-Two INT8 Quantization Scales and the Limits of Tolerance-Based Conformance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00363)
*Teng-Ruei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantized GEMM conformance tests with single spacing tolerance miss all epilogue faults in quantized kernels, but power-of-two scale requantization enables bitwise agreement between implementations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what tolerance-based conformance suites for quantized GEMM kernels can actually detect, and to investigate whether implementations can be made bitwise identical through careful design constraints like power-of-two scales.

Method: Inject nine specific faults into a reference INT8 pipeline over 8,232 layer-fault-regime cells of Qwen3-1.7B model. Analyze detection across different fault types (epilogue faults vs. accumulator precondition faults). Test power-of-two scale requantization to achieve bitwise agreement between CUTLASS and Triton implementations.

Result: All five epilogue faults move output by at most a single bfloat16 spacing, making them undetectable by single-spacing tolerance suites. Power-of-two scale requantization enables bitwise agreement between CUTLASS and Triton at all layers (196/196 and 252/252) and yields byte-identical generated token sequences. Perplexity changes are minimal (+0.32%, -0.28%, +0.48%) with 90% intervals covering zero at smaller model sizes.

Conclusion: Tolerance-based conformance suites establish only that preconditions hold, operands are shared, and differences stay within one spacing - narrower than full interchangeability. Power-of-two scale constraints can achieve bitwise equivalence between implementations with negligible accuracy impact, correcting previous misattributed perplexity degradation.

Abstract: Conformance suites for quantized GEMM kernels ask whether two implementations agree within a tolerance. We measure what such a suite can detect. Injecting nine faults into a reference INT8 pipeline over 8,232 layer--fault--regime cells of Qwen3-1.7B, we find that every one of five epilogue faults -- scale precision, double rounding, multiplication order, output truncation, fused ordering -- moves the output by at most a single bfloat16 spacing, and by exactly one whenever it moves it at all, across 5,880 cells. A tolerance of one spacing is therefore blind to the entire class by construction: four of the five faults are detected by no check in the suite, and the fifth only under power-of-two scales. Faults that violate the accumulator's exactness preconditions, or that break operand sharing, are detected without exception, and a null fault never fires. What a tolerance-based suite of this shape establishes is therefore narrower than interchangeability: that the preconditions hold, that operands are shared, and that differences stay within one spacing. The power-of-two constraint that exposes the one detected fault is also deployable. Requantizing every weight scale to its nearest power of two makes CUTLASS and Triton agree bitwise at every linear layer (196/196 and 252/252, against 8/196 and 10/252 under the checkpoints' own scales) and yields byte-identical generated token sequences at 1.7B, 8B and 14B (8/8 prompts, against 0/8 at all three). Observed perplexity point estimates are +0.32%, -0.28% and +0.48%; the 90% intervals cover zero at the two smaller sizes but not at 14B, reaching +0.71% and +0.76%. A previously reported +157% perplexity for this intervention was an artifact of a probe that rewrote scales without requantizing the weights; separating the effects attributes 99.8% of it to the resulting weight--scale mismatch rather than to the power-of-two constraint itself.

</details>


### [147] [Counterfactual Fragility Certificates: Exposing High-Confidence Brittleness under Structured Evidence Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00366)
*Filippo Cenacchi,Longbing Cao,Runze Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CFC introduces fragility certificates that audit predictions against evidence failures, identifying high-confidence brittle cases better than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods (calibration, uncertainty, explanations) fail to show when individual predictions lose support due to feature-family failures (unavailable, noisy, stale features), needing recomputable audit objects for evidence-failure protocols.

Method: Counterfactual Fragility Certificates (CFC): model-agnostic protocol-level audit certificates that map each prediction to ordered evidence-failure trajectories with metrics like greedy flip budget, normalized margin-collapse area, degradation thresholds, and fragility dominance score (CFC-FDS).

Result: CFC-FDS achieves 0.915 AUROC identifying brittle high-confidence cases (+0.405 improvement over best non-certificate method), captures 88.9% of cases under 20% review budget (vs 31.8-37.4% for confidence/energy scores), validated across 7 benchmarks and baselines.

Conclusion: CFC provides a concrete reliability framework for exposing high-confidence brittleness missed by ordinary evaluation, enabling better risk assessment and secondary uses like fragility-aware regularization.

Abstract: High test accuracy and good aggregate calibration do not show whether an individual prediction is structurally supported by its evidence. In tabular decision systems, failures often occur when a feature family becomes unavailable, delayed, noisy, stale, or low-trust while the model remains highly confident. Existing calibration, uncertainty, selective-prediction, explanation, and perturbation methods provide scalar scores or attribution maps, but not a recomputable audit object answering: under a declared evidence-failure protocol, what trajectory makes this prediction lose support? We introduce Counterfactual Fragility Certificates (CFC), a model-agnostic protocol-level audit certificate-not a formal robustness certificate-that maps each prediction into an ordered evidence-failure trajectory summarized by greedy flip budget, normalized margin-collapse area, degradation thresholds, and fragility dominance score. Across seven tabular benchmarks and strong linear, tree-based, boosting, and neural baselines, CFC-FDS identifies independently brittle high-confidence cases with 0.915 AUROC, improving over the strongest non-certificate score by +0.405. The advantage persists across perturbation, permutation-importance, group-SHAP, baseline-choice, seed-variance, budgeted-review, and naturalistic field-unavailability checks. Under a 20% review budget, CFC-FDS captures 88.9% of brittle high-confidence cases, compared with 31.8-37.4% for confidence and energy scores. We also evaluate fragility-aware regularization and brittleness-aware temperature correction as secondary uses. CFC provides a concrete reliability framework for exposing high-confidence brittleness missed by ordinary score-centric evaluation.

</details>


### [148] [Adapting Without Gradients: Affine Statistics Transport and What Its Certificate Can Tell You](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00374)
*Salim Khazem,Ibrahim Mohamed Serouis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CASTER is a gradient-free test-time adaptation method for frozen models that uses affine transformations of source class statistics in a discriminative subspace, requiring no gradient updates, optimizer state, or stored source features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard test-time adaptation (TTA) methods assume model parameters can be updated at inference time, which is problematic for inference-only accelerators, frozen/third-party models, and memory-constrained deployments. BatchNorm-based TTA also fails on architectures without BatchNorm.

Method: CASTER stores source class statistics in a discriminative subspace, estimates a class-shared affine transformation from target-batch moments, and analytically transports source class distributions before classification. It includes a transportability certificate to gate unreliable transformations and prevent performance degradation.

Result: CASTER outperforms k-NN on identical frozen features in 27 of 28 backbone-dataset settings while using 18x less state. On ImageNet-C with small batches, unconditional transport loses performance, but gating with the transportability certificate converts negative effects into positive gains (+1.69 points vs -3.35 points).

Conclusion: CASTER provides a lightweight adaptation mechanism for frozen-model deployment with an explicit safety signal. The transportability certificate effectively distinguishes benign from destructive transport regimes, though not perfectly, enabling safe deployment across diverse settings.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) typically assumes that model parameters can be updated at inference time. This assumption is restrictive for inference-only accelerators, frozen or third-party models, and memory-constrained deployments, and standard BatchNorm-based TTA configurations may also become inactive on architectures without BatchNorm. We study adaptation when the learned model must remain frozen. We introduce CASTER, a gradient-free method that stores source class statistics in a discriminative subspace, estimates a class-shared affine transformation from target-batch moments, and analytically transports the source class distributions before classification. CASTER requires no backward pass, optimizer state, or stored source feature bank. Across four backbones and seven datasets, it outperforms k-NN on identical frozen features in 27 of 28 backbone-dataset settings while retaining a median of 18x less state. Affine transport is not always reliable. On ImageNet-C, where batches contain only 64 samples for 1000 classes, unconditional transport loses 21.2 top-1 points. We therefore introduce an empirical residual-to-margin transportability certificate. Across 307 evaluation cells, every transport losing more than 10 points has certificate value above 3.9, although benign and destructive regimes are not perfectly separated. Gating converts an average $-3.35$-point effect of unconditional transport into a +1.69-point gain, and performance remains within 0.3 points of the best threshold over a broad threshold range. Finally, we show that this certificate is mechanism-specific: when applied to Tent, it accepts only $4.3\%$ of updates and preserves 0.6% of Tent's available gain. These results position CASTER as a lightweight adaptation mechanism for frozen-model deployment, together with an explicit account of when its safety signal is informative and when it is not.

</details>


### [149] [Neural means and kernel corrections for operator learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00389)
*Yitzchak Shmalo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural networks combined with exact Matérn kernel regression on residuals and learned features outperform or match state-of-the-art baselines on two physics emulation problems, with theoretical analysis of why this hybrid approach works.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve emulation performance by combining neural networks and kernel methods, leveraging the strengths of both approaches to achieve better accuracy than either method alone on challenging physics emulation problems.

Method: Hybrid approach combining neural network means with exact Matérn kernel regressions applied to: 1) neural network residuals, and 2) learned neural network features. The method is evaluated on two public emulation benchmarks with published baselines.

Result: On structural mechanics: Reaches 4.55% test error (matching best published architecture) and 5.38% vs published 6.49% in low-data regime. On OCO-2 radiative transfer: Improves on published Gaussian process emulator, outright on two of three spectral bands. Kernel method that performs poorly on raw state overtakes neural network when applied to network's features.

Conclusion: The hybrid neural-kernel approach provides state-of-the-art or superior performance on challenging physics emulation problems. The analysis reveals why this works (target's squared native-space norm drops ~40x at fixed effective dimension) and identifies when neural and kernel methods tie (residuals correlate above 0.86, shared component is flat). The paper provides theoretical mechanisms and practical tools including predictive stacking outcomes, optimal-recovery certificate, and distribution-free coverage band.

Abstract: We combine neural network means with exact Matérn kernel regressions of their residuals and of their learned features, and evaluate the pairing on two public emulation problems with published baselines: the structural-mechanics benchmark of de Hoop et al. and the OCO-2 radiative-transfer emulator of Lamminpää et al. On structural mechanics the combination reaches 4.55% test error, matching the best published architecture, and 5.38% against a published 6.49% in the low-data regime. On OCO-2 it improves on the published Gaussian-process emulator on that problem's own test points, outright on two of the three spectral bands; the same kernel that trails the network tenfold on the raw state overtakes it on the network's features, and we measure why (the target's squared native-space norm drops about fortyfold at fixed effective dimension) and prove the mechanism. Where the two families tie instead, the residuals of every architecture we train correlate above 0.86 and their shared component is flat in diversity and sample size, which reads the published plateau as a property of the data. Supporting results include a second-moment identity that predicts stacking outcomes from measured correlations, an optimal-recovery certificate, and a distribution-free coverage band, the only uncertainty signal that survives our tests.

</details>
