### [150] [A Multi-Branch Feature Fusion Approach for Health Misinformation Detection and Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00403)
*Mkululi Sikosana,Sean Maudsley-Barton,Oluwaseun Ajao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multi-branch fusion framework combining transformer semantics, rhetorical cues, stance representations, and psychological proxies to detect health misinformation, with interpretable Cognitive Propagation Score for diffusion risk assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health misinformation spreads rapidly in online social networks, but current detection methods lack interpretability and comprehensive integration of psychological and rhetorical signals. There's a need for models that not only classify misinformation but also explain its potential propagation risk, especially when engagement data is incomplete.

Method: Multi-branch fusion framework grounded in ELM and TPB theories, fusing transformer-based semantics with rhetorical cues, stance representations, and psychologically motivated proxies. Uses multi-task architecture with binary classification and introduces interpretable Cognitive Propagation Score (CPS) derived from argument complexity, emotional intensity, and virality potential cues.

Result: Achieves ROC-AUC up to 0.9999 on COVID-19_FNIR, near-perfect Spearman's ρ of 0.9952 on Monkeypox with engagement supervision, and 0.9954 on COVID-19_FNIR with proxy supervision. Outperforms baselines on Constraint and COVID-19_FNIR datasets. Ablation shows psychological and rhetorical branches provide complementary gains beyond semantic embeddings.

Conclusion: The framework successfully bridges cognitive theory and neural modeling to improve misinformation detection transparency and scalability. Cognitive Propagation Score provides interpretable risk assessment, but future validation against human-centered diffusion judgments is needed. The approach shows strong performance across multiple health misinformation domains.

Abstract: This paper presents a multi-branch fusion framework for detecting and characterising the propagation of health misinformation in online social networks (OSNs). Grounded in the Elaboration Likelihood Model (ELM) and the Theory of Planned Behaviour (TPB), the model fuses transformer-based semantics with rhetorical cues, stance representations, and psychologically motivated proxies in a unified multi-task architecture. In addition to binary classification, we introduce the Cognitive Propagation Score (CPS), an interpretable post-hoc auxiliary score computed from psychologically motivated, text-derived cues capturing argument complexity, emotional intensity, and content-derived virality potential, to support diffusion-risk reasoning when engagement ground truth is incomplete or unavailable. Experiments on three benchmark datasets, Constraint, COVID--19\_FNIR, and Monkeypox, show strong classification performance, achieving ROC--AUC up to 0.9999 on COVID--19\_FNIR, while propagation-oriented ranking achieves near-perfect agreement when engagement-derived supervision is available (Monkeypox, Spearman's $ρ= 0.9952$) and similarly high ranking alignment under proxy-based supervision on COVID--19\_FNIR ($ρ= 0.9954$). Compared with representative literature baselines, the fusion model improves detection on Constraint and COVID--19\_FNIR, while Monkeypox remains more challenging, reflecting domain- and signal-specific differences. Ablation analysis further indicates that psychological and rhetorical branches provide complementary gains beyond semantic embeddings. Overall, the framework bridges cognitive theory and neural modelling to improve transparency and to support scalable misinformation monitoring, with future work required to validate CPS against human-centred diffusion judgements.

</details>


### [151] [How Temporal Correlations Shape Memory in Linear Recurrent Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00420)
*Arnol Manuel Fokam,Fasseu Sieyondji Akpevwoghene,Edem Fiifi Dawson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LRNNs trained on correlated sequences learn memory dynamics where memory builds, overshoots, then partially drops, settling with less past retention than with uncorrelated inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work studied LRNN memory dynamics for uncorrelated inputs, but real-world sequences are correlated. The paper aims to understand how input correlations affect what memory networks learn and their training dynamics.

Method: Exact solution of learning dynamics for linear recurrent neural networks with correlated input sequences, analyzing how correlation affects memory retention costs and network behavior.

Result: Three key findings: 1) Correlation reshapes learning course - memory builds, overshoots, then partially removed. 2) Memory switches off at threshold set by input similarity to previous input. 3) Best network includes feedthrough path for zero error when given extra hidden dimension.

Conclusion: Input correlation determines whether networks learn memory - memory is only kept when task needs previous input more than current input already supplies through correlation. This explains why correlated data turns recurrent networks into change detectors.

Abstract: The linear recurrent neural network (LRNN) is a simple model for studying how much memory a network builds up as it trains. For uncorrelated inputs, earlier work found that training itself settles the network between keeping the past and reacting only to the present. Real sequences are correlated, and we solve the learning dynamics exactly for correlated inputs. In the solution, keeping the past carries a cost. The whole effect of correlation lands on that cost. This cost reduces to the earlier one when inputs are uncorrelated and grows once they are positively correlated. Three findings follow. (1) Correlation reshapes the course of learning, not only its end. Memory builds, overshoots, and is partly removed, and the settled network keeps less of the past. (2) Memory switches off at a threshold set by one number, how much each input resembles the one just before it. Neither sequence length nor longer-range correlation moves this threshold. Memory is worth keeping only when the task needs the previous input more than the current input already supplies it through correlation with the past. (3) The best network changes too. Zero error demands a feedthrough, a path that passes the current input straight to the network's output and remembers nothing, and training builds it unprompted when given one spare hidden dimension. Our work turns one property of the input into a prediction of whether a network learns memory and explains why correlated data turns recurrent networks into change detectors.

</details>


### [152] [Group Adaptive Clipping Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00444)
*Sheng Jia,Xiao Wang,Shiva Prasad Kasiviswanathan,Rein Houthooft*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GAPO is a plug-in method that adapts importance-sampling ratio clipping boundaries to rollout advantage, improving over fixed clipping for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed importance-sampling ratio clipping treats all rollouts equally, but rare correct rollouts on hard problems (which carry stronger learning signals) get clipped at comparable rates to abundant correct rollouts on easy problems, despite their different contributions to learning.

Method: Group Adaptive Clipping Policy Optimization (GAPO) adapts the clipping boundary based on rollout advantage. It's motivated by a reverse-KL trust-region perspective and requires no reward shaping while preserving the standard PPO/GSPO surrogate. It adjusts only the clipping threshold.

Result: GAPO consistently improves both Pass@1 and Pass@k metrics over fixed clipping and advantage-shaping baselines on math reasoning and coding benchmarks using Qwen and Llama models, particularly when base model pass rates are low.

Conclusion: Adaptive clipping based on rollout advantage is more effective than fixed clipping for RLVR, as it gives stronger learning signals from low-success rollouts more update headroom while suppressing the influence of abundant correct rollouts appropriately.

Abstract: Group relative policy optimization for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically uses a fixed importance-sampling (IS) ratio clipping boundary across all rollouts. We identify a key limitation: rare correct rollouts on harder problems and abundant correct rollouts on easier problems are clipped at comparable rates, despite contributing very different learning signals. Rollouts with low group success exhibit larger IS ratios and carry stronger gradient signal for exploration and solving new problems, yet are disproportionately suppressed by fixed clipping.
  To address this, we propose Group Adaptive Clipping Policy Optimization (GAPO), a plug-in modification to GRPO methods that adapts the clipping boundary to the rollout advantage. GAPO is motivated by a reverse-KL trust-region perspective, which suggests that rollouts with larger learning signal should receive proportionally greater update headroom. GAPO requires no reward shaping and preserves the standard PPO/GSPO surrogate while adapting only the clipping threshold. Across Qwen and Llama models, GAPO consistently improves both Pass@1 and Pass@k over fixed clipping and advantage-shaping baselines on math reasoning and coding benchmarks where the pass rates by the base model are relatively low.

</details>


### [153] [CRAD: Class-wise Reliability-Aware Distillation for Decentralized Heterogeneous Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00446)
*Baraa Bilbeisi,Mengchen Fan,Baocheng Geng,Qing Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CRAD is a federated learning framework that uses decentralized knowledge distillation with class-wise reliability-aware teacher selection and weighting, enabling heterogeneous architectures and non-IID data without a central server.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional federated learning requires homogeneous client architectures and suffers under non-IID data distributions, but real-world deployments often violate both assumptions, necessitating a more flexible approach.

Method: The paper proposes Class-wise Reliability-Aware Distillation (CRAD): a decentralized knowledge distillation framework where clients share model snapshots, evaluate peers on local data, distill from soft predictions, and use class-wise filtering and weighted averaging based on teacher reliability (precision/variance).

Result: CRAD consistently outperforms competing methods in global accuracy on three image-classification benchmarks (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, PathMNIST) under heterogeneous architectures and severe non-IID data skew.

Conclusion: CRAD provides an effective solution for practical federated learning by enabling heterogeneous client architectures and handling non-IID data through class-wise reliability-aware knowledge distillation without requiring a central server or data sharing.

Abstract: Conventional federated learning (FL) relies on parameter averaging, which forces clients to be doubly homogeneous: it demands an identical architecture and degrades under non-IID data. Real-world deployments usually break both assumptions. We sidestep both by building a decentralized knowledge distillation framework in which each client evaluates its peers' model snapshots on its own local data and distills from the resulting soft predictions. Because knowledge is transferred through the shared class posterior, clients are free to run different architectures; and because every teacher is evaluated on the student's own device, raw data never leaves the client, with no central server or public dataset required. Within this setting, we identify and address an under-examined problem: how to combine the peer teacher predictions. Existing methods, like uniform averaging, ignore how knowledge reliability varies across teachers and classes. We propose Class-wise Reliability-Aware Distillation (CRAD), which, per class, first discards teachers that disagree with the peer consensus and then takes a weighted average of the rest, weighting each teacher by its per-class reliability (precision, or inverse variance). Since the variance of an accuracy from $n$ samples scales as $1/n$, support enters automatically: among the teachers that survive filtering, a teacher is trusted for a class to the degree that it is both accurate and well-evidenced for it. On three image-classification benchmarks (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and PathMNIST colon pathology), across heterogeneous architectures under severe non-IID skew, CRAD consistently outperforms competing methods in global accuracy.

</details>


### [154] [HBQ: Hierarchical Scaling Block Quantization with Hardware-Efficiency-Aware Design for Accurate LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00450)
*Chun-Ting Chen,Dongmin Han,Hangyeol Mun,Jake Hyun,Arnab Raha,Amit Agarwal,Mark Anders,Mohamed Abdelfattah,Jae-sun Seo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HBQ (Hierarchical Block Quantization) enables efficient LLM deployment using large-block quantization with novel significand scaling, achieving W4A5 accuracy comparable to W4A16 while reducing hardware costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Block Quantization offers hardware efficiency but suffers from accuracy degradation with larger blocks. There's a need for BQ methods that can use large blocks for efficiency without sacrificing accuracy, overcoming limitations of prior approaches.

Method: Proposes Hierarchical Block Quantization (HBQ) using large blocks for efficiency, with low-overhead significand scaling for second-level quantization. This method effectively allocates quantization levels and accounts for distinct activation/weight distributions. HBQ-A focuses on accuracy, HBQ-E on efficiency.

Result: HBQ-A achieves W4A16-level accuracy using only W4A5 while requiring less silicon area than NVFP4. HBQ-E reduces hardware cost by 17% while maintaining higher accuracy than existing BQ methods. The ASIC accelerator shows 2.3×/4.6× higher area/energy efficiency vs WoQ and 1.6-3.3× system energy reduction vs prior BQ methods.

Conclusion: HBQ successfully resolves the efficiency-accuracy trade-off in block quantization by combining large blocks with intelligent scaling, offering superior hardware efficiency and accuracy compared to both WoQ and prior BQ methods, enabling practical low-precision LLM deployment.

Abstract: Block Quantization (BQ) is a promising approach for efficient deployment of large language models (LLMs), enabling low-precision computation with controlled accuracy degradation. Compared to scalar weight-only quantization (WoQ), BQ quantizes both weight and activation, offering higher hardware efficiency and end-to-end inference on a unified datapath, but its design space, spanning bit-width, block size, scaling, and numeric formats, remains underexplored.
  We provide hardware/benchmark results through design space exploration (DSE). We find that increasing block size improves hardware efficiency by amortizing dequantization and accumulation costs, but degrades accuracy. This trade-off limits conventional BQ methods.
  Motivated by this insight, we propose Hierarchical Block Quantization (HBQ). Unlike prior methods [1], [2], which use small blocks and conventional Power-of-Two (PoT) or integer-based scaling, HBQ uses large blocks to maximize efficiency and introduces low-overhead significand (SIG) scaling for second-level quantization. By allocating quantization levels effectively and accounting for distinct activation and weight distributions, SIG scaling compensates for large-block errors more effectively than prior PoT and INT schemes. HBQ-A (accurate) achieves W4A16-level accuracy using only W4A5 while requiring less silicon area than NVFP4. HBQ-E (efficient) further reduces hardware cost by 17% while maintaining higher accuracy than all existing BQ methods.
  We implemented a 28nm ASIC accelerator applying HBQ to weights, activations, and KV cache, and integrated a novel partial-sum BQ scheme to further reduce EMA energy.
  Compared to state-of-the-art WoQ, HBQ delivers $2.3\times$/$4.6\times$ higher area/energy efficiency at the same accuracy level; $1.6$--$3.3\times$ system energy reduction and $1.5$--$3.0\times$ speedup over prior BQ methods while providing best accuracy.

</details>


### [155] [Can LLMs Use Relational Transformer Embeddings?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00457)
*Francisco Galuppo Azevedo,Clarissa Lima Loures*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Soft-token fusion of frozen relational-encoder embeddings into LLMs for relational prediction fails to consistently outperform standalone relational transformers and shows multiple failure modes despite its conceptual appeal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to combine the strengths of relational encoders (handling multi-table structure) and LLMs (handling language and reasoning) without lossy text serialization, creating a conceptually appealing fusion strategy for relational prediction tasks.

Method: Inject frozen Relational Transformer embeddings into Qwen3.5-4B via learned MLP projection and LoRA adaptation, trained with supervised fine-tuning on chain-of-thought reasoning traces followed by group-based reinforcement learning (GSPO). Evaluated across 10 binary classification tasks on 6 relational databases from RelBench under four supervision regimes: single-task, within-dataset, cross-dataset, and all-task.

Result: Negative results: the hybrid model does not consistently outperform standalone relational transformers, frequently performs below random, is highly sensitive to serialization format and relational-token budget, and unstable under RL training.

Conclusion: Soft-token fusion requires stronger alignment objectives and schema-aware design before it can serve as a reliable route to relational prediction, as current approaches show significant limitations and failure modes.

Abstract: Injecting frozen relational-encoder embeddings as soft tokens into a large language model (LLM) is a conceptually appealing fusion strategy: the encoder handles multi-table structure, the LLM handles language and reasoning, and no lossy text serialization is required. We test this hypothesis concretely by injecting embeddings from a frozen Relational Transformer (RT) into Qwen3.5-4B via a learned MLP projection and LoRA adaptation, trained first with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on chain-of-thought reasoning traces and then with group-based reinforcement learning (GSPO). We evaluate across 10 binary classification tasks on 6 relational databases from RelBench, under four supervision regimes: single-task (ST), within-dataset (WD), cross-dataset (CD), and all-task (ALL). The hybrid model does not consistently outperform standalone RT: it is frequently below random, highly sensitive to serialization format and relational-token budget, and unstable under RL training. We report these negative results and analyze the failure modes, arguing that soft-token fusion requires stronger alignment objectives and schema-aware design before it can serve as a reliable route to relational prediction.

</details>


### [156] [Context Window Failures in Relational Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00460)
*Denis Oliveira Correa,Francisco Galuppo Azevedo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Relational deep learning models struggle with high-cardinality data; a simple pre-aggregation step dramatically improves performance, suggesting current models aren't ready for real-world high-volume relational data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent relational deep learning architectures are proposed as foundation models for multi-table relational data, but they impose constrained neighborhood budgets that force row truncation when an entity has many related records. The authors aim to test whether these models can handle high-cardinality real-world data.

Method: The authors introduce Animus, a synthetic financial dataset where predicting customer income requires aggregating up to tens of thousands of transactions. They evaluate three recent models (RT, Griffin, RelGT) on the raw data and then with a single temporal pre-aggregation step.

Result: On the raw representation, the three models achieve R² ≤ 0.18. With a single temporal pre-aggregation step, R² recovers up to 0.65. This significant improvement questions the readiness of current relational foundation models for high-cardinality data.

Conclusion: Current relational deep learning foundation models are not yet ready for high-cardinality real-world data, as they fail to effectively handle entities with many related records without preprocessing.

Abstract: Recent Relational Deep Learning architectures have been proposed as foundation models for multi-table relational data, yet they impose constrained neighborhood budgets that force row truncation when an entity has many related records. We introduce Animus, a synthetic financial dataset in which predicting customer income requires aggregating up to tens of thousands of transactions. On the raw representation, three recently proposed models (RT, Griffin, RelGT) achieve $R^2 \le 0.18$; a single, routine, temporal pre-aggregation step recovers $R^2$ up to $0.65$. This questions whether current relational foundation models are ready for high-cardinality real-world data.

</details>


### [157] [Higher Structures in Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00472)
*Michael L. Roberts,Carlos Zapata Carratalá. Nicholas J. Cooper,Lijun Chen,François G. Meyer,Danna Gurari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explores the role of higher-arity tensor operations in deep learning, empirically studies higher-arity phenomena in trained networks, proposes hypergraphical MLPs, and connects findings to evolutionary algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the importance of higher-arity tensor operations in deep learning that haven't been thoroughly explored, suggesting they may offer computational advantages or representational power beyond traditional binary operations.

Method: 1. Provides expository introduction to higher-arity tensor operations. 2. Conducts novel empirical investigation of higher-arity phenomena in trained neural networks. 3. Introduces a hypergraphical generalization of multilayer perceptrons (MLPs). 4. Explores connections between these concepts and evolutionary algorithms.

Result: The paper likely demonstrates empirical evidence of higher-arity phenomena in existing neural networks, introduces new hypergraphical MLP architectures that leverage higher-arity operations, and shows connections to evolutionary computation methods.

Conclusion: Higher-arity tensor operations represent a promising research direction in deep learning, with potential applications in neural architecture design, evolutionary algorithms, and understanding computational aspects of neural networks, warranting further investigation.

Abstract: We provide an expository introduction on the importance of higher-arity tensor operations to deep learning. Then, we conduct a novel empirical investigation of higher-arity phenomenon in trained neural networks, introduce a hypergraphical generalization of the multilayer perceptron, and explore connections to evolutionary algorithms. We conclude with a discussion of promising directions for future research.

</details>


### [158] [AdaptNTK: Adaptive Uncertainty Quantification and Active Learning for Neural Network Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00488)
*Prajwal Ananth,Shuwen Yue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaptNTK is a novel single-model framework for uncertainty quantification in active learning of interatomic potentials that uses regularized Mahalanobis distance in neural tangent kernel feature space, enabling efficient batch selection without retraining while reducing redundancy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current uncertainty quantification methods for active learning in machine learning interatomic potentials face trade-offs between computational cost and reliability, and cannot account for redundancy during batch acquisition. There's a need for more efficient and reliable uncertainty estimation that works with single models rather than ensembles.

Method: Proposed AdaptNTK framework measures uncertainty as a regularized Mahalanobis distance in empirical neural tangent kernel (NTK) feature space. The NTK features remain fixed during acquisition, allowing uncertainty to be updated recursively after each selection without retraining, which reduces redundancy within acquisition batches.

Result: On rMD17 data, AdaptNTK achieved highest mean correlations with force errors (Spearman 0.68, Pearson 0.71) and matched three-member ensemble in error retention. In active learning experiments, AdaptNTK achieved lowest force errors across rMD17 and Transition-1X datasets, with particularly strong performance on transition-state configurations. It provides 2.6-fold speedup per Transition-1X cycle relative to ensembles.

Conclusion: AdaptNTK provides efficient single-model uncertainty estimation with sequential updates for data-efficient active learning, offering computational advantages over ensemble methods while maintaining or exceeding their performance in uncertainty quantification and active learning applications.

Abstract: Machine learning interatomic potentials bridge the gap between quantum chemical precision and classical computational speed, enabling molecular dynamics simulations with first-principles accuracy. Their reliability is often improved through active learning, which iteratively expands the training set by identifying uncertain, out-of-distribution configurations. Existing uncertainty-quantification methods often involve a trade-off between computational cost and reliability, and generally cannot account for redundancy as an acquisition batch is assembled. Here, we introduce AdaptNTK, a single-model framework that measures uncertainty as a regularized Mahalanobis distance in empirical neural tangent kernel (NTK) feature space. With the NTK features fixed during acquisition, the uncertainty depends on the acquired configurations but not their reference labels. This allows the uncertainty to be updated recursively after each selection without retraining, reducing redundancy within an acquisition batch. On held-out rMD17 data, AdaptNTK achieves the highest mean correlations with force errors (Spearman 0.68, Pearson 0.71) and matches a three-member ensemble in error retention. In active learning experiments, AdaptNTK achieves the lowest force errors across rMD17 and Transition-1X, with particularly strong performance on transition-state configurations in Transition-1X. AdaptNTK provides a 2.6-fold speedup per Transition-1X cycle relative to the ensemble, providing efficient single-model uncertainty estimation with sequential updates for data-efficient active learning.

</details>


### [159] [A hybrid quantum-classical neural network for learning to route](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00489)
*Marcus Rolf Peter Ritt,Alexsandro Santos da Rosa Júnior,Marcos Vinicius Reballo,Cesar Augusto do Amaral,Fernando Augusto Caletti de Barros*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hybrid quantum-classical neural networks can compress parameter-heavy routing models by replacing encoder feed-forward modules with small quantum neural networks, maintaining solution quality at small/medium scales while reducing parameters by 56.6%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether small quantum neural networks can effectively replace parameter-heavy classical modules in attention-based routing models to achieve model compression while maintaining competitive performance in neural combinatorial optimization.

Method: The researchers study hybrid quantum-classical neural networks for routing heuristics, specifically examining whether small quantum neural networks can replace certain modules in attention-based routing models. They focus on the capacitated vehicle routing problem and test different design approaches, ultimately finding encoder feed-forward replacement to be the most promising strategy.

Result: Encoder feed-forward replacement reduces model parameters by 56.6% while keeping the hybrid model close to classical baseline performance at small and medium instance sizes. However, performance gap grows with larger instances. Classical routing algorithms remain highly competitive and often superior on fixed Euclidean test sets.

Conclusion: While no quantum advantage or solver dominance is demonstrated, encoder feed-forward replacement is identified as a viable hybrid-module compression strategy for neural combinatorial optimization, offering parameter reduction without significantly sacrificing performance at smaller scales.

Abstract: This work studies hybrid quantum-classical neural networks for learning routing heuristics. Specifically, this paper asks whether small quantum neural networks can replace parameter-heavy modules inside a competitive attention-based routing model while maintaining solution quality. For the capacitated vehicle routing problem, encoder feed-forward replacement emerges as the most promising design: it reduces the number of model parameters by 56.6% while keeping the hybrid model close to the classical neural baseline at small and medium instance sizes, although the gap grows for larger instances. This work also compares to classical routing algorithms, which remain highly competitive and often superior on the fixed Euclidean test sets. Our results therefore do not indicate quantum advantage or solver dominance, but identify encoder feed-forward replacement as a viable hybrid-module compression strategy for neural combinatorial optimization.

</details>


### [160] [VATO: A Vortex-Force-Aware Transformer Operator for Unsteady Separated Aerofoil Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00507)
*Xingxin Yang,Zhan Zhang,Yichen Li,Juan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VATO introduces a vortex-force-aware transformer operator for unsteady separated flow prediction, using two VFM-based methods to improve flow field accuracy and aerodynamic force predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate prediction of unsteady separated flows is challenging due to nonlinear dynamics, and while high-fidelity CFD is accurate, it's too expensive for repeated design/control use. Standard surrogate methods don't prioritize flow regions most relevant to aerodynamic loads.

Method: VATO couples Vortex Force Map (VFM) method with geometry-aware neural operator via two mechanisms: VATO-S adds VFM force-contribution field supervision during training only, and VATO-A uses VFM contribution/sensitivity fields to prioritize force-relevant source locations for residual cross attention.

Result: VATO-S reduces velocity, pressure, and vorticity errors by 10.4%, 1.0%, and 15.6% over 1-20ms lead times. VATO-A achieves reductions of 15.8%, 7.5%, and 31.2%. VATO-S has lowest VFM-derived drag error, VATO-A has lowest pressure-derived lift/drag errors. Both maintain improvements beyond training range.

Conclusion: Force-aware operator learning through VATO improves both flow-field prediction accuracy and aerodynamic functional accuracy in unsteady separated flows, demonstrating the value of incorporating physics-based force awareness into neural operators.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of unsteady separated flows is challenging because the aerodynamic loads depend on nonlinear separation and vortex-shedding dynamics. Although high-fidelity CFD resolves these mechanisms, its cost limits repeated use in design and control. Standard field-level surrogate training, however, does not distinguish the flow regions that contribute most strongly to the aerodynamic loads. We introduce VATO (Vortex-Force-Aware Transformer Operator), which couples the Vortex Force Map (VFM) method to a geometry-aware neural operator through two complementary mechanisms. VATO-S adds training-only supervision of the local VFM force-contribution field, with no increase in model size or inference cost. VATO-A uses VFM contribution and sensitivity fields to prioritise force-relevant source locations for residual cross attention. The methods are evaluated on unsteady CFD data for double-edged-plate aerofoils over 54 trajectories from nine geometries. Over lead times of 1-20~ms, VATO-S reduces velocity, pressure, and vorticity errors by 10.4\%, 1.0\%, and 15.6\%, respectively, while VATO-A achieves reductions of 15.8\%, 7.5\%, and 31.2\%. VATO-S gives the lowest VFM-derived drag error, whereas VATO-A gives the lowest pressure-derived lift and drag errors. Over lead times extending 50\% beyond the training range, VATO-A retains a 26.9\% reduction in vorticity error and larger improvements in all four force readouts, despite reduced gains in velocity and pressure. These results show that force-aware operator learning can improve both flow-field prediction and aerodynamic functional accuracy in unsteady separated flows.

</details>


### [161] [Learning Task-Specific Antibody Representations via Function-Aware Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00518)
*Ayan Goel,Thomas A. Walton,Amirali Aghazadeh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Function-aware masking strategies for antibody language models that align mask placement with functional priors improve performance on specific tasks up to 14% on structure tasks and 5.9x on CDR tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current antibody language models pretrained via MLM don't leverage the corruption process as inductive bias, and while CDR masking helps binding predictions, antibodies have diverse biological priors across various functions.

Method: Introduced function-aware masking algorithms that align mask placement with specific functional priors (IMGT annotations or structure predictions) to shape representation space. Developed hybrid masking strategies integrating multiple priors to balance reconstruction across binding, structural, and biophysical objectives.

Result: Specialist masking strategies significantly improve performance on respective objectives: up to 14% gain on structure-related tasks and up to 5.9x improvement on CDR-related tasks. Hybrid masking improves performance across multiple functional axes.

Conclusion: Informed mask placement provides a parameter-free mechanism for imposing functional inductive biases in antibody language model training, enabling better performance on diverse antibody tasks.

Abstract: Antibody-specific language models pretrained via masked language modeling (MLM) learn representations that are critical for downstream sequence design and property prediction tasks. Yet, the corruption process itself is rarely leveraged as a source of inductive bias during pretraining. While preferentially masking complementarity-determining regions (CDRs) improves binding-related predictions, antibodies possess diverse biological priors over a variety of functions. Herein, we introduce function-aware masking, a family of pretraining algorithms that align mask placement with specific functional priors (e.g., from IMGT annotations or structure predictions) to shape the learned representation space. We show that these specialist masking strategies significantly improve performance on their respective objectives, yielding up to a 14% gain on structure-related tasks and up to a 5.9x improvement on CDR-related tasks. To further improve performance across multiple functional axes, we develop hybrid masking strategies that integrate multiple priors, balancing reconstruction over binding, structural, and biophysical objectives. Our results demonstrate that informed mask placement provides a parameter-free mechanism for imposing functional inductive biases in antibody language model training.

</details>


### [162] [Why Multi-Layer Message Passing Works: Completeness Theory for Graph Neural Network Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00528)
*Pingbing Ming,Han Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hypergraph Neural Networks with 3-body message passing are universal approximators for potential energy surfaces, with multi-layer completeness theory justifying common practice of using smaller cutoffs than physical interaction ranges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide rigorous theoretical justification for the common practice in machine-learned interatomic potentials where multi-layer message passing neural networks use per-layer cutoffs smaller than the physical interaction range, which lacks theoretical foundation despite being widely adopted.

Method: Develops a multi-layer completeness theory showing that L layers of message passing on sparse, cutoff-based graphs achieve same representational power as full L-hop neighborhoods, under certain conditions (generic configurations, overlap condition, connectivity condition). Analyzes Hypergraph Neural Networks with 3-body message passing.

Result: Proves that Hypergraph Neural Network with 3-body message passing is a universal approximator for potential energy surfaces. Shows that DPA3 and CHGNet architectures inherit universal approximation capabilities as immediate consequences.

Conclusion: Provides first rigorous justification for practical GNN-based interatomic potentials using multi-layer message passing with smaller cutoffs than physical ranges, establishing theoretical foundation for widely used architectures.

Abstract: We prove that the Hypergraph Neural Network, an invariant architecture with 3-body message passing, is a universal approximator for potential energy surfaces. Our main contribution is a multi-layer completeness theory. We show that $L$ layers of message passing on sparse, cutoff-based graphs achieve the same representational power as having access to the full $L$-hop neighborhood, provided the configurations are generic, satisfy an overlap condition and a connectivity condition. This provides the first rigorous justification for the common practice of using multi-layer message passing with a per-layer cutoff smaller than the physical interaction range, the setting used by virtually all practical graph neural network based machine-learned interatomic potentials. As immediate consequences, we show that both DPA3 and CHGNet architectures inherit universal approximation.

</details>


### [163] [DeSyR: A Decoupled Symbolic Recovery Framework with PINN-Guided Structure Search and Physics-Informed Coefficient Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00530)
*Pancheng Niu,Jun Guo,Qiaolin He,Jingcai Guo,Yanchao Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeSyR is a decoupled symbolic recovery framework that uses physics-informed neural networks to discover symbolic topologies for differential equations, then refines coefficients using only physics constraints to achieve near-exact recovery with 14 orders of magnitude error reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering compact explicit solutions from neural approximations is challenging because imperfect teacher data can guide symbolic topology search and coefficient estimation, potentially imposing teacher errors on the final recovered coefficients. There's a need for methods that can use approximate neural teachers for topology discovery without inheriting their error scale.

Method: DeSyR uses a physics-informed neural network to guide repeated searches constructing candidate topologies with provisional constants. Once a topology is fixed, coefficients are refined solely from the governing equation and prescribed constraints, followed by gated selection and verification. The framework decouples topology discovery from coefficient refinement, with physics-only refinement for coefficients.

Result: Evaluated on 15 differential-equation problems across 18 configurations (high-order, space-time, multidimensional, nonlinear, coupled systems). Achieved 99.23% convergence rate among free-parameter refits, with configuration-level median refined relative L2 errors of 2.31×10⁻¹⁴ or lower. In same-topology comparisons, refinement reduced error by 8-14 orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: Approximate neural teachers can effectively guide topology discovery without imposing their error scale on final recovered coefficients, provided a target-capable topology is retained and physics-only refinement converges. The decoupled approach enables near-exact recovery of symbolic solutions from neural approximations.

Abstract: Recovering compact explicit solutions from neural approximations is challenging when imperfect teacher data guide symbolic topology search and coefficient estimation. We present DeSyR, a decoupled symbolic recovery framework for differential equations. A physics-informed neural network guides repeated searches to construct candidate topologies with provisional constants. Once a topology is fixed, its coefficients are refined solely from the governing equation and prescribed constraints, followed by gated selection and verification. For linear fixed-topology parameterizations, we characterize teacher-error inheritance and show that finite-weight mixed data--physics fitting retains an $O(β^{-1})$ teacher-dependent contribution when the teacher error projects onto the model space. Under well-posedness, representability, zero-residual attainment, and discrete determinacy, physics-only refinement conditionally recovers exact coefficients; for nonlinear parameterizations, the corresponding guarantees are local. DeSyR is evaluated on 15 differential-equation problems across 18 configurations covering high-order, space--time, multidimensional, nonlinear, and coupled systems. A candidate-level audit yields a 99.23% convergence rate among free-parameter refits, while every selected refinement involving free coefficients converges. Configuration-level median refined relative $L_2$ errors are $2.31\times10^{-14}$ or lower. In same-topology comparisons, refinement reduces error by eight to fourteen orders of magnitude. These results show that an approximate neural teacher can guide topology discovery without imposing its error scale on final recovered coefficients, provided a target-capable topology is retained and physics-only refinement converges.

</details>


### [164] [GenONet: A Generative operator Network for High-Resolution Precipitation Nowcasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00544)
*Mohammad Kian Golkar,Luciano Alves de Oliveira,Mohammad Khanjani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GenONet combines Deep Operator Network (DeepONet) with GAN framework for 3-hour precipitation forecasting, using physics-informed loss to produce sharp, physically consistent predictions that outperform baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for precipitation nowcasting deteriorate over longer forecast horizons, producing blurry forecasts that fail to capture complex storm evolution, limiting their practical utility for severe weather impacts reduction.

Method: Proposes Spatio-Temporal U-DeepONet (GenONet) that integrates Deep Operator Network (DeepONet) as generator in GAN framework. Uses adversarial training with spatio-temporal discriminator to produce sharp forecasts, and incorporates physics-informed loss based on Moisture Conservation Equation for physical consistency.

Result: Quantitative evaluations show GenONet achieves higher scores on most metrics, especially for high-intensity events and longer lead times. Qualitatively produces structurally coherent forecasts that maintain integrity while baselines degrade. Ablation study confirms benefit of physics-informed loss.

Conclusion: GenONet successfully addresses limitations of existing deep learning models by combining operator learning with adversarial training and physical constraints, enabling accurate long-range (3-hour) precipitation forecasting with sharp, physically plausible results.

Abstract: High-resolution precipitation nowcasting is critical for reducing the impacts of severe weather but remains difficult because of rapid storm evolution. Deep learning models have shown great promise for this task, but their predictive skill often deteriorates over longer forecast horizons. This leads to increasingly blurry forecasts that fail to capture the complex, non-linear evolution of storm systems. In order to address these limitations, we introduce Spatio-Temporal U-DeepONet (GenONet), a novel architecture for long-range precipitation forecasting up to 3 hours, specifically designed to produce sharp and physically consistent results. GenONet's architecture pioneers the use of a Deep Operator Network (DeepONet) as a generator within a Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) framework for this task. The DeepONet learns the continuous-time dynamics of precipitation, ensuring stability over long forecast horizons. Adversial training against a spatio-temporal discriminator compels the model to produce sharp, coherent forecasts, while a physics-informed loss regularizer, derived from the Moisture Conservation Equation, improves physical plausibility in our ablation setting. Quantitative evaluations show that our model achieves consistently higher scores on most of the metrics, especially for highintensity events and at longer lead times. Qualitatively, GenONet produces structurally coherent forecasts that maintain their integrity, whereas baseline models degrade into indistinct patterns. Finally, an ablation study confirms the benefit of this physics-informed loss, highlighting the strength of combining operator learning with adversarial training.

</details>


### [165] [Manifold-Aware General Coded Computing for Straggler-Resilient Distributed Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00552)
*Parsa Moradi,Mohammad Ali Maddah-Ali*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Manifold-aware encoding for coded computing that preserves data structure instead of eliminating it, significantly reducing recovery error under straggling compared to standard methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coded computing designs ignore the intrinsic structure of input data, while in machine learning tasks, data structure is crucial for meaningful computation. Traditional communication system approaches (compression before coding) don't work well for coded computation.

Method: Proposes manifold-aware encoding inspired by graph-based manifold learning. Instead of imposing artificial low-dimensional structures, designs coded samples that follow the natural low-dimensional manifolds where high-dimensional datasets concentrate. General coded computing (GCC) framework with structure-preserving encoding.

Result: Experiments on neural network inference and high-dimensional polynomial evaluation show the proposed strategy consistently and significantly reduces mean squared recovery error under straggling compared with standard GCC.

Conclusion: Coded-computing schemes should preserve and leverage data structure rather than eliminate it. Manifold-aware encoding effectively exploits the intrinsic geometry of data, leading to better error recovery performance in distributed computing systems with stragglers.

Abstract: Existing coded-computing designs do not explicitly exploit the intrinsic structure of the input data. In communication systems, statistical structure and redundancy are often removed through source coding (or compression) before channel coding is applied. This principle, however, does not transfer directly to coded computation. In many computational tasks, particularly in machine learning, the structure of the data is precisely what the computation seeks to exploit to infer outputs or learn meaningful patterns. Consequently, coded-computing schemes should preserve and leverage this structure in their code design, rather than ignoring or eliminating it through source coding.
  This observation motivates a different perspective on code construction. In many channel-coding schemes, such as Reed-Solomon codes, coded symbols are generated by evaluating a low-dimensional algebraic representation at selected points. In contrast, many high-dimensional datasets naturally concentrate near low-dimensional manifolds. In this paper, we exploit this intrinsic geometry by designing coded samples that follow the natural manifold of the data, rather than imposing an artificial low-dimensional structure unrelated to the data distribution. Inspired by graph-based manifold learning, we propose a manifold-aware encoding strategy for general coded computing (GCC). Experiments on neural network inference and high-dimensional polynomial evaluation demonstrate that the proposed strategy consistently and significantly reduces the mean squared recovery error under straggling compared with standard GCC.

</details>


### [166] [EEG-VID: Task-Guided Latent Predictive Pretraining for EEG Decoding and Assistive Target Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00566)
*Guanzhong Sun,Junyi Ma,Yuxuan Wu,Yanzi Miao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EEG-VID: A task-guided latent predictive pretraining framework for EEG decoding that improves accuracy across multiple datasets by predicting future latent EEG states and using supervised fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of EEG decoding under session and subject shifts (domain shift problem), where EEG signals vary across different recording sessions and individuals. Current approaches may not adequately handle these shifts, limiting practical applicability.

Method: EEG-VID uses a two-stage framework: 1) Self-supervised pretraining predicting future latent EEG states from recent history using an exponential-moving-average target encoder with weak task guidance, 2) Supervised fine-tuning on downstream tasks. The approach includes candidate-constrained target selection for assistive applications.

Result: Across VIG-48 and BCI Competition IV-2a/IV-2b datasets, Stage 1 improved mean accuracy in 41 of 42 matched comparisons, with maximum gain of 16.22 percentage points. Achieved 6.52% Top-1 and 30.50% Top-5 accuracy on cross-day VIG-48 task. In offline robot-scene study, candidate-constrained target selection reached 40.24% vs 25% chance level after subject-specific calibration.

Conclusion: Task-guided latent prediction is an effective, transferable pretraining strategy for EEG decoding, particularly beneficial for handling session and subject shifts. The framework shows promise for practical applications including scene-constrained assistive target selection in brain-computer interfaces.

Abstract: We propose EEG-VID, a task-guided latent predictive pretraining framework for EEG decoding under session and subject shifts. EEG-VID predicts future latent EEG states from recent history using an exponential-moving-average target encoder and weak task guidance, followed by supervised fine-tuning. Across VIG-48 and BCI Competition IV-2a/IV-2b, Stage 1 improves mean accuracy in 41 of 42 matched backbone-dataset-protocol comparisons, including all 12 leave-one-subject-out settings, with a maximum gain of 16.22 percentage points. On the 48-region cross-day VIG-48 task, EEG-VID achieves 6.52% Top-1 and 30.50% Top-5 accuracy. In a separate six-participant offline robot-scene study, candidate-constrained target selection reaches 40.24% versus a 25% chance level after subject-specific calibration. These results support task-guided latent prediction as a transferable pretraining strategy for EEG decoding and scene-constrained assistive target selection.

</details>


### [167] [GeoPAR: Large-Scale Multi-Agent Combinatorial Optimization with Geometry-Guided Parallel Autoregressive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00577)
*Wenjian Wu,Zesheng Jia,Jiaying Tang,Benyuan Yang,Jin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoPAR is a geometry-guided parallel autoregressive RL framework that improves large-scale multi-agent combinatorial optimization by incorporating local geometric structures and conflict-aware assignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing parallel autoregressive neural solvers for multi-agent combinatorial optimization suffer from performance degradation on large-scale instances due to weak modeling of local geometric structures and post-generation handling of conflicting task selections.

Method: GeoPAR integrates three components: (1) projection-window sparse geometry mechanism for building lightweight local candidate neighborhoods, (2) sparse edge-biased attention to inject geometric relations into node representations, and (3) cache-guided conflict-aware assignment that reuses geometric cache during decoding to suppress duplicate selections.

Result: Experiments on heterogeneous vehicle routing and open multi-depot pickup-and-delivery problems show that GeoPAR improves large-scale zero-shot generalization while substantially reducing rollout steps and maintaining efficient inference.

Conclusion: GeoPAR successfully addresses limitations of existing parallel autoregressive solvers by incorporating geometry guidance and conflict-aware mechanisms, enabling scalable multi-agent combinatorial optimization with improved performance and efficiency.

Abstract: Multi-agent combinatorial optimization problems are notoriously challenging due to their NP-hard nature. Recent parallel autoregressive neural solvers improve inference efficiency by allowing agents to make decisions simultaneously, but their performance often degrades on large-scale instances. This is largely attributable to weak modeling of local geometric structures and the fact that conflicting task selections are handled only after action generation. To address these limitations, we propose GeoPAR, a geometry-guided parallel autoregressive reinforcement learning framework for scalable multi-agent combinatorial optimization. GeoPAR integrates three key components: (1) a projection-window sparse geometry mechanism that builds lightweight local candidate neighborhoods through multi-directional projections, (2) sparse edge-biased attention that injects these geometric relations into node representations, and (3) cache-guided conflict-aware assignment that reuses the geometric cache during decoding to suppress duplicate selections of exclusive tasks. Experiments on heterogeneous vehicle routing and open multi-depot pickup-and-delivery problems show that GeoPAR improves large-scale zero-shot generalization while substantially reducing rollout steps and maintaining efficient inference.

</details>


### [168] [CRAFT: Fine-Tuning Pre-hoc Explainability in AI-native 6G RAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00590)
*Pranshav Gajjar,Vijay K Shah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CRAFT enables pre-hoc reasoning in telecom AI by overcoming cold-start barriers for small language models through data-centric fine-tuning with verified reasoning traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current telecom LLM training paradigms (like SFT) only provide post hoc rationalization, making decision processes unauditable. Pre-hoc reasoning (causal reasoning trace before output) is needed for transparent AI in 6G RAN, but existing RL methods like GRPO fail due to cold-start problems where SLMs struggle to learn both output format and label prediction simultaneously.

Method: CRAFT (Cold-start Reasoning Alignment via Fine-Tuning) is a data-centric method that autonomously generates verified datasets of (input, trace, label) triplets. It fine-tunes small language models on this verified data using low-rank adaptation (LoRA), requiring substantially less compute than GRPO-based methods.

Result: On TRACTOR and IC xApp telecom datasets, CRAFT achieves 86.5% accuracy and 94.6% F1 with no parse failures, while GRPO and SFT+GRPO achieve ≤28% and ≤53.5% F1 with multiple parse failures. CRAFT-initialized policies provide robust foundation for subsequent GRPO fine-tuning and consume 59% less energy than GRPO baselines.

Conclusion: CRAFT offers a sustainable, efficient path to deployable, auditable AI in 6G RAN by enabling pre-hoc reasoning through cold-start resolution, achieving superior performance with lower energy consumption while maintaining compatibility with subsequent RL fine-tuning.

Abstract: The next generation of mobile networks is envisioned as fully AI-native, with AI-RAN architectures embedding small language models (SLMs) to perform reasoning over real-time telemetry. The state-of-the-art training paradigms for telecom LLMs, exemplified by RANSTRUCT-style supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on curated instruction data, are limited to post hoc rationalization. Here, the explanations, when produced at all, are generated after or independently of the decision, leaving the decision process unauditable. Pre-hoc reasoning, where a causal reasoning trace is produced before the output label, is preferable, and the broader LLM reasoning literature has made real progress toward it via RL methods such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Here we observe that transplanting this recipe into the telecom setting runs into a cold-start barrier: SLMs either learn to output the desired format or learn to predict the label, but rarely both. We identify this barrier and propose CRAFT, which stands for Cold-start Reasoning Alignment via Fine-Tuning, a data-centric method to autonomously generate a verified dataset of (input, trace, label) triplets. CRAFT fine-tunes SLMs on this verified data using low-rank adaptation (LoRA), requiring substantially less compute and wall-clock time than GRPO-based methods. On the TRACTOR and IC xApp telecom datasets, CRAFT achieves up to 86.5% and 94.6% for accuracy and F1 with no parse failures, while direct GRPO and SFT+GRPO fail to exceed 28% and 53.5% F1 with multiple parse failures. We further show that CRAFT-initialized policies serve as a robust foundation for subsequent GRPO fine-tuning, as under diverse reward functions the performance remains consistent with no parse failures. Finally, we demonstrate that CRAFT consumes 59% less energy than GRPO-based baselines, making it a sustainable path to deployable, auditable AI in 6G RAN.

</details>


### [169] [Topological Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00597)
*Benoît Guérand,Tan Minh Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Topological Steering uses topological data analysis (persistence diagrams) to create a more robust framework for steering LLM behavior compared to existing activation-space methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing behavioral control methods for LLMs that intervene directly in activation/feature space are sensitive to outliers, distributional shifts, noise, and other local perturbations. There's a need for more robust steering approaches that can handle these limitations.

Method: Proposes Topological Steering, a framework that uses topological data analysis (specifically persistence diagrams) to capture global structure rather than purely local structure in activation spaces. This connects activation-based steering with TDA principles.

Result: The method consistently modifies LLM behavior across multiple model families and model sizes, demonstrating more robust behavioral control compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: Topological Steering provides a novel and robust framework for controlling LLM behavior by leveraging topological representations of activation spaces, addressing the sensitivity issues of current methods through global structural analysis.

Abstract: With the rapid rise of large language models (LLMs), controlling undesirable model behaviors has become increasingly important. Existing behavioral control methods typically intervene directly in activation or feature space, but such approaches can be sensitive to outliers, distributional shifts, noise, and other local perturbations. Motivated by Topological Data Analysis (TDA), which captures global rather than purely local structure, we propose Topological Steering, a new framework for steering LLM behavior through the topological representation of activation spaces. Using persistence diagrams, our method connects activation-based steering with TDA and enables more robust behavioral control. We show that Topological Steering consistently modifies LLM behavior across multiple model families and model sizes.

</details>


### [170] [Confess What You Know: Forget-Set Misalignment with Model Knowledge in LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00605)
*Miso Kim,Georu Lee,Seungwon Jeong,Woojin Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses the problem of forget-set misalignment in machine unlearning for LLMs, where predefined forget sets don't match what models actually memorized, and proposes CONFS - a data-blind framework that constructs model-aligned forget sets by eliciting the model's memorized knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current machine unlearning approaches for LLMs assume that pre-defined forget sets match what the model has actually memorized, but this breaks in realistic privacy settings where original training data is inaccessible, leading to either incomplete unlearning or unnecessary degradation of model utility.

Method: The authors first analyze the problem of forget-set misalignment through gradient-level analysis, identifying two cases (Under Unlearning and Out-of-Knowledge Unlearning). They then propose CONFS (CONfession-to-Forget-Set), a data-blind framework that constructs model-aligned forget sets by eliciting and formalizing the model's memorized knowledge through inference-time probing.

Result: CONFS approaches gold-standard performance on several metrics across synthetic, multimodal, and real-world benchmarks, achieving a competitive forgetting-utility balance while preserving utility better than other data-blind forget-set constructions.

Conclusion: Forget-set misalignment is a fundamental issue in LLM unlearning that arises from misaligned targets rather than optimization choices, and CONFS provides an effective data-blind solution by constructing model-aligned forget sets that balance privacy protection with utility preservation.

Abstract: Machine unlearning for large language models (LLMs) often assumes that a pre-defined forget set matches what the model has memorized, but this frequently breaks in realistic privacy settings where the original training data is inaccessible. We term this gap forget-set misalignment and identify two cases. In Under Unlearning, the forget set omits memorized information and leakage persists. In Out-of-Knowledge Unlearning, the algorithm is driven to "forget" knowledge the model never learned, perturbing parameters and degrading utility. Using gradient-level analysis, we show these behaviors arise from misaligned unlearning targets rather than specific optimization choices. We then propose CONfession-to-Forget-Set (CONFS), a data-blind framework that constructs model-aligned forget sets by eliciting and formalizing the model's memorized knowledge. Across synthetic, multimodal, and real-world benchmarks, CONFS approaches Gold-standard performance on several metrics and achieves a competitive forgetting-utility balance, while preserving utility better than other data-blind forget-set constructions.

</details>


### [171] [Breaking the Structural Identity: Personalized Federated LoRA Fine-tuning under Rank Heterogeneity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00632)
*Lei Wang,Jieming Bian,Letian Zhang,Jie Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedRoRA is a federated learning framework that enables fine-grained personalization for LLMs by decoupling adaptation into shared global directions and personalized rank-wise magnitudes, addressing both resource and data heterogeneity challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face challenges when adapting to privacy-sensitive distributed datasets through federated learning. Existing rank-heterogeneous methods for federated fine-tuning provide unified global models that fail to capture client-specific features in non-IID (non-independent and identically distributed) data scenarios, while also struggling with resource heterogeneity issues.

Method: FedRoRA decouples adaptation into two components: shared global directions and personalized rank-wise magnitudes governed by learnable diagonal scales. The framework uses singular value decomposition (SVD) to extract a global subspace on the server side, then redistributes client-specific initializations through a personalized projection and top-k selection mechanism.

Result: Extensive experiments on Natural Language Understanding (NLU) and Natural Language Generation (NLG) benchmarks demonstrate that FedRoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: FedRoRA successfully addresses the dual challenges of resource and data heterogeneity in federated learning for LLMs by providing fine-grained personalization through rank-wise adaptation mechanisms, enabling better adaptation to non-IID distributed datasets while maintaining efficiency.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success across diverse domains, but their adaptation to privacy-sensitive, distributed datasets remains a challenge. While Federated Learning (FL) combined with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) provides a resource-efficient paradigm for collaborative fine-tuning, practical deployments are hindered by the dual challenges of resource heterogeneity and data heterogeneity. Existing rank-heterogeneous methods primarily focus on bridging dimension mismatches for aggregation but typically provide a unified global model for all clients sharing the same rank, failing to capture client-specific features in non-IID scenarios. In this paper, we propose FedRoRA (Federated Rank-wise Personalized LoRA), a novel framework that enables fine-grained personalization within rank-heterogeneous federations. FedRoRA decouples adaptation into shared global directions and personalized rank-wise magnitudes governed by learnable diagonal scales. On the server side, it extracts a global subspace via singular value decomposition (SVD) and redistributes client-specific initializations through a personalized projection and top-$k$ selection mechanism. Extensive experiments on NLU and NLG benchmarks demonstrate that FedRoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [172] [DK-GBMKKM: Dynamic Kernel-Space Granular-Ball Multiple Kernel $k$-Means Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00647)
*Xiaoyu Lian,Yuchao Zhang,Shuyin Xia,Siqi Zhong,Xuzhao Xiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DK-GBMKKM is a new clustering method that combines multiple kernel k-means with granular-ball representations, dynamically updating granular balls in kernel space for better noise resistance and performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard multiple kernel k-means has two main limitations: 1) Pointwise optimization is sensitive to noisy and boundary samples, and 2) It repeatedly operates on sample-scale kernel matrices which is computationally intensive. Additionally, existing granular-ball methods generate balls in input space, which may not align with evolving fused-kernel geometry during multiple kernel learning.

Method: The proposed DK-GBMKKM method: 1) Generates granular balls dynamically in the current fused kernel space rather than static input space, 2) Alternates between kernel-weight learning and granular-ball membership updates to adapt representation to changing kernel geometry, 3) Constructs sample-size-weighted granular-ball kernel that preserves contributions from balls of different sizes, with established positive semidefiniteness properties.

Result: Experiments on 12 public datasets demonstrate strong overall clustering performance of DK-GBMKKM compared to existing methods. The method has been open-sourced for reproducibility.

Conclusion: DK-GBMKKM successfully integrates multiple kernel learning with granular-ball representations by dynamically adapting balls to fused-kernel geometry, achieving robust performance against noise while being more efficient than pointwise optimization approaches.

Abstract: Multiple kernel $k$-means integrates complementary nonlinear similarities by learning a combination of base kernels. Its pointwise optimization, however, is sensitive to noisy and boundary samples and repeatedly operates on sample-scale kernel matrices. Granular-ball representations organize local sample groups into mesoscopic units, but granular balls generated once in the input space may be inconsistent with the fused-kernel geometry that evolves during multiple kernel learning. We propose dynamic kernel-space granular-ball multiple kernel $k$-means (DK-GBMKKM). The method generates granular balls in the current fused kernel space and alternates kernel-weight learning with granular-ball membership updates, allowing the representation to adapt to changes in the fused-kernel geometry. A sample-size-weighted granular-ball kernel is further constructed to preserve the contributions of balls of different sizes, and its positive semidefiniteness and related equivalence properties are established. Experiments on 12 public datasets demonstrate the strong overall clustering performance of DK-GBMKKM. The code has been open-sourced for reproducibility: https://github.com/lianxiaoyu724/DK-GBMKKM.

</details>


### [173] [EEG-AS: Instance-Level Foundation Model Selection for EEG Foundation Models via Behavior Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00653)
*Yunzhen Zhang,Ruoxi Piao,Hasan Onur Keles,Mustafa Misir*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EEG-AS is an instance-level algorithm selection framework that efficiently chooses the best EEG foundation model for individual neural signals by learning to predict model performance without running all models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While EEG foundation models perform well on neural decoding tasks, no single model consistently performs best across all datasets or individual EEG instances. Current approaches lack instance-level model selection methods, creating a gap between single best solver performance and oracle performance.

Method: Formulates EEG foundation model selection as instance-level Algorithm Selection problem. Uses EEG embeddings, handcrafted neurophysiological features, and an anchor foundation model. During training, learns to reconstruct unavailable foundation-model behaviors from privileged prediction tokens. During inference, estimates these behaviors without executing entire model portfolio.

Result: Experiments on seven public EEG benchmarks show EEG-AS substantially narrows the gap between Single Best Solver performance and oracle upper bound for each instance.

Conclusion: Instance-level Algorithm Selection is effective for adaptive deployment of EEG foundation models, enabling more efficient and accurate model selection for individual EEG instances.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is a non-invasive technique for measuring neural activity and has been widely used in neuroscience applications. Recent advances in EEG foundation models have enabled strong performance across diverse neural decoding tasks. However, no single foundation model consistently performs best across datasets or individual EEG instances, while instance-level model selection remains largely unexplored. To address this limitation, we formulate EEG foundation model selection as an instance-level Algorithm Selection (AS) problem. We propose \textbf{EEG-AS}, an instance-level algorithm selection framework that characterizes each EEG instance using inference-available latent EEG embeddings, handcrafted neurophysiological features, and an anchor foundation model. During training, EEG-AS learns to reconstruct unavailable foundation-model behaviors from privileged prediction tokens conditioned on an anchor foundation model, while during inference it estimates these behaviors without executing the entire model portfolio, enabling efficient selection from seven EEG foundation models. Experiments on seven public EEG benchmarks demonstrate that EEG-AS substantially narrows the gap between the Single Best Solver (SBS) and the oracle upper bound for each instance. These results highlight the effectiveness of instance-level AS for adaptive deployment of EEG foundation models.

</details>


### [174] [HarmoCore: Functional Latent Diffusion for Sparse Reconstruction of Oscillatory Wave Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00679)
*Lihao Chen,Xinyu Zhang,Panqi Chen,Lei Cheng,Ting Zhang,Jianlong Li,Shikai Fang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HarmoCore: A compact continuous latent representation for reconstructing highly oscillatory complex-valued wave fields from extreme sparse observations using structured functional Tucker cores and frequency-conditioned diffusion prior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstructing oscillatory wave fields from sparse sensors is severely underdetermined. Wave responses are complex-valued, frequency-sensitive, highly oscillatory, and often only extremely sparse observations are available due to costly simulation and sensing. Existing methods (low-rank, operator, diffusion) are designed for real-valued, smoother fields and inefficient for oscillatory complex fields, especially scaling poorly to 3D.

Method: Proposes HarmoCore which uses a generative prior in a compact continuous structured wave-field latent. It represents joint real-imaginary channels with Functional Tucker cores over shared continuous spatial bases, learns a frequency-conditioned core diffusion prior, and performs Diffusion Posterior Sampling directly in core space. Uses multilinear decoder to induce explicit likelihood guidance at fixed sensor coordinates, avoiding dense pixel-space correction, with optional target-equation residual guidance for physical consistency.

Result: Experiments on 2D Helmholtz, 2D synthetic wave fields, and 3D Helmholtz show substantial gains under 1%-2% sensing while remaining practical in three dimensions, demonstrating effective reconstruction from extreme sparse observations.

Conclusion: HarmoCore provides an effective framework for reconstructing oscillatory complex-valued wave fields from extremely sparse observations by using structured functional latent representations and frequency-conditioned diffusion in compact core space, overcoming limitations of existing methods while maintaining practicality for 3D applications.

Abstract: Reconstructing oscillatory wave fields from scattered sensors is a severely underdetermined inverse problem. Beyond the challenges of general physical-field reconstruction, wave responses are complex-valued, frequency-sensitive, and highly oscillatory, while costly simulation and sensing often leave only extreme-sparse observations. Existing low-rank, operator, and diffusion approaches are largely designed for real-valued, smoother fields; dense pixel-space diffusion is particularly inefficient for oscillatory complex fields and difficult to scale to 3D. We propose HarmoCore, which places a generative prior in a compact, continuous, and structured wave-field latent. HarmoCore represents joint real--imaginary channels with Functional Tucker cores over shared continuous spatial bases, learns a frequency-conditioned core diffusion prior, and performs Diffusion Posterior Sampling directly in core space. At fixed sensor coordinates, the multilinear decoder induces an explicit likelihood guidance operator, avoiding dense pixel-space correction. Optional target-equation residual guidance further promotes physical consistency. Experiments on 2D Helmholtz, 2D synthetic wave fields, and 3D Helmholtz show substantial gains under 1%--2% sensing while remaining practical in three dimensions.

</details>


### [175] [A Study of Hidden-State Optimization Order in Predictive Coding Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00686)
*Xueyuan Li,Danilo Vasconcellos Vargas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Boundary-first inference schedule for predictive coding networks improves feature learning by optimizing chunk boundaries first, then internal representations, boosting CIFAR-10 accuracy by up to 9.77%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local learning methods like predictive coding have weak feature learning due to unstructured local objectives. The researchers investigate whether optimizing hidden-state order can address this limitation.

Method: Proposed boundary-first inference schedule that partitions models into chunks, first coordinates hidden states at chunk boundaries, then refines representations within each chunk. Implemented in predictive coding networks (PCNs) which expose hidden activities and prediction errors during inference.

Result: On CIFAR-10: 9.77% accuracy improvement over standard predictive coding under standard parametrization; 5.51% improvement under μ-parametrization. Diagnostic analyses show: more non-trivial early-layer updates, lower initial-to-final CKA (Centered Kernel Alignment), more diverse layerwise gradients - all indicating stronger feature learning.

Conclusion: Boundary-first, chunk-based inference is a practical design principle for predictive-coding training that enhances feature learning. The approach motivates study in broader local-learning systems beyond PCNs.

Abstract: Local learning methods offer an alternative to end-to-end backpropagation, but their unstructured local objectives can produce weak feature learning in deep networks. We study whether the order of hidden-state optimization can address this limitation. We propose a boundary-first inference schedule that partitions a model into chunks, first coordinates hidden states at chunk boundaries, and then refines representations within each chunk. We instantiate this schedule in predictive coding networks (PCNs), a local-learning framework in which hidden activities and prediction errors are explicitly exposed during inference. On CIFAR-10, the resulting boundary-first predictive-coding instantiation improves accuracy over standard predictive coding by $9.77\%$ under a standard parametrization and by $5.51\%$ under a $μ$-parametrization. Diagnostic analyses further show more non-trivial early-layer updates, lower initial-to-final CKA, and more diverse layerwise gradients, consistent with stronger feature learning. These results support boundary-first, chunk-based inference as a practical design principle for predictive-coding training and motivate its study in broader local-learning systems.

</details>


### [176] [Verdict Instability of OOD Scores under Reference Resampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00691)
*Donghoon Lee,Shinjin Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method called "verdict instability" quantifies the reproducibility of out-of-distribution detection scores by measuring how verdicts change with different reference sets, showing that far-OOD queries have more stable verdicts than near-OOD ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-hoc OOD detectors rely on finite reference sets, making their scores estimates rather than exact values. Different reference sets would produce different verdicts, creating uncertainty that practitioners need to measure but current methods don't capture well.
