Method: Propose "verdict instability" metric using bootstrap resampling of the reference set to compute the standard deviation of scores. Derived a closed-form expression: instability = within-class dispersion of assigned class along query's direction / √(class's reference count).

Result: Far-OOD queries lie along low-variance directions in anisotropic embeddings, making distance-based scores most reproducible for them. Only local dispersion estimators have the expected sign correlation, while wrong-signed scores lead to worse abstention than random.

Conclusion: Verdict instability provides a parameter-free way to measure OOD detection reliability. Class imbalance is key to separating instability from score geometry. Practitioners must ensure their scores have the correct sign correlation to avoid harmful abstention.

Abstract: Post-hoc out-of-distribution detectors are fitted on a finite reference set, so every score they produce is an estimate. If we had chosen a different set, some verdicts would have moved. We measure that movement by resampling the reference set and recording the bootstrap standard deviation of the score, which we call verdict instability. It admits a closed form with no fitted parameters. The instability of a verdict is the within-class dispersion of the assigned class along the query's direction, divided by the square root of that class's reference count. That count is what separates verdict instability from the geometry of the score distribution, and it is identifiable only under class imbalance. Instability grows with the local dispersion. Far-OOD queries lie along the low-variance directions of an anisotropic embedding, so every distance-based score we test assigns its highest values to the verdicts that are most reproducible. Only estimators of local dispersion carry the sign a practitioner expects. We give a rule that predicts this sign for any score from a single label-free correlation, and abstention driven by a wrong-signed score turns out worse than abstention at random on every dataset we test.

</details>


### [177] [MUGEN: Generating Unlearnable Graph Examples for Multiple Learning Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00696)
*Ziyan Liu,Chengshuai Zhao,Huan Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MUGEN is the first framework for generating unlearnable graph examples that jointly protects all enabled downstream tasks (node classification, graph classification, link prediction) from unauthorized representation learning, using a shared GNN encoder with task-specific heads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph data across domains can expose valuable relational information to unauthorized representation learning, creating a need for data-level protection. Existing methods only protect against specific downstream tasks, leaving releases vulnerable to other plausible uses that data owners cannot anticipate.

Method: MUGEN uses a shared GNN encoder with task-specific heads to generate feature-perturbed releases protecting all enabled tasks. It introduces Task-Aligned Separability Objective (TASO) leveraging task prediction and classwise separability, and Type-Adaptive Perturbation (TAP) that tailors optimization to node-attribute types (discrete vs continuous).

Result: Experiments across 5 benchmarks, 4 GNN backends, and 3 learning paradigms show MUGEN generates transferable unlearnable graph examples across GNN backbones and all three tasks, remaining effective under adversarial training and data augmentation.

Conclusion: MUGEN provides the first comprehensive solution for protecting graph data against unauthorized representation learning across all enabled downstream tasks, addressing limitations of task-specific protection methods.

Abstract: Graph data across diverse domains can expose valuable relational information to unauthorized representation learning, creating a pressing need for protection against such misuse. Unlearnable examples offer a data-level defense by perturbing a training release so that models trained on it fail to generalize to clean data. Existing methods generate unlearnable graph examples for only a specified downstream task. Consequently, a release protected against one task may remain learnable for other plausible uses, including node classification, graph classification, and link prediction, which the data owner cannot anticipate. We introduce MUGEN, to our knowledge the first framework for generating unlearnable graph examples that jointly protect all enabled tasks. From one clean dataset, MUGEN produces a single feature-perturbed release that protects every enabled task through a shared GNN encoder and task-specific heads. We devise a Task-Aligned Separability Objective (TASO), which leverages task prediction and classwise separability to strengthen unlearnability and its transfer across GNN backbones and enabled tasks. We further introduce Type-Adaptive Perturbation (TAP), which tailors perturbation optimization to node-attribute type, with direct search over feasible hard flips that accept only loss-improving updates for discrete node attributes and customized gradient-based updates for continuous node features, thereby enabling strong unlearnability across both settings. Experiments across five benchmarks, four backends and three learning paradigms demonstrate that MUGEN generates transferable unlearnable graph examples across GNN backbones and all three tasks, and remains effective under adversarial training and data augmentation.

</details>


### [178] [Patterning in Practice: Debiasing Reward Models with Susceptibilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00699)
*George Wang,Elizabeth Donoway,Daniel Murfet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Patterning method reweights preference pairs by their susceptibility to debias reward models, achieving +14.2 pp improvement on RM-Bench Hard with transferability across model sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward models trained on human preferences suffer from length, formatting, and other stylistic biases that affect their performance, particularly on challenging benchmarks where style cues point against correctness.

Method: Using patterning technique that reweights each preference pair according to its measured effect on posterior expectation values of benchmark losses (its susceptibility) to debias a Gemma 2 9B Instruct reward model trained on Skywork-Reward-Preference v0.2 dataset.

Result: Achieved +14.2 ± 1.2 pp improvement on RM-Bench Hard split while preserving overall RM-Bench accuracy, comparable to SteerRM's +13.2 pp. The reweighting is interpretable and transferable: weights computed on Gemma 2 9B successfully debias Gemma 2 2B and 27B without recomputation, and partially transfer to Llama 3.1 8B.

Conclusion: Patterning provides an effective, interpretable, and transferable method for debiasing reward models against stylistic biases, successfully scaling beyond small models and synthetic tasks to large language models with practical implications for improving alignment.

Abstract: Reward models trained on human preferences are known to suffer from length, formatting, and other stylistic biases. In this paper we use patterning, which reweights each preference pair according to its measured effect on posterior expectation values of benchmark losses (its susceptibility), to debias a Gemma 2 9B Instruct reward model trained on Skywork-Reward-Preference v0.2. We obtain $+14.2 \pm 1.2$ pp on RM-Bench Hard, the split where style cues point against correctness (mean $\pm$ s.e.\ over 5 seeds), with overall RM-Bench accuracy preserved, comparable to the strongest Hard-split gain reported by the closest published comparator (SteerRM, $+13.2$ pp). We demonstrate in a simple case that the reweighting is interpretable by tracing a side effect of the intervention (a regression on a safety subset of RM-Bench) to a small class of training pairs, which we confirm by ablation. The weights also transfer: those computed on Gemma 2 9B debias Gemma 2 2B and 27B with no recomputation, and transfer partially to Llama 3.1 8B. This is the first application of patterning, a program grounded in singular learning theory, beyond small models and synthetic tasks.

</details>


### [179] [Online Self-Weighted Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00734)
*Haiquan Wen,Yiwei He,Bei Peng,Guangliang Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OSW-FT is a fine-tuning method that dynamically weights expert demonstrations based on the model's current performance, using online rollouts to adapt loss weights while keeping optimization anchored to expert trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard SFT treats all expert demonstrations equally regardless of the model's evolving capabilities during training, while RL methods require extensive sampling and can be unstable on difficult tasks. There's a need for a more adaptive yet stable approach that bridges these methods.

Method: OSW-FT augments SFT with online, trajectory-level weighting. For each query, it estimates the model's current success rate using a small number (typically 2) of inference-only rollouts, then rescales the standard SFT loss accordingly. The method maintains optimization direction from expert trajectories while adapting update magnitude online, connecting to variance-reduction principles.

Result: OSW-FT consistently improves over standard SFT on Qwen3 models ranging from 0.6B to 4B across multiple challenging benchmarks like AIME. It offers a favorable compute-performance trade-off, requiring only 2 online rollouts while achieving better performance.

Conclusion: OSW-FT provides a practical approach for fine-tuning small-to-medium LLMs on binary-verifiable reasoning tasks, bridging SFT and RL methods with online adaptive weighting that improves performance while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) assigns the same explicit loss weight to every expert demonstration, regardless of the model's changing competence over training queries. Reinforcement learning (RL) based methods adapt update strength using model-generated rollouts, but often require substantially more sampling and can be unstable on hard tasks. We propose \textbf{Online Self-Weighted Fine-Tuning (OSW-FT)}, a simple method that augments SFT with online, trajectory-level weighting. For each query, OSW-FT estimates the model's current success rate using a small number of inference-only rollouts and rescales the standard SFT loss accordingly. The optimization direction remains anchored to the expert trajectory, while the update magnitude adapts online. For binary-verifiable reasoning, we connect this weighting to SFT and RL at the gradient level, inspired by variance-reduction principles. The resulting estimator is unbiased for the exact OSW-FT surrogate update for any finite rollout count, and we analyze convergence with respect to the corresponding surrogate objective. Evaluated across Qwen3 series ranging from 0.6B to 4B on multiple challenging benchmarks (e.g., AIME), OSW-FT consistently improves over SFT on small-to-medium scale models. OSW-FT offers a favorable compute-performance trade-off as a practical approach for fine-tuning small-to-medium LLMs on binary-verifiable reasoning tasks with only \textbf{2 online rollouts}.

</details>


### [180] [Text Capability Loss in Vision-Language Adaptation: An Attention-Sink Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00746)
*Minsik Choi,Geewook Kim,Young Geun Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sink Strength metric predicts vision-language fine-tuning degradation without training; attention-sink corruption causes text capability loss in LLMs adapted to VLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning pretrained LLMs into vision-language models erodes their text capabilities, particularly for tasks requiring strict output formats like instruction following and chain-of-thought reasoning with strict graders. This degradation is poorly understood and current mitigation methods fail.

Method: Introduced Sink Strength - a single scalar metric computed on base LLMs that predicts post-VL degradation without any training. Analyzed attention-sink corruption mechanism where VL fine-tuning perturbs early attention positions. Tested across six VLM-LLM pairs and multiple format-sensitive tasks.

Result: Sink Strength consistently tracks relative degradation across all tested model pairs. Found that existing interventions (post-pretraining QK-RMSNorm injection, weight-merging) fail to protect or recover lost capabilities. Shows attention-sink preservation correlates with capability survival.

Conclusion: The attention-sink corruption mechanism explains VLM fine-tuning degradation. Sink Strength provides a practical screening tool for backbone selection before VL training. Negative results with existing interventions highlight the need for new training-time protection methods, likely focusing on specific attention heads.

Abstract: Fine-tuning a pretrained LLM into a vision-language model (VLM) can erode the backbone's text capability, with the damage concentrated on tasks that require following exact output rules, such as instruction following, chain-of-thought reasoning graded on a strictly parsed final answer, and similar evaluations with strict graders. We trace this gap to attention-sink corruption: VL fine-tuning perturbs the early sink position that anchors a large fraction of attention probability, and how well the base LLM preserves its sink tracks how much of the affected capability survives adaptation. Building on this view, we introduce Sink Strength, a single scalar computed on the base LLM in a few seconds on a single GPU that predicts post-VL degradation without any VL training. It consistently tracks relative degradation across the six VLM-LLM pairs and multiple format-sensitive tasks. Complementing this diagnostic, we find that post-pretraining QK-RMSNorm injection fails to reproduce the protection of native QK-RMSNorm, while several off-the-shelf weight-merging settings fail to recover the lost capability after VL training. These negative results underscore the value of screening backbones with Sink Strength before VL training and narrow the intervention space toward head-selective training-time protection.

</details>


### [181] [How Do Language Models Choose Between Context and Memory?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00753)
*Benjamin Shih,John Winnicki,Arianna Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Authority directions in language models can be used to steer between contextual and parametric knowledge, but their causal nature and cross-task reusability are limited.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses two key limitations in understanding activation directions used to steer language models: 1) whether steering along a direction establishes causality (does the unedited model naturally use it?), and 2) whether these directions are reusable across different tasks.

Method: The authors conduct counterfactual experiments in unambiguous settings. First, they estimate authority directions from agreement prompts where context and parametric knowledge support the same answer. Then they interchange coordinates along these directions between matched prompts that direct models to prioritize either context or parametric knowledge. They test across multiple models (Qwen, Llama, OLMo) and evaluate cross-task transfer by learning authority directions on two tasks separately.

Result: The intervention reproduces 30-68% of authority-induced shifts in source choice, while matched controls reproduce almost none. Cross-task transferability closes only 9% of the authority gap, whereas local directions learned on the given task close 57% of the gap.

Conclusion: The results distinguish between authority representation, causal use, and cross-task causal reuse, suggesting that authority computations in language models are likely task-dependent rather than reusable across different tasks.

Abstract: When contextual information conflicts with the knowledge stored in model parameters, activation directions can be used to decode and steer which source the model follows. However, steering along a direction does not establish causality: whether the unedited model would naturally use that direction or whether the direction is reusable across tasks. We test these distinctions through counterfactual experiments in unambiguous settings. First, we estimate authority directions from agreement prompts, in which the context and parametric knowledge support the same answer. We then interchange naturally occurring coordinates along these directions between matched prompts that direct the model to prioritize either the supplied context or its parametric knowledge. Across Qwen, Llama, and OLMo models, this intervention reproduces 30-68% of the authority-induced shift in source choice, whereas matched controls reproduce almost none. To test cross-task reuse, we learn authority directions on two tasks separately and see that cross-task transferability closes only 9% of the authority gap while the local direction learned on the given task closes 57%. These results distinguish authority representation, causal use, and cross-task causal reuse, and suggest that authority computations may be task-dependent, rather than reusable across tasks.

</details>


### [182] [Frozen Cores Need Task Signal: Fisher-Whitened Cross-Covariance for Low-Resource LLM Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00762)
*Wentao Ye,Zhanming Shen,Zhiqing Xiao,Yao Ding,Haobo Wang,Gang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Frozen-core adaptation (FCCA) method fixes weight bases and fine-tunes only small cores, achieving strong performance with minimal parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under severe trainable-state budgets, parameter-efficient fine-tuning needs to optimize not just how many parameters to update, but where those updates act. The paper aims to find the most effective fixed subspaces for adaptation.

Method: Frozen-Core Component Adaptation (FCCA): 1) Calibration pass fixes left/right bases for each weight matrix, 2) Estimates signed input-error cross-covariance, 3) Whitens with diagonal Fisher moments, 4) Truncates in resulting local metric, 5) Maps selected directions back, 6) Applies thin QR for stable core coordinates.

Result: FCCA achieves 83.0 macro-average on Qwen2.5-3B (2.3 points above next-best), exceeds RawGrad control on all 11 tasks, ranks first at all Qwen scales, within 0.13 points of best method on Llama-3.2-1B. Within 0.32-0.23 points of LoRA/DoRA while using 36.9K vs 7.4M parameters.

Conclusion: Carefully selected fixed spans can recover most benefit of movable low-rank factors at much smaller trainable and optimizer-state cost, demonstrating the importance of where parameters act under severe budgets.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning is usually framed as a question of how many parameters to update. Under a severe trainable-state budget, however, where those coefficients act is equally consequential. We study this choice through frozen-core adaptation: a calibration pass fixes left and right bases for each weight matrix, and fine-tuning optimizes only an $r\times r$ core. This removes the ability of trainable factors to repair a poor initial span and makes subspace quality directly observable. We introduce FCCA, which estimates the signed input--error cross-covariance, whitens it with diagonal Fisher moments, truncates it in the resulting local metric, maps the selected directions back, and applies thin QR to obtain stable core coordinates. Under a matched $r^2$ budget, we compare eight basis constructors on 11 tasks, four model settings, and three seeds. On Qwen2.5-3B, FCCA reaches an 83.0 macro-average, 2.3 points above the next-best matched-budget constructor, and exceeds its unwhitened RawGrad control on all 11 tasks. It ranks first at all three Qwen scales and finishes within 0.13 points of the best method on Llama-3.2-1B. Controlled ablations show gains of 2.7--17.2 points from whitening and identify QR as necessary for stable core optimization in the tested regime. Finally, FCCA comes within 0.32 and 0.23 average points of LoRA and DoRA while optimizing 36.9K rather than roughly 7.4M parameters. These results show that a carefully selected fixed span can recover most of the benefit of movable low-rank factors at a much smaller trainable and optimizer-state cost.

</details>


### [183] [Are You Thinking What I am Thinking? : Examining Conceptual Separation in Neural Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00764)
*Jaee Ponde,Roshni Agarwal,Subhashis Banerjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural networks form coherent internal representations of concepts (conceptual separation) where related concepts cluster together in activation space, revealing structure not visible through output accuracy alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While neural networks can identify both well-defined and ambiguous concepts, output-level metrics don't reveal how these concepts are internally represented. The paper aims to examine whether neural networks exhibit "conceptual separation" - coherent representations of concepts with related concepts positioned closer together in the representation space.

Method: The study uses geometric and distributional analysis of internal activations in two neural architectures: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Large Language Models (LLMs). They examine how examples of the same concept cluster together and how related concepts are positioned relative to each other in the representation space.

Result: In CNNs, familiar ImageNet concepts form coherent and semantically ordered representations, but this coherence weakens for unseen concepts and suffers from within-class domain shift. In LLMs, clearly distinct domains remain well separated, related subdomains move closer together, and ambiguous topics collapse at both mean and covariance levels. Both architectures show evidence of conceptual separation.

Conclusion: Conceptual separation reveals structural patterns in neural network representations that output accuracy alone cannot detect. This can serve as a useful diagnostic tool for understanding how robustly models represent the concepts they identify, potentially offering insights into model generalization and failure modes.

Abstract: Neural networks are increasingly employed to identify both well-defined and ambiguous concepts, yet output-level metrics reveal little about how those concepts are represented internally. Our study asks if these networks exhibit \textit{conceptual separation}: if examples of the same concept form coherent representations, and whether related concepts lie closer together in the representation space. We examine this conceptual organisation in Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) through geometric and distributional analysis of their internal activations. In CNNs, familiar ImageNet concepts form coherent and semantically ordered representations, while this coherence weakens for unseen concepts and suffers within-class domain shift. In LLMs, clearly distinct domains remain well separated, related subdomains move closer together, and the distinction between ambiguous topics collapses at both the mean and covariance level. These results suggest that conceptual separation can reveal structure that output accuracy alone cannot, and may serve as a useful diagnostic of how robustly a model represents the concepts it is asked to identify. Code and data available on \href{https://github.com/JaeeRoshniCapstoneProject/Are-You-Thinking-What-I-m-Thinking-Examining-Conceptual-Separation-in-Neural-Architectures}{GitHub}.

</details>


### [184] [Subspace Levenberg Marquardt Algorithms in Training Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00789)
*M. Duc Hoang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper evaluates subspace Levenberg-Marquardt algorithms for neural network training, comparing their performance with classical LM and first-order methods like SGD and Adam on regression and classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm has strong convergence properties for small to medium neural networks, but becomes computationally expensive for larger networks due to high memory and computational costs. Subspace methods have been proposed to address this scalability issue.

Method: The authors evaluate subspace variants of the LM algorithm, specifically Krylov subspace LM (KSLM) and hybrid subspace LM (HSLM), for neural network training. They compare these subspace methods against classical LM and first-order optimization algorithms (SGD, Adam) on both regression and classification tasks.

Result: The abstract doesn't specify results, but the evaluation would presumably show whether subspace LM variants achieve competitive performance with reduced computational costs compared to classical LM, and how they compare against popular first-order methods in terms of convergence speed and final performance.

Conclusion: Subspace Levenberg-Marquardt methods appear to be promising alternatives that could maintain the convergence benefits of second-order methods while addressing the scalability limitations of classical LM for training neural networks with large numbers of parameters.

Abstract: The Levenberg-Marquardt (LM) algorithm is a well-known second-order method for rapid convergence and strong robustness when training small- to medium-sized neural networks (NNs). However, its computational and memory costs increase significantly as the number of parameters in an NN grows. To address this limitation, subspace methods have been proposed, such as the Krylov subspace LM (KSLM) and the hybrid subspace LM (HSLM), making second-order algorithms more efficient. In this work, we evaluate the subspace Levenberg-Marquardt algorithms for regression and classification tasks in neural networks. We compare the performance of subspace LM variants with the classical LM method, as well as other popular first-order algorithms, such as stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and Adam.

</details>


### [185] [HarnessEvolve: Learning from Reference Trajectories for Reliable Agent Self-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00829)
*Wen Jiang,Mingmin Chu,Yimeng Tian,Qianxin Zhang,Haofei Yang,Rui Yang,Yang Liu,Tao Lv,Fangming Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HarnessEvolve is a self-evolving agent framework that addresses credit assignment failure, shortcut learning, and catastrophic forgetting through reference trajectory learning and gated updates for reliable agent improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current self-evolving agent paradigms face three major challenges: credit assignment failure (ambiguous error attribution due to terminal feedback), shortcut learning (task-specific pattern memorization rather than generalization), and catastrophic forgetting (degradation of previously learned skills during updates). These issues hinder reliable autonomous agent evolution.

Method: HarnessEvolve decouples execution from evolution using independent modules for execution, evaluation, optimization, and gating. It overcomes credit assignment by generating reference trajectories with ground-truth answers and aligning failed executions against them to extract error signals, which are clustered to identify systematic failures. To prevent shortcut learning and catastrophic forgetting, updates must pass two gates: a quality gate filtering data leakage/prompt bloat, and a performance gate requiring improvement on current batches without degrading recent ones, with epoch-end validation on held-out sets.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks in open-domain and enterprise scenarios using different models and agent frameworks show HarnessEvolve consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across all benchmarks and settings.

Conclusion: HarnessEvolve provides a reliable framework for agent self-evolution that effectively addresses the core challenges of credit assignment failure, shortcut learning, and catastrophic forgetting through its modular architecture and gated update mechanisms, enabling stable and generalizable harness improvements.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents advance toward autonomy by optimizing their harness---prompts, skills, tools, and execution logic---based on environmental feedback. This paradigm, however, is hampered by three challenges: \textit{credit assignment failure}, where terminal success/failure feedback makes it ambiguous which step caused the error; \textit{shortcut learning}, where agents memorize task-specific patterns rather than acquire generalizable capabilities; and \textit{catastrophic forgetting}, where unguarded updates degrade previously acquired competence. In this paper, we introduce HarnessEvolve, a self-evolving framework that learns from reference trajectories to achieve reliable agent self-evolution. HarnessEvolve decouples the execution agent from the evolutionary pipeline, assigning execution, evaluation, optimization, and gating to independent agent modules, enabling generalizable and stable harness improvements. Specifically, HarnessEvolve overcomes credit assignment failure by generating reference trajectories (execution paths produced when given the ground-truth answers) and aligning failed executions against them to extract error signals, which are clustered to reveal systematic failure patterns. To prevent shortcut learning and catastrophic forgetting, candidate harness updates must pass two gates: a quality gate that filters data leakage and prompt bloat, and a performance gate that accepts each update if it improves on the current batch without degrading recent batches, with epoch-end validation on a held-out set selecting the best-performing accepted agent snapshot. We conduct extensive experiments on several benchmarks spanning open-domain and enterprise scenarios, using different models and agent frameworks. Results demonstrate that HarnessEvolve consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across all benchmarks and settings, confirming reliability across task domains.

</details>


### [186] [Conditional Flow Matching for ML-Based Inverse Design Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00863)
*Juliana Felder,Milad Habibi,Soheyl Massoudi,Mark Fuge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CFM outperforms diffusion models and cGANs in engineering inverse design, achieving better optimization results, lower computational cost, and higher sampling speed with fewer network evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Engineering inverse design faces challenges with high computational costs of iterative PDE solvers and sensitivity to initialization. Generative models can produce candidate designs without simulation at inference time, but different approaches have trade-offs.

Method: Added conditional flow matching (CFM) to EngiOpt and compared it with conditional diffusion models and conditional GANs on structural (beams2d) and thermal (heatconduction2d) benchmarks from EngiBench using the same downstream optimization protocol.

Result: CFM achieved the lowest COG, FOG, MMD, and volume-fraction deviation on both tasks. CFM has mean volume-fraction deviations of 0.4% and 1.0% compared to 3.8% and 11.2% for diffusion. CFM achieves 53.2 samples/s at Euler s=16, about 66× faster than diffusion baseline, with comparable COG performance.

Conclusion: CFM produces better warm starts for gradient-based refinement than diffusion models and cGANs, with lower optimality gaps, fewer network evaluations, and significantly higher sampling throughput.

Abstract: Engineering inverse design is often limited by the high computational cost of iterative solvers for optimization problems constrained by partial differential equations (PDEs) and by their sensitivity to initialization. Deep generative models can produce candidate designs without rerunning the simulator at inference time. Generative adversarial networks (GANs) sample in one forward pass, whereas diffusion models require iterative reverse-time integration.
  In this work, we add conditional flow matching (CFM) to EngiOpt and compare it with a conditional diffusion model and a conditional generative adversarial network (cGAN) on structural (beams2d) and thermal (heatconduction2d) benchmarks from EngiBench using the same downstream optimization protocol. We use cumulative optimality gap (COG) and final optimality gap (FOG) as the primary metrics for evaluating the generated designs as warm starts for gradient-based refinement. On the evaluated EngiOpt implementations and two EngiBench tasks, CFM achieves the lowest measured COG, FOG, maximum mean discrepancy (MMD), and volume-fraction deviation on both tasks. CFM has mean volume-fraction deviations of 0.4% and 1.0% on beams2d and heatconduction2d, respectively, compared with 3.8% and 11.2% for diffusion. At Euler s = 16, CFM achieves 53.2 samples/s on beams2d, about 66 times the measured throughput of the evaluated diffusion baseline using 1000 network evaluations under the same timing protocol, with COG 1.182 +/- 3.126, compared with 1.173 +/- 3.100 for Euler s = 32.
  Across the two tasks, CFM produces warm starts with lower measured COG than both baselines and uses fewer network evaluations than diffusion.

</details>


### [187] [MemoryWalker: Stop Training Agents on Contexts They Never Saw](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00865)
*Zinco J,Xunjie Zhu,Shen Huang,Zhenyi Wang,Pengjun Xie,Jieping Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Production agents compress context during rollout, creating training conditioning problems. The paper introduces exact correction methods (LogitTree, 4D attention mask) and a variational relaxation method (SDCC) to solve the train-deployment mismatch caused by context eviction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production agents like Claude Code and Qwen-Agent use context compression during rollout to manage long sequences, but this creates a fundamental conditioning problem during training. When context is evicted (compressed), it branches the effective history, making the learning objective a tree rather than a sequence. Existing linearization approaches either suffer from time-travel leakage (retaining only rightmost path) or train-inference mismatch (depth-first traversal), leading to suboptimal training.

Method: The paper introduces three approaches: 1) LogitTree - a segmented K-forward traversal method that requires K+1 backward passes, 2) a packed 4D attention mask requiring custom kernels and white-box eviction records, and 3) SDCC (Self-Distillation for Conditioning Consistency) - a single-backward-pass variational relaxation that minimizes forward KL divergence between compressed student and stop-gradient teacher on reconstructed pre-eviction prefixes, with residual per-junction KL giving theoretical bounds on train-deployment gap.

Result: On seven web-search benchmarks with TC-RAG, AgentFold, MemexRL, Claude Code, and OpenCode, naive training significantly inflates the train-rollout log-probability gap, especially on eviction-heavy batches. The exact methods (LogitTree and 4D mask) maintain performance at the no-compression floor, while SDCC substantially closes the gap, showing lower logit drift and higher rollout rewards. SDCC also works with black-box harnesses.

Conclusion: Context compression in production agents creates fundamental conditioning problems during training that existing linearization methods fail to address properly. The proposed exact methods provide gradient-equivalent corrections, while SDCC offers a practical variational relaxation with theoretical guarantees. These approaches successfully bridge the train-deployment gap caused by context eviction, with SDCC being particularly valuable for its single-backward-pass efficiency and black-box compatibility.

Abstract: Production agent harnesses such as Claude Code and Qwen-Agent compress context during rollout, but training under compression creates a conditioning problem: every eviction branches the effective history, so the learning object is a tree rather than a sequence. Existing linearizations either retain the rightmost path, causing time-travel leakage, or replay a depth-first traversal, causing train-inference mismatch. We introduce two exact, gradient-equivalent corrections: LogitTree, a segmented K-forward traversal, and a packed 4D attention mask. LogitTree requires K+1 backward passes; the 4D mask requires a custom kernel and white-box eviction records. We also propose SDCC (Self-Distillation for Conditioning Consistency), a single-backward-pass variational relaxation. At each eviction, it minimizes forward KL between the compressed student and a stop-gradient teacher on the reconstructed pre-eviction prefix. A residual per-junction KL of epsilon_KL gives an O(sqrt(epsilon_KL)) bound on the train-deployment total-variation gap. SDCC also applies to black-box harnesses. On seven web-search benchmarks with TC-RAG, AgentFold, MemexRL, Claude Code, and OpenCode, naive training inflates the train-rollout log-probability gap, especially on eviction-heavy batches. The exact methods stay at the no-compression floor, and SDCC substantially closes the gap, with lower logit drift and higher rollout rewards.

</details>


### [188] [iPINN for Broadband CARS Phase Retrieval: A Framework for Function Approximation and Inverse Modeling Problems in Nonlinear Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00883)
*Ravi Teja Vulchi,Carl Messerschmidt,Mohammadsadegh Vafaeinezhad,Rajendhar Junjuri,Tobias Meyer-Zedler,Juergen Popp,Thomas Bocklitz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An inverse physics-informed neural network (iPINN) for robust phase retrieval in broadband coherent anti-Stokes Raman spectroscopy that predicts Lorentzian peak parameters and reconstructs resonant susceptibility through differentiable physical modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phase retrieval in BCARS is an ill-posed inverse problem where Raman-like signals are encoded in imaginary parts of resonant susceptibility that mix with varying non-resonant background across acquisitions. Existing methods struggle with varying acquisition conditions.

Method: iPINN uses a transformer encoder to assign spectral features to 24 learnable peak slots, predicts Lorentzian peak parameters from raw BCARS spectra, and reconstructs resonant susceptibility through a differentiable analytical forward model. Multi-view consistency loss enforces invariance across NRB pattern, strength, and noise.

Result: Achieved lowest error on public benchmark (MAE 0.016 vs. next-best 0.046). On 28 zero-shot test spectra across seven solvents and four focal positions, accuracy was depth-invariant in five of seven solvents. Retains accuracy under varying acquisition conditions unlike direct spectral regression.

Conclusion: Inverse parametric prediction with a differentiable physical decoder supports robust phase retrieval across measurement conditions in BCARS spectroscopy, demonstrating superior performance and generalization compared to existing baselines.

Abstract: Phase retrieval in broadband coherent anti-Stokes Raman spectroscopy (BCARS) is an ill-posed inverse problem. The Raman-like signal is encoded in the imaginary part of the resonant susceptibility, which mixes coherently with a non-resonant background (NRB) that varies across acquisitions. We introduce an inverse physics-informed neural network (iPINN) that predicts Lorentzian peak parameters from raw BCARS spectra and reconstructs the resonant susceptibility through a differentiable analytical forward model. A transformer encoder assigns spectral features to 24 learnable peak slots, and a multi-view consistency loss enforces invariance across NRB pattern, NRB strength, and noise. Unlike direct spectral regression approaches, the method retains accuracy under varying acquisition conditions. On a public benchmark, iPINN achieves the lowest error among the tested baselines (MAE 0.016 vs. next-best 0.046). On 28 zero-shot test spectra acquired across seven solvents and four focal positions, accuracy is depth-invariant in five of seven solvents. These results show that inverse parametric prediction with a differentiable physical decoder supports robust phase retrieval across measurement conditions.

</details>


### [189] [Poisson-Gamma Dynamical Systems with Time-varying Transition Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00896)
*Jiahao Wang,Yijun Wang,Nan Fang,Sikun Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new Bayesian model called Poisson-Gamma Dynamical System with time-varying transition kernel (TV-PGDS) is proposed to better capture evolving dependency structures in count time series by allowing transition matrices to change over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Poisson-Gamma Dynamical Systems (PGDSs), while effective for capturing evolving dynamics in count time series, fail to adequately capture transition dynamics commonly observed in real-world data. The state-of-the-art PGDS models have limitations in modeling time-varying dependency structures.

Method: The authors propose TV-PGDS, which incorporates time-varying transition kernels to allow underlying transition matrices to evolve over time. They construct three specialized Dirichlet Markov chains (Dir-Dir, Dir-Gam-Dir, PR-Gam-Dir) to handle heterogeneous structural mutations in dependencies. Using Dirichlet-Multinomial-Beta data augmentation techniques, they develop a fully-conjugate and efficient Gibbs sampler for posterior simulation.

Result: Experiments show that the proposed TV-PGDS model achieves improved predictive performance compared to related models, attributed to its ability to learn time-varying dependency structures through time-evolving transition matrices.

Conclusion: The TV-PGDS model successfully addresses the limitation of existing PGDS models by introducing time-varying transition kernels, enabling better capture of real-world transition dynamics in count time series and yielding superior predictive performance through its flexible, evolving dependency structure learning.

Abstract: Bayesian methodologies for handling count-valued time series have gained prominence due to their ability to infer interpretable latent structures and to estimate uncertainties. Among these Bayesian models, Poisson-Gamma Dynamical Systems (PGDSs) are proven to be effective in capturing the evolving dynamics underlying observed count sequences. However, the state-of-the-art PGDS still falls short in capturing the transition dynamics that are commonly observed in real-world count time series. To mitigate this limitation, a PGDS with time-varying transition kernel (TV-PGDS), is proposed to allow the underlying transition matrices to evolve over time. Three specifically-designed Dirichlet Markov chains (Dir-Dir, Dir-Gam-Dir, PR-Gam-Dir) are constructed to accommodate heterogeneous structural mutations within these dependencies. Leveraging Dirichlet-Multinomial-Beta data augmentation techniques, a fully-conjugate and efficient Gibbs sampler is developed to perform posterior simulation. Experiments show that, in comparison with related models, the proposed PGDS achieves improved predictive performance due to its capacity to learn time-varying dependency structure captured by the time-evolving transition matrices.

</details>


### [190] [When Metropolis and Hastings Meet Bradley and Terry: Exact MCMC From Preference Voting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00905)
*Ariel Smogorghevski,Nir Rosenfeld,Yaniv Romano*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Pref-MH: An exact Metropolis-Hastings sampler for conditional generation using only stochastic binary preference comparisons instead of exact density evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling from distributions conditioned on semantic properties is challenging in generative modeling. While Metropolis-Hastings provides principled conditional sampling, it requires exact pointwise target-density evaluations which are unavailable in generative settings. Meanwhile, binary pairwise comparisons by humans or AI "judges" are highly accessible and valuable across applications.

Method: Pref-MH leverages the insight that the MH unnormalized density ratio matches the preference odds of the Bradley-Terry choice model. The method develops a valid accept/reject rule using only stochastic binary comparisons from judges, provably converging to the target distribution. It's shown to be optimal among exact reversible acceptance rules for fixed proposal kernel and budget.

Result: Pref-MH provides a practical and flexible approach to conditional sampling when comparative feedback is available. Experiments demonstrate successful application in text generation and molecular design with LLM judges, and image generation with VLM judges.

Conclusion: Pref-MH enables exact conditional sampling using only stochastic binary preference comparisons, bridging the gap between principled MH sampling and accessible pairwise comparison feedback in modern generative modeling applications.

Abstract: Sampling from distributions conditioned on desired semantic properties is an emerging challenge in modern generative modeling. Metropolis-Hastings (MH) provides a principled route to conditional sampling, but requires access to exact pointwise target-density evaluations, which are not available in generative settings. Meanwhile, pairwise comparisons by humans or model "judge" are highly accessible and have proved valuable across diverse applications. We introduce Pref-MH, a general exact MH sampler for judge-induced conditional distributions using only stochastic binary pairwise comparisons. Our key observation is that the MH unnormalized density ratio matches the preference odds of the Bradley-Terry (BT) choice model. The central challenge is that while MH requires precise ratio computation, BT judges provide only sampled binary feedback. To this end, we develop a valid accept/reject rule whose resulting Markov chain provably converges to the target distribution. We further show that, for a fixed proposal kernel and budget, Pref-MH is optimal in the Peskun-Tierney sense among this class of exact reversible acceptance rules. Experiments on text generation and molecular design with LLM judges, as well as image generation with VLM judges, demonstrate that Pref-MH provides a practical and flexible approach to conditional sampling when comparative feedback is relatively easy to obtain.

</details>


### [191] [The Multiple Timescales of Gradient Descent on the Edge of Stability: A Perturbative Derivation of the Central Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01034)
*Raphaël Berthier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A formal singular perturbation analysis shows that gradient descent dynamics converge to Cohen et al.'s central flow when loss decomposes as f = g + εh, revealing three timescales: fast oscillations, intermediate self-stabilization, and slow central flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cohen et al.'s central flow model is empirically accurate for gradient descent at the edge of stability in deep learning, but its derivation is heuristic. This paper aims to provide a more formal mathematical foundation for why this central flow emerges as the limiting dynamics of gradient descent.

Method: The authors propose a perturbative regime where loss decomposes as f = g + εh. Using singular perturbation theory, they treat gradient descent as a singularly perturbed dynamical system in ε. They apply the method of multiple scales to derive expansions in ε, revealing three emergent timescales: fast oscillations along sharpest direction, intermediate self-stabilization, and slow central flow dynamics.

Result: In the ε→0 limit, gradient descent with learning rate η converges to gradient flow of h constrained to minimizers of g with sharpness at most 2/η. The central flow emerges as the leading-order term, while self-stabilization appears in next-order term. The analysis extends beyond previous work: for single eigenvalue at edge of stability, they compute slow drift of fluctuation energy; for multiple eigenvalues, they derive self-stabilization system explaining persistent fluctuations.

Conclusion: The paper provides a formal mathematical justification for Cohen et al.'s central flow, showing it emerges naturally as the leading-order dynamics in a perturbative regime. The singular perturbation framework explains the emergent three-timescale structure and extends understanding of self-stabilization mechanisms in gradient descent at the edge of stability.

Abstract: The central flow of Cohen et al. (2025) is an empirically accurate continuous-time model of gradient descent at the edge of stability in deep learning, However, its derivation is heuristic. We propose a perturbative regime in which the central flow is the limit of gradient descent: we assume that the loss decomposes as $f = g + \varepsilon h$; in the limit $\varepsilon \to 0$, the dynamics of gradient descent with learning rate $η$ converge to the gradient flow of $h$ constrained to the minimizers of $g$ of sharpness at most $2/η$. Our approach is formal rather than rigorous; it treats gradient descent as a singularly perturbed dynamical system in $\varepsilon$. Three timescales emerge: a fast timescale of oscillations along the sharpest direction, an intermediate timescale of the self-stabilization mechanism, and a slow timescale of the dynamics along the minimizers of $g$-the central flow. Using the method of multiple scales, a classical formal method from singular perturbation theory, we derive the expansion of the dynamics in $\varepsilon$: the central flow emerges as the leading-order term in the expansion, while the self-stabilization mechanism appears in the next-order term. We study this mechanism beyond previous analyses: with a single eigenvalue at the edge of stability, we compute the slow drift of the energy of the fluctuations; with several eigenvalues at the edge of stability, we derive the self-stabilization system and explain why fluctuations persist.

</details>


### [192] [From Truncation to Commitment: Persistent Context in Uniform Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01043)
*Satoshi Hayakawa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CRS (Committed Reveal Sampling) is a training-free sampler for discrete diffusion models that makes selected tokens persistent context, improving generation quality-perplexity tradeoffs compared to standard top-p sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard uniform-state discrete diffusion models allow all tokens to be revised at each step, even when top-p selection narrows choices. The paper asks what happens when selected hypotheses become persistent context instead of being revisable.

Method: Proposes CRS (Committed Reveal Sampling) - stores selected argmax tokens and inserts them as persistent context in subsequent model inputs. Analyzes when to select tokens (selecting later) and why to keep them visible (helps parallel predictions agree). Uses paired experiments to separate persistent context effects from top-p restriction and temperature scaling.

Result: CRS without top-p truncation achieves lower generative perplexity (GenPPL) than fixed p=0.95 and p=0.9 baselines across 8-64 NFE budgets. At 64 NFE with matched unigram entropy, CRS gives lower GenPPL and better GenPPL-entropy tradeoff. Shows same trend in base Duo model.

Conclusion: Support restriction (top-p) and persistent context (CRS) are distinct controls affecting the generation quality-diversity tradeoff, with persistent context offering improved perplexity-entropy balance.

Abstract: Uniform-state discrete diffusion models update all tokens in parallel while keeping every position revisable. Even when the commonly used top-$p$ rule leaves only one candidate at a position, that choice affects only the current reverse step and can be revised at the next sampling step. We ask what changes when selected hypotheses instead become persistent context for later predictions. We therefore propose committed reveal sampling (CRS), a training-free sampler that stores selected argmax tokens and inserts them into subsequent model inputs. Our analysis gives a rationale for selecting later and for keeping selected tokens visible. Under the exact forward process, the Bayes error of selecting a clean token cannot increase as noise decreases, while in a simple latent-mode model, keeping the selected token visible helps later parallel predictions agree on the same sequence-level choice. Empirically, paired experiments on Duo-distilled then separate this persistent effect from single-step top-$p$ restriction and scalar temperature scaling. Under the same finalization rule, CRS without top-$p$ truncation reaches lower generative perplexity (GenPPL) than fixed $p=0.95$ and $p=0.9$ baselines across budgets of 8--64 function evaluations (NFE). At 64 NFE, the comparison at matched unigram entropy also gives lower GenPPL for CRS, yielding a more favorable GenPPL--entropy tradeoff. Base Duo shows the same direction in a descriptive comparison, while other diversity and continuation metrics can rank these operating points differently. These results identify support restriction and persistent context as distinct controls of that tradeoff.

</details>


### [193] [SAGE: Subpopulation-Aware Generative Enhancement for Mitigating Spurious Correlations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01051)
*Yiming Luo,Rongqiang Zhao,Jie Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGE is a two-stage generative augmentation framework that mitigates spurious correlations without group labels by generating targeted synthetic data to balance underrepresented groups and improve worst-group accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spurious correlations in imbalanced datasets cause ERM models to rely on majority spurious attributes, leading to poor performance on minority groups. Existing group-label-free methods often upsampling minority groups reduces diversity and encourages overfitting, creating a need for better approaches when spurious attributes are unavailable.

Method: SAGE uses a two-stage framework: 1) fine-tunes a conditional generative model and text encoder using cluster-derived sub-labels and class labels, 2) generates targeted synthetic data to fill underrepresented regions in the training set and constructs a balanced validation set for last-layer reweighting, all without requiring group labels.

Result: SAGE achieves 89.5% worst-group accuracy on Waterbirds, 85.7% on CelebA, and 79.1% on MetaShift, outperforming the best group-label-free baselines by up to 7.7 percentage points.

Conclusion: SAGE effectively mitigates spurious correlations from a data-centric perspective without requiring prior knowledge of spurious attributes, demonstrating superior performance over existing group-label-free methods through targeted generative augmentation.

Abstract: Spurious correlations pose a significant challenge to the robustness of modern machine learning. The inherent imbalance in dataset distributions often leads traditional Empirical Risk Minimization (ERM) models to rely on majority spurious attributes for classification, resulting in poor performance on minority groups. This problem becomes particularly challenging when the spurious attributes are unavailable. Existing group-label-free methods often upsample minority groups or misclassified real training examples; repeating the same instances can reduce effective diversity and encourage overfitting. To mitigate these spurious correlations from a data-centric perspective in the absence of prior knowledge, we introduce Subpopulation-Aware Generative Enhancement (SAGE), a two-stage generative augmentation framework. Using cluster-derived sub-labels and class labels, we fine-tune a conditional generative model and text encoder, generating targeted synthetic data to fill underrepresented regions in the training set and construct a balanced validation set for last-layer reweighting. We experimentally show that SAGE achieves 89.5%, 85.7%, and 79.1% worst-group accuracy on Waterbirds, CelebA, and MetaShift, respectively, outperforming the best group-label-free baselines by up to 7.7 percentage points.

</details>


### [194] [Let Confidence Change, Not the Prediction: Prediction-Preserving Repair for Post-hoc Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01072)
*Daehwan Kim,Haejun Chung,Ikbeom Jang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CORD is a post-hoc adapter that repairs calibrated probability vectors to preserve original top-1 predictions while maintaining calibration benefits, achieving zero prediction change rate by design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard multiclass calibration can change top-1 predictions even when correcting confidence scores. Current metrics like accuracy only measure net correctness, not prediction stability. There's a need for calibration methods that preserve original decisions while improving calibration metrics.

Method: CORD (Calibrator-Output Repair for Top-1 Decision Preservation) repairs the full calibrated probability vector using only original and calibrated outputs. It determines mass assigned to original top-1 class, then distributes remaining mass over other classes while preserving the calibrated conditional distribution. The method requires no additional supervised fitting or hyperparameter tuning.

Result: CORD achieves zero Top-1 Prediction Change Rate (TPCR) by construction. It lowers mean ECE, NLL, and Brier scores relative to direct calibrated outputs across CIFAR-10/100 and ImageNet-1K. These gains persist under distribution shift and across different calibration-set sizes.

Conclusion: CORD successfully decouples calibration fitting from prediction preservation by using post-fit output repair. It removes the preservation constraint during calibrator fitting while ensuring exact recovery of original decisions, offering both calibration improvement and prediction stability.

Abstract: Post-hoc calibration corrects reported confidence, yet a multiclass calibrator can also change the associated top-1 prediction. Accuracy captures only the net effect of these changes on correctness, not how often predictions change; the Top-1 Prediction Change Rate (TPCR) instead measures this frequency. We propose Calibrator-Output Repair for Top-1 Decision Preservation (CORD), the first post-fit adapter to impose exact prediction preservation by repairing the full calibrated probability vector. From the original and calibrated outputs alone, CORD determines the mass assigned to the original top-1. The calibrated conditional distribution allocates the remaining mass over the other classes, yielding a repaired vector whose own argmax recovers the original prediction. On the calibration split, CORD coordinates the repaired masses to retain the calibrated outputs' mean mass on original predictions whenever attainable. The adapter alters neither the fitted calibrator nor its direct output, fits no additional supervised map, and requires no user- or validation-tuned hyperparameter. Across CIFAR-10/100 and ImageNet-1K, CORD attains zero TPCR by construction and lowers mean ECE, NLL, and Brier relative to the corresponding direct outputs in every dataset; paired gains persist under distribution shift and across calibration-set sizes. CORD thus removes the preservation constraint from calibrator fitting and assigns exact recovery of the original decision to subsequent output repair. Our code is available at https://github.com/labhai/ORCU.

</details>


### [195] [Modelpedia: A Catalog of Model Findings for the Meta-Science of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01090)
*Franciszek Bernat,Dawid Płudowski,Michał Jan Włodarczyk,Luca Longo,Jianlong Zhou,Andreas Holzinger,Riccardo Guidotti,Wojciech Samek,Przemysław Biecek*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Modelpedia is an automated LLM-assisted framework that extracts and organizes findings about AI models from published papers into a searchable public catalog, enabling better research meta-analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI research is advancing faster than the community can organize knowledge. New foundation models emerge frequently with hundreds of papers documenting their behaviors and failures, but these findings remain scattered and unretrievable, creating a gap in systematic knowledge organization.

Method: Developed an automated, LLM-assisted framework that extracts findings about models from published papers, links them to relevant models, datasets, methods, and concepts, and aggregates results into a searchable public catalog.

Result: Applied to ICLR 2024 and 2025 papers, extracting over a thousand findings. Conducted meta-analysis of how the community investigates models using the catalog itself as an object of study.

Conclusion: Modelpedia provides a shared foundation for AI meta-science. The authors invite the community to explore, contribute to, and build on the open catalog to establish model findings as a collaborative knowledge base.

Abstract: Scientific knowledge about AI models is produced faster than the community can organize it. Every few months a new foundation model reshapes the field and hundreds of papers, blogs, and technical reports document how each behaves or fails. Yet, these findings remain scattered and effectively unretrievable. To address this gap we present Modelpedia, an automated, LLM-assisted framework that extracts findings about models from published papers, links it to the model, dataset, method, and concept it concerns, and aggregates the result into a searchable public catalog. Applying the prototype to accepted ICLR 2024 and 2025 papers, we extract over a thousand findings and, treating the catalog itself as an object of study, run a meta-analysis of how the community investigates models. Now, we invite the community to explore, contribute to, and build on the open catalog, and to help establish model findings as a shared foundation for the meta-science of AI.

</details>


### [196] [Subliminal Learning as Trait-Direction Drift: A Mechanism and Targeted Control under SFT Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01091)
*Zhixuan Liu,Zhichen Dong,Yuyu Fan,Xiangtian Li,Chao Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Subliminal learning in model distillation transfers hidden traits from teacher to student via trait-direction drift. Proposed corridor regularization defends against this by constraining drift along calibrated trait directions during distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model distillation can unintentionally transfer hidden biases/traits from teacher to student beyond intended capabilities, creating security risks. Prior work identified parts but didn't explain how signals build up during training, making targeted mitigation difficult.

Method: Propose trait-direction drift as mechanism: biased teacher generation creates preference gaps in training data, causing trait-aligned updates during supervised fine-tuning that accumulate into behavioral transfer. Introduce probe-space corridor regularization - constrains drift along calibrated trait direction during distillation.

Result: Corridor regularization substantially reduces hidden-trait transfer while preserving task performance: lowers malicious-response transfer from 29.55% to 6.45% with low accuracy cost, and consistently suppresses animal-preference transfer in main Qwen setting.

Conclusion: Subliminal learning operates via trait-direction drift mechanism. Preference gaps, training trajectories, and intervention evidence link subliminal learning to this drift. Corridor regularization provides targeted control against hidden-trait transfer during distillation.

Abstract: Beyond intended capabilities, model distillation can transfer hidden traits from a teacher. A teacher biased by a system prompt can generate semantically clean training data, such as numeric sequences, that still causes a downstream student to inherit the hidden preference, a phenomenon known as subliminal learning. Prior work has identified several parts of this process. How the signal builds up during training and produces behavioral transfer remains unclear, making targeted mitigation difficult. We propose and validate trait-direction drift as a mechanism for subliminal learning: biased generation creates measurable preference gaps in teacher data, and student-recognizable gaps induce trait-aligned updates during supervised fine-tuning that accumulate into behavioral transfer. Guided by this mechanism, we propose probe-space corridor regularization, a targeted defense that constrains drift along a calibrated trait direction during distillation. The method substantially reduces hidden-trait transfer, preserving task performance: for example, it lowers malicious-response transfer from 29.55% to 6.45% with low main-task accuracy cost, and consistently suppresses animal-preference transfer across the main Qwen setting. The preference-gap, training-trajectory, and intervention evidence links subliminal learning to trait-direction drift and motivates corridor regularization as a targeted control during distillation.

</details>


### [197] [Neural Symbollic Regression Using Deep Learning and Sparse Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01102)
*Ravi Kumar U,Sumitra S*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neural symbolic regression framework that uses neural networks as functional preconditioners to learn noise-robust approximations, then extracts sparse interpretable expressions via LASSO, outperforming traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional symbolic regression methods like Genetic Programming have scalability issues and noise sensitivity, while sparse regression techniques like SINDy rely heavily on predetermined feature libraries, limiting their effectiveness for discovering fundamental mathematical relationships in data.

Method: A Neural Symbolic Regression (NSR) framework with decoupled pipeline: 1) neural network learns smooth, noise-robust approximations in interaction-aware nonlinear feature space, 2) LASSO extracts sparse interpretable closed-form expressions, 3) integrated distributed hyperparameter optimization using Ray Tune and ASHA scheduling.

Result: The approach consistently outperforms SINDy and non-tuned neural baselines on the Nguyen benchmark suite in RMSE, noise robustness, and out-of-distribution generalization. Ablation studies confirm importance of feature interactions, neural depth, and tuning strategies.

Conclusion: The study presents a scalable and understandable neural-symbolic framework that creates a strong link between neural approximation and sparse equation discovery for scientific machine learning, offering improved interpretability and robustness over traditional methods.

Abstract: Symbolic Regression (SR) seeks to find succinct mathematical expressions that represent the fundamental relationships within data, providing interpretability and scientific understanding that exceeds that of black-box models. Nevertheless, traditional methods like Genetic Programming face challenges with scalability and are highly sensitive to noise, while sparse regression techniques such as SINDy rely significantly on predetermined feature libraries. In this work, we present a Neural Symbolic Regression (NSR) framework that treats neural networks as functional preconditioners for symbolic discovery. Our approach uses a decoupled pipeline: a neural network first learns a smooth, noise-robust approximation of the target function in an interaction- aware nonlinear feature space. LASSO is then applied to extract sparse, interpretable closed-form expressions. To improve predictive accuracy and symbolic fidelity by integrating distributed hyperparameter optimization with Ray Tune and ASHA scheduling. Experiments on the Nguyen benchmark suite show that our approach consistently outperforms SINDy and non-tuned neural baselines in RMSE, noise robustness, and out-of-distribution generalization. Ablation studies confirm the significance of feature interactions, neural depth, and tuning strategies. In general, this study presents a scalable and understandable neural-symbolic framework, creating a solid link between neural approximation and the discovery of sparse equations for scientific machine learning.

</details>


### [198] [Replicating TRACE: A Practitioner's Guide to Its Threshold and Particle Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01108)
*Alex Chadyuk,Alicia Zhang,Roy Kucukates*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE method for reading causal graphs from pretrained models shows strong lag-1 edge recovery but limited longer-lag performance; optimal threshold depends on ground truth margin, and benchmark design inflates results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To replicate and critically analyze the TRACE method (Math & Lienhart, arXiv:2602.01135) for extracting causal graphs from pretrained autoregressive models, examining its true capabilities and limitations beyond the original paper's synthetic benchmark results.

Method: Replication study using conditional mutual information estimates thresholded at fixed τ to read causal graphs from pretrained sequence models. Analysis includes: 1) varying τ against ground truth margins, 2) evaluating performance across different edge lags, 3) testing on different data distributions with flatter decay patterns, 4) studying convergence with number of particles (N).

Result: 1) Optimal threshold τ* aligns with ground truth margin (δ=0.05), not a constant. 2) Single global threshold recovers mostly lag-1 edges (0.97-0.99 recall), lag≥2 edges much lower. 3) Per-lag thresholds improve lag-2 recovery (33-50%). 4) Original benchmark's lag decay concentrates 85% of truth at lag-1, inflating F1 scores. 5) F1 saturates at N=2 particles due to threshold margin, not estimator convergence.

Conclusion: TRACE effectively identifies direct adjacent influences but has limited capability for longer causal lags. The method's performance is heavily dependent on benchmark design and threshold selection relative to ground truth margins. Practitioners need careful threshold tuning and benchmark evaluation beyond the original paper's skewed results.

Abstract: TRACE (Math & Lienhart, arXiv:2602.01135) reads causal graphs over event types out of a pretrained autoregressive sequence model by thresholding a per-position conditional-mutual-information estimate at a fixed tau. We independently replicate its headline synthetic result: with tau selected on a validation split, mean per-sequence F1 against exact interventional truth reaches 0.90-0.91 at vocabulary size 1000 (paper: 0.91) and 0.86-0.91 from 100 to 2000. First, the optimal threshold is pinned to the truth margin, not to any constant: at every size the errors at tau* straddle the delta = 0.05 margin defining ground truth (missed true edges lie just above it, accepted false ones just below), and the blind optimum lands near delta/2 times the estimator's calibration, confirmed out of sample at 5000. Second, at a single global threshold TRACE mostly recovers a direct, adjacent-influence graph: lag-1 true edges are recalled at 0.97-0.99, while true edges at lag 2 or more read orders of magnitude lower---the reading-scale price of randomizing mediating positions, which an exact test of direct causal effect requires when the truth is unknown. A per-lag threshold family recovers a third to a half of lag-2 truth; on lag-uniform data one validated threshold recalls every lag at 0.40-0.87, 8-26 pp below an atomic-intervention control at lags 3-6. Third, the default lag decay of the paper's synthetic benchmark concentrates about 85% of interventional truth at lag 1 and pushes the rest below the estimator's noise floor, so headline F1 there certifies lag-1 recovery only and conflates the benchmark's skew with the algorithm's own limit; a flatter decay separates the two. Fourth, F1 saturates from N = 2 particles at the selected threshold---a property of the threshold's margin over the noise floor, not of the estimator, which converges as N^(-1/2). We distill five practitioner rules.

</details>


### [199] [When Does Online Adaptation Pay on the Edge? A Leakage-Free Evaluation of Warmup, Learning-Rate Selection, and Resource Trade-offs for Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01126)
*Takumi Fujimoto,Hiroaki Nishi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies two sources of bias in evaluating online adaptation for edge time-series forecasting: warmup budget effects and optimizer comparisons, proposing a validation-only commissioning procedure that yields more reliable performance estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online adaptation can benefit edge time-series forecasting under distribution drift, but current evaluation methods are flawed due to sensitivity to warmup budgets and unfair optimizer comparisons, leading to unreliable estimates of adaptation benefits.

Method: The authors study six public multivariate streams using a leakage-free streaming protocol, analyze warmup budget effects on static baselines, compare SGD+momentum and Adam optimizers with proper learning rate selection, and characterize accuracy against adaptation-state memory and latency. They propose using a held-out pre-drift validation slice for selecting both warmup budget and optimizer learning rates without accessing test data.

Result: Warmup budget changes affect estimated adaptation benefit by 3.0-18.8 percentage points; Adam outperforms SGD+m in 310 of 360 evaluated cells when properly tuned; several parameter-efficient variants are nondominated on adaptation-state-memory axis; smart-meter gains depend on selection rules.

Conclusion: The findings support a validation-only commissioning procedure for evaluating edge time-series adaptation methods, addressing comparison biases from warmup budgets and optimizer selection, though target-device latency and energy measurements remain for future work.

Abstract: Online adaptation can help edge time-series forecasting under distribution drift, but its measured benefit is sensitive to evaluation choices. We study six public multivariate streams, including building-sensor and smart-meter data, under a leakage-free streaming protocol. We identify two additional sources of comparison bias. First, the warmup budget of the static baseline has a two-sided effect: insufficient warmup undertrains the baseline, whereas excessive warmup can degrade its pre-drift generalization. Across six dataset-backbone settings, the estimated adaptation benefit changes by 3.0 to 18.8 percentage points (pp) over the 1,000-20,000-step warmup range. Second, comparing SGD with momentum (SGD+m) and Adam at a shared default learning rate conflates optimizer quality with rate sensitivity. We select both the warmup budget and each optimizer's online rate using a held-out pre-drift validation slice without accessing test data. Under this validation-only procedure, Adam outperforms SGD+m in 310 of 360 evaluated cells, while 4 Adam cells remain below the static baseline. We further characterize accuracy against adaptation-state memory and A100-measured per-update latency for full, head-only, and calibration-based adaptation. In the evaluated PatchTST frontier settings, several parameter-efficient variants are nondominated on the adaptation-state-memory axis. Smart-meter analyses also show that reported gains depend on meter-selection rules. These findings support a validation-only commissioning procedure, while target-device latency and energy remain to be measured. Code, data, and all reported numbers: https://github.com/keiotakmin/tsf-edge-adaptation.

</details>


### [200] [Scaled Idempotence in Transformer Attention: Paired OV Geometry and Shared-Value Algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01129)
*Jiming Feng,Junliang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformers exhibit sparse idempotence in attention heads: a subset of OV operators approximately square to themselves (T²≈αT), representing a trained orientation rather than universal geometric capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the algebraic structure of Transformer attention mechanisms, specifically identifying whether observed idempotence-like properties are inherent to the architecture or emerge through training.

Method: Analyzed six pretrained models (2.8B-235B parameters) to measure closure alignment (T² alignment with T). Used exact principal-coordinate factorization T=Q_O K Q_V^⊤ and performed controlled experiments scrambling orientation matrices while preserving geometric properties.

Result: 4-8% of heads achieve high closure (P≥0.9), far exceeding random baselines. Scrambling orientation reduces median closure by 3000×, showing closure is a trained property. Under value sharing, closure extends to a right-action algebra T_i T_j=α_j T_i with shared kernel structure.

Conclusion: Scaled idempotence is a sparse, trained orientation that emerges in attention heads, not an inherent geometric property. This algebraic structure extends under value sharing to form local operator algebras, revealing systematic structure in learned attention mechanisms.

Abstract: We identify a recurrent algebraic regularity in Transformer attention: a sparse subset of effective OV operators $T=OV^\top$ nearly closes under composition, $T^2\approxαT$. Across six pretrained endpoints spanning 2.8B--235B parameters, 3.98--8.00% of heads reach squared closure alignment $\mathcal{P}\geq0.9$, while no matched within-layer O/V mismatch does. An exact principal-coordinate factorization, $T=Q_OKQ_V^\top$ and $T^2=Q_O(KDK)Q_V^\top$, separates within-support transport from read--write return geometry. Across all 7,304 heads in nine MHA/GQA models, scrambling only the orientation of $K$ while preserving singular values, norms, factor spans, and principal angles reduces median closure from 0.336 to $1.04\times10^{-4}$; trained orientation wins for 98.64% of heads and in every layer. Constructive searches show that high closure is feasible in every surveyed layer, but usually not attained. Retrospective trajectories in three independently trained lineages further separate broadly available capacity from the orientations attained by final strong heads. Under exact value sharing, headwise closure extends to a right-action algebra, $T_iT_j=α_jT_i$. Seven-model experiments verify the approximate law and reveal distinct oblique projections with a shared value-defined kernel. These results characterize scaled idempotence as a sparse trained orientation within broadly available geometric capacity and show how value sharing extends a headwise relation into a local operator algebra.

</details>


### [201] [Superposed Latent Autoencoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01158)
*Quanling Zhao,Jiaying Yang,Tianqi Zhang,Ziyang Hao,Fatemeh Asgarinejad,Flavio Ponzina,Tajana Rosing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLAE preserves high-capacity latent representations by using learned superposition to share storage among multiple wide latents, improving reconstruction quality and downstream tasks under the same memory budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
