Motivation: Traditional autoencoders sacrifice representational capacity by making latent representations smaller to meet tight memory budgets. The authors question whether multiple wider latents could be stored together instead, preserving capacity while sharing storage.

Method: SLAE transforms latents into storage-friendly codes, binds them with randomized keys, superposes multiple codes into a single memory tensor, and learns to recover each latent before decoding. This replaces dimensional bottlenecks with structured interference that can be suppressed.

Result: SLAE substantially improves reconstruction-memory tradeoff across multiple datasets (CIFAR-10/100, SVHN, STL-10, Tiny ImageNet), reducing reconstruction error by up to 56% over conventional autoencoders at matched storage. Downstream classification improves by up to 16.79 percentage points under the same memory budget.

Conclusion: Instead of making every latent smaller, representations should be kept wide and allowed to share memory. SLAE demonstrates that learned superposition enables preservation of high-capacity representations while meeting storage constraints, offering a new principle for representation compression.

Abstract: Autoencoders typically meet tight latent-memory budgets by making each latent representation smaller, sacrificing representational capacity. We ask a different question: can multiple wider latents be stored together instead? We introduce the Superposed Latent Autoencoder (SLAE), which preserves high-capacity latent representations while sharing storage through learned superposition. SLAE transforms latents into storage-friendly codes, binds them with randomized keys, superposes multiple codes into a single memory tensor, and learns to recover each latent before decoding. Under the same storage budget, SLAE replaces irreversible dimensional bottlenecks with structured interference that can be suppressed. Across CIFAR-10/100, SVHN, STL-10, Tiny ImageNet, and a wide range of memory budgets, SLAE substantially improves the reconstruction--memory tradeoff, reducing reconstruction error by up to 56% over conventional autoencoders at matched storage. Further analysis shows that SLAE's advantage comes from making wider representations usable under the same storage budget. These gains also extend beyond reconstruction: the information preserved by SLAE improves downstream classification by up to 16.79 percentage points under the same memory budget. Our results suggest a new principle for representation compression: instead of making every latent smaller, keep representations wide and let them share memory.

</details>


### [202] [CopyShield: A Cross-Level Benchmark of Copyright Defenses in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01161)
*Maryam Alshehyari,Dushyant Singh Chauhan,Samuele Poppi,Martin Takac,Salem Lahlou,Nils Lukas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CopyShield is a benchmark comparing three copyright defense methods for LLMs at different intervention levels (output, behavioral, representation) using controlled memorization tests on public-domain books.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current copyright defenses for LLMs are evaluated under incompatible protocols, making it difficult to compare different approaches. There's a need for a controlled benchmark to systematically compare representative defense methods at distinct intervention levels.

Method: Created CopyShield benchmark with controlled memorization over five public-domain books. Evaluated three representative defenses: contrastive decoding (output-level), Direct Preference Optimization (behavioral-level), and activation intervention (representation-level). Tested on LLaMA-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B-v0.3 using metrics for literal leakage, calibrated non-literal leakage, utility, and degeneracy.

Result: Intervention level correlates with distinct compliance-utility trade-offs. On LLaMA: contrastive decoding stays near-degeneracy-free but hits literal-suppression floor; DPO eliminates literal leakage but causes 58% degeneracy; activation intervention blocks 84% of non-literal queries. Human evaluation shows DPO has low coherence while activation reduces copyright risk via broad refusal. On Mistral, patterns persist but DPO degeneracy drops to 10-14%, showing model dependence.

Conclusion: CopyShield provides cross-level reference baselines and identifies targeted non-literal suppression as an open challenge. Different defense approaches have varying trade-offs between copyright compliance and model utility, with effectiveness depending on both method and model architecture.

Abstract: Large language models can reproduce memorized text verbatim, yet copyright defenses are usually evaluated under incompatible protocols. We introduce CopyShield, a controlled benchmark comparing three representative defenses at distinct intervention levels: contrastive decoding (output), Direct Preference Optimization (behavioral), and activation intervention (representation). We evaluate CopyShield on two model families, LLaMA-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B-v0.3, using controlled memorization over five public-domain books and a shared protocol measuring literal leakage, calibrated non-literal leakage, utility, and degeneracy. Across these methods, intervention level is associated with distinct compliance-utility trade-offs. On LLaMA-3.1-8B, contrastive decoding remains near-degeneracy-free (0-2%) but reaches a literal-suppression floor at NV-Recall 0.192-0.203. DPO nearly eliminates literal leakage (0.263 to 0.002) but induces paraphrase-loop degeneracy in 58% of QA outputs, with no utility gain over the SFT baseline. Activation intervention attains the lowest non-literal flagging rate (1/200) by blocking 84% of non-literal queries before generation. Human evaluation confirms that DPO has low coherence, whereas activation lowers perceived copyright risk through broad refusal. On Mistral-7B-v0.3, the output- and representation-level patterns persist, while DPO degeneracy falls to 10-14%, showing that its severity is model-dependent. Together, CopyShield provides cross-level reference baselines and identifies targeted non-literal suppression as an open challenge. The code is available at https://github.com/spotai-mbzuai/CopyShield.git.

</details>


### [203] [Pre-carved Niches: The Formation Dynamics of Modular Task Partitions in Early LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01170)
*Guangqi Li,Yongxin Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM modularity formation is pre-carved at initialization, locks in via sharp jumps unrelated to learning rate, and domain-specific deviation only occurs during learning of that domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs show modular organization similar to human brain networks, how this modularity forms during training remains unknown. Previous research examined finished models but not the formation process itself.

Method: Trained a Pythia-410M model from scratch (two trajectories: bf16 and fp32) and tracked formation using attribution patching at every step, alongside probes for gradient norms, effective updates, weight norms, and first-order loss decomposition across 14 tasks in four cognitive domains.

Result: 1) Modular map is pre-carved at initialization - task pairs already overlap significantly before learning. 2) Partition locks in through two sharp jumps not tracking learning-rate schedule, with gradient-level relative deprivation favoring winning tasks. 3) Deviation from substrate appears only in the domain being learned, supporting modularity-learning link.

Conclusion: Modularity formation involves architecture-level pre-carving, sharp jumps unrelated to learning rate, and domain-specific deviation during learning. The study separates defensible feature-level findings from unanswered mechanistic questions and pre-registers a scale-threshold hypothesis for ongoing 2.8B experiments.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit a modular internal organization that mirrors well-studied functional networks of the human brain, but how this organization forms during training is unknown: prior work has characterized finished models, not the formation process. We track formation step by step: we train a Pythia-410M model from scratch (two trajectories, bf16 and fp32) and run attribution patching at every step, alongside probes for gradient norms, effective updates, weight norms, and first-order loss decomposition across 14 tasks in four cognitive domains. Three findings. First, the modular map is pre-carved: before any learning, the dominant task pair already overlaps at ~3.6x the attribution substrate (a task-independent baseline), and its layer-0 concentration is an architecture-level constant on this model family. Second, the partition locks in through two sharp jumps whose amplitudes do not track the learning-rate schedule (the second reaching 20.4 sigma quiet-window / 6.2 sigma global), accompanied by gradient-level relative deprivation--winners receive 2.25->2.73x the loser's gradient supply, 9.5-11.5 standard deviations below a random control--that does not propagate to updates or weights. Third, deviation from the substrate appears only in the domain being learned, consistent with the hypothesis that modularity tracks learning. We close by separating the feature-level account we can defend from the mechanistic questions we cannot, and we pre-register the scale-threshold hypothesis behind our ongoing 2.8B experiments.

</details>


### [204] [Births are difficult to predict even with rich survey and full-population register data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01194)
*Elizaveta Sivak,Emily M. Cantrell,Thomas Emery,Javier Garcia-Bernardo,Flavio Hafner,Kasia Karpinska,Malte Lüken,Adrienne Mendrik,Joris Mulder,Hanzhang Ren,Varun Satish,Mark Verhagen,Angelica M. Maineri,Paulina Pankowska,Jasmin Abdel Ghany,Bruno Arpino,Giovanni Cassani,Julia Hellstrand,Katya Ivanova,Sanni Kuikka,Ana Macanovic,Charles Rahal,Felix C. Tropf,Roland J. Veen,Nicole Walasek,Daniël van Wijk,Kelsey Q. Wright,Emilio Zagheni,Henry Abbink,Emanuele Aliverti,Matteo Amestoy,Tilbe Atav,Nicola Barban,Sunnee Billingsley,Goan J. Booij,Louis Boucherie,Yael Broos,Li Ya Chang,Jamie C. Chiu,Chiara Ludovica Comolli,Boris Cule,Qixiang Fang,Dennis M. Feehan,Rachel Ganly,Erwin Gielens,Rolando M. Gonzales Martinez,Andrea Gradassi,Rosember Guerra-Urzola,Mario Guerra-Urzola,Stéphane Guerrier,Enamul Hassan,Vincent A. Haverhoek,Andrew T. Hendrickson,Amber Howard,Yuxuan Jin,Sayash Kapoor,Erik-Jan van Kesteren,Iris ten Klooster,Marie Labussiere,Lydia T. Liu,Tiffany Liu,Adam Maghout,Simone Meneghello,Lasse Mohr,Clara H. Mulder,Saul J. Newman,Jessica Nisén,Janis Norden,Mikkel Odgaard,Riccardo Omenti,Ozancan Ozdemir,Christina Pao,Paige Park,Gaia Penta,Juan C. Perdomo,Tanzir Pial,Alessio Piraccini,Federica Querin,Ziwei Rao,Christian Rellama,Adrien Remund,Frederieke Richert,Arnout van de Rijt,Mojtaba Rostami Kandroodi,Stijn J. Rotman,Lucas Sage,Germans Savcisens,Katrin Schwanitz,Steven Skiena,Alessandro Spata,Yannick Stadtfeld,Benedikt Stroebl,Gaetano Tedesco,Mathilde Theelen,Gianluca Tori,Abigail Tun-Mendicuti,Rishabh Tyagi,Keyon Vafa,Luiz Felipe Vecchietti,Linda Vecgaile,Willem R. J. Vermeulen,Maria-Pia Victoria Feser,Lionel A. Voirol,Thom B. Volker,Xinran Wang,Jiani Yan,Xinyi Zhao,Flora Zhou,Zuzana Zilincikova,Malvina Nissim,Matthew J. Salganik,Gert Stulp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers conducted a prediction challenge for childbirth within 3 years using Dutch data, finding advanced models didn't outperform classical ones and identifying a biological ceiling for prediction accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why major life events like childbirth are difficult to predict - whether it's due to limitations in theory, data, and algorithms, or the inherent role of chance in human lives.

Method: A data challenge with 147 researchers predicting births within 3 years for Dutch residents aged 18-45. Used both survey data and full-population registers, with methods ranging from logistic regression to large language models and transformers.

Result: Predictions were moderately accurate (best F1: register 0.59, survey 0.76). Advanced models did not outperform classical ones. Larger register data didn't outperform survey data. Simulated biological ceiling for prediction was estimated at F1 ~0.86-0.94 for survey and 0.88-0.96 for registers.

Conclusion: Observed performance falls short of the biological ceiling, indicating imperfect data and methods, while the ceiling itself shows that chance in reproduction alone sets a non-trivial limit on predicting individual lives.

Abstract: Major life events have proven difficult to predict. Does this reflect limits of theory, data, and algorithms, or the large role of chance? We examine one outcome - having a child within three years - through a near-ideal setting for prediction: a data challenge where 147 researchers predicted births for Dutch residents aged 18-45, using survey data and full-population registers. Methods ranged from logistic regression to a large language model and transformers. Predictions were moderately accurate (best F1: register 0.59, survey 0.76); advanced models did not outperform classical ones; and the larger registers did not beat the survey. Simulating the stochastic biology of conception and pregnancy, we estimated a predictive ceiling (survey F1 ~ 0.86-0.94, register 0.88-0.96). Observed performance falls short of this ceiling, implicating imperfect data, methods, and unmodelled chance, while the ceiling itself shows that chance in reproduction alone sets a non-trivial limit on predicting individual lives.

</details>


### [205] [Recent Developments in Transformer Inference Deployment on FPGA Platforms: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01212)
*Arjan Blankestijn,Uraz Odyurt,Amirreza Yousefzadeh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic review of recent advances in FPGA-based Transformer inference accelerators, analyzing trends, design choices, and optimization techniques for improved operational performance and energy efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth in Transformer-based machine learning models creates high demand for capable deployment solutions that address both operational performance (throughput, latency) and efficiency (energy consumption). FPGAs offer a promising alternative to CPUs and GPUs due to their flexibility, energy efficiency, improved latency, and suitability for on-site deployment.

Method: Conducted a systematic literature review of recent advances in Transformer inference on FPGA platforms. Extracted and analyzed preferred techniques for implementation and optimization, creating a taxonomy of topics to guide both academic and industrial researchers.

Result: Identified the most recent advances, trends, and design choices for Transformer inference on FPGA platforms. Developed a comprehensive taxonomy that serves as a guide for researchers working on FPGA-based Transformer acceleration.

Conclusion: FPGA platforms provide a viable and attractive alternative for Transformer inference acceleration, offering implementation flexibility, energy efficiency, and improved latency. The systematic review and taxonomy can help guide future research and development in this emerging field, benefiting both academia and industry.

Abstract: With the rapid and continuous growth in the incorporation of machine learning models based on the Transformer architecture, capable deployment is in high demand. In this context, capable deployment refers to operational performance aspects, e.g., throughput and latency, as well as efficiency aspects, e.g., energy consumption. When it comes to the task of inference using such models, purpose-built hardware accelerators provide a lucrative alternative to common deployment choices, such as Central Processing Units (CPUs) and Graphics Processing Units (GPUs). The Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA) platforms category is an example of such alternative accelerators, promising implementation flexibility, energy efficiency, improved latency and suitability for on-site deployment. We investigate the most recent advances, trends, and design choices for Transformer inference on FPGA platforms. We perform a systematic literature review, extracting and delving into preferred techniques for implementation and optimisation. This study and the provided taxonomy of topics could act as a guide for researchers from the academia and industry alike.

</details>


### [206] [REFACTOR-VLA: Unsupervised Library Learning of Typed Motor Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01215)
*Riyaaz Shaik,Chandru Venkataraman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REFACTOR-VLA introduces a wake/sleep system that learns reusable skills from robot demonstrations by clustering behaviorally-equivalent action sequences and representing them as typed lambda terms, outperforming monolithic VLA models on long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vision-language-action (VLA) models like OpenVLA, π₀, RT-2, and RDT-1B are monolithic and emit raw motor commands without organizing behavior into reusable abstractions, leading to degradation on long-horizon tasks and poor interpretability. Existing skill-discovery methods fail to properly identify when two action sequences are behaviorally equivalent.

Method: REFACTOR-VLA uses a wake/sleep system: (1) Sleep phase clusters motor-program fragments using a Behavioral-Equivalence Kernel (BEK) computed from rollouts of a learned latent world model Mφ, (2) Wake phase emits typed lambda terms over a Hindley-Milner-inspired vocabulary consumed by a library-conditioned rectified-flow action decoder. Abstractions must pass Minimum Description Length and return-preservation gates.

Result: On LIBERO benchmarks, adding an auxiliary supervised contrastive (InfoNCE) loss during world-model warmup significantly improves sleep-phase clustering: Normalized Mutual Information scores of 0.462±0.021 (object), 0.867±0.025 (spatial), 0.915±0.013 (goal), and 0.754±0.010 (LIBERO-10). Outperforms strongest published baselines on all 4 suites by mean Δ=+0.184. The decoder uses 2 of 3 admitted abstractions and rewrites all 256 sampled demonstrations.

Conclusion: Training objective matters more than model capacity alone for skill learning. REFACTOR-VLA's approach of learning reusable skills through behavioral-equivalence clustering and typed lambda abstractions enables better long-horizon performance and produces the first real-LIBERO task-language library, addressing key limitations of monolithic VLA models.

Abstract: Most vision-language-action (VLA) models -- OpenVLA, $π_0$, RT-2, RDT-1B -- are monolithic: they emit raw motor commands or short action chunks without organizing behavior into reusable abstractions, so they degrade on long-horizon tasks and resist interpretation. Existing skill-discovery methods sidestep the core question of when two action sequences are behaviorally equivalent, either clustering contrastive embeddings or delegating the judgment to a language model uncalibrated to the robot's dynamics. We introduce REFACTOR-VLA, a wake/sleep system for learning reusable skills. Its sleep phase clusters motor-program fragments under a Behavioral-Equivalence Kernel (BEK) computed from rollouts of a learned latent world model $M_φ$; its wake phase emits typed lambda terms over a Hindley--Milner-inspired vocabulary, consumed by a library-conditioned rectified-flow action decoder. Abstractions are admitted only if they pass Minimum Description Length and return-preservation gates. On LIBERO we report two findings. First, enlarging the world model from 188M to 430M parameters worsened performance on 4 of 4 suites, so capacity alone does not help. Second, the training objective matters far more: adding an auxiliary supervised contrastive (InfoNCE) loss during world-model warmup substantially improves sleep-phase clustering, giving Normalized Mutual Information at $n=3$ seeds of $0.462 \pm 0.021$ (object), $0.867 \pm 0.025$ (spatial), $0.915 \pm 0.013$ (goal) and $0.754 \pm 0.010$ (LIBERO-10), and beating the strongest published baseline on all 4 suites by a mean $Δ= +0.184$. Across providers ($n=12$) the 95% bootstrap confidence interval for mean pairwise NMI is $[0.683, 0.729]$ (mean $0.705$). The sleep phase also yields the first real-LIBERO task-language library: the decoder uses 2 of 3 admitted abstractions and rewrites all 256 sampled demonstrations.

</details>


### [207] [Multi-Head Self Attention is a Parameter Identification Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01231)
*W. Ross Morrow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-head attention has inherent parameter identification issues where some parameters are not uniquely determined, and more attention heads improve parameter identification ratios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why certain transformer architectural choices (like more attention heads, RoPE, GQA) improve performance from a mathematical/statistical identification perspective, explaining the structural properties of attention mechanisms.

Method: Mathematical analysis of multi-head scaled dot product attention as parameter identification strategy, theoretical derivation of identification ratios, analysis of bias term effects, extension to modern improvements (RoPE, GQA), and numerical experiments with rebalancing approach to confirm theoretical results.

Result: Multi-head attention cannot be fully identified; unidentified parameters ratio scales as 1/(2H) where H is number of heads; bias terms can be ineffective in softmax attention; modern improvements like RoPE and GQA improve meaningful parameter ratios; training updates can overlap model-invariant subspaces due to lack of identification.

Conclusion: Transformer architectural improvements (more heads, RoPE, GQA) enhance performance by improving parameter identification, making models structurally more identified and increasing meaningful parameter ratios, though attention can never achieve full identification.

Abstract: We prove that a multi-head scaled dot product attention can be viewed as a parameter identification strategy. The ratio of unidentified parameters to the total number of parameters scales like the reciprocal of the number of heads ($1/2 \to 1/(2H)$), meaning models with more heads are structurally more identified. A subtle side effect of the mathematics observation that attention can never be fully identified. Similarly we also show that some bias terms can have no effect on softmax-based attention layers in both the single- and multiple-head settings, though this is mostly a curiosity that should have a marginal effect on model size and model training/prediction efficiency. We also touch on modern improvements to transformers including RoPE and GQA from this perspective, illustrating how those as well can improve the ratio of ``meaningful'' parameters to all parameters. Simple numerical examples demonstrate that training can indeed involve updates that overlap model-invariant subspaces that arise from a lack of identification. As part of our experiments we use a ``rebalancing'' approach that can ``fix'' updates that overlap unindentified subspaces but do not try to present evidence this should actually be adopted. Instead we simply view our numerical results as exploring and confirming the theoretical results. As a whole we discuss a purely mathematical/statistical explanation, identification, for why specific architectural choices in transformers may have improved performance.

</details>


### [208] [Post-Training Science for Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01244)
*Charles O'Neill,Mudith Jayasekara,Harry Partridge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Large-scale empirical analysis of supervised fine-tuning decisions across model families and datasets, measuring optimal hyperparameters, LoRA vs full fine-tuning tradeoffs, validation metrics, and scaling laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supervised fine-tuning requires repeated rediscovery of optimal settings (learning rate, batch size, LoRA vs full fine-tuning, epochs, optimizer choices) for each new model and dataset, creating inefficiency and lack of systematic understanding.

Method: Comprehensive sweep experiments varying one lever at a time across dense and mixture-of-experts models from Qwen3 and Llama families, on four real-world customer SFT datasets, for both LoRA and full fine-tuning, with built-in customer evaluations.

Result: Analysis provides empirical recommendations for optimal learning rate/batch size scaling with model size, LoRA vs full fine-tuning tradeoffs, validation metric reliability, post-training scaling laws, optimal training epochs, and optimizer performance, with uncertainty quantification.

Conclusion: Systematic empirical study establishes evidence-based guidelines for supervised fine-tuning decisions, providing transferable rules and quantifying uncertainties to improve efficiency and effectiveness across model families and datasets.

Abstract: Every supervised fine-tuning run forces the same chain of decisions, such as learning rate, batch size, LoRA or full fine-tuning, how many epochs, which optimiser, and what data to feed the model. Each of these is typically rediscovered from scratch for every new model and dataset. Here we measure them under one instrument: a sweep that varies one lever at a time, and spans dense and mixture-of-experts models in two families (Qwen3 and Llama), on four real-world customer SFT datasets, for both LoRA and full fine-tuning. These datasets give a controlled testbed: each task carries an evaluation built with the customer, and its training data is produced by iterative supervised fine-tuning that refines model outputs until they pass that evaluation, so the supervised target is internally consistent and the task judge we report against is the criterion the data was built to satisfy. We ask how the optimal learning rate and batch size move with model scale, family, and data, and whether one selection rule transfers across them; what LoRA trades against full fine-tuning, and how its rank and alpha set what the adapter can learn; whether validation loss (or other metrics, such as loss landscape flatness) faithfully ranks downstream quality; whether post-training gains scale with model size and data volume, on a model ladder extended through mixtures-of-experts to 235B parameters; how many epochs to train before general instruction-following erodes; and whether a geometry-aware optimiser improves on AdamW. Each recommendation is paired with a measure of its uncertainty.

</details>


### [209] [Explore More, Drift Less: Outcome-Only Reinforcement Learning Can Suffice for Long-Horizon Interactive Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01245)
*Liming Pu,Xiaoxia Li,Yifu Liu,Teng Cao,Bin Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CANOPY (Coverage-Anchored On-Policy RL) is a minimalist RL protocol that enables small open language models to master long-horizon interactive tasks through scaled exploration and KL-anchored on-policy updates, achieving state-of-the-art results without task-specific supervision or auxiliary signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The shared belief that outcome-only RL hits a ceiling for small open models on long-horizon interactive tasks has led to complex workarounds like denser rewards, SFT priors, skill libraries, curated memory, or multi-agent orchestration. The authors argue this ceiling is an artifact of two common practice failures: signal starvation (under-scaled exploration silences the hardest tasks) and policy drift (unanchored objectives degrade policies when informative samples are rare).

Method: CANOPY addresses both problems: 1) Scale same-task exploration until natural signal reappears, 2) Keep every update on-policy, KL-anchored, and confined to the agent's own action tokens. The method uses only environment interaction without task-specific supervision, auxiliary credit signals, or elaborate agent scaffolding.

Result: On AppWorld (long-horizon interactive coding benchmark), a Qwen3-14B policy trained with CANOPY topped the public leaderboard (Feb 2026; Test-Normal TGC 86.9, Test-Challenge 67.6). The same principles lifted Qwen3.5-9B on SWE-bench Verified by 16.6 points, showing that agentic RL alone can internalize long-horizon capability directly into small open models.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that the perceived ceiling for outcome-only RL on small open models is an artifact of methodological shortcomings, not a fundamental limitation. CANOPY's minimalist protocol of scaled exploration and anchored on-policy updates enables small models to master complex long-horizon tasks through pure RL, challenging the need for complex auxiliary systems.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is a natural way to post-train LLM agents for long-horizon interactive tasks judged only by end-of-task verification, yet a shared belief holds that outcome-only RL soon hits a ceiling on small open models. Recent work therefore compensates around the training with denser rewards, SFT priors, skill libraries, curated memory, or multi-agent orchestration. We argue the ceiling is an artifact of two failures of common practice. Signal starvation: group-relative RL with sparse outcome-only rewards yields a gradient only when a task's rollout group mixes successes and failures, so under-scaled exploration silences exactly the hardest, most instructive tasks. Policy drift: squeezing many updates out of a small task pool degrades the policy itself, as an unanchored objective lets the sampling distribution collapse exactly when saturation has already made informative groups rare. We present CANOPY (Coverage-ANchored On-PolicY RL), a minimalist protocol attacking both directly: scale same-task exploration until the natural signal reappears, keep every update on-policy, KL-anchored, and confined to the agent's own action tokens, then cash in an enlarged interaction budget at test time. On AppWorld, a long-horizon interactive coding benchmark, a Qwen3-14B policy trained with CANOPY through environment interaction alone--without task-specific supervision, auxiliary credit signals, or elaborate agent scaffolding--topped the public leaderboard (Feb. 2026; Test-Normal TGC 86.9, Test-Challenge 67.6), and the same design principles lift Qwen3.5-9B on SWE-bench Verified by 16.6 points. Agentic RL alone thus internalizes long-horizon capability directly into a small open model; we plan to release the complete training stack at https://github.com/AlibabaResearch/SignalCoverageRL.

</details>


### [210] [Solving In-Table Prediction Problems by Deep Neural Networks with Performance Evaluation Using Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01262)
*Xiao Zhao,Daniela Oelke*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces In-Table Prediction (ITP), a self-supervised learning approach where neural networks predict values of arbitrarily selected columns in tabular data based on remaining columns, focusing on continuous features and proposing a novel neural layer for missing value embedding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional tabular deep learning follows supervised learning with explicit targets, but the authors want to explore whether neural networks can learn relationships among columns themselves without pre-defined targets. They identify three usage scenarios not extensively studied in literature: predicting missing values, generating synthetic data, and understanding column relationships.

Method: The paper uses self-supervised learning where columns are randomly masked as learning targets. For continuous features, they propose a novel neural layer that embeds both numerical values and empty/missing values. They generate synthetic data with predefined column relationships and insert missing values using two mechanisms. Three NN architectures (MLP, Resnet, Transformer) are evaluated with an adapted masking strategy for test data.

Result: The attention-based Transformer architecture outperforms MLP and Resnet when sufficient training examples are available and with relatively large embedding lengths. However, these findings are limited to controlled synthetic conditions with small numbers of columns.

Conclusion: This work represents an initial, narrowly-scoped investigation of In-Table Prediction rather than a general characterization for real-world tabular data. The proposed method shows promise for column relationship learning in synthetic settings, but further research is needed for real-world applications and datasets.

Abstract: Tabular deep learning (TDL) leverages neural networks (NN) to extract patterns from tabular data. Traditional TDL methods follow a supervised learning paradigm, where a target feature is explicitly given. In this work, however, we explore a different approach by employing deep NNs to learn relationships among individual columns within a given table. We investigate whether NNs can predict the values of arbitrarily selected columns in a given table based on the remaining known columns. We call this problem In-Table Prediction (ITB), which is slightly different from table imputation methods and the pretraining task of TDL. Three potential usage scenarios are identified, which, to our best knowledge, have not been extensively studied in the literature. A self-supervised learning approach is applied to address this problem by randomly selecting columns to be masked out and used as learning targets. This work focuses on tabular datasets containing only continuous features. To handle missing values in continuous features, a novel neural layer is proposed to embed both numerical and empty values. Synthetic data is generated based on predefined column relationships, with empty values inserted using two distinct mechanisms. Additionally, an adapted masking strategy is employed to create test data. Performances of three NN architectures, namely MLP, Resnet and Transformer, are evaluated using the generated synthetic data. We conclude that, the attention-based structure outperforms the other two networks, when a sufficiently large number of training examples is available and a relatively large embedding length is chosen. We stress that these findings are obtained under controlled, synthetic conditions with a small number of columns and it should therefore be regarded as an initial, narrowly-scoped investigation rather than a general characterization of ITP on real-world tabular data.

</details>


### [211] [Position: Privacy Is a Claim, Not a Property of Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01273)
*Jiachen Zhao,Antonia Januszewicz,Taeho Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper critiques the current practice of using synthetic data in privacy-sensitive ML research, showing it often lacks explicit privacy frameworks and calls for verifiable, evidence-based privacy claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observe that synthetic data usage in ML research has evolved from being based on residual inference risk under stated assumptions to relying on appearance-based properties inferred from data generation itself, reflecting a problematic shift in community privacy standards.

Method: The paper uses an empirical analysis of recent publications across major ML venues to examine how synthetic data is used in privacy-sensitive settings, specifically looking at the articulation of threat models, inference risks, and falsifiable privacy claims.

Result: The analysis reveals that synthetic data is frequently used in privacy-sensitive settings without explicit articulation of threat models, inference risks, or falsifiable privacy claims, leading to privacy assurance that is implicit, difficult to verify, and unevenly distributed with heightened exposure for rare and minority records.

Conclusion: The paper argues for treating privacy as an explicit, evidence-based scientific claim and recommends that ML venues adopt norms requiring privacy-relevant assertions to be clearly scoped, testable, and contestable to improve scientific rigor and accountability.

Abstract: Synthetic data has become a common component of machine learning research. While widely adopted, its use in privacy-sensitive contexts has quietly shifted from a claim of residual inference risk under stated assumptions to an appearance-based property inferred from data generation itself. In this position paper, we argue that this shift reflects an implicit change in community standards for what counts as sufficient privacy evidence, rather than a misunderstanding of well-established privacy principles. Drawing on an empirical analysis of recent publications across major ML venues, we show that synthetic data is frequently used in privacy-sensitive settings without explicit articulation of threat models, inference risks, or falsifiable privacy claims. As a result, privacy assurance often remains implicit, difficult to verify, and unevenly distributed, with heightened exposure for rare and minority records. We argue for treating privacy as an explicit, evidence-based scientific claim and recommend that ML venues adopt norms requiring privacy-relevant assertions to be clearly scoped, testable, and contestable.

</details>


### [212] [The Constitutional Coverage Trilemma in AI Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01275)
*Natalija Mitic,Soona Sedahmed A. O.,Mamadou Selly Ly,Moustapha Cisse*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Frontier AI systems encode implicit constitutional rankings of values like safety, helpfulness, honesty, autonomy, and equity. The study finds mismatched alignment between human preferences and current AI model designs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to investigate whether frontier AI systems align with human constitutional preferences. It addresses the concern that deployed models encode implicit value rankings that may not match what users actually want across safety, helpfulness, honesty, autonomy, and equity.

Method: Combined paraphrase-controlled audit of 23 frontier LLM archetypes' default constitutions with a pairwise-tradeoff study of 1,649 US participants using the same instrument. Analyzed supply-demand mismatch, temporal drift, and proposed solutions.

Result: 1) Demand is broad across all five values; 2) Supply is narrow (~2% coverage) and drifting away from undercovered values like autonomy; 3) A 2-vertex menu {honesty, autonomy} outperforms all 23 current archetypes by 47% on mean regret; 4) Three vertex additions improve regret by up to 81%/64%.

Conclusion: Current frontier AI constitutional types poorly match human demand, drifting away from undercovered values like autonomy. The solution lies in offering a sparse menu of constitutional types rather than the current frontier of models. There's a budgeted-pluralism trilemma where current models are empirically in the binding regime.

Abstract: Frontier AI systems function as \emph{constitutional institutions}: each deployed model encodes an implicit ranking among safety, helpfulness, honesty, autonomy, and equity. We ask whether the supply of frontier constitutional types covers human demand. Combining a paraphrase-controlled audit of the as-shipped default constitutions of $23$ frontier LLM archetypes with a pairwise-tradeoff study of $1{,}649$ US participants on the same instrument, we report three facts. \emph{Demand is broad}: it spans all five values, with the largest constituency under one-third. \emph{Supply is narrow and drifting}: the $23$-archetype hull occupies ${\sim}2\%$ of the demand hull under conservative noise-matched estimation ($0.10\%$ at full audit precision), no archetype puts helpfulness or autonomy first ($37\%$ of users are constitutionally homeless), and across six model families autonomy decreases in $5/6$, equity increases in $5/6$, and safety increases in $4/6$, with monotone within-family version trends (order-permutation $p = 0.013$) and the autonomy decline concentrated in scenarios where safety is not at stake. The drift's importance is directional: \emph{away} from a value already undercovered, mechanically worsening the welfare floor for the least-served users. \emph{The fix is sparse}: a $2$-vertex menu $\{e_{\mathrm{HON}}, e_{\mathrm{AUT}}\}$ beats the full $23$-archetype frontier by $47\%$ on mean regret (CI $[43\%, 52\%]$); three vertex additions cut mean/worst-group regret by up to $81\%$/$64\%$. We formalize these findings as a budgeted-pluralism trilemma, show the binding regime is empirically realized, and verify the conclusions are robust to distance-based welfare and to degraded routing. The instrument and audit harness are described in full in the appendices.

</details>


### [213] [One-Layer Transformer Provably Learns Multiclass One-Nearest Neighbor in Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01311)
*Skanda Athreya,Yutong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: One-layer transformers with argmax head are equivalent to one-nearest-neighbor classifiers in multiclass classification, using simplex encoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work established equivalence between one-layer transformers and nearest-neighbor classifiers in binary settings, but the multiclass result relied on a non-standard rounding-based approach rather than the typical argmax head used in practice.

Method: Extending binary equivalence work to multiclass via simplex encoding, showing identical behavior between one-layer transformers with argmax classification head and one-nearest-neighbor classifiers.

Result: Demonstrated that one-layer transformers with argmax classification head behave identically to one-nearest-neighbor classifiers in multiclass setting.

Conclusion: One-layer transformers with an argmax head are equivalent to one-nearest-neighbor classifiers in multiclass settings, closing a prior gap in theoretical understanding.

Abstract: We extend recent work establishing an equivalence between one-layer transformers and nearest-neighbor classifiers in the binary setting to the multiclass case. By leveraging the simplex encoding, we show that one-layer transformers with an argmax classification head behave identically to a one-nearest-neighbor classifier in the multiclass setting. This closes a gap left by prior work, whose multiclass result relied on a non-standard rounding-based approach rather than the typical argmax head used in practice.

</details>


### [214] [Bandits in Prod: Hyperparameter Optimization at Inference Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01335)
*Louis Abraham,Tuan-Anh Nguyen,Nicolas Devatine*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IMABO is a framework for Online Hyperparameter Optimization that treats it as an infinitely many-armed bandit problem, combining any bandit policy with any oracle for proposing configurations to optimize live systems without validation data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many production systems (especially modern agentic systems) must be configured using live requests with noisy feedback, often lacking representative validation data. Traditional hyperparameter optimization methods aren't suitable for this online setting where configurations can only be assessed through live deployment.

Method: Formalizes OHPO as infinitely many-armed bandit over mixed/conditional search spaces. Introduces IMABO framework combining bandit policies with oracles for proposing configurations. Presents IMOSS, a restart-free anytime policy with active set growing as t^β, and proves regret bounds. Combines IMOSS with three practical oracles: Tree-structured Parzen Estimator, incumbent-mutation oracle, and pretrained tabular foundation model.

Result: Proves expected cumulative quantile-regret bound of O(p_ρ^{-1/β} + T^{(1+β)/2}) where β controls active-set growth and p_ρ bounds proposal quality probability. All three oracles improve over uniform random baseline. IMABO achieves lowest cumulative regret across diverse OHPO settings including tuning ML models and configuring LLM-based agents.

Conclusion: IMABO provides a general, theoretically grounded framework for Online Hyperparameter Optimization that effectively handles the challenges of configuring live systems without validation data, demonstrating strong empirical performance across various application domains.

Abstract: Many production systems can assess a configuration only by using it on live requests and observing noisy feedback. Modern agentic systems are a prominent example, with inference-time choices such as model selection, retrieval depth, prompting strategy, and decoding temperature, yet often with no representative validation data. We formalize this setting as Online Hyperparameter Optimization (OHPO) and cast it as an infinitely many-armed bandit over mixed and conditional search spaces. We introduce IMABO, a general framework that combines any bandit policy for choosing among already sampled configurations with any oracle for proposing new ones. We instantiate it with IMOSS, a restart-free anytime policy whose active set grows as $t^β$, and prove an expected cumulative quantile-regret bound of $O(p_ρ^{-1/β} + T^{(1+β)/2})$, where $β\in(0,1)$ controls active-set growth and $p_ρ$ lower-bounds the probability that a proposed configuration falls in the top-$ρ$ fraction of the search space. We combine IMOSS with three practical oracles: a Tree-structured Parzen Estimator, an incumbent-mutation oracle driven by a per-coordinate bandit, and a pretrained tabular foundation model, all three improving over the uniform random oracle baseline. IMABO obtains the lowest cumulative regret across diverse OHPO settings, from tuning classical machine-learning models to configuring LLM-based agents.

</details>


### [215] [SMELT: Scaling Laws for Compute-Matched MoE Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01343)
*Shaowen Wang,Ge Zhang,Kairong Luo,Yuhao Wu,Shaofan Liu,Jiaheng Liu,Wenhao Huang,Shen Yan,Jian Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Looping middle layers twice in sparse MoE Transformers (SMELT) improves performance while matching computational budgets, saving 6.8-18% training FLOPs with advantages growing on code tasks and longer sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous evaluations of looped Transformers conflated architectural advantages with extra FLOPs by not controlling for computational budgets. The authors aim to study whether looping provides genuine architectural benefits when properly matching FLOPs, parameters, and KV cache.

Method: The authors propose SMELT (Sparse MoE Transformer, middle layers Loop Twice), which loops the middle half of layers twice while matching baseline on per-token FLOPs, total non-embedding parameters, and KV cache. They scale SMELT across four sizes up to 54B parameters and fit separate Chinchilla-style scaling laws for each architecture.

Result: SMELT achieves 6.8-18.0% training FLOPs savings on the compute-optimal frontier. The advantage transfers to downstream benchmarks beyond validation loss predictions, is largest on Code tasks, and grows with sample length and number of in-context examples. Mechanistic analysis shows the second visit reduces attention sink and redirects mass toward content-relevant tokens.

Conclusion: Looping can improve Transformers even under budget matching, turning depth reuse into measurable gains. The SMELT recipe offers practical benefits for sparse MoE architectures with particular advantages for code generation and long-context tasks.

Abstract: Looped Transformers increase effective depth by iterating a shared block of layers, but most evaluations compare at fixed model size, conflating architectural advantage with extra FLOPs. We study looping on Mixture-of-Experts Transformers while closely matching per-token FLOPs, total non-embedding parameters, and KV cache. Through a series of ablations, we arrive at a recipe we call SMELT (Sparse MoE Transformer, middle layers Loop Twice), which loops the middle half of layers twice while matching the unlooped Baseline on all three budgets. We scale SMELT across four sizes up to 54B non-embedding parameters and fit a separate Chinchilla-style scaling law for each architecture. SMELT's loss drops faster with compute, saving 6.8--18.0\% of training FLOPs on the compute-optimal frontier. The advantage transfers to downstream benchmarks beyond what validation loss predicts, is largest on Code, and grows with sample length and the number of in-context examples. Mechanistic analysis shows that the second visit reduces the attention sink and redirects mass toward content-relevant tokens, an inductive bias that may underlie the observed performance gains. These results show that looping can improve Transformers even under budget matching, offering a practical recipe that turns depth reuse into measurable gains.

</details>


### [216] [Contribution-Aware Bandwidth Allocation for Multimodal Split Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01406)
*Iason Ofeidis,Leandros Tassiulas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ModalShare is a novel inter-modality allocation method for multimodal edge learning that dynamically allocates uplink bandwidth across modalities based on their Shapley contribution scores, improving accuracy by 15-12 percentage points over equal allocation schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal edge models face a bottleneck: training requires substantial uplink bandwidth for all modality activations in split learning, but existing compression methods allocate bandwidth equally across modalities without considering their varying contributions to predictions, wasting bandwidth on less important modalities.

Method: Proposes ModalShare, which allocates different keep-ratios for each modality based on Shapley contribution scores computed server-side. The server evaluates how different coalitions of already-received activations contribute to predictions, then dynamically allocates the fixed uplink budget proportionally to each modality's importance score, requiring no client-side computation or prior modality knowledge.

Result: ModalShare improves accuracy by 15.4 percentage points on CREMA-D and 12.4 percentage points on MVSA datasets at 5x compression compared to equal keep-ratio allocation. It demonstrates strong performance across three different compressors, three datasets, and four budget levels, recovering significant gains left behind by existing compressors in multimodal settings.

Conclusion: Inter-modality allocation is a critical dimension in multimodal edge learning compression, and ModalShare's Shapley-based dynamic allocation outperforms uniform allocation by intelligently distributing bandwidth according to each modality's contribution to fused predictions, with minimal overhead and no prior modality identification needed.

Abstract: Multimodal models are increasingly the default option for perception at the network edge, yet they are trained almost entirely in the datacenter, because a client holding several sensor streams cannot host an encoder per modality. Split Learning makes such training feasible by keeping only the first layers on the device, at the cost of an uplink that must carry smashed activations for every modality at every step. Existing compression schemes give each modality the same keep-ratio, so the shared budget is divided in proportion to smashed-activation dimension, a quantity unrelated to how much each modality contributes to the fused prediction. We make that division an explicit decision and call it inter-modality allocation: under a fixed uplink budget, every policy transmits the same expected payload and differs only in how that payload is split across modalities. Our allocator, ModalShare, sets each modality's keep-ratio from a Shapley contribution score that the server computes over coalitions of activations it has already received. Measuring this score adds no uplink traffic and no client-side computation, and needs no prior knowledge of which stream is which. ModalShare improves accuracy over equal keep-ratios by 15.4 and 12.4 percentage points on CREMA-D and MVSA at matched payload in 5x compression, with strong performance across three compressors, three datasets, and four budgets. We show that existing compressors underperform in multimodal settings, with ModalShare recovering what gains are left behind.

</details>


### [217] [Predicting Subsurface Abnormalities Growth using Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01417)
*Mehrdad Shafiei Dizaji,Hoda Azari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PINN-based model combines CNN, SFCA, ConvLSTM, and TFFA modules to predict GPR data by embedding electromagnetic wave physics, achieving enhanced accuracy for civil infrastructure assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve Ground-Penetrating Radar (GPR) data prediction for civil infrastructure evaluation by integrating physical principles into deep learning models, similar to how medical imaging predicts tumor behavior. This addresses the need for more accurate non-destructive evaluation methods.

Method: Develops a specialized Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) with three main components: CNN for feature extraction, spatial feature channel attention (SFCA) mechanism, and ConvLSTM with temporal feature frame attention (TFFA) modules. The model embeds electromagnetic wave propagation physics into its architecture and uses self-adaptive attention mechanisms to fine-tune visual and temporal feature responses.

Result: The PINN model demonstrates enhanced accuracy in forecasting GPR data compared to conventional methods. This improvement is significant for bridge deck condition assessment and other civil infrastructure evaluations.

Conclusion: Physics-Informed Neural Networks have transformative potential for Non-Destructive Evaluation by improving infrastructure deterioration prediction accuracy and providing deeper insights into deterioration mechanisms through physics-based modeling.

Abstract: The research explores the pioneering integration of Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) into the domain of Ground-Penetrating Radar (GPR) data prediction. This research presents a detailed development framework for a specialized PINN model, proficient at interpreting and forecasting GPR data, much like how medical imaging models predict tumor behavior. By harnessing the synergy between deep learning algorithms and the physical laws governing subsurface structures or in medical terms, human tissues the model effectively embeds the physics of electromagnetic wave propagation into its architecture. This ensures that predictions not only align with fundamental physical principles but also mirror the precision needed in medical diagnostics for detecting and monitoring tumors. The suggested deep learning structure comprises three components: a CNN, a spatial feature channel attention (SFCA) mechanism, and ConvLSTM, along with temporal feature frame attention (TFFA) modules. The attention mechanism computes channel attention and temporal attention weights using self-adaptation, thereby fine tuning the visual and temporal feature responses to extract the most pertinent and significant visual and temporal features. By integrating physics directly into the neural network, our model has shown enhanced accuracy in forecasting GPR data. This improvement is vital for conducting effective assessments of bridge deck conditions and other evaluations related to civil infrastructure. The use of Physics Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) has demonstrated the potential to transform the field of Non-Destructive Evaluation (NDE) by enhancing the precision of infrastructure deterioration predictions. Moreover, it offers a deeper insight into the fundamental mechanisms of deterioration, viewed through the prism of physics-based models.

</details>


### [218] [Provably Safe Sim-to-Real Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01418)
*Tingting Ni,Maryam Kamgarpour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a computational framework for safe sim-to-real transfer in reinforcement learning, where policies trained in simulators can be safely deployed and refined in the real world under safety constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world RL faces high sample complexity and safety concerns, especially in domains like robotics and healthcare. Simulator training is cheap but suffers from sim-to-real mismatch, while real-world data collection is expensive and must satisfy safety constraints.

Method: The authors formulate safe sim-to-real transfer as a reward-free safe RL problem. They develop a computationally efficient algorithm that leverages simulator information to safely explore the real world while reducing interaction requirements.

Result: The proposed algorithm ensures safe exploration and enables computation of near-optimal feasible policies for any potential reward function. It provides real-world sample complexity bounds that quantify the benefits of simulator usage in terms of sim-to-real mismatch.

Conclusion: The paper presents a principled approach to safe sim-to-real transfer that reduces real-world interaction while maintaining safety guarantees, with theoretical analysis showing how simulator quality affects sample complexity.

Abstract: To mitigate the sample complexity of real-world reinforcement learning (RL), a common practice is to first train a policy in a simulator, where samples are cheap, and then deploy the learned policy in the real world with the hope that it generalizes effectively. Such direct sim-to-real transfer is not guaranteed to succeed: simulator-trained policies can be suboptimal in the real world due to sim-to-real mismatch. Correcting this mismatch requires collecting data from the real system, but in many applications, such as robotics and healthcare, this data-collection process is itself subject to safety constraints. This gives rise to the problem of safe sim-to-real transfer: how can an agent exploit an imperfect simulator while ensuring safe real-world data collection and learning a near-optimal feasible policy for the target system? We address this problem by formulating safe sim-to-real transfer within the framework of reward-free safe RL. We design a computationally efficient algorithm that exploits simulator information to provably reduce real-world interaction while ensuring safe exploration and enabling the computation of a near-optimal feasible policy for any potential reward function. Our real-world sample complexity bound characterizes the benefit of using the simulator in terms of the sim-to-real mismatch.

</details>


### [219] [CATeye: Coupled Attribute-Topology Invariance Learning for Voucher Abuse Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01425)
*Tian Tian,Shuaicheng Niu,Hao Kuang,Yuanhang Hu,Dong Li,Zhiqi Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CATeye is a framework for detecting voucher abuse in e-commerce that addresses coupled attribute-topology distribution shifts using learnable selectors to identify invariant patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Voucher abuse is a major e-commerce problem where fraud patterns rapidly evolve across time and regions, causing distribution shifts that degrade existing detection models. Current approaches fail when facing coupled attribute-topology shifts where attribute changes induce topological shifts that amplify through GNN message passing.

Method: The Coupled Attribute-Topology Invariance Learning (CATeye) framework uses two learnable selectors: an Attribute Invariance Selector (AIS) that learns node-adaptive masks to filter non-invariant attributes, and an Edge Invariance Selector (EIS) that samples invariant subgraphs conditioned on retained attributes. It then constructs multiple views and applies view-specific objectives to emphasize domain-invariant representations while suppressing domain-specific variations.

Result: Experiments on Lazada's proprietary dataset and public benchmarks show CATeye consistently outperforms nine strong domain generalization and graph anomaly detection baselines, achieving up to 8.61% improvement in average F1 score over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: CATeye effectively addresses the challenge of coupled attribute-topology shifts in voucher abuse detection, providing a robust framework that maintains performance across evolving fraud patterns and regional variations without frequent retraining.

Abstract: Voucher abuse poses a major challenge in e-commerce, where malicious users exploit promotional vouchers for profit. Unfortunately, fraud patterns evolve rapidly over time and across regions, causing distribution shifts that degrade existing detection models unless retrained frequently. To tackle this, we propose the Coupled Attribute-Topology Invariance Learning framework (CATeye). The key challenge arises from coupled attribute-topology shift, where edges built from attribute proximity cause environment-driven attribute shift to induce shifted topology, thereby amplifying variant signals through GNN message passing. CATeye sees through such coupled shifts with two learnable selectors. First, an Attribute Invariance Selector (AIS) learns node-adaptive masks to filter out non-invariant attributes. Then, conditioned on retained invariant attributes, an Edge Invariance Selector (EIS) samples an invariant subgraph and isolates non-invariant edges. Using the resulting invariant and non-invariant components, CATeye constructs multiple views and applies view-specific objectives to emphasize domain-invariant representations while suppressing domain-specific variations. Experiments on both a proprietary dataset from Lazada, a major Southeast Asian e-commerce platform, and a public benchmark show that CATeye consistently outperforms nine strong domain generalization and graph anomaly detection baselines, achieving up to an 8.61% improvement in average F1 score over the strongest baseline. Source code is publicly available at https://github.com/Tian0426/CATeye.

</details>


### [220] [TRIAGE: Three-level Routing and Intelligent Agent Guidance for Efficient Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01428)
*Ruocan Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRIAGE is a three-level routing framework that reduces LLM token consumption by reusing historical execution trajectories as reusable skills (TaaS), achieving up to 76.3% token reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ReAct paradigm suffers from efficiency problems where every query triggers complete reasoning from scratch, and similar queries repeat identical steps without leveraging historical experience, wasting tokens and computational resources.

Method: Proposes TRIAGE with three-level routing: (1) Direct Reuse for identical queries (0 tokens), (2) Skill Substitution for similar queries via deterministic parameter substitution (0 tokens), (3) Full ReAct for novel queries. Introduces TaaS (Trajectory-as-a-Skill) to abstract trajectories into reusable skills and automatic skill extraction from high-frequency patterns.

Result: 62.3% token savings on 1,007 security monitoring queries (56.0% Level 2, 5.5% Level 1, both zero cost). 76.3% token reduction on ToolBench cross-domain validation (15 domains, 345 queries). Online learning shows L2 hit rate rises from 0% to 57% within 100 queries, average token cost drops from 198 to 74.7.

Conclusion: TRIAGE effectively addresses ReAct's efficiency limitations by reusing execution trajectories, creating a "the more you use it, the more efficient it becomes" positive feedback loop with significant token savings and cross-domain generalizability.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents based on the ReAct paradigm have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in tool use and task execution. However, ReAct suffers from a fundamental efficiency problem: every query triggers a complete reasoning loop from scratch, and similar queries repeat identical steps without leveraging historical experience. We propose TRIAGE,a three-level routing framework that reduces token consumption by reusing historical execution trajectories. Its core innovation is TaaS (Trajectory-as-a-Skill), which abstracts historical execution trajectories into reusable skills, realizing 'experience as a service'. TRIAGE classifies queries into three levels: (1) Direct Reuse-identical queries, 0 tokens; (2) Skill Substitution-similar queries, 0 tokens via deterministic parameter substitution; (3) Full ReAct-novel queries, automatically stored for future reuse. In large-scale experiments on 1,007 security monitoring queries, TRIAGE achieves 62.3% token savings, with 56.0% of queries at Level 2 and 5.5% at Level 1, both executing at zero cost. Cross-domain validation on ToolBench (15 domains, 345 queries) achieves 76.3% token reduction, confirming the generalizability of semantic routing. An online learning experiment demonstrates cold-start-to-mature evolution: the L2 hit rate rises from 0% to 57% within the first 100 queries, and the average token cost drops from 198 to 74.7. We also propose an automatic Skill extraction mechanism that distills high-frequency trajectory patterns into deterministic Skills, creating a positive feedback loop of 'the more you use it, the more efficient it becomes'.

</details>


### [221] [Learning Sparse Decision Trees via Transformer Variational Auto-Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01430)
*Giacomo Fidone,Alessio Cascione,Riccardo Guidotti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TREVIS is a new approach for learning decision trees using Tree Transformer Variational Auto-Encoders to map trees to continuous latent space, enabling gradient-based optimization for complex objectives like predictive performance plus sparsity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decision trees are widely used for high-stakes decisions due to their transparency, but existing algorithms focus mostly on predictive performance and overlook joint optimization of other desirable properties like structural sparsity.

Method: TREVIS explores the latent space of a Tree Transformer Variational Auto-Encoder (TTVAE) to map decision trees to continuous latent representations, replacing discrete search space with continuous one to enable gradient-based optimization via a differentiable surrogate model.

Result: TREVIS discovers decision trees that match the predictive performance of existing near-optimal algorithms while improving their structural sparsity through joint optimization of both objectives.

Conclusion: TREVIS successfully enables learning decision trees with respect to complex objectives by leveraging latent space representations and gradient-based optimization, providing a promising approach for optimizing multiple desirable properties simultaneously in decision tree learning.

Abstract: Decision trees are among the most widely used models in machine learning, largely due to their transparent decision logic, making them well-suited for high-stakes decision-making contexts. However, most existing learning algorithms focus on predictive performance, overlooking the joint optimization of other desirable properties, such as structural sparsity. In this work we propose TREVIS, an approach for learning decision trees with respect to complex objectives, based on the exploration of the latent space of a Tree Transformer Variational Auto-Encoder (TTVAE). By mapping decision trees onto latent representations, TREVIS replaces the discrete search space with a continuous one, enabling gradient-based optimization via a differentiable surrogate model. We experiment with TREVIS for learning decision trees that jointly optimize predictive performance and sparsity. Results show that TREVIS discovers decision trees matching the predictive performance of existing near-optimal algorithms while improving their structural sparsity.

</details>


### [222] [Efficiently Estimating Optimal Hyperparameter Scaling Laws through Power-Law Entropy Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01431)
*Zhiliang Chen,Sebastian Ament,David Eriksson,Maximilian Balandat,Eytan Bakshy,Jihao Andreas Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new Bayesian optimization method called Power-Law Entropy Search (PLES) efficiently estimates optimal hyperparameter scaling laws for LLMs using much less computational budget than grid search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for estimating optimal hyperparameter scaling laws require exhaustive grid searches with thousands of training runs, consuming enormous computational resources. There's a need for more efficient approaches to determine how optimal hyperparameters scale with model and data size.

Method: Introduces Power-Law Entropy Search (PLES), a computational cost-aware acquisition function built on multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization. PLES searches for candidates that reduce uncertainty in scaling law estimates (rather than optimizing a single objective) and selects configurations that maximally reduce uncertainty per unit computational cost, naturally favoring informative small-scale experiments.

Result: PLES converges to accurate optimal hyperparameter scaling laws using less than one-tenth of the computational budget required by conventional grid search and other baselines. Evaluated on synthetic benchmarks, surrogate models fitted to real LLM training data, and actual LLM pre-training runs.

Conclusion: PLES provides an efficient method for estimating hyperparameter scaling laws, dramatically reducing the computational cost compared to traditional grid search approaches while maintaining accuracy, enabling better prediction of optimal configurations at production scales.

Abstract: Optimal hyperparameter scaling laws describe how the best hyperparameters for large language model (LLM) training change with model and data scale, enabling practitioners to predict optimal configurations at production scales without expensive large-scale tuning. However, estimating these scaling laws conventionally requires exhaustive grid searches over thousands of training runs, consuming enormous computational resources. We introduce Power-Law Entropy Search (PLES), a computational cost-aware acquisition function built on multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization that efficiently estimates optimal hyperparameter scaling laws through adaptive experimentation. A key innovation in PLES is that it searches for candidates that reduce the overall uncertainty of a scaling law estimate, instead of optimizing a single objective function. At each iteration, PLES selects the candidate configuration that maximally reduces the uncertainty of the scaling law estimates per unit computational cost, naturally favoring informative small-scale experiments. We evaluate PLES on synthetic benchmarks, surrogate models fitted to real LLM training data, and actual LLM pre-training runs. Across all settings, PLES converges to accurate optimal hyperparameter scaling laws using less than one-tenth of the computational budget required by conventional grid search and other baselines.

</details>


### [223] [Edge-Girth as a Structural Edge Feature for Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01441)
*Lilian Marey,Charlotte Laclau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EGAGNN: A graph neural network using edge-girth (length of shortest cycle through an edge) and its multiplicity as structural descriptors, outperforming fixed-size cycle-counting methods on molecular property prediction while being limited by 1-WL on graphs with uniform cycle patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GNNs based on message passing are limited to 1-WL expressiveness. Common fixes use precomputed structural descriptors like triangle counts, but these require committing to a fixed substructure size in advance, which is suboptimal and data-blind.

Method: Proposes using edge-girth (length of shortest cycle through an edge) and its multiplicity (number of such shortest cycles) as per-edge invariants. These are computable via single BFS per edge and injected into a gated message-passing architecture called EGAGNN.

Result: EGAGNN achieves test MAE 3x lower than closest gated comparator on ZINC-12k regression with 104k parameters. It matches cycle-counting dictionaries up to length 8 with half the channels, while cycle-4 dictionaries perform no better than no structural info.

Conclusion: Edge-girth descriptors effectively capture cyclic structure without fixed-size commitment, but have limitations: on graphs where every edge sees identical shortest cycles, the descriptor becomes constant and models collapse back to 1-WL bound, as shown on BREC benchmark.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNN) based on message passing are provably no more powerful than the one-dimensional Weisfeiler--Leman colour-refinement test (1-WL): two graphs it cannot tell apart receive identical representations, however deep or wide the network. A common remedy augments node or edge features with precomputed structural descriptors, most often counts of a fixed small subgraph such as triangles or longer cycles, but such counts require committing in advance to the size of the substructure counted, a choice usually made blind to the data. We study a descriptor that avoids this choice. The edge-girth of an edge is the length of a shortest cycle through it, and its multiplicity is the number of such shortest cycles; together they form a per-edge invariant that reports cycles of arbitrary length, computable exactly by a single breadth-first search per edge. Injected into a gated message-passing architecture, EGAGNN, it reaches a test MAE a factor three below the closest gated comparator on the ZINC-12k regression benchmark at 104k parameters; against bounded cycle-counting descriptors under the same architecture, it matches only a dictionary counting cycles up to length eight, using twice as many channels, while a dictionary capped at length four performs no better than no structural information at all. On graph discrimination we prove a matching limitation: on graphs where every edge sees the same number of shortest cycles of the same length, the descriptor becomes constant and any model built on it collapses back to the 1-WL bound. This holds without exception across all 400 pairs of the BREC benchmark: not one of the 90 such pairs is distinguished.

</details>


### [224] [Diffusion as a Training Curriculum for Timestep-Free Iterative Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01449)
*Mariia Drozdova,Aidan Sirbu,Pietro Miotti,Robert Obryk,Mayalen Etcheverry,Eyvind Niklasson,Blake Richards*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based model with persistent hidden state becomes an anytime solver that keeps improving with inference depth, achieving near-perfect Sudoku and Maze solving without progressive denoising.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models and recursive reasoners both use iterative processes but handle information across iterations differently. The researchers aim to combine their strengths by creating a model that can improve solution quality indefinitely through deeper inference.

Method: Add persistent hidden state to a diffusion denoiser while removing timestep conditioning, creating a single shared update function that can run to arbitrary depth. Use maximum corruption (fresh Gaussian noise) at every inference step instead of progressive denoising. Train with ordered annealed corruption curriculum.

Result: Achieved 99.90% exact solve on Sudoku-Extreme and 98.93% solve rate on Maze-Unique. Model accuracy keeps improving with inference depth beyond training rollout lengths and backpropagation window. Single trajectories efficiently explore solution space without parallel rollouts, candidate selection, or external verifiers.

Conclusion: The key contribution of diffusion to reasoning models isn't the sampling procedure at inference, but the denoising training curriculum. Maximum corruption during inference enables efficient solution space exploration, while the persistent hidden state allows the model to function as an anytime solver that improves indefinitely.

Abstract: Diffusion models and recursive reasoners are both iterative, but they carry information across iterations differently. We add a persistent hidden state to a diffusion denoiser and remove its timestep conditioning, leaving a single shared update that can be run to arbitrary depth. The result is an anytime solver: accuracy keeps improving with inference depth far beyond the rollout lengths and backpropagation window used in training, reaching 99.90% exact solve on Sudoku-Extreme. We also obtain 98.93% solve rate on Maze-Unique. Surprisingly, progressive denoising is unnecessary at inference: holding corruption at its maximum by replacing every non-clue variable with fresh Gaussian noise at each step retains near-perfect solving and converges to stable solutions. This simple noise-injection mechanism enables a single trajectory to efficiently explore the solution space and settle on the correct answer without parallel rollouts, candidate selection, or external verifiers required by prior reasoning models. Nonetheless, ordered annealed corruption remains critical during training, which suggests that diffusion's primary contribution to our anytime solver is not a sampling procedure at inference, but a denoising training curriculum.

</details>


### [225] [Rethinking Learnability in Offline Data-driven Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01493)
*Chao Qian,Chen-Guang Wang,Rong-Xi Tan,Ke Xue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Offline data-driven optimization can achieve efficient black-box optimization by learning from fixed datasets; a trajectory-based learnability framework is proposed and shown to outperform existing methods on benchmark tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evolutionary algorithms and Bayesian optimization face efficiency challenges as real-world black-box optimization problems grow increasingly complex. Offline data-driven optimization aims to improve efficiency but lacks theoretical understanding of what learnability conditions are sufficient for successful optimization without additional online evaluations.

Method: Proposes algorithm-dependent learnability requiring accuracy only on the optimizer's trajectory. Formalizes a trajectory-learning framework with three components: trajectory construction, trajectory modeling, and candidate generation. Introduces UGTL (Uncertainty-aware Gradient-guided Trajectory Learning) which constructs locally coherent improvement trajectories, models them with conditional diffusion, and selects diverse candidates.

Result: Proves algorithm-dependent learnability is sufficient for discrete (greedy/local search for submodular maximization) and continuous (projected gradient descent for convex minimization) settings. UGTL achieves best aggregate mean rank (3.1/25) among 25 methods on five Design-Bench tasks. Controlled analyses confirm trajectory construction significantly contributes to performance.

Conclusion: Algorithm-dependent learnability provides sufficient conditions for offline optimization, and trajectory-based learning frameworks offer practical approaches that outperform existing methods by focusing on relevant search paths rather than requiring global accuracy.
