Abstract: Black-Box Optimization (BBO) has found broad applications, but evolutionary algorithms and Bayesian optimization face efficiency challenges as real-world BBO problems grow increasingly complex. Data-driven optimization improves the efficiency of BBO algorithms by learning from data. Offline data-driven optimization seeks high-quality solutions using only a fixed set of previous evaluations, attracting substantial attention because it requires no additional online evaluations. Many offline optimization methods have been proposed, but a fundamental question remains unanswered: what learnability is sufficient for offline optimization? Prior theoretical studies show that Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) learnability is insufficient, as the optimal region may remain poorly learned even when most regions are well learned. In this paper, we propose algorithm-dependent learnability, which requires accuracy only on the optimizer's trajectory. We prove that its value-query form is sufficient for representative discrete settings, including greedy and local search for submodular maximization, while its first-order analogue is sufficient for projected gradient descent on convex minimization. Motivated by this notion, we formalize a trajectory-learning framework comprising trajectory construction, trajectory modeling, and candidate generation, and analyze existing trajectory-based methods under it. We further propose Uncertainty-aware Gradient-guided Trajectory Learning (UGTL), which constructs locally coherent improvement trajectories reflecting plausible search paths, models them with conditional diffusion, and selects a diverse candidate set. On five Design-Bench tasks, UGTL achieves the best aggregate mean rank, $3.1/25$, among 25 methods. Controlled trajectory analyses and cross-architecture replacements confirm that our trajectory construction plays a significant role in the improvement.

</details>


### [226] [Optimizing Byzantine Node Placement in Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01495)
*Edoardo Gabrielli,Gabriele Tolomei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BPI (Byzantine Placement Influence) is a method to identify optimal Byzantine node placements in decentralized federated learning networks to maximize attack impact without testing every possible configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current security evaluations of decentralized federated learning focus on Byzantine behavior but overlook the critical role of Byzantine node placement. Since aggregation is distributed over communication graphs, malicious influence propagation depends heavily on which nodes are compromised.

Method: Proposes Byzantine Placement Influence (BPI) - a set-level measure derived from gossip dynamics that quantifies cumulative exposure of honest nodes to Byzantine sources over training. Develops efficient algorithms for optimizing BPI placement under fixed compromise budgets.

Result: BPI-guided placements consistently identify highly damaging configurations across six graph families and remain effective with Byzantine-robust aggregation. Shows Byzantine placement significantly affects attack effectiveness, making it a critical dimension for security evaluations.

Conclusion: Byzantine placement is a critical but under-modeled dimension of DFL threat models and robustness evaluations. BPI provides a practical way to identify worst-case placements without extensive testing.

Abstract: Security evaluations of decentralized federated learning (DFL) typically focus on how Byzantine participants behave, while largely overlooking which participants are compromised. Yet, because aggregation is distributed over a communication graph, the placement of Byzantine nodes determines how malicious influence propagates through the network. We therefore treat Byzantine placement as an explicit adversarial decision and formulate the attacker's objective as selecting, under a fixed compromise budget, the set of participants that maximizes its finite-time impact on honest nodes. To approximate this objective without executing the learning process for every candidate placement, we introduce Byzantine Placement Influence (BPI), a set-level measure derived from the actual gossip dynamics that quantifies the cumulative exposure of honest nodes to Byzantine sources over the training horizon. Unlike placement criteria based on node centrality heuristics, BPI directly accounts for weighted multi-hop propagation and interactions among compromised nodes. We develop efficient algorithms for optimizing BPI and evaluate them across six heterogeneous graph families, untargeted model poisoning, and backdoor attacks. BPI-guided placements consistently identify highly damaging configurations across different network structures and remain effective when the linear gossip assumption is relaxed through Byzantine-robust aggregation. Our results show that Byzantine placement is a critical but under-modeled dimension of DFL threat models and robustness evaluations.

</details>


### [227] [LatentPress: Context Compression Beyond Text and Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01507)
*Zhengze Zhou,Hejian Sang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LatentPress compresses conversational histories and documents into continuous memory tokens that frozen language models can read directly, achieving 4-16× compression while outperforming text summaries and OCR-based methods on memory QA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current compressed context representations (human-readable text or rendered images) require decoding even when the consumer is a language model, creating unnecessary overhead. There's a need for a machine-facing context interface that allows language models to read compressed information directly without text reconstruction.

Method: Introduces LatentPress, which compresses contexts into continuous memory tokens that a frozen decoder reads directly through its input-embedding interface. Uses a small reader-matched writer (4.2M-26.2M parameters, ~0.1% of decoder) to achieve 4-16× compression. Only trains an adapter rather than the full model.

Result: Achieves 0.504 accuracy at 7.70× compression vs 0.490 for uncompressed evidence on LongMemEval, outperforming text summaries (0.184) and OCR-based compression (0.426 to 0.312). On LongBench-QA, in-domain writers match/exceed raw-context reading at 4-8× compression. Writing is 43ms per conversation (~10× faster than text summarization/OCR), reading is 5-9× faster than raw context or cached OCR. Successfully transfers from UltraChat to LongMemEval and across document domains.

Conclusion: Direct soft tokens represent a practical machine-facing context interface beyond traditional text and vision approaches, enabling efficient compression and retrieval for language models without reconstruction overhead.

Abstract: Compressed context is usually carried as human-readable text or as rendered images that must be decoded, even when its consumer is a language model. We introduce LatentPress, which writes conversational histories and long documents into a third representation: continuous memory tokens that a frozen decoder reads directly through its input-embedding interface, with no text reconstruction at inference. A small reader-matched writer compresses $4$-$16\times$ while training only an adapter (4.2M-26.2M parameters, $\sim\!0.1\%$ of the decoder). On LongMemEval, LatentPress reaches $0.504$ accuracy at $7.70\times$ compression versus $0.490$ for uncompressed evidence, outperforming text summaries (0.184) and OCR-based compression (0.426 to 0.312). On LongBench-QA, in-domain writers match or exceed raw-context reading at $4$-$8\times$ compression, while $16\times$ trails raw. Writing takes 43ms per conversation, roughly an order of magnitude faster than text summarization or OCR reconstruction, and reading is $5$-$9\times$ faster than raw context or cached OCR. We validate the interface under two transfer settings, zero-shot from UltraChat to LongMemEval memory QA and from LongMemEval-derived QA to unseen LongBench document domains, establishing direct soft tokens as a practical machine-facing context interface beyond text and vision. The implementation of the experiments could be found at: https://github.com/xuyd16ai/context_softtoken_compress .

</details>


### [228] [Quantum Sparse Autoencoders for Q-Matrix Estimation in Cognitive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01537)
*Arif Hassan Zidan,Yi Pan,Bowen Guo,Xiang Li,Yu Bao,Yingfeng Wang,Tianming Liu,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel quantum sparse autoencoder (QSAE) is introduced for Q-matrix estimation in cognitive diagnosis, showing enhanced robustness over classical methods despite similar average accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-driven Q-matrix estimation remains challenging due to many correlated skills in assessments and departure of real response patterns from idealized generative assumptions. There's a need for more robust methods to handle the complexity of real educational assessment data.

Method: The QSAE embeds binary response vectors into quantum circuits using an encoder, compresses them into sparse latent representations, and maps these to Q-matrices. This represents the first application of quantum machine learning to cognitive diagnosis.

Result: When benchmarked against classical autoencoders (CAE) across 60 simulated and 9 real datasets, QSAE showed substantial stability advantages (lower variance in 49/60 conditions) and outperformed CAE on 6/9 real datasets, though CAE sometimes achieved higher average accuracy in simulations.

Conclusion: The principal advancement of quantum machine learning in cognitive diagnosis is not universal accuracy improvement, but enhanced robustness and capability to explore latent-structure complexity in real-world educational datasets.

Abstract: Q-matrices play a central role in cognitive diagnosis within educational data mining (EDM), specifying which latent skills each assessment item requires. Data-driven Q-matrix estimation remains challenging when assessments involve many correlated skills and when real response patterns depart from idealized generative assumptions. We introduce a novel quantum sparse autoencoder (QSAE) for Q-matrix estimation, which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first application of quantum machine learning (QML) to cognitive diagnosis. Overall, the QSAE embeds each student's binary response vector into a quantum circuit using an encoder, compresses it into a sparse latent representation, and maps that representation to the Q-matrix. We benchmark the QSAE against a classical autoencoder (CAE) across 60 simulated datasets and 9 real-world assessment datasets. The results reveal complementary strengths. Although the CAE partially achieves higher average accuracy under several simulation conditions, the QSAE is substantially more stable across replications, exhibiting lower variance in 49 of the 60 conditions. Moreover, on real assessment data, the QSAE outperforms the CAE on 6 of the 9 datasets. These findings suggest that the principal advancement of QML in this setting is not universal accuracy improvement, but enhanced robustness and capability to explore latent-structure complexity in real datasets.

</details>


### [229] [NashDreamer: Model-Based Reinforcement Learning for Zero-Sum Imperfect-Information Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01549)
*Tomáš Holeček,Viliam Lisý*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NashDreamer: A centralized model-based RL framework for two-player zero-sum imperfect information games that uses a multi-agent recurrent state-space model to improve sample efficiency while maintaining Nash equilibrium convergence guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model-based RL works well in single-agent settings but faces challenges in competitive imperfect information games due to opponent-induced non-stationarity and identifiability issues with decentralized model learning. The authors argue that centralized model learning becomes a mathematical necessity in multi-agent settings.

Method: Proposes NashDreamer framework with centralized Multi-Agent Recurrent State-Space Model (MARSSM) that decouples environment dynamics from players' strategies' effects on individual observations. It can use arbitrary policy gradient algorithms and inherits their convergence properties.

Result: Empirical evaluations across four benchmark games show NashDreamer substantially improves sample efficiency over model-free baselines early in training. Theoretically analyzed optimization landscape, identifying vulnerability of Dreamer algorithms to posterior collapse in stochastic environments.

Conclusion: NashDreamer provides a principled MBRL framework for competitive imperfect information games with demonstrated sample efficiency improvements. The authors identify posterior collapse in stochastic environments as an open challenge for Dreamer-family algorithms.

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) has achieved remarkable results in single-agent domains, yet its extension to competitive imperfect information games (IIGs) remains underexplored. In multi-agent settings, opponent-induced non-stationarity complicates the learning process, and decentralized model learning faces severe identifiability barriers, which we argue make centralized model learning a mathematical necessity. Building on this analysis, we propose NashDreamer, a principled MBRL framework for two-player zero-sum IIGs. It introduces a centralized Multi-Agent Recurrent State-Space Model (MARSSM) that decouples environment dynamics from the effect of players' strategies on their individual observations. NashDreamer is designed to use arbitrary policy gradient algorithms and inherits their convergence guarantees towards Nash equilibria under an idealized model. Empirical evaluations across four benchmark games demonstrate that NashDreamer substantially improves sample efficiency over model-free baselines early in the training. Finally, we theoretically analyze the architecture's optimization landscape, identifying the vulnerability of the Dreamer family of algorithms to posterior collapse in stochastic environments. We leave it as an open challenge.

</details>


### [230] [A Mathematical Theory of Reusable Neural Bases for Network Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01550)
*Binshuai Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LRNBA is a new neural architecture that represents network blocks as linear combinations of shared neural bases, achieving high compression rates and stable training while reducing memory costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory cost has become a critical bottleneck in both training and inference of large AI models, necessitating more parameter-efficient approaches.

Method: Inspired by RNN designs, the Linear Reusable Neural Bases Architecture represents each network block as a linear combination of a shared set of neural bases, enabling high compression rates and stable training.

Result: The architecture allows building significantly wider and deeper networks with the same parameter budget, achieving comparable or faster convergence and lower loss than classical architectures while maintaining stable training dynamics.

Conclusion: LRNBA effectively addresses memory bottlenecks in large AI models through parameter-efficient neural basis sharing, enabling more scalable model architectures with stable training.

Abstract: As large AI models become increasingly prevalent across a wide range of applications, memory cost has become a critical bottleneck in both training and inference. To mitigate this issue, we introduce the Linear Reusable Neural Bases Architecture (LRNBA), a novel framework aimed at improving parameter efficiency and reducing memory cost. Inspired by recurrent neural network (RNN) designs, the core idea of our approach is to represent each network block as a linear combination of a shared set of neural bases, thereby enjoying highly network compression rate while maintaining stable training. The proposed architecture allows for the construction of significantly wider and deeper networks under the same parameter budget. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our model achieves comparable or even faster convergence and lower loss than classical architectures, while maintaining stable training dynamics.

</details>


### [231] [Retrieved but not ranked: surface-form bias in structural retrieval, from mathematics to agent trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01556)
*Nabira Rashid,Manolis Kellis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper finds that current embedding models fail to retrieve items with similar underlying structure but different wording, performing at or below chance levels when surface forms differ, while lexical rerankers can help only when variation is incidental, not adversarial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to evaluate embedding retrieval performance when surface form and meaning are deliberately separated - testing whether models can retrieve items that share underlying structure but not wording, which is crucial for understanding the limitations of current embedding approaches in handling semantic vs. lexical similarity.

Method: The method uses two unrelated domains under a unified protocol: (1) competition mathematics (MathNet-Retrieve with 500 queries and 117,088-item corpus) and (2) embodied-agent trajectories (ALFWorld-derived with 118 queries and 336 trajectories). They evaluate production embedders, lexical rerankers as controls, and LLM rerankers, measuring retrieval performance when gold items must differ in wording or objects.

Result: In mathematics: complete failure with 0.0% Hit@1 at the heaviest disguise tier; correct items are in top 10 nearly always but lose to lexically similar distractors (95.2-99.8% of misses). In trajectories: models perform at or near hypergeometric chance when gold involves different objects, and below chance when differing in both object and receptacle. Lexical rerankers hurt mathematics but help trajectories (closing 26-36% of gap). LLM rerankers recover 5-63% gap in mathematics and 43-76% in trajectories. Oracle vs. bad retrieval shows no significant difference in downstream solver performance.

Conclusion: Current embedding retrieval anchors on literal tokens rather than task structure. The sign of lexical reranker effectiveness reveals whether a benchmark's surface variation is adversarial (mathematics) or incidental (trajectories). LLM rerankers show domain-dependent effectiveness, and downstream experiments reveal no headroom for improvement as solver accuracy is largely a truncation proxy.

Abstract: We evaluate embedding retrieval where surface form and meaning are pulled apart on purpose: retrieving items that share underlying structure but not wording, in two unrelated domains under one protocol, competition mathematics (MathNet-Retrieve; 500 queries, 117,088-item corpus) and embodied-agent trajectories (ALFWorld-derived; 118 queries, 336 trajectories). In mathematics the failure is complete: strict Hit@1 at the heaviest disguise tier is 0.0% for both production embedders (bootstrap 95% CI [0.0, 0.0]) while the correct item sits in the top 10 nearly always, and in 95.2 to 99.8% of misses the winner is more lexically similar to the query than the correct answer. In trajectories, where surface variation is incidental, the same models land at or near hypergeometric chance when gold must involve a different object, and below chance for all three embedders once gold must differ in object and receptacle: retrieval anchors on literal tokens, not task structure. A lexical reranker control hurts in mathematics and helps in trajectories (closing 26 to 36% of the gap, CIs excluding zero); its sign reveals whether a benchmark's surface variation is adversarial or incidental. An LLM reranker recovers 5 to 63% of the gap in mathematics and 43 to 76% in trajectories; direction replicates across three judges (all 21 cells positive), but effect sizes, tier profiles, and the outlier judge change with domain (paired differences excluding zero everywhere). Mathematics gains concentrate on well-known competitions (+19.8 points, CI [+6.7, +33.2], one of six cells), so part of the recovery is memorization. In a paired downstream experiment (210 queries, graders at 96 to 99% agreement), oracle retrieval was indistinguishable from adversarially bad retrieval (McNemar p = 0.678); the solver's 69.5% zero-shot accuracy is largely a truncation proxy (97 to 100% on finished answers), leaving no headroom.

</details>


### [232] [Gradient-Update Mismatch: Rethinking Conflict-Free Training of Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01558)
*Jing Xiao,Xinhai Chen,Qinglin Wang,Menghan Jia,Zhiquan Lai,Dongsheng Li,Jie Liu,Tiejun Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gradient-Update Mismatch (GUM) occurs when optimizer transformations reintroduce conflict in PINN training despite gradient surgery. The proposed Gradient-Update Alignment (GUA) projects optimizer updates onto the conflict-free cone, eliminating GUM and significantly improving PINN accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address Gradient-Update Mismatch (GUM), where even when gradient surgery constructs a conflict-free direction (a_t), modern optimizers can transform it into an update (u_t) that reintroduces conflict, undermining the benefits of gradient surgery in Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs).

Method: The authors propose Gradient-Update Alignment (GUA), which projects the optimizer's update proposal (u_t) onto the conflict-free cone (C_t) to obtain an aligned update (p_t). When the optimizer maintains internal state, GUA further adjusts this state toward targets reconstructed from the applied update.

Result: Experimental results show that GUM is widespread across momentum, adaptive, and curvature-based optimizers, with conflict rates reaching up to 86.3%. GUA achieves conflict-free applied updates and consistently improves various gradient surgery methods, reducing the relative L2 error by up to 98.2% in individual PINN settings.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that Gradient-Update Alignment (GUA) effectively mitigates Gradient-Update Mismatch (GUM) across a wide range of optimizers, consistently improving the performance of gradient surgery methods in PINNs. The proposed method ensures conflict-free applied updates, leading to substantial reductions in relative L2 error (up to 98.2%) across various PINN settings.

Abstract: Training Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) requires jointly optimizing physics residual and initial/boundary condition loss terms, which often induce conflicting gradients. Gradient surgery methods mitigate this issue by constructing directions from loss-specific gradients to reduce conflict before optimizer transformation. However, even when the constructed direction is conflict-free, this property may not be preserved after optimizer transformation. Let $a_t$ denote the direction constructed by gradient surgery, $u_t$ the optimizer proposal, and $\mathcal{C}_t$ the conflict-free cone induced by the loss-specific gradients. We show that modern optimizers can transform $a_t$ through mechanisms such as historical state, adaptive scaling, preconditioning, or decoupled weight decay, so $a_t \in \mathcal{C}_t$ does not generally imply $u_t \in \mathcal{C}_t$. We refer to this optimizer-induced discrepancy in conflict-freeness between $a_t$ and $u_t$ as Gradient-Update Mismatch (GUM). Accordingly, we propose Gradient-Update Alignment (GUA), which projects $u_t$ onto $\mathcal{C}_t$ to obtain the aligned update $p_t$ and applies $p_t$ to the parameters. When the optimizer maintains internal state, GUA further adjusts this state toward targets reconstructed from the applied update. We conduct extensive experiments and find that GUM is widespread across momentum, adaptive, and curvature-based optimizers, with conflict rates reaching up to 86.3%. Across all PINN settings, GUA achieves conflict-free applied updates and consistently improves various gradient surgery methods, reducing the relative $L_2$ error by up to 98.2% in individual settings. Data and code are available at https://github.com/JingXiao10/GUA.

</details>


### [233] [The Structure of Quantization Damage in LLMs: Why the Next Bit Should Be Spent Globally](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01587)
*Jundong Hu,Shekar Ramachandran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PTQ accuracy recovery is diffuse across layers for most LLMs, making global fine quantization granularity more effective than selectively protecting critical layers for the same precision budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training quantization reduces LLM serving costs but causes uneven accuracy loss that varies per model. The paper aims to understand where quantization damage occurs and how to optimally allocate limited precision budget for best accuracy recovery.

Method: Used causal mixed-precision intervention across 9 open-weight models in 4 architecture families. Raised each layer to 8-bit sequentially to measure accuracy recovery, testing hypotheses about quantization damage location (task circuits, compute locations, weight statistics). Compared global granularity improvement vs. local layer protection.

Result: Quantization damage recovery is diffuse: for 8 of 9 models, 75% gap recovery requires about half the layers (exception: Qwen3-8B). Global fine quantization granularity outperforms selective layer protection by 21-52 points across all group-128-compatible models. 8-bit quantization is near-lossless, and recovery patterns correlate within architecture families but not across families.

Conclusion: For limited precision budgets, global fine quantization granularity is superior to selectively protecting critical layers. Cheap signals don't reliably predict where restoring precision improves accuracy - causal intervention testing is necessary. Architecture family influences recovery patterns.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is widely used to reduce the cost of serving large language models (LLMs), but its accuracy cost is uneven and is often tuned per model. We study where quantization damage occurs and how to allocate a small additional precision budget. Using causal mixed-precision intervention as ground truth (raise each layer to 8-bit in turn and measure the accuracy it recovers) across 9 open-weight models in 4 architecture families, we test 3 intuitive hypotheses: that quantization damage lives in task circuits, where the model computes, or in weight statistics. None of them predicts which layers benefit from restored precision. Recovery is instead diffuse: for 8 of 9 models, recovering 75% of the gap takes roughly half the layers; the lone exception, Qwen3-8B, is sharply concentrated. At a matched precision budget, spending it globally on finer quantization granularity beats locally repairing the most recoverable layers for all 8 group-128-compatible models (all but OpenLLaMA, whose width rules out group-128), by 21-52 points, including the concentrated Qwen3-8B. We report 2 secondary findings: the residual is budget-limited (8-bit is near-lossless in our evaluation across RTN, GPTQ, and AWQ), and the location of peak recovery correlates with architecture within a family, though not across families. Within this budget setting, global granularity is a better default than selectively protecting critical layers. More broadly, cheap signals that correlate with quantization damage do not necessarily identify where restoring precision improves accuracy; this must be tested with causal intervention.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [234] [RAPIDMap: Rapid Multi-Agent Pipeline for Interpretable Disaster Mapping from Satellite and Street-view Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00046)
*Yifan Yang,Lei Zou*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: RAPIDMap is an AI pipeline that uses multiple agents to create zero-shot disaster maps from satellite and street-view imagery without manual tuning, working across different disaster types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI disaster mapping methods need lots of manual annotation, don't work well across different disaster types, and rely on single data sources. There's a need for rapid, reliable disaster mapping for emergency response.

Method: Uses a multi-agent pipeline with four specialized agents: Disaster Perception Agent (detects affected areas), Image Restoration Agent (improves damaged imagery), Damage Recognition Agent (identifies damage types), and Disaster Mapping Agent (creates structured maps). Combines satellite and street-view data for cross-modal analysis.

Result: The framework eliminates manual fine-tuning requirements, generalizes across multiple disaster categories, and generates structured, map-ready intelligence with recovery recommendations.

Conclusion: RAPIDMap addresses key limitations in existing disaster mapping by providing a rapid, zero-shot, interpretable solution that integrates multi-modal data and works across various disaster scenarios without extensive manual intervention.

Abstract: Rapid and reliable disaster mapping of impacted areas, damaged infrastructure, and affected populations is essential for emergency response and recovery. However, existing AI-based approaches often require extensive manual annotation, lack cross-hazard generalization, and rely on single-modal observations. To address these challenges, this paper proposes RAPIDMap, a rapid multi-agent pipeline for zero-shot interpretable disaster mapping from satellite and street-view imagery. The framework integrates four intelligent agents: Disaster Perception Agent (DPA), Image Restoration Agent (IRA), Damage Recognition Agent (DRA), and Disaster Mapping Agent (DMA). By combining remote sensing and street-view data, RAPIDMap eliminates the need for manual fine-tuning, generalizes across multiple disaster categories, and generates structured, map-ready disaster intelligence with recovery recommendations.

</details>


### [235] [Classic AI Scaffolding for LLM Social Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01167)
*Anatole Gershman*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: EpisodeSim: A hybrid LLM-agent architecture that combines LLMs with classic-AI structures for coherent social simulation, not just fluent turn generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can produce locally plausible social turns, but fluent next-turn generation alone is insufficient for social simulation. Human encounters like restaurant lunches and hotel check-ins are bounded social episodes with complex structures including roles, scripts, material state, obligations, commitments, timing, and closure conditions.

Method: EpisodeSim is a hybrid LLM-agent architecture that represents classic-AI structures as natural-language control state interpreted by LLM calls. It features a World Master component that maintains shared reality, constructs scenes, adjudicates proposed actions, tracks effects and obligations, and controls closure.

Result: Experiments with small qualitative ablations on two held-out settings support the design claim: LLM fluency provides local texture, but coherent social simulation benefits from persistent classic-AI-style scaffolding that organizes behavior over time.

Conclusion: For effective social simulation, hybrid architectures combining LLMs with structured, persistent scaffolding are necessary to go beyond fluent local turn generation and achieve coherent episode-level organization.

Abstract: Large language models can produce locally plausible social turns, but fluent next-turn generation is not enough for social simulation. Human encounters such as restaurant lunches and hotel check-ins are bounded social episodes with roles, scripts, material state, obligations, commitments, timing, and closure conditions. We present EpisodeSim, a hybrid LLM-agent architecture that represents classic-AI structures as natural-language control state interpreted by LLM calls. A World Master maintains shared reality, constructs scenes, adjudicates proposed actions, tracks effects and obligations, and controls closure. Experiments with small qualitative ablations on two held-out settings support a design claim: LLM fluency supplies local texture, but coherent social simulation benefits from persistent classic-AI-style scaffolding that organizes behavior over time.

</details>
